### [1] [TEXAS: Task-Expert-Aware Supervision for Downstream Mixture-of-Experts LLM Adaptation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06396)
*Guanzhi Deng,Haibo Wang,Kuan Wu,Xiangru Jian,Shing Yin Wong,Sichun Luo,Zhuoran Wang,Linqi Song*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces TEXAS, a method that leverages Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) routing patterns—conditioned on correctness—to discover task-relevant experts and to allocate stronger supervision to their activations during fine-tuning, yielding consistent performance gains across models and benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In MoE language models, only a subset of experts is activated per token, creating a rich routing signal that could be exploited during downstream adaptation. Existing methods typically identify task experts using aggregate routing frequencies, which capture how often experts are used but not whether their activations correlate with successful task completion. Moreover, they underutilize token-level expert activations as a way to guide where supervision (gradient signal) should be concentrated during fine-tuning. The paper aims to develop a principled way to (1) discover which experts matter for a specific task based on success vs. failure, and (2) adjust training so that these experts receive more focused, informative supervision, improving task performance without rigidly constraining routing behavior.

Method: The authors propose Task-Expert-Aware Supervision (TEXAS), which has two main components. First, they perform correctness-conditioned expert discovery: for a given task, they compare expert activation patterns on instances that the base MoE model solves correctly versus instances it gets wrong, identifying experts that are more strongly activated on successful examples. These are deemed task-relevant experts. Second, during fine-tuning, they modify supervision at the token level: for failed instances, they upweight the loss (i.e., supervision signal) on the answer tokens when those tokens activate the discovered task experts. This concentrates learning on tokens and experts that appear most associated with success, while leaving the overall routing distribution flexible and not restricting the model to a fixed subset of experts or enforcing a target routing pattern.

Result: On three different MoE language models and six benchmarks, TEXAS achieves the best or tied-best performance in 17 out of 18 evaluated settings. Averaged over tasks, TEXAS improves performance by roughly 1.3–1.5 points compared with the strongest existing baseline approach. Additional ablation studies show that both components—the correctness-conditioned discovery of task experts and the token-level supervision upweighting based on those experts—are important for the observed gains. Further analyses confirm that the discovered experts are indeed meaningfully related to task success and that the proposed supervision allocation strategy effectively exploits this relationship.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that correctness-conditioned analysis of MoE routing is an effective way to identify task-relevant experts and that using these experts to guide token-level supervision significantly improves downstream adaptation of MoE language models. TEXAS leverages the model’s existing routing behavior without constraining it to a fixed expert subset or imposing explicit routing targets, yet still yields consistent, measurable gains across architectures and benchmarks. This suggests that expert activations can serve as powerful signals for structuring learning and that similar correctness-aware routing analyses may benefit other modular or sparsely activated architectures.

Abstract: Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) language models route each token through a small subset of experts, making routing patterns useful for identifying task-relevant experts during downstream adaptation. Yet current approaches have two limitations: task experts are typically identified from aggregate routing statistics that reflect usage rather than association with successful task completion, and task-expert activations remain underexplored as signals for supervision allocation. We introduce Task-Expert-Aware Supervision (TEXAS), which combines correctness-conditioned task expert discovery with token-level supervision allocation. TEXAS compares expert activations on instances that the base model solves successfully and those it fails to solve, and retains experts more strongly activated on successful instances. During fine-tuning, it upweights answer tokens in failed instances when they activate these experts. TEXAS therefore leverages existing routing behavior without restricting adaptation to a fixed expert subset or imposing an explicit target routing distribution. Across three MoE models and six benchmarks, TEXAS achieves the best or tied-best performance in 17 of 18 settings and improves over the strongest baseline by 1.3--1.5 points on average. Ablations and further analyses validate both the discovered experts and the resulting supervision strategy.

</details>


### [2] [Separating Decision-Rule Misalignment from Readout-Coverage Limitations in Speech Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06409)
*Linkai Peng,Baorian Nuchged*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes a diagnostic framework to dissect where speech language models fail on paralinguistic (emotion) tasks, separating errors due to generation, decision rules, and representational readout.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing evaluations of speech language models on paralinguistic tasks use answer accuracy, which conflates different error sources: whether the model’s hidden states contain the needed information, whether the mapping from states to class logits is adequate, and whether the generation process properly uses the logits. A clearer understanding of where performance is lost is needed to improve models and evaluation.

Method: The authors introduce a “generation-aligned diagnostic ladder” that inspects four successive stages at the same answer token: (1) the emitted (generated) answer, (2) the option logits, (3) an affine readout of those logits, and (4) a linear readout from the hidden state. Comparing performance at these stages yields three gaps: endpoint (generation vs. logits), decision-rule (logits vs. affine readout), and readout-coverage (affine vs. linear state readout). They test five speech LMs on two emotion-recognition corpora, apply label-free logit correction, and conduct rank-matched analyses of emotion information in readout-external directions, controlling for acoustic descriptors.

Result: Across five systems and two emotion datasets, decoding emotion directly from hidden states outperforms generated answers by an average of 27.8 accuracy points. All ten evaluated conditions show positive decision-rule and readout-coverage gaps, indicating that both the decision rule and the readout layer lose usable information. A label-free logit correction consistently boosts generated accuracy, demonstrating that some decision-rule errors are fixable. Emotion information that lies outside the model’s native readout directions generalizes to unseen speakers and remains after controlling for standard acoustic features, but perturbing these external directions typically leaves generated answers largely unchanged.

Conclusion: Speech language models internally encode more paralinguistic (emotion) information than their generated answers reveal. Performance losses arise both from suboptimal decision rules and from incomplete state-to-answer readouts, rather than from representational absence. The proposed diagnostic ladder provides a way to localize these losses and to distinguish information availability from behavioral use, suggesting avenues—such as improved readouts or decision rules—for boosting paralinguistic performance without changing core representations.

Abstract: Speech language models are increasingly evaluated on paralinguistic tasks by the accuracy of prompted answers, but answer accuracy combines failures at different stages of the audio-to-answer computation. We introduce a generation-aligned diagnostic ladder that compares the emitted answer, the option logits, an affine readout of those logits, and a linear readout of the hidden state at the same answer token. Successive differences separate endpoint, decision-rule, and readout-coverage gaps. Across five systems and two emotion corpora, state decoding exceeds generation by 27.8 accuracy points on average, and both the decision-rule and readout-coverage gaps are positive in all ten conditions. A label-free logit correction improves generated accuracy in every condition, showing that part of the decision-rule gap is actionable. In rank-matched comparisons, emotion information outside the native readout generalizes to held-out speakers and survives controls for measured acoustic descriptors, but replacing the selected readout-external directions usually has little effect on emitted answers. These results distinguish information availability from behavioral use and localize performance losses across the decision rule and the state-to-answer readout.

</details>


### [3] [NTDH: Complex Reasoning for Comprehensive Affective Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06425)
*Tianlei Zhu,Zhiwei Liu,Yuyan Wang,Xiao-Yang Liu,Sophia Ananiadou*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper reframes affective analysis (sentiment & emotion) as an explicit reasoning task and introduces NTDH, a data-synthesis and training framework that generates high-quality reasoning traces to train a small LLM (Qwen3-8B), achieving strong performance with much less data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Affective analysis is hard because (1) it must handle heterogeneous output formats (continuous, ordinal, multi-label) across sentiment and emotion tasks, and (2) affective meaning depends on context and conflicting cues that need reconciliation, not simple label mapping. Current methods map text directly to labels without explicitly modeling this reconciliation process, and existing reasoning-trace synthesis methods are misaligned with the specific needs and evaluation tolerances of affective tasks and often mishandle or discard failure cases.

Method: The authors recast affective analysis as a complex reasoning problem where the model produces an interpretable reasoning trajectory before outputting labels, enabling a unified interface across different label spaces and an optimizable reward signal. They introduce NTDH, a framework for synthesizing and filtering affective reasoning traces with four key components: (1) Naturalisation: set the final training answer equal to the gold label so that outputs are correct by construction; (2) Tolerance-aware gate: accept or reject synthesized answers using the task’s own scoring margins (e.g., correlation/thresholds), ensuring alignment with official evaluation; (3) Domain-aware strategies: inject structured reasoning patterns and cues from affective science (e.g., valence/arousal, context cues) into the synthesis process; (4) Directional Hints: when the model is wrong, provide only the direction and type of the error (e.g., “too positive”, “missed one label”) without revealing the exact target label. They train Qwen3-8B first via supervised fine-tuning (SFT) on NTDH-constructed data and then refine it with GRPO (a policy-optimization method) using the same tolerance criteria used in evaluation, with a slightly looser acceptance gate for the multi-label task. An ablation study removes components of NTDH in turn to measure their impact on data quality and downstream performance.

Result: With 16,302 synthesized training records (about 14× fewer than datasets used for comparable instruction-tuned systems), the final GRPO-trained policy significantly improves over its SFT-only checkpoint on 5 of 6 official test metrics across sentiment and emotion tasks. It attains the strongest result on the emotion intensity regression (EI-reg) benchmark among compared systems, achieving a Pearson correlation of 0.862, and maintains competitive or improved performance on the other tasks.

Conclusion: Explicitly modeling affective analysis as a reasoning process, combined with carefully designed, tolerance- and domain-aware data synthesis (NTDH), can substantially improve performance of a relatively small LLM while using far less training data. The four NTDH components jointly raise the quality and task-alignment of reasoning traces, and the GRPO optimization under task-specific tolerances further boosts performance. This approach offers a unified interface across heterogeneous affective label spaces and demonstrates that high-quality, domain-tailored reasoning supervision is more important than large generic instruction datasets for affective modeling.

Abstract: Comprehensive affective analysis is challenging for two reasons: it spans heterogeneous prediction tasks with continuous, ordinal, and multi-label outputs, and affective meaning is context-dependent, requiring conflicting cues to be reconciled rather than mapped directly to labels. Existing methods learn this mapping directly and do not model the reconciliation explicitly. We recast the task as a complex-reasoning problem, which yields one output interface across heterogeneous label spaces and a trajectory over which a verifiable reward can be optimised; to our knowledge, this is the first such treatment covering both sentiment and emotion. The obstacle is on the data side: affective reasoning traces must be synthesised, and generic synthesis is misaligned with the targets, tolerances, and phenomena of affect, and discards or leaks its failure cases. We propose NTDH, which addresses these four failures. Naturalisation sets the training answer to the gold label, so it is correct by construction. A Tolerance-aware gate checks each answer against the task's own scoring margin. Domain-aware strategies refine the reasoning using ideas from affective science. Directional Hints report only the type and direction of an error, without exposing the target. We train Qwen3-8B with SFT and then GRPO under the same tolerance used for verification (up to a more permissive construction gate on the multi-label subtask), and a component ablation quantifies the data-quality effect of each part. Using 16,302 training records, about 14x fewer than comparable instruction-tuned systems, the final policy improves over its SFT checkpoint on five of six official-test metrics and achieves the strongest EI-reg result among the compared systems, at a Pearson correlation of 0.862.

</details>


### [4] [Recovering Lesion Parameters from Aphasic Picture Naming Error Profiles in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06429)
*Yong Yang,Roger Newman-Norlund,Xiang Guan,Saeed Ahmadi,Regan Willis,Nadra Salman,Kalil Warren,Sophie Arheix-Parras,Srihari Nelakuditi,Leonardo Bonilha,Christopher Rorden,Rutvik H. Desai,Julius Fridriksson*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper studies whether we can infer which artificial ‘lesions’ were applied inside a multimodal LLM from the pattern of its errors, and uses this to learn about redundancy and causal structure in transformer models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing interpretability methods describe correlations between internal activations and behavior but rarely test whether a particular internal state is causally sufficient to produce that behavior. Prior work showed that lesioning an LLM can induce picture-naming errors that resemble those of aphasia patients, but it remained unclear whether we can invert this mapping: given an observed error profile, can we reliably infer the underlying perturbation, and what would that tell us about how transformers implement functions and about redundancy across layers?

Method: The authors systematically perturb LLaVA‑Vicuna 13B with artificial lesions parameterized by three variables: transformer layer index, fraction of parameters modified, and noise magnitude (sigma), generating 4,840 distinct perturbation configurations. For each configuration they run a picture‑naming task and categorize the model’s responses into seven clinically inspired error types (correct, semantic, unrelated, formal, mixed, neologism, no‑response). These distributions over error types form an ‘error profile’. They then train a multi‑task neural network inverse model that takes an error profile as input and predicts the lesion parameters. Performance is evaluated on held‑out configurations and via counterfactual validation: using a fresh copy of the LLM, they apply the predicted lesion parameters and test how often the resulting error profile matches the target profile. They also apply the trained inverse model, without retraining, to real picture‑naming error profiles from 278 stroke survivors as an out‑of‑distribution test of whether recovered lesion parameters align with clinical syndromes.

Result: The inverse mapping from error profiles to lesion parameters is only partially identifiable. Across 10 independently trained inverse models, the model can reliably recover the modification percentage and noise sigma, but it recovers the layer index only coarsely—within a neighborhood rather than exactly. Despite this, counterfactual validation shows that using the recovered parameters on a new LLaVA‑Vicuna instance reproduces the target error profile in 81.4% of cases, indicating that many different layer locations can yield similar behavioral patterns when perturbation strength and noise are matched. When applied to human stroke patients’ error profiles, the recovered lesion parameters, especially perturbation intensity, are discriminative of aphasia syndromes, suggesting some clinically meaningful structure and generalization beyond the synthetic training distribution.

Conclusion: Behavioral error profiles in a picture‑naming task contain enough information to recover key aspects of internal perturbations in a large multimodal language model, particularly the strength and intensity of the lesion, but not its precise layer location. The combination of partial identifiability and high counterfactual fidelity supports the view that transformer layers are functionally redundant: similar behaviors can be produced by perturbing different layers as long as perturbation strength is appropriate. The proposed counterfactual validation approach offers a general framework for strengthening interpretability claims in LLMs by testing whether hypothesized internal causes are sufficient to reproduce observed behaviors, and the successful application to human aphasia data hints at a bridge between computational lesions and clinical neuropsychology.

Abstract: Interpretability methods for large language models (LLMs) describe internal state but do not directly test whether that state is causally sufficient to produce the observed behavior. In earlier work, we lesioned LLMs to produce error profiles in picture naming, a central task for assessing aphasia, and found that specific lesions produced errors resembling those of individual stroke survivors. Here we ask the inverse question: given an error profile, can the lesion parameters that produced it be recovered, and what does this inverse problem reveal about transformer computation? Lesions in LLaVA-Vicuna 13B were parameterized by layer index, modification percentage, and noise sigma across 4,840 configurations, and error profiles were characterized by a seven-category clinical taxonomy (correct, semantic, unrelated, formal, mixed, neologism, no-response). We trained a multi-task neural network to map error profiles back to perturbation parameters. The problem admitted a partial solution: across 10 independently trained inverse models, modification percentage and noise sigma were recoverable, whereas layer index was recoverable only within a neighborhood. In counterfactual validation, a fresh model instance perturbed with the recovered parameters reproduced the target behavior in 81.4% of cases. This dissociation between low layer recovery and high counterfactual fidelity is consistent with functional redundancy across transformer layers, a property not captured by standard interpretability methods. As an out-of-distribution test, we applied the trained model to picture-naming error profiles from 278 stroke survivors; recovered parameters were syndrome-discriminative, most strongly for perturbation intensity, indicating generalization beyond the training distribution. Counterfactual validation provides a general framework for LLM interpretability claims beyond inverse mapping.

</details>


### [5] [Do AI Personas Grow? Analyzing and Benchmarking Personality Evolution in LLM Agents After Life Events](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06485)
*Ming Wang,Peidong Wang,Xiaocui Yang,Daling Wang,Shi Feng,Fiona Fui-Hoon Nah,Ee-Peng Lim*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper investigates how personality-conditioned LLM agents change their Big Five traits after simulated life events, compares these changes with human longitudinal findings, and introduces a benchmark (BFI-Adapt) to evaluate the realism and robustness of such personality dynamics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Personality-based LLM agents are used for long-term, socially rich tasks such as emotional support and role-play, where consistency and believable evolution of personality over time are important. While it is known that LLMs’ apparent personalities can shift with context, we lack a systematic understanding of how these shifts depend on specific life events, traits, personas, and models, and how realistic they are compared with human personality psychology. The authors aim to fill this gap to better characterize and improve lifelong, personality-conditioned agents.

Method: The authors simulate 11 major life events for personality-conditioned LLM agents and measure pre/post changes on the Big Five personality traits, using psychometric-style prompting. They analyze event-induced personality trajectories along four diagnostic axes, and compare them to human longitudinal evidence on how traits typically change after similar events, including both direction and magnitude of change. They test prompts with different gender and cultural-region specifications and examine the dispersion of personality across personas. They propose BFI-Adapt, a benchmark that scores directional fidelity of trait changes and uses it to evaluate and rank 14 LLMs. A validation suite checks robustness: comparing shifts to no-event retest noise, testing paraphrased prompts, examining convergence with scenario-based behavioral decisions, and verifying persistence across intervening dialogues.

Result: PC-Agents show measurable personality shifts following life events, but these shifts occur at similar rates regardless of whether human data provide a clear expected change direction. When the direction of change matches human findings, effect sizes are typically smaller than those seen in longitudinal human studies. Gender and cultural-region prompts have minimal moderating influence. Personality variation across personas is much narrower (3–4x compressed) than in human samples. Using BFI-Adapt, the authors rank 14 models and demonstrate that the observed changes are robust: they are larger than no-event retest noise, stable to prompt paraphrasing, somewhat but not strongly aligned with behavioral choices, and remain after unrelated intervening conversations.

Conclusion: Current personality-conditioned LLM agents can reliably exhibit event-conditioned changes in Big Five traits, but these changes lack the rich, varied structure observed in human personality development. The agents tend to approximate an average or smoothed version of human personality dynamics rather than capturing the full distribution and nuanced patterns across traits, events, and individuals. BFI-Adapt provides a standardized way to quantify and compare models’ fidelity in simulating psychologically grounded personality change, highlighting that improving the realism and diversity of personality evolution is a key open challenge for lifelong LLM agents.

Abstract: Personality-conditioned LLM agents (PC-Agents) are increasingly used in emotional support, social simulation, and role-playing, motivating the development of lifelong agents that remain coherent over extended interactions. A key component of such coherence is personality evolution: agents should undergo plausible, psychology-grounded changes as they experience life events in different contexts. Although prior work shows that LLM personalities can shift under contextual perturbations, how these shifts vary across traits, events, personas, and models remains poorly understood. We study event-induced personality change after 11 major life events, using the Big Five traits as a psychometric anchor and interpreting the resulting trajectories against longitudinal evidence from human personality psychology. Across four diagnostic axes, PC-Agents exhibit measurable trait shifts at similar rates for event-trait pairs with and without documented human change directions. Even when shifts follow the expected direction, their magnitudes usually fall below human effect-size ranges. Gender and cultural-region prompts show little moderating effect, while persona-level dispersion is compressed three- to four-fold relative to human samples. To enable systematic comparison, we introduce BFI-Adapt, a reusable benchmark for scoring the directional fidelity of event-induced personality change, and use it to rank 14 models. A validation suite shows that the measured shifts exceed no-event retest noise, remain stable under independently paraphrased prompts, exhibit limited and model-dependent convergence with scenario-based behavioral choices, and persist across intervening unrelated dialogue. Together, these checks establish the measured trajectories as robust event-conditioned response patterns. Our results suggest that current PC-Agents simulate the mean of human personality dynamics, but not its shape.

</details>


### [6] [ConstructCIE: A Dataset for Extracting Causal Information from Construction Accident Narratives](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06495)
*Hung Nguyen,Jaehoon Lee,Namgyun Kim,Kuan-Hao Huang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: They build and release a new annotated dataset (ConstructCIE) for extracting causal information from construction accident reports, test different models on a hierarchical extraction task, and find that models are good at high-level accident labeling but weak at accurate evidence-span extraction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Construction accident reports contain rich but implicit and scattered causal information that could support safety analysis and prevention. However, existing resources and models are limited for systematically extracting detailed, structured causes and evidence from these narratives, especially in a hierarchical way. A dedicated dataset and evaluation are needed to understand model capabilities and gaps.

Method: They manually annotate OSHA construction accident reports with a hierarchical schema: accident types, causal factors, sub-causal factors, and supporting text spans (evidence). They then cast this as an end-to-end hierarchical Causal Information Extraction task and evaluate both supervised sequence-tagging models and instruction-tuned large language models, comparing different extraction strategies (e.g., JHE vs. IHE) and analyzing error types such as evidence selection and span boundaries.

Result: Most evaluated models can accurately predict overall accident types and capture broad causal meanings, but they struggle with precise, span-level extraction of supporting evidence. The JHE strategy generally yields better exact and soft span-matching performance, while the IHE strategy can sometimes achieve higher keyword-level F1. Error patterns differ across methods, but mis-selection of evidence and inaccurate span boundaries are consistently common.

Conclusion: Current models are not yet reliable for fine-grained Causal Information Extraction from construction accident narratives: while they handle high-level labels reasonably well, they lack accurate domain grounding and fine-grained evidence-span extraction. Improving domain-specific modeling and evidence selection is essential for robust use of such systems in construction safety analysis.

Abstract: Construction accident narratives contain rich causal information, but the evidence is often implicit, long-span, and distributed. We introduce ConstructCIE, a manually annotated dataset for Causal Information Extraction from OSHA construction accident reports. The dataset uses a hierarchical schema for accident types, causal factors, sub-causal factors, and supporting evidence spans. We evaluate supervised sequence taggers and instruction-tuned LLMs in an end-to-end hierarchical extraction setting. Results show that most evaluated models achieve strong accident-type prediction and recover broad causal meaning but remain limited in precise span-level extraction. JHE generally achieves stronger exact and soft matching, while IHE sometimes achieves higher keyword F1. Error distributions vary by extraction strategy, but evidence-selection and span-boundary errors remain common. These findings show that reliable Causal Information Extraction for construction accidents requires stronger domain grounding and more accurate evidence extraction.

</details>


### [7] [Measuring the Cross-Lingual Comprehension Gap: How the language of the evidence shapes what language models understand](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06506)
*Rafael da Silva,Jeff Eicher*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces and measures the Cross-Lingual Comprehension Gap (CLCG): how much an LLM’s answer quality drops when identical content/questions are asked in non-English languages versus English.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most LLM evaluations are English-centric but implicitly assume that the same capabilities hold in other languages. Existing multilingual benchmarks usually change multiple factors at once (language, content, question, and evaluation), so they cannot cleanly measure how much performance loss is due purely to language. The authors want a precise, controlled estimate of how much response quality degrades when moving away from English, especially for lower-resource languages.

Method: They construct and use ParallelQA-18, a professionally human-translated parallel QA corpus: the same 150 articles and questions are available in 18 languages (English as reference, Portuguese as a strong high-resource non-English baseline, plus 16 target languages spanning resource levels per Joshi et al. 2020). Five different LLMs from five labs are evaluated with a within-item design where only passage language varies. They compute Token-F1 scores on more complex open-ended questions, then estimate CLCG as the difference between English and pooled target-language Token-F1 micro-means, with article-cluster bootstrap confidence intervals. They also compute macro summaries, gaps net of Portuguese, and correlate language-level CLCG with language resource class. Finally, they run blinded paired human evaluations comparing higher-resource versus lower-resource language answers.

Result: The overall pooled CLCG is 0.078 (95% CI 0.072–0.084), roughly a 17% drop versus the English score. The equal-language macro CLCG is 0.077. When Portuguese is treated as baseline and removed, the macro gap across remaining languages is 0.016 (95% CI 0.013–0.020). The CLCG per language is significantly negatively correlated with language resource class (rho = -0.594, p = 0.015, n = 16), meaning lower-resource languages suffer larger gaps. In blinded human preference tests, answers in higher-resource languages are preferred in 61.6% of decisive comparisons, with an estimated preference probability of 0.655 (95% CI 0.558–0.741).

Conclusion: LLMs that perform well in English lose notable answer quality when handling the same content in other languages, particularly lower-resource ones. English-based evaluations therefore overstate the real-world performance experienced by many users, and language should be explicitly controlled and measured in model assessment. Capabilities demonstrated in English cannot be assumed to transfer uniformly across languages.

Abstract: Language models are often evaluated as though capabilities demonstrated in English remain equally available when the same content is presented in other languages. Traditional multilingual benchmarks rarely isolate language while holding content, question, reference answer, model, and evaluation unit constant. We define the Cross-Lingual Comprehension Gap (CLCG) as the reduction in response quality when the same content and question are presented in a target language rather than in English.
  Using ParallelQA-18, a professionally human-translated parallel corpus, we evaluate five models from five laboratories on a stratified sample of 150 articles across 18 languages (English reference; Portuguese high-resource baseline; 16 targets spanning Joshi et al. 2020 classes 0-4). A within-item design varies only passage language. The primary estimator contrasts English versus pooled target-language Token-F1 micro-means on higher-complexity open-ended questions, with article-cluster bootstrap intervals.
  The primary pooled CLCG is 0.078 (95% CI 0.072-0.084), about a 17% reduction relative to the English score; the equal-language macro summary is 0.077. Net of Portuguese, the macro gap is 0.016 (95% CI 0.013-0.020). Language-level CLCG is negatively associated with Joshi resource class (rho = -0.594, p = 0.015, n = 16). In blinded paired human evaluations, higher-resource responses are preferred in 61.6% of decisive judgments (estimated preference probability 0.655, 95% CI 0.558-0.741).
  Capabilities shown in English should not be assumed to transfer equally to other languages; English-centered evaluations may overestimate quality for users of low-resource languages.

</details>


### [8] [GRASP: Reinforcing Language Model Anonymizers with Group Relative Policy Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06526)
*Sajjad Ghiasvand,Nader Sehatbakhsh*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: They propose GRASP, a small on-device model trained with a new policy optimization scheme to anonymize text, outperforming prior distillation-based anonymizers in the privacy–utility trade-off while being cheaper and locally deployable.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large language models can infer sensitive personal attributes (age, location, occupation) from normal text, which turns everyday writing into a privacy risk. Existing adversarial anonymization sends raw text to powerful third-party models and relies on offline distillation (e.g., via DPO), which imitates teacher choices without directly optimizing the desired balance between privacy and text utility. There is a need for an on-device method that directly optimizes the privacy–utility objective without exposing data to external services.

Method: They introduce GRASP (Group-Relative Anonymization via Self-refinement Policy-optimization), which uses a single small model that simultaneously plays three roles: anonymizer, adversary, and utility judge. It is trained using Group Relative Policy Optimization with a self-generated reward that encourages hiding sensitive attributes while preserving semantic meaning. The training design explicitly tries to prevent reward hacking. The system is instantiated with Llama-3.1-8B as the base model, and its performance is evaluated using multiple independent large language model judges and against strong adversarial anonymization baselines driven by frontier models.

Result: On top of Llama-3.1-8B, GRASP improves the privacy–utility trade-off compared with a DPO-distilled baseline across three independent LLM judges. When compared with adversarial anonymization driven by stronger frontier teacher models such as Gemini 2.5 Flash and Claude, GRASP achieves comparable or better overall privacy–utility performance while removing substantially more private information. It also runs fully on-device and costs about 1% of using the original GPT-4o teacher model for anonymization.

Conclusion: GRASP demonstrates that a single small, fully on-device model trained with group-relative policy optimization and self-generated rewards can effectively anonymize text, surpassing DPO-distilled approaches and competing with frontier-model-driven anonymization. It improves privacy, maintains utility, avoids sending raw data to third-party services, and operates at significantly lower cost, offering a practical path toward privacy-preserving text anonymization on user devices.

Abstract: Large language models can infer sensitive personal attributes, such as age, location, and occupation, from ordinary text, turning everyday writing into a privacy risk. Adversarial anonymization defends against this by rewriting a text with a capable language model that also plays the attacker, but it needs a powerful model at inference time and thus sends private text to a third party, the very exposure anonymization should prevent. Recent work distills this behavior into a small on-device model using supervised fine-tuning and direct preference optimization (DPO), but DPO only imitates the teacher's offline choices and never directly optimizes the privacy--utility objective we care about. We introduce \textbf{GRASP} (\textbf{G}roup-\textbf{R}elative \textbf{A}nonymization via \textbf{S}elf-refinement \textbf{P}olicy-optimization), which reinforces the local anonymizer online with Group Relative Policy Optimization. A single small model acts as anonymizer, adversary, and utility judge, trained against a self-generated reward that hides attributes while preserving meaning, with a design that guards against reward hacking. Trained on Llama-3.1-8B, \ours{} improves the privacy--utility trade-off over the DPO-distilled baseline, consistently across three independent LLM judges. Against adversarial anonymization driven by frontier models such as Gemini~2.5~Flash and Claude, it achieves a comparable or better overall trade-off while removing substantially more private information, and it runs entirely on-device at roughly $1\%$ of the GPT-4o teacher's cost.

</details>


### [9] [Lost in Interpolation: Why Predictive Feedback Fails in Diffusion Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06529)
*Lavanya Nigam,Ishaan Bansal,Aryan Sood,Vidit Aggarwal,Gaurav Kumar Nayak*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper identifies that MDLM embedding spaces behave hyperspherically rather than Euclidean, making standard linear soft-masking suboptimal. It proposes Spherical Soft-Masking (S-SM), which uses spherical geometry (Fréchet mean + SLERP) to blend mask and token predictions, improving convergence-time performance and generation quality without harming entropy or convergence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Masked Diffusion Language Models rely on soft-masking that linearly interpolates between mask and token embeddings in Euclidean space. However, if the true embedding geometry is closer to a hypersphere, this linear assumption can slow convergence or degrade training. The authors are motivated to better understand MDLM embedding geometry and design a masking mechanism that respects the observed structure, in order to improve training stability and generative quality, particularly under constrained inference-time step budgets.

Method: 1) Empirically analyze MDLM embedding space, measuring angles between mask and predicted-token embeddings and examining norm distributions across vocabulary-frequency ranks to infer geometric structure. 2) Observe near-constant angles (~73°) and flat norms, suggesting a hyperspherical manifold. 3) Propose Spherical Soft-Masking (S-SM): compute a Fréchet mean of top-k predicted token embeddings on the hypersphere, then use spherical linear interpolation (SLERP) between this mean direction and the mask direction, and finally rescale to the original mask norm. 4) Implement S-SM as a drop-in replacement for standard LERP-based soft-masking. 5) Evaluate via continued pre-training of a 169M-parameter MDLM checkpoint, testing across a range of inference-time step budgets and comparing to vanilla MDLM and TopK/LERP baselines with metrics such as MAUVE, generative perplexity, output entropy, and convergence behavior.

Result: The analysis confirms that MDLM embeddings exhibit hyperspherical characteristics: mask and predicted-token embeddings maintain a stable ~73° angle throughout training, and embedding norms remain largely constant across vocabulary-frequency rank. Replacing LERP with S-SM (Fréchet mean + SLERP + norm restoration) avoids the training degradation seen with LERP feedback. Experimentally, S-SM achieves up to 2x MAUVE improvement over the vanilla MDLM baseline and 27.5–56.1% gains over TopK/LERP across different sampling budgets. It also consistently reduces generative perplexity by 16.9–19.6% relative to baseline, while output entropy and overall convergence dynamics are effectively unchanged.

Conclusion: MDLM embedding spaces are better modeled as hyperspherical rather than Euclidean, making standard linear soft-masking mathematically mismatched to the underlying geometry. By respecting this geometry through Spherical Soft-Masking—using Fréchet means and SLERP—the model achieves better generative quality and lower perplexity without sacrificing convergence or diversity, and it can be applied as a simple drop-in replacement for existing LERP-based soft-masking in MDLMs.

Abstract: Soft-masking accelerates the convergence of Masked Diffusion Language Models (MDLMs). Existing formulations build this blend with linear interpolation (LERP) in the raw embedding space, which implicitly treats that space as Euclidean. We analyze the embedding space of MDLMs and find that the mask and predicted-token embeddings maintain a near-constant angle of (\approx 73^\circ) throughout training, while embedding norms remain essentially flat across vocabulary-frequency rank. These indicate a hyperspherical geometry, for which LERP is the wrong interpolation primitive. We introduce Spherical Soft-Masking (S-SM), a drop-in replacement that aggregates the top-(k) predictions with a Fr'echet mean on the hypersphere and blends this mean with the mask direction using spherical linear interpolation (SLERP), then restores the native mask norm. We evaluate S-SM on continued pre-training of a released 169M-parameter MDLM checkpoint across a wide range of inference-time step budgets, SLERP feedback avoids the training degradation that LERP feedback induces and delivers MAUVE gains of up to 2x over the vanilla MDLM baseline and 27.5-56.1% over TopK/LERP at various sampling budgets, alongside consistently lower generative perplexity (16.9-19.6% over the baseline), while leaving output entropy and convergence essentially unchanged.

</details>


### [10] [Confidence Estimation for Financial Vision-Language Models in Chart and Document Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06532)
*Reza Khanmohammadi,Simerjot Kaur,Charese H. Smiley,Ivan Brugere,Mohammad M. Ghassemi*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: This paper studies how to decide which answers from large vision-language models (LVLMs) reading financial visuals can be trusted and safely automated, focusing on confidence estimation and deferral rather than raw accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In financial settings, LVLMs read charts, tables, and documents where even a single wrong number can have serious consequences. Models sometimes produce authoritative answers without actually using the visual evidence. Practitioners need mechanisms to know which model outputs are trustworthy enough to act on automatically and which should be escalated to human review.

Method: The authors benchmark seven confidence estimation methods (three inference-time baselines and four trained internal probes) across five open-weight LVLMs and four settings derived from three financial visual QA benchmarks, including a bilingual one. All probes are trained only on natural-image data and then applied to financial tasks without any domain adaptation, to test out-of-distribution transfer. They analyze calibration vs ranking quality, estimator performance across model–task combinations, language robustness structure, and performance of confidence-based deferral under different error budgets.

Result: (1) Inference-only baselines can rank correct above incorrect answers reasonably well but are severely miscalibrated and overconfident; only trained probes provide scores that allow usable decision thresholds. (2) Confidence reliability is structured: the best estimator depends strongly on both LVLM and task, and no single estimator dominates. Apparent bilingual robustness is largely an artifact that disappears when assessing each language–model pair separately. (3) Under deferral with explicit error budgets, automation is primarily limited by the model’s base competence; confidence only narrows this. Deferral can safely automate a substantial part of easy conditions but almost none of the hardest under a strict 5% error budget.

Conclusion: Effective use of LVLMs in high-stakes financial visual QA requires calibrated, thresholdable confidence estimates and model-specific deferral policies. Two trained probes meet these calibration needs, and a grounding-aware probe uniquely downweights answers that are not visually grounded, helping distinguish genuine, evidence-based answers from fluent guesses and enabling safer automation.

Abstract: LVLMs are increasingly used to read financial charts, tables, and documents, where a single misread figure can move a decision and the most authoritative-looking answer is sometimes one the model produced without reading the exhibit. The operational question is therefore trust, not accuracy: which answers can be acted on, and which escalated to a reviewer. We evaluate seven confidence estimators, three inference-only and four trained internal probes, across five open-weight LVLMs and four conditions from three financial visual question-answering benchmarks, one bilingual; every probe is trained only on natural images and applied to finance without adaptation, so the results measure out-of-distribution transfer. Three findings hold. First, the scarce property is calibration, not ranking: the inference baselines rank correct above incorrect answers competitively but are badly overconfident, calibration error far above what a threshold can tolerate, and only the trained probes produce a thresholdable score. Second, reliability is structured rather than global, along two axes a practitioner can read directly: the best estimator shifts with both model and task, none leading more than eight of twenty (model, condition) cells, and a controlled bilingual contrast exposes an apparent language robustness as a composition artifact that dissolves once models are read one at a time. Third, cast as deferral under an error budget, how much can be safely automated is set first by the model's competence and only narrowed by its confidence, so deferral clears a real share of the easiest condition and almost none of the hardest, near zero at a strict 5% budget. Two trained probes carry the calibration a deferral policy needs, and among them only the grounding-aware one lowers its confidence on answers a model gives without using the figure, separating detected non-grounding from a fluent guess.

</details>


### [11] [Don't `Well, Actually' Me Unless You Know What You're Talking About: Weak Presupposition Verification Degrades General QA Performance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06539)
*Shenran Wang,Vered Shwartz,Hila Gonen*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper studies how well large language models handle questions with false presuppositions, finding that methods that improve performance on such trick questions often harm performance on normal questions due to overly aggressive fact-checking modules.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current evaluations of false-presupposition question answering (FPQA) focus almost exclusively on questions that contain false assumptions and over-represent them relative to real-world distributions. This inflates perceived performance and ignores how methods behave on ordinary, true-presupposition questions. The authors want to understand this trade-off and highlight that better FPQA scores may not translate to better real-world QA systems.

Method: The authors conduct extensive experiments across multiple LLM families, model sizes, and FPQA benchmarks. They compare standard FPQA approaches—typically involving explicit presupposition extraction plus fact-checking—on both false-presupposition questions (FPQs) and true-presupposition questions (TPQs). They analyze how fact-checking modules influence acceptance or rejection of presuppositions and measure the impact on both FPQ and TPQ performance.

Result: They find a consistent trade-off: methods that achieve higher scores on benchmarks dominated by FPQs typically do worse on TPQs. The main culprit is weak or noisy fact-checking components, which not only flag false presuppositions but also incorrectly reject many true ones. This leads to unnecessary abstentions or corrections on normal questions, degrading overall QA quality.

Conclusion: Benchmark designs and FPQA methods must be reconsidered. Over-optimizing for FPQ-heavy benchmarks encourages systems that aggressively reject presuppositions, hurting performance on normal questions. Future FPQA approaches should focus on more accurate, calibrated fact-checking and on evaluation setups that mirror realistic mixes of false and true presupposition questions, so that improvements on FPQA translate into better real-world QA behavior.

Abstract: False-presupposition QA (FPQA) tests LLMs on their ability to identify false presuppositions in questions and abstain or correct them rather than reinforcing false assumptions. The common approach reduces the task to prompting LLMs to extract presuppositions and fact checking each presupposition. While the performance on dedicated benchmarks keeps improving, evaluation largely focuses on questions with false presuppositions (FPQs) while ignoring the performance on ``normal'' questions (TPQs). Since many benchmarks over-represent FPQs compared to their natural occurrence, the result is that performance on these benchmarks doesn't reflect real-world QA performance. Through extensive experiments across various model families, sizes, and benchmarks, we show that methods that perform better on FPQs tend to perform worse on TPQs. Our analysis reveals this is the result of weak fact checking modules that reject also true presuppositions. We hope our findings will help guide future work toward FPQA methods that generalize well to realistic settings.

</details>


### [12] [TradeVerse: A Longitudinal Benchmark of Political Negotiation in International Trade](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06549)
*Debodeep Banerjee,Amitangshu Dasgupta*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces TradeVerse, a benchmark built from WTO trade negotiation transcripts to test LLMs on longitudinal, multi-turn political trade discussions across three tasks: product classification, country identification, and response generation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM benchmarks for institutional and political texts focus on isolated documents or single-step tasks, while real-world political negotiations are multi-round, longitudinal processes where each turn depends on the full prior context. There is a need for a benchmark that reflects this sequential, argumentative nature in the domain of international trade negotiations.

Method: The authors reconstruct minutes from 1,170 WTO meetings covering 5 groups and 89 product groups, forming multi-round negotiation trajectories called specific trade concerns. From these proceedings, they automatically derive labels without manual annotation and design three tasks: (1) predict the HS chapter codes of products discussed in a meeting based on full longitudinal records; (2) infer the responding country’s identity from anonymized meeting content; and (3) generate the responding country’s final-round statement, effectively role-playing that actor in context.

Result: Through experiments with current LLMs on TradeVerse, the authors find that these models struggle notably with the benchmark’s tasks, revealing difficulties in tracking long negotiation histories, correctly linking them to product categories and country identities, and generating contextually appropriate final responses.

Conclusion: TradeVerse is presented as, to the authors’ knowledge, the first benchmark specifically targeting LLM understanding of longitudinal political trade negotiations. The results indicate a substantial gap between current LLM capabilities and the complex reasoning and memory needed for such multi-round, institutional negotiation settings, positioning TradeVerse as a challenging testbed for future model and method development.

Abstract: LLMs are increasingly being applied to tasks involving institutional and political texts, but existing benchmarks evaluate them on isolated documents or single tasks. In realpolitik, negotiations are longitudinal data, where participating parties can align or argue over multiple iterations and each turn is an outcome of the previous turns, hence, understanding one turn requires tracking everything before it. We introduce TradeVerse, a benchmark built from the World Trade Organisation (WTO) specific trade concerns, where member states challenge one another and exchange arguments over multiple rounds, sometimes for years. We, in TradeVerse, reconstruct minutes of $1170$ meetings, spanning across 5 groups and $89$ product groups and define three tasks: first, the system has to analyze the longitudinal meeting records and predict the harmonized system codes (HS chapters) of the products under discussion in the particular meeting, second, we examine whether the system, upon analyzing the anonymized content of the meeting, can guess the name of the responding country and third, we ask the system to play the role of the responding country and provide the statement for the very last round. All labels are recovered directly from the proceedings, requiring no manual annotation. Our experiments highlight the challenges these tasks pose for current LLMs. To the best of our knowledge, TradeVerseis the first benchmark to investigate potential of LLMs in understanding longitudinal political trade negotiations.

</details>


### [13] [Beyond "AI Language": The case for the idiolectal nature of LLM output](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06589)
*Karolina Rudnicka,Thomas Stephan Juzek*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper shows that different large language models (LLMs) each have their own distinctive writing style (“idiolect”), and that styles shift across model generations, rather than forming one uniform “AI language.”


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Researchers and practitioners often talk about “AI language” as if all LLM outputs are stylistically homogeneous. However, this overlooks possible model-specific styles and how they evolve over time. Understanding these differences matters for linguistic theory, detection of AI-generated text, and forensic or variationist applications.

Method: The authors analyse two corpora of LLM-generated texts on social topics: one from 2024 covering six models (Improta et al. 2024) and a newly generated 2026 corpus with six contemporary models, prompted in the same way. They apply computational linguistic descriptors and stylometric principal component analysis (PCA) to quantify and compare stylistic features, including measures such as contraction frequencies, across models and across the two time points.

Result: Stylometric PCA and other computational descriptors show (1) a clear generational shift in style between the 2024 and 2026 model cohorts, and (2) stable, distinctive linguistic profiles for each individual model within a cohort. For instance, contraction frequencies in the 2026 models range widely from around 1,200 to over 30,000 per million words, even though the texts respond to similar prompts.

Conclusion: LLM outputs are best understood as having idiolect-like, model-specific linguistic signatures layered on top of broader, generation-level patterns. Conceptualising LLM text in terms of idiolects opens up productive avenues for studying language variation and change in AI systems and has practical applications for AI-text detection, forensic linguistics, and usage-based linguistic research.

Abstract: While large language model outputs are frequently analysed as a collective super variety termed "AI language," this chapter argues that this perspective coexists with distinct, model-specific linguistic signatures akin to human idiolects. We analyse two datasets of LLM-generated texts on societal topics: a 2024 corpus of six models (Improta et al. 2024) and a newly generated 2026 corpus using the same prompts featuring six contemporary models. Our findings, utilising computational descriptors and stylometric principal component analysis reveal a generational shift between the style of the 2024 and 2026 cohorts, while demonstrating that each individual model maintains a unique linguistic profile. This multi-layered interplay is illustrated by contraction frequencies, which vary from over 1,200 to over 30,000 per million words within the same cohort of models (2026). Ultimately, we conclude that treating LLM output as idiolectal in nature provides a valuable framework with potential implications for research on variation and change, LLM-generated text detection, forensic linguistics and usage-based approaches to language.

</details>


### [14] [Pre-Inference Routing for Cost-Efficient Document Field Extraction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06607)
*Sreerekha Rajendran*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper studies when and how to route documents between a cheap and an expensive extraction model using pre-extraction difficulty prediction, showing routing only helps in certain genres and conditions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Using a single, strong extraction model for all documents is simple but inefficient: it wastes resources on easy documents and may still underperform on difficult ones. The authors want to know if we can cut cost without losing accuracy by predicting document difficulty up front and selectively using a cheaper or stronger extractor.

Method: They design inexpensive, document-based difficulty signals (e.g., image quality, layout, bag-of-words) and train a router that, given these signals, decides whether to send a document to a cheap or a strong extractor. They formalize two necessary conditions under which routing can help, build a practical test to check these conditions, and evaluate routing on five document genres using calibrated routers and multiple model pairs with different cost ratios. They compare routers using engineered interpretable features versus simple bag-of-words and test cross-dataset transfer.

Result: Routing yields substantial cost savings when the cheaper model both fails frequently enough and its failures are predictable from observable document features. In such cases, they achieve 31–33% cost reduction on receipts and 77% on degraded ad-buy forms while keeping F1 within 0.02 of always using the large model. Routing brings no benefit when documents are uniformly easy (clean digital invoices, nutrition labels). A small labeled pilot can accurately predict in advance whether routing will be beneficial. Bag-of-words routers perform similarly to more engineered feature routers, and routers must be retrained per dataset, as they do not transfer well across datasets even within the same genre. Their findings are consistent across two model pairs with 5x and 3x cost gaps.

Conclusion: Routing between cheap and strong document extractors is not universally beneficial; it only helps in genres where the cheap model’s failures are both common and predictable from low-cost signals. In such settings, calibrated routing can substantially reduce inference cost with minimal quality loss. However, routers must be tailored to each dataset, and limitations come primarily from the document genre characteristics rather than router architecture, so practitioners should run a small pilot and use interpretable features to decide whether to deploy routing at all.

Abstract: Most document-extraction systems use a single model for all documents. This is simple but can be costly for easy cases and less effective for difficult ones. We examine whether we can predict a document's difficulty before extraction using inexpensive, document-based signals, and use this to choose between a cheaper and a stronger extractor. We find that routing only helps if two conditions hold: the cheaper model fails often enough to make routing worthwhile, and those failures can be predicted from visible features such as image quality and layout. We turn these into a practical test and apply it to five genres. When both conditions are met, the calibrated router reduces cost by 31-33% on receipts and 77% on degraded ad-buy forms while keeping quality within 0.02 F1 of always choosing the large model. Routing does not help if either condition is missing, as with clean digital invoices or nutrition labels that are already easy to read. A small labeled pilot can predict whether routing will work, and in the two cases where we ran it first, the prediction was correct. A simple bag-of-words router works about as well as engineered features, showing that the main limit is the genre, not the router design; we use interpretable features to help explain which genres can be routed. The router must be retrained for each dataset and does not transfer across datasets, even within the same genre. These results hold for two model pairs with cost differences of 5x and 3x.

</details>


### [15] [Factorized Hypothesis Search for Evidence-to-Taxonomy Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06614)
*Linhai Ma,Ethan F. Wei,Xueqing Peng,Yan Wang,Lingfei Qian,Víctor Gutiérrez-Basulto*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper addresses the problem that many retrieval systems fail when input evidence does not explicitly state the target concept, introducing Factorized Hypothesis Search (FHS) to bridge this gap and improve taxonomy tagging and clinical coding performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large-taxonomy retrieval typically assumes that queries directly express the target concept (e.g., a diagnosis code or taxonomy label). In practice, inputs are often indirect, like table cells whose meaning depends on row, column, type, and context, causing a retrieval readiness gap where relevant concepts are ranked low. The paper is motivated by the need to better interpret such indirect evidence so that retrieval systems can reliably surface the correct taxonomy items at the top of the ranking.

Method: The authors propose Factorized Hypothesis Search (FHS), which represents multiple partial interpretations of the input along named semantic dimensions (e.g., row semantics, column semantics, datatype, context). FHS maintains these partial hypotheses, uses them to construct structured queries, performs multi-hypothesis retrieval in parallel, and then verifies candidates at the dimension level. They also compare this factorized approach against alternatives such as free-text ensembles and sequential refinement methods.

Result: On two tasks—financial taxonomy tagging and CodiEsp clinical coding—FHS outperforms non-oracle baselines on key ranking and classification metrics: Recall@1, mean reciprocal rank (MRR), and final accuracy. When the factorized hypothesis mechanism is replaced with a simple free-text ensemble, performance degrades substantially, particularly in ranking top results. Additionally, a sequential refinement process does not yield improvements over FHS's parallel first-round approach, highlighting the efficiency and effectiveness of the proposed method.

Conclusion: The study concludes that addressing the retrieval readiness gap by explicitly modeling multiple semantic dimensions with factorized hypotheses improves large-taxonomy retrieval from indirect evidence. FHS’s structured, parallel hypothesis search yields superior head-ranking and accuracy compared to non-oracle baselines and alternative designs such as free-text ensembles or sequential refinement, making it a strong approach for tasks like financial taxonomy tagging and clinical coding.

Abstract: Large-taxonomy retrieval often assumes that the input already expresses the target concept. In many settings, however, the input is indirect evidence, such as a table cell whose meaning depends on its row, column, datatype, and context. We call this mismatch the retrieval readiness gap. Our analysis shows that the current index retrieves the target reliably when its semantics are explicit, while raw evidence often leaves it deep in the ranking. We propose Factorized Hypothesis Search (FHS), which maintains multiple partial interpretations over named semantic dimensions. These hypotheses support structured query rendering, multi-hypothesis retrieval, and dimension-level candidate verification. On both financial taxonomy tagging and CodiEsp clinical coding tasks, FHS achieves the best Recall@1, MRR, and final accuracy among the non-oracle methods. Replacing the factorized hypothesis path with a free-text ensemble causes the largest drop in head-ranking performance, while sequential refinement provides no additional gain over FHS's strong parallel first round.

</details>


### [16] [Discovering Conceptual Metaphors Across Topics and Media Types](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06652)
*Alexandria Leto,Rohan Das,Juan Vásquez,Abram Handler,Maria Leonor Pacheco*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes an unsupervised method to automatically detect conceptual metaphors by clustering linguistic metaphors, and uses it to compare how left- and right-leaning podcasts metaphorically frame topics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conceptual metaphors shape how people reason about abstract issues, and different metaphors produce different framings and attitudes. Manually identifying these metaphors at scale is difficult, but doing so would reveal how different groups (e.g., political leanings) frame events and topics.

Method: The authors develop an unsupervised pipeline that first extracts linguistic metaphors from a text corpus, then applies a structured clustering algorithm to group these expressions into clusters that correspond to higher-level conceptual metaphors. They then apply this method to corpora of left- and right-leaning podcasts.

Result: The system automatically discovers clusters of metaphorical expressions that map onto conceptual metaphors. When applied to politically oriented podcasts, it reveals systematic differences in metaphor usage between left- and right-leaning shows, such as distinct ways of conceptualizing media and the economy.

Conclusion: Unsupervised extraction and clustering of linguistic metaphors can effectively approximate conceptual metaphors and expose framing differences across ideological groups. This provides a scalable way to study metaphor use and framing in large text corpora, with clear applications to analyzing political and media discourse.

Abstract: Conceptual metaphors guide our thinking and actions by allowing us to reason about more abstract experiences (e.g., paying taxes) in terms of more concrete or embodied experiences (e.g., carrying a physical load) (Lakoff and Johnson, 2011). It follows that different conceptual metaphors can result in different reasoning: framing paying taxes as an investment in a community rather than a physical load leads to a very different outlook on taxation. Identifying the conceptual metaphors guiding a speaker or writer thus helps to reveal their framing of events. Though these metaphors can't be observed directly, groups of linguistic metaphors, metaphorical expressions as they appear in language, serve as evidence for them. Motivated by this, we present an unsupervised method that extracts linguistic metaphors from a corpus and uses a structured clustering approach to form groups corresponding to conceptual metaphors. Using this method, we point to key topical and framing differences in left- vs. right-leaning podcasts. For example, left-leaning podcasts tend to conceptualize media stories as a weapon, while right-leaning sources commonly discuss the economy as a system subject to vertical changes.

</details>


### [17] [The Horizon Gap: Planning, Memory, Execution, Training, and Evaluation for Long-Horizon LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06663)
*Mingguang Chen,Licheng Wang,Bo Qu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Survey of long-horizon reasoning challenges in language-model systems and how current research addresses them.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Frontier language models can solve impressive reasoning problems in a single pass but systematically fail on multi-hour, multi-step tasks, revealing a ‘horizon gap’ between current capabilities and reliable long-horizon behavior.

Method: Systematic survey of 1,547 arXiv papers from 2024–2026, collected via seed harvesting with a specified bleed filter and targeted supplementation, then organized along two axes: (1) six stages of a long-horizon task lifecycle (planning, memory, execution, training, evaluation, foundations/safety) and (2) where task horizons are represented (within-context, within-task-beyond-context, cross-task-persistent).

Result: Across the surveyed literature, outcome-only supervision becomes ineffective as task horizons grow. In response, methods increasingly use denser, step-level signals—such as process reward models, improved credit assignment mechanisms, and trajectory-level diagnostics—and integrate critical/diagnostic work directly with methodological advances.

Conclusion: The paper clarifies key concepts (long-horizon vs long-context vs long-term memory), identifies a unifying pattern in how research tackles long-horizon tasks (shifting from outcome-only to process-level signals), and highlights open challenges in measurement, disentangling model vs. system contributions, handling bias in process-level signals, and understanding whether long-horizon reliability can be captured by a general predictive theory.

Abstract: Frontier language models solve reasoning problems in a single forward pass that would have been research contributions years ago, yet fail at multi-hour tasks: losing track of earlier decisions, declaring half-finished work done, or drifting from goals. We call this the horizon gap and survey 1,547 arXiv papers (2024-2026) collected via systematic seed harvest with a disclosed 26.8% bleed filter, extended by targeted supplementation. We disambiguate three routinely conflated properties: long-horizon (task property: required steps), long-context (model property: token capacity), and long-term memory (system property: persistence across steps/sessions). We organize the corpus into six categories tracking a long-horizon task's lifecycle -- planning, memory, execution, training, evaluation, and foundations/safety -- crossed with an axis capturing where horizons are carried (within-context, within-task-beyond-context, or cross-task-persistent). Across all categories, we find the same pattern: outcome-only signals grow uninformative as horizons lengthen, and the field's response -- whether process reward models, credit assignment, or trajectory-level diagnostics -- manufactures denser step-level signals. We treat critical and diagnostic literature as first-class threads throughout, arguing that segregating critique from method would routinely split single papers across chapters. We close by naming open measurement problems: decomposing model versus harness capability, managing correlated bias in process-level signals used for both training and evaluation, and whether long-horizon reliability admits general predictive theory.

</details>


### [18] [TA-RAG: Tone Awareness as a Design Imperative for Retrieval-Augmented Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06672)
*Yong-Bin Kang,Anthony McCosker*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper identifies a key limitation in standard Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG): retrieved documents impose their own tone and style, causing LLMs to ignore users’ tone preferences. It defines this as contextual decoupling and proposes Tone-Aware RAG (TA-RAG), a framework that jointly optimizes factual accuracy and communicative alignment via tone-related constraints across the RAG pipeline, along with an evaluation agenda for this new objective.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard RAG architectures focus almost exclusively on factual accuracy and relevance of retrieved content. However, in many real-world, socially sensitive, and high-stakes settings (e.g., public health peer support), how information is communicated—its tone, empathy, stigma level, and readability—is as important as what is said. Existing RAG systems often inherit the style and tone of source documents (e.g., academic or professional jargon), which can clash with the user’s needs or explicit instructions for tone. This mismatch can undermine usability, trust, and support, revealing a critical gap: there is no principled way to align retrieval-based systems with the social and emotional context of recipients. The paper aims to formalize and address this gap.

Method: The paper conducts a conceptual and design-oriented analysis rather than an empirical algorithmic study. It introduces the concept of contextual decoupling to describe how RAG systems prioritize factual accuracy while ignoring communicative context, and it categorizes communicative misalignments into linguistic, cognitive, and relational types. Building on these concepts and prior work in public health peer-support, it proposes the Tone-Aware RAG (TA-RAG) framework. TA-RAG specifies four tone-related constraints—stigma-free language, readability alignment, recipient-sensitive adaptation, and empathetic framing—and maps how these constraints should be integrated into four stages of the RAG pipeline: retrieval, context construction, generation, and constraint validation. The paper also outlines an evaluation agenda for jointly measuring factual fidelity and communicative alignment but does not detail specific models or datasets in the abstract.

Result: From the abstract, the main results are conceptual rather than empirical. The authors define the phenomenon of contextual decoupling and characterize three forms of communicative misalignment that can persist even when retrieval is relevant and outputs are factually correct. They formulate these as failures of communicative transformation, showing that current accuracy-focused metrics overlook them. They then provide a structured architectural proposal—TA-RAG—with explicit constraints and integration points across the RAG pipeline, and they articulate directions for developing evaluation methods that capture both accuracy and tone alignment.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that tone and communicative alignment should be first-class design objectives in RAG systems, especially for socially sensitive and high-stakes applications. Standard RAG pipelines, by inheriting the tone of retrieved documents, often fail to honor users’ tone requirements, leading to contextual decoupling. To remedy this, the authors advocate for Tone-Aware RAG (TA-RAG), which embeds tone-related constraints—stigma-free language, readability alignment, recipient-sensitive adaptation, and empathetic framing—into every stage of the RAG pipeline and calls for new evaluation frameworks that jointly consider factual correctness and communicative fit. They argue that tone awareness is not a minor enhancement but a necessary design imperative.

Abstract: Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) has become a robust architecture for grounding large language models (LLMs) in trusted knowledge. However, standard RAG systems exhibit a structural limitation: retrieved documents carry their own communication styles-professional jargon, formal tone, or academic writings-that shape the behavior of a RAG system before any tone instructions are processed, often causing the system to ignore user requests for a specific tone. We term this phenomenon contextual decoupling, in which a system optimises for factual accuracy while remaining decoupled from the social or operational context of the recipient. Building on prior research in public health peer-support communities, we identify three communicative misalignment-linguistic, cognitive, and relational-that can persist even when retrieval is relevant and the generated response is factually accurate. We conceptualise these as failures of communicative transformation, which remain largely invisible to accuracy-centred RAG evaluation metrics. To address this gap, we propose Tone-Aware RAG (TA-RAG), a conceptual architectural framework that positions communicative alignment alongside factual accuracy as a core design objective. TA-RAG operationalises four constraints-stigma-free language, readability alignment, recipient-sensitive adaptation, and empathetic framing-across the retrieval, context construction, generation, and constraint validation phases in the proposed RAG pipeline. We further highlight an evaluation agenda for jointly assessing factual fidelity and communicative alignment, and identify open challenges. We argue that tone awareness should be treated not as an optional refinement, but as a present design imperative for RAG systems operating in socially sensitive and high-stakes contexts.

</details>


### [19] [Do Audio Language Models Use Paralinguistic Evidence? Counterfactual Audits for Response Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06718)
*Kevin Miller,Arjun Chandra,Venkatesh Saligrama*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes a way to test whether audio-language models actually use paralinguistic audio cues (like affect and prosody) when judging speech-to-speech systems, and finds that current models often appear more reliable than they really are.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Audio-language models are increasingly used as automatic judges of speech-to-speech system quality. However, it is unclear whether these models truly rely on paralinguistic information in the audio (such as affect, prosody, and timing of emotional shifts) or whether they primarily base their judgments on transcripts and textual style. Without knowing this, we risk overestimating their reliability and deploying them in settings where they systematically fail on important aspects of spoken communication.

Method: The authors introduce "counterfactual audits" for paralinguistic evaluation. For each audit item, they keep the verbal transcript fixed while manipulating specific paralinguistic variables in the audio: affect (emotional tone), prosody (intonation and rhythm), and the timing of affective changes. This design forces any competent judge to pay attention to audio cues rather than text alone.
