They test several audio-language models (Gemini, GPT-based models, and open-source audio models) using: (1) a native one-context judgment protocol, where the model judges the quality or appropriateness of a single audio response, and (2) a contrastive recoverability control, where the model must pick the correct option among alternatives. They further break down each audit item into two underlying skills: perception of the paralinguistic cue and mapping that perception to the correct evaluative response, enabling skill-level diagnostics.

Result: Experiments show that models often perform well in contrastive recoverability setups but are less reliable when making native, single-context judgments, indicating that contrastive success can exaggerate their true robustness as judges. The decomposition into perception vs. response-mapping reveals distinct failure modes across models: some struggle to detect specific paralinguistic cues, while others perceive them but fail to translate them into correct evaluative decisions. Models with similar overall accuracy can exhibit qualitatively different error patterns, which aggregate metrics obscure.

Conclusion: The study concludes that current audio-language models, though promising, are not reliably using paralinguistic information when serving as judges. Simple accuracy metrics and contrastive evaluation protocols can be misleading, overstating model reliability. To responsibly deploy ALM judges, practitioners should apply detailed behavioral audits—like the proposed counterfactual paralinguistic tests and skill decompositions—to uncover where and how models fail, rather than relying on aggregate scores alone.

Abstract: Audio-language models (ALMs) are increasingly used as judges for speech-to-speech systems, but a judge that receives audio may not actually use paralinguistic evidence. We introduce counterfactual audits for paralinguistic response evaluation. Each audit item holds the transcript fixed while varying affect, prosody, or the timing of an affective shift, forcing a valid judge to track the audio cue rather than lexical content or response style. We evaluate ALM judges using a native one-context judgment protocol and a contrastive recoverability control, then further decompose each item into its constituent perception and response-mapping skills. This yields useful diagnostic states that identify different sources of judge failures. Across Gemini, GPT, and open audio models, we find that contrastive success often overstates native judge reliability, and that similar aggregate accuracies can hide different failure modes. These results suggest that ALM judges should not be evaluated by accuracy alone, instead requiring thorough behavioral audits before deployment.

</details>


### [20] [Progressive Content Refinement with Decaying Reward Joint LinUCB](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06750)
*Shion Ishikawa,Pablo Loyola,Young-joo Chung,Yun Ching Liu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes a new contextual bandit algorithm with explicit reward decay modeling to improve iterative refinement for LLMs, reducing over-exploitation of repeated prompts and yielding better performance on sentiment reversal and GSM8K tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing iterative refinement methods for LLMs, such as feedback-based Self-Refine and standard bandit approaches, often treat prompts/arms as having static rewards and ignore that repeatedly using the same prompt leads to diminishing returns (saturation). This causes over-exploitation of once-good prompts and ultimately degrades performance. There is a need for a method that models how rewards change (decay) over time and that can capture shared structure among prompts rather than treating them as independent options.

Method: The authors design a contextual bandit algorithm that explicitly models reward decay for each arm. They employ an Expectation-Maximization (EM) procedure to jointly estimate both the intrinsic value of each arm (prompt) and its decay parameters over time. Prompts are embedded into a shared representation space and treated as arms in a joint learning setup, contrasting with traditional disjoint LinUCB-style models where each arm is learned separately. This unified embedding-based modeling enables the bandit to generalize across prompts while accounting for time-varying (decaying) rewards.

Result: On Sentiment Reversal and GSM8K benchmarks, the proposed algorithm outperforms strong existing baselines, including self-refinement methods and standard bandit algorithms without decay modeling. The empirical results indicate higher task accuracy and better sample efficiency in the iterative refinement process.

Conclusion: Explicitly modeling reward decay within a contextual bandit framework helps avoid over-exploitation of prompts whose effectiveness saturates over time. By combining EM-based decay estimation with a shared embedding space for prompts, the proposed method more effectively manages exploration–exploitation in iterative LLM refinement, leading to consistent performance gains. The ablation studies validate that the decay modeling component is a key contributor to these improvements.

Abstract: Iterative refinement has significantly enhanced Large Language Model (LLM) performance; however, existing methods ranging from feedback-based Self-Refine to traditional bandit approaches often rely on static options or overlook the saturation effect. This neglect leads to over-exploitation, where the continuous use of identical prompts or arms results in diminishing rewards over time.
  To address this challenge, we propose a novel contextual bandit algorithm that explicitly incorporates reward decay modeling. Utilizing an Expectation-Maximization (EM) algorithm, our method simultaneously estimates both arm-specific and decay parameters. Furthermore, by embedding prompts as arms, we facilitate the joint learning of arm values, distinguishing our approach from the traditional disjoint Linear Upper Confidence Bound (LinUCB) framework.
  Experimental results on Sentiment Reversal and GSM8K benchmarks demonstrate that our method achieves significant performance gains over strong baselines. Finally, our ablation study confirms that the integration of reward decay modeling within the bandit framework is crucial for mitigating over-exploitation and optimizing the iterative refinement process.

</details>


### [21] [Stockmark-Nemotron-3-Nano-Omni-JapanDocReader: Structured Document Parsing via Capability Injection and Forgetting Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06758)
*Shi Chen,Hayato Aida,Makoto Morinaga,Shohei Tanaka,Kosuke Arima*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces Stockmark-Nemotron-3-Nano-Omni-JapanDocReader, a Japanese document understanding model that specializes in structured document parsing while retaining document VQA skills, using a combination of supervised fine-tuning and reinforcement learning with tailored data and rewards.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Many real-world Japanese documents (e.g., forms, invoices, contracts) require accurate structured parsing, not just question answering, yet existing multimodal reasoning models are not optimized for high-precision structured extraction and often forget prior capabilities when fine-tuned. The authors aim to inject strong Japanese structured parsing ability into a reasoning-oriented vision-language model while carefully controlling catastrophic forgetting of its document VQA capability.

Method: Starting from Nemotron-3-Nano-Omni-30B-A3B-Reasoning-BF16, the authors explore: (1) parsing-centric supervised fine-tuning (SFT) using only structured document parsing data; (2) mixed SFT that blends structured parsing data with document VQA data to balance new skills and old capabilities; and (3) DAPO-based parsing-centric reinforcement learning that directly optimizes task-level structured parsing rewards on top of the mixed-SFT model. They build a data engine with two synthetic streams: a Japanese Document VQA stream and a programmatic structured parsing stream, and design continuous structured parsing rewards plus variance-based prompt filtering to stabilize RL on long reasoning tasks.

Result: Parsing-centric SFT significantly boosts structured document parsing accuracy but introduces noticeable forgetting in document VQA performance. Mixed SFT largely alleviates this forgetting while preserving nearly the same parsing gains. Applying DAPO-based parsing-centric RL further surpasses the performance ceiling achieved by SFT alone, yielding the final Stockmark-Nemotron-3-Nano-Omni-JapanDocReader model with improved structured parsing and retained VQA ability.

Conclusion: Capability injection with forgetting control is effective for specializing a general reasoning multimodal model into a high-precision Japanese structured document parser. A combination of mixed SFT and DAPO-based RL, along with carefully designed continuous rewards and prompt filtering, enables improved structured parsing performance beyond SFT while maintaining document VQA skills. The resulting JapanDocReader model demonstrates that targeted training pipelines and synthetic data engines can successfully adapt large reasoning models to demanding structured document understanding tasks.

Abstract: We present Stockmark-Nemotron-3-Nano-Omni-JapanDocReader, a Japanese document understanding model built from Nemotron-3-Nano-Omni-30B-A3B-Reasoning-BF16. The central goal of this work is structured document parsing via capability injection and forgetting control: we inject Japanese structured document parsing capability into a reasoning-oriented multimodal model while preserving its document VQA capability as much as possible. We study parsing-centric SFT, which uses only structured document parsing data; mixed SFT, which combines structured document parsing and VQA data; and parsing-centric RL, which optimizes structured parsing with a task-level reward. Our experiments show that parsing-centric SFT substantially improves structured document parsing performance but causes measurable VQA forgetting. Mixed SFT mitigates this forgetting while preserving nearly the same structured parsing performance. Applying DAPO-based parsing-centric RL on top of the mixed SFT checkpoint further improves structured document parsing beyond the SFT ceiling, producing the final released model. The training data is constructed with a data engine consisting of two complementary synthetic streams: a Japanese Document VQA Stream and a programmatic structured document parsing stream. We also discuss reward design and variance-based prompt filtering for continuous structured document parsing rewards, highlighting their importance for making RL effective in long-reasoning structured document parsing tasks.

</details>


### [22] [Multi-Perspective Triad Interaction Graph Neural Network for Cognitive Distortion Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06785)
*Jun Seo Kim,Hye Hyeon Kim*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: They propose MTI-GNN, a graph neural network that explicitly models Beck’s cognitive triad (self, world, future) using LLM-based perspective decomposition and multi-perspective graph interactions to better detect cognitive distortions across multiple languages and datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing cognitive distortion detectors largely treat utterances as flat text and ignore the well-established psychological structure of distorted thinking, particularly Beck’s cognitive triad (negative views of self, world, future). This limits interpretability, cross-perspective reasoning, and possibly performance, especially across languages and categories of distortions. The authors aim to build a model that leverages this triadic structure for more accurate and psychologically grounded detection.

Method: 1) Use a large language model (LLM) to decompose each user utterance into three separate texts reflecting views of the self, world, and future. 2) For each perspective, construct a similarity graph among utterances and encode them with a Multi-Perspective GNN, producing perspective-specific node embeddings. 3) Introduce a Triad Interaction module that lets each perspective representation update the others sequentially via source-conditioned message passing and feature-wise gating, capturing cross-perspective dependencies. 4) Apply Prototype-Guided Perspective Fusion, which aggregates the triad representations using label prototypes and distortion labels to form a final, label-aware representation. 5) Use label-expanded supervision so that when multiple distortion annotations exist, they are all leveraged during training. The model is trained and evaluated on multi-lingual datasets.

Result: On 9,764 samples from four datasets in Korean, English, and Chinese, covering 10 cognitive distortion categories, MTI-GNN achieves significantly better performance than strong supervised baselines and outperforms eight different prompted generative LLM setups in both zero-shot and few-shot regimes. Ablation where one perspective (self, world, or future) is removed shows notable performance degradation each time, indicating that all three contribute meaningfully. Human expert evaluation further suggests that the model’s inferred triad perspectives align reasonably well with the intended cognitive dimensions.

Conclusion: Explicitly encoding Beck’s cognitive triad in a graph neural network yields more accurate and psychologically interpretable cognitive distortion detection than prior text-only approaches. The triad decomposition, cross-perspective interaction, and prototype-guided fusion together provide performance gains across languages and categories, while expert judgments indicate that the model’s perspectives are consistent with established cognitive theory. This suggests a promising direction for integrating structured psychological models with modern NLP architectures in mental health applications.

Abstract: Cognitive distortion detection is a key task in computational mental health, yet existing approaches often overlook the psychological structure of distorted thoughts. We propose MTI-GNN (Multi-Perspective Triad Interaction Graph Neural Network), which models Beck's cognitive triad---negative views of the self, world, and future---as complementary perspectives for classification. An LLM decomposes each utterance into the three perspectives, from which perspective-specific similarity graphs are constructed and encoded by a Multi-Perspective GNN. A Triad Interaction module models cross-perspective dependencies through sequential source-conditioned updates and feature-wise gating, while Prototype-Guided Perspective Fusion performs label-conditioned aggregation. Label-expanded supervision incorporates all available distortion annotations during training. We evaluate MTI-GNN on 9,764 samples from four Korean, English, and Chinese datasets spanning ten distortion categories. MTI-GNN significantly outperforms all supervised variants and exceeds eight prompted generative models under zero-shot and few-shot settings. Leave-one-perspective-out ablations show that all three perspectives contribute significantly, while human expert evaluation provides preliminary evidence of their alignment with the intended cognitive dimensions.

</details>


### [23] [Simple-OPD: Demystifying Warm-up for On-policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06802)
*Tao Liu,Taiqiang Wu,Mao Zheng,Xuan Luo,Runming Yang,Xuewei Yang,Junjie Wang,Yujiu Yang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper studies why and how the warm-up stage matters for on-policy distillation (OPD) in training language model students from teacher models, and proposes Simple-OPD, a better warm-up method using teacher chain-of-thought and LoRA.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: OPD’s performance is highly sensitive to the warm-up phase, but the mechanisms behind effective warm-up are unclear. Understanding what data and training setups make warm-up effective can improve OPD’s robustness and efficiency for aligning and compressing models.

Method: The authors empirically dissect warm-up from two angles: (1) data: they compare different warm-up datasets, including teacher chain-of-thought (CoT) vs other supervision, and correct vs incorrect teacher rollouts, to see what matters; (2) training: they compare low-rank adaptation (LoRA) warm-up vs full-parameter supervised fine-tuning (SFT), and analyze training duration/saturation. Based on insights, they design Simple-OPD: first warm up the student on teacher-generated CoT using LoRA to near-saturation, then run standard OPD.

Result: They find that: (a) warm-up works best when the student is exposed to teacher-compatible CoT supervision, even if many rollouts are incorrect; (b) this indicates that warm-up mainly transfers thinking style rather than just final answers; (c) LoRA-based warm-up with sufficiently long training yields a better tradeoff between in-domain performance and generalization than full SFT. With these, Simple-OPD consistently improves OPD across various benchmarks and settings.

Conclusion: Effective warm-up for OPD is about instilling a teacher-compatible reasoning pattern through CoT supervision and using a well-calibrated LoRA-based adaptation procedure. The proposed Simple-OPD provides a simple, robust way to initialize students before OPD, improving performance and generalization without complex changes to the OPD framework.

Abstract: On-policy distillation (OPD) trains a student on its own rollouts with token-level supervision from teacher models, but its effectiveness can depend strongly on the warm-up stage before OPD. In this paper, we demystify warm-up for OPD from both data and training perspectives. For data, we find that effective warm-up relies on teacher-compatible chain-of-thought supervision, and that even incorrect teacher rollouts can provide comparable benefits to correct ones. This suggests that warm-up primarily transfers a teacher-compatible thinking pattern rather than merely correct answers. For training, we show that low-rank adaptation (LoRA) with a near-saturation training duration better balances in-domain adaptation and out-of-distribution generalization than full-parameter SFT. Based on these findings, we propose Simple-OPD, a plug-and-play initialization method that warms up the student on teacher-generated CoT with LoRA before OPD. Experiments across diverse settings demonstrate the effectiveness and robustness of Simple-OPD.

</details>


### [24] [FutureBridge: Token Selection Beyond Local Preference in Collaborative Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06819)
*Quanquan Li,Hongbo Zhang,Yihe Chi,Jingyu Li,Xidong Xi,Liuyang Song,Hongzhen Zhang,Yuxiang Huang,Jing Ke,Siyuan Ma,Junyi Lin,Guitao Cao*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: FutureBridge improves collaboration between a large and a small language model by choosing tokens that the small model can best continue reasoning from, rather than just following the large model’s local token preferences.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing token-level collaboration methods between a large and small language model depend heavily on the large model’s immediate next-token probabilities or interventions, which may produce tokens that the small model struggles to build on. This mismatch can limit downstream reasoning quality, especially in math tasks where extended coherent reasoning is crucial.

Method: The authors introduce FutureBridge, a token reranking framework. During training, they use an answer-verified LLM-generated trajectory as a fixed future context. A frozen SLM evaluates different candidate tokens under this shared future, producing counterfactual scores that reflect how well each token supports the SLM’s later reasoning. These scores train a lightweight reranker that only sees the current state and candidate token. At test time, the LLM is used only to expand a pool of candidate tokens; FutureBridge then selects a single token for continuation and hands generation back to the SLM, without appending any future suffix from the LLM.

Result: On five mathematical reasoning benchmarks, FutureBridge applied to Qwen3-1.7B yields a 35.1% relative improvement in Math Avg. compared to greedy decoding by the SLM alone, showing significantly better performance through more suitable token selection.

Conclusion: Token-level collaboration is more effective when token choices are optimized for the receiving small model’s ability to continue reasoning, rather than solely following the large model’s local probability preferences. FutureBridge operationalizes this via a learned reranker that anticipates the SLM’s future reasoning quality, leading to substantial gains on math reasoning tasks.

Abstract: Token-level collaboration allows a large language model (LLM) to assist a small language model (SLM) when their predictions diverge. Existing methods either use LLM-generated intervention tokens or rank candidates with the LLM's next-token probabilities. Both rely on the LLM's local preference, even though an LLM-selected token may be difficult for the SLM to build on. We present FutureBridge, which ranks joint LLM-SLM token candidates according to how well they support the SLM's subsequent reasoning. During training, an answer-verified LLM trajectory supplies a fixed shared future, and a frozen SLM evaluates every candidate under this common context. The resulting counterfactual scores supervise a lightweight token reranker that observes only the current state and candidate token. At inference, FutureBridge uses the LLM only to expand the candidate pool, selects one token, and returns generation to the SLM without generating or appending a future suffix. Across five mathematical reasoning benchmarks, FutureBridge improves the Qwen3-1.7B SLM's Math Avg. by 35.1% relative to greedy SLM decoding. These results indicate that token selection benefits from modeling whether the receiving SLM can use each candidate to continue reasoning, rather than relying on the LLM's local preference alone.

</details>


### [25] [Autonomy-of-Heads: Data-Free Sparse Attention from Frozen Query-Key Geometry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06849)
*Yehan Yang,Junyuan Shang,Yang Li,Guanqun Zhao,Shuohuan Wang,Dianhai Yu*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes AoH, a data-free method to statically identify which attention heads can be sparsified while maintaining performance, thereby accelerating long-context LLM inference and reducing KV-cache costs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-context LLMs are slowed and memory-limited by quadratic attention and large KV-caches. Existing sparsification and KV-compression approaches depend on data, runtime attention patterns, or calibration procedures, which makes them input-dependent, expensive to deploy, and less interpretable in terms of head functions. The authors aim to find a cheap, data-free, and architecture-level way to classify attention heads by function (retrieval vs. streaming) so that sparsification can be applied in a principled way for efficient long-context inference.

Method: They introduce Autonomy-of-Heads (AoH), which analyzes each attention head purely in weight space. For a head h, they define a kernel attention operator Mh = W_K^{h⊤} W_Q^h and study the spectrum (eigenvalues) of this matrix. They use the effective rank of Mh as a scalar signature: a concentrated spectrum (few large eigenvalues) implies a small number of dominant query-key matching directions (retrieval-like behavior), while a diffuse spectrum (many comparable eigenvalues) implies no dominant global matching direction (streaming-like behavior). They also derive an efficient computation that works in d_head-dimensional space instead of constructing the full d_model×d_model operator, making AoH practical for large models. Based on this classification, they sparsify attention/KV usage while preserving critical heads.

Result: Across multiple LLMs and benchmarks, they show that using AoH to drive 50% attention sparsity preserves on average 96.5% of the performance of full attention. In long-context regimes up to 256K tokens, this sparsification yields up to 41.4% prefill latency reduction, 66.0% decode latency reduction, and 50.0% KV-cache memory savings, while maintaining high task performance.

Conclusion: Spectral properties of the query-key weight product of each attention head are sufficient to reliably infer functional roles such as retrieval versus streaming without any data. This data-free, static, and efficient characterization enables principled attention sparsification that significantly accelerates long-context LLM inference and reduces memory usage with minor performance loss, suggesting that head function is strongly encoded in weight-space geometry and can be exploited for scalable deployment.

Abstract: Long-context LLM inference is bottlenecked by quadratic attention computation and growing KV-cache costs. Existing sparse attention and KV-compression methods typically decide which tokens or heads to preserve from runtime attention scores, observation windows, calibration prompts, or learned gates, making head diagnosis input-dependent and costly to deploy. We propose Autonomy-of-Heads (AoH), a data-free method that identifies retrieval and streaming heads from the spectral geometry of query-key projections. AoH defines the kernel attention operator $M_h = W_K^{h\top}W_Q^h$ and uses its effective-rank as a weight-space measure of head function: concentrated spectra indicate a small number of dominant query-key matching directions and are associated with retrieval heads, whereas diffuse spectra indicate the absence of a dominant global matching direction and are associated with streaming heads. We further derive an efficient $d_\text{head}$-dimensional computation that avoids constructing the full $d_\text{model}\times d_\text{model}$ matrix. We conducted extensive experiments across models demonstrating that at 50\% sparsity, AoH retains 96.5\% of Full Attention performance on average while reducing prefill and decode latency by up to 41.4\% and 66.0\%, respectively, and KV-cache memory by 50.0\% at 256K tokens.

</details>


### [26] [LLMRouter: Unified Infrastructure for Developing, Evaluating, and Deploying LLM Routers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06867)
*Tao Feng,Fangxu Yu,Haozhen Zhang,Zhongjie Dai,Liangqi Yuan,Zijie Lei,Weizhi Zhang,Kunlun Zhu,Haodong Yue,Keyang Xuan,Ge Liu,Jiaxuan You*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Unified framework and benchmark for routing queries among multiple LLMs to optimize quality vs. cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Different LLMs have varying strengths, weaknesses, and costs, so no single model is best for all tasks or budgets. To use them efficiently in practice, systems must decide which model to call for each query, but existing routing methods are fragmented, hard to compare fairly, and difficult to extend.

Method: Formulate LLM routing as a general sequential decision process defined by five key components: context encoders, model encoders, scoring functions, decision rules, and learning signals, covering scenarios such as single-turn, multi-turn, and personalized routing. Build an automated pipeline to generate supervision for training routers and to evaluate them on both answer quality and inference cost. Construct xRouteBench, a broad benchmark covering generic LLM routing and several specialized routing tasks. Implement LLMRouter, a modular, open-source infrastructure hosting over 16 representative routing algorithms.

Result: Empirical evaluation on xRouteBench shows that learned routing methods significantly outperform a strong baseline that always uses a fixed model, with a 14.6% relative gain in performance. Under strict cost limits, simpler, lightweight routers can be more competitive. Incorporating user-specific information into routing policies consistently enhances personalization quality.

Conclusion: A unified decision-process view, standardized benchmark (xRouteBench), and modular infrastructure (LLMRouter) make it easier to design, compare, and extend LLM routing methods. Learned routers provide substantial quality gains over fixed-model strategies, and cost-aware as well as user-conditioned routing are key for practical, personalized, and budget-efficient LLM deployment.

Abstract: No single large language model (LLM) is optimal across all queries and budget constraints, making model routing essential for cost-effective deployment. Existing routers adopt diverse formulations and implementations, making fair comparison and extension difficult. We present a unified formulation of LLM routing as a sequential decision process characterized by five components: context encoders, model encoders, scoring functions, decision rules, and learning signals, covering single-turn, multi-turn, and personalized routing. Based on this formulation, we develop an automated pipeline for constructing routing supervision and evaluating routers jointly on response quality and inference cost. The resulting benchmark, xRouteBench, spans generic LLM, memory-augmented, vision, time-series, and personalized routing tasks. We further introduce LLMRouter, an open-source modular infrastructure with more than 16 representative routers. Our empirical study shows that learned routers outperform the strongest fixed-model baseline by 14.6% relatively, lightweight routers become more competitive under tight cost constraints, and user-conditioned routing consistently improves personalization.

</details>


### [27] [Georeferencing Non-Gazetteered Place Names using Biological Specimen Records](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06884)
*Aneesha Fernando,Surangika Ranathunga,Kristin Stock,Raj Prasanna,Christopher B. Jones*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper uses digitised herbarium specimen records to identify historical or colloquial place names missing from gazetteers and compares deterministic, probabilistic, and LLM-based methods to infer their geographic locations from textual spatial relations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Many locality descriptions in biological specimen records contain historical or vernacular place names that do not appear in modern gazetteers, making it difficult to georeference specimens accurately for biodiversity and historical landscape analyses. There is a need to exploit the rich but noisy textual information in these records to recover the locations of such non-gazetteer place names and to understand which computational approaches best support high-precision spatial inference.

Method: Using digitised records from the Allan Herbarium (New Zealand), the authors first automatically detect place names in locality descriptions that do not match existing gazetteers, labelling them as non-gazetteer place names (NGPs). They then leverage repeated occurrences of the same NGP in different specimen records that also contain other, georeferenced locations and spatial relation terms (e.g. '5 km north of X'). By extracting and inverting these spatial relations, they derive geometric constraints (e.g. buffers, directional sectors) on the location of each NGP. They instantiate three inference frameworks over these constraints: (1) a deterministic method that combines constraints via rule-based logic; (2) a probabilistic model that treats constraints as likelihood functions over space to estimate a probability surface and a best point; and (3) an LLM-based approach that uses a large language model to interpret text and propose coordinates. They evaluate these methods on a pseudo-NGP benchmark where true locations are known but hidden.

Result: On the pseudo-NGP benchmark, the probabilistic inference framework yields the best spatial accuracy, with a median localisation error of 1.43 km and 36% of predictions within 1 km of the true location (A@1 km). The LLM-based method produces competitive but less precise estimates, with a median error of 1.80 km and A@1 km of 31%. Deterministic methods perform worse than the probabilistic model, especially when constraints are noisy or conflicting. Overall, traditional probabilistic modelling outperforms the LLM in high-precision georeferencing tasks using only specimen text and spatial relations.

Conclusion: Historical, vernacular, and colloquial place names embedded in biological specimen records can be systematically identified and georeferenced even when absent from current gazetteers, by exploiting repeated textual co-occurrences and spatial relations. While large language models can contribute to text-based spatial inference, probabilistic constraint-based methods currently provide superior spatial precision, suggesting that traditional modelling remains preferable when accurate coordinates are critical. The work demonstrates a pathway for enriching gazetteers with non-standard place names and for improving the spatial utility of legacy biodiversity data.

Abstract: Biological specimen records collected by natural history institutions constitute a rich source of temporal geographic knowledge, capturing biodiversity information about regional landscapes as they were recorded at different times. Using digitised data from the Allan Herbarium (New Zealand), this study identifies place names in these specimen locality descriptions that are absent from current gazetteers; we refer to these as non-gazetteer place names (NGPs). These place names are typically historical, vernacular, or colloquial and were used as landmarks to describe a specimen's location at the time of collection. We then investigate the problem of georeferencing the NGPs using only the limited information available in the specimen records. To resolve this, we leverage repeated occurrences of the same place name across specimen records with different specimen locations and spatial relation terms, extracting and inverting these relations to derive constraints on NGP locations. This approach is instantiated within deterministic, probabilistic, and LLM-based methods, enabling a comparative analysis of their strengths and limitations for text-based spatial inference. On a pseudo-NGP benchmark, probabilistic inference achieves the highest accuracy (median error 1.43 km; A@1 km 36%), while the LLM yields competitive but less precise estimates (median error 1.80 km; A@1 km 31%), indicating that, despite advances in LLMs, traditional modelling remains advantageous when high spatial precision is required.

</details>


### [28] [Calibrating WEAT Against Anisotropy: ZCA Whitening as a Geometric Pre-Processing Step for Embedding Association Tests](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06908)
*Seitaro Ono,Senna Ross,Jun Saiki*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces ZCA whitening as a preprocessing step to make WEAT bias measurements more reliable by restoring isotropy in word embedding spaces, showing that many past bias results may be misestimated and should be recalibrated.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: WEAT uses cosine similarity to measure social biases in word embeddings, implicitly assuming an isotropic embedding space. However, many common language models violate this assumption due to anisotropy, which can distort bias estimates. There is a need for a principled calibration method that fixes the geometry of the embedding space with minimal distortion so that WEAT results are trustworthy.

Method: The authors apply Zero-phase Component Analysis (ZCA) whitening to word embeddings before running WEAT. ZCA whitening transforms the embeddings so that their covariance matrix becomes the identity (enforcing isotropy) while minimally altering the original vectors in a least-squares sense. They test this across ten standard WEAT test suites and seven language models from three architectural families, generating 70 model–task combinations. They quantify anisotropy reduction, evaluate impacts on standard semantic similarity benchmarks, and analyze how WEAT effect sizes and significance levels change after calibration.

Result: ZCA whitening substantially decreases anisotropy in the embedding spaces for all evaluated models. For models that are originally highly anisotropic, whitening also improves performance on standard semantic similarity benchmarks, suggesting that the calibrated spaces capture semantic associations more faithfully. After calibration, more than 30% of WEAT scores change their statistical significance, with effect sizes increasing or decreasing depending on the bias category, indicating both over- and underestimation of bias in the uncalibrated setting.

Conclusion: Geometric calibration via ZCA whitening restores the isotropy assumption underlying WEAT, leading to more reliable bias measurements. The findings imply that many previously reported WEAT-based bias results on anisotropic embeddings may be unreliable and should be reconsidered using calibrated embeddings. The proposed approach strengthens the methodological foundation of WEAT-based analyses in computational social science and AI fairness research by providing a simple, effective preprocessing step that corrects for embedding anisotropy.

Abstract: We propose Zero-phase Component Analysis (ZCA) whitening as a geometric pre-processing step for the Word Embedding Association Test (WEAT). WEAT is a bias measurement method widely used in both computational social science and AI fairness research. It relies on cosine similarity as a measure of semantic association, which assumes that the embedding space is approximately isotropic. However, prior work has reported that many widely used language models do not satisfy this assumption, raising concerns about the reliability of bias measurements. ZCA whitening transforms the covariance of the embedding space into the identity matrix while minimizing perturbation to the original vectors. This transformation restores the isotropy condition on which WEAT relies. We evaluate our approach on ten standard WEAT test suites and seven models spanning three architectural families, yielding 70 model-task combinations. The results show that ZCA whitening substantially reduces the anisotropy of the embedding spaces across all models. Particularly for highly anisotropic models, we further observe improvements on standard semantic similarity benchmarks, indicating that the calibrated space better captures semantic associations. After calibration, over 30% of WEAT results change significance status, and effect sizes shift in both directions depending on bias category. These shifts suggest that uncalibrated measurements may both overestimate and underestimate the associations encoded in the embedding space. These findings indicate that previously reported bias measurements in anisotropic embedding spaces should be interpreted with caution and may benefit from re-evaluation with calibrated methods. Our approach contributes to restoring the measurement foundation of WEAT across both computational social science and AI fairness research.

</details>


### [29] [Ask-E: An Environment for Calibrated Question Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06933)
*Sarah Pratt,Jae Sung Park,Scott Geng,Ali Farhadi*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Paper proposes Ask-E, an environment to benchmark and train models on generating questions at specific difficulty levels between two models’ capabilities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Creating evaluation and training problems at the frontier of model abilities is hard and itself requires capabilities beyond the model, leading to a bottleneck as models improve. The authors want a way to turn this constraint into a measurement and training signal.

Method: Define target skill bands using two existing language models with different capabilities. A question is considered correctly calibrated if exactly one of the two models can solve it. Use this setup both as a benchmark (measure calibration rate) and as a training environment where models are optimized to generate questions that fall into specified capability ranges.

Result: Frontier models currently achieve less than 50% calibration on Ask-E, leaving room for improvement. Training models in this environment, even without new domain data, stronger models, or correctness-based rewards, improves performance on downstream math benchmarks.

Conclusion: Question-generation calibration can act as a powerful proxy for super-threshold capability: models that can reliably generate questions at specific difficulty levels must understand those levels. Ask-E provides a scalable way to benchmark and train such capabilities, and it yields transferable gains on math tasks.

Abstract: Today, we improve models by training and evaluating them on problems at the frontier of their abilities. Creating such problems is itself a demanding task, requiring the ability to probe model limits and generalize beyond existing question distributions. It also means placing problems at a precise difficulty level, which requires understanding what it takes to solve them. In short, generating problems calibrated to a model's current frontier demands capability beyond it, an increasingly burdensome constraint as models improve. Our key insight is that we can leverage this constraint to our advantage: a model that can generate problems consistently calibrated to a given frontier must possess capability beyond it. Accordingly, we present Ask-E, an environment that benchmarks and trains models on their ability to write questions at a given skill level, rather than answer them. Concretely, we define target skill levels as ranges bounded by the capabilities of two existing language models. A generated question is successfully calibrated if exactly one of the two models can solve it, placing it precisely within the target range and differentiating the capabilities of these models. Ask-E serves both as a benchmark and a training environment, where models generate problems calibrated to a variety of skill levels. We find that even frontier models achieve below 50% calibration on the benchmark, leaving significant headroom to measure future progress. We also show that training on this environment leads to improvements across a number of downstream math benchmarks even with no new math data, no interaction with stronger models, and no correctness-based reward.

</details>


### [30] [Explicit, Not Longer: What Makes Epistemic Stance Survive Memory Compression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06953)
*Alex Kwon*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Study on how formatting affects whether AI models retain epistemic stance of claims in memory.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AI agents compress memory and often drop qualifiers like uncertainty or source; this paper wants to know what formatting choices make epistemic stance survive compression.

Method: Paired-note experiments where claims are identical but stance location/format varies (label field vs bracketed aside; sentence vs shorter; length, labels, wording ablations). Same compression budget, same filler notes. Blind scoring by a separate model; multiple models tested, with pre-registered replication on Haiku. Also deterministic readout and human hand labels.

Result: Writing epistemic stance as an explicit labelled field instead of inline aside increases retention of stance by ~15 percentage points across 60 claims and seven registers on two models, strongly significant. Ablations show labels help both models; length alone does not. Full-sentence wording is main driver for one model but negligible for the other. Deterministic readout reproduces overall pattern but not all sub-effects; humans agree on direction with moderate-high reliability.

Conclusion: To preserve epistemic stance in compressed agent memory, make stance explicit (e.g., dedicated field), not just longer or wordier. Optimal explicitness format is model-dependent, so mechanisms inferred from a single model may be misleading. Some previously claimed effects in the paper were later withdrawn upon further analysis.

Abstract: Agent memory systems compress what they store, and compression is built to drop qualifiers, so a claim's epistemic standing tends not to survive being written to memory. We ask what governs whether it does. Matched notes carry the identical claim and identical stance and differ only in where that stance sits; one model compresses both under the same budget among the same filler notes, and a blind reader that never sees the condition scores the result. Across 60 claims in seven registers, writing the stance as a labelled field rather than a bracketed aside raises retention by about 15 points on two models (37 claims to 2 on one, 30 to 8 on the other; permutation p=0.00005), and a pre-registered replication on Haiku, its prediction and decision rule committed before the run, gives +15.6 points, 38 claims to 1. Ablating the format on both models gives the same net effect from different parts: labels help on both (+9.7 and +12.8) and length helps on neither, but wording the stance as a full sentence is the largest component on one model (+12.5) and worth nothing on the other (+0.6). Either model alone would have licensed a confident and different mechanism, so we claim only the intersection: make the stance explicit, not merely longer, and expect the best way of being explicit to depend on the model. A deterministic readout with no model reproduces the two-cell direction and five of seven ablation contrasts, but not length or labels, which we therefore do not claim on one instrument. Fifty hand labels (kappa=0.75) agree on direction; we print their seven disagreements in full. We also report nine withdrawn claims, three of them former title claims of this paper.

</details>


### [31] [Can Language Models Imagine Without Seeing? Ekphrasis: Measuring Visual Creative Ideation in Text-Only LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06967)
*Hongyu Luo,He Wang,Huihao Jing,Hong Ting Tsang,Yuxuan Liu,Wuganjing Song,Yauwai Yim,Chunyang Li,Yangqiu Song*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces Ekphrasis, a benchmark for evaluating whether text-only language models can generate genuinely novel and useful visual concepts in text, beyond fluent but clichéd descriptions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing evaluations of language models do not test whether models can truly originate visual concepts before images are generated. Fluent descriptions can mask failures in visual planning, where models reuse clichés or produce scenes that are not actually renderable. The authors want a way to rigorously measure "visual creative ideation" in text alone.

Method: They define Visual Creative Ideation (VCI) as the capacity to produce textual visual plans that are useful, expressive, and novel relative to a population. They build Ekphrasis, a 400-task benchmark organized into Abstraction, Combination, Transformation, and Adaptation. Human judges perform anonymized pairwise comparisons of model outputs using detailed, dimension-specific checklists. Preferences are aggregated via Bradley-Terry models. They also construct Typed Idea Graphs to formalize and extract population clichés, using these as reference points for assessing novelty.

Result: Testing 14 language models on Ekphrasis shows that VCI decomposes into distinct axes of usefulness, expressiveness, and novelty, rather than being a single dimension aligned with surface fluency. High-performing models achieve similar overall VCI scores via different mixtures of these axes, and some highly useful visual plans are still visually clichéd. In a cross-modal grounding study, where the textual plans are faithfully rendered into images and evaluated blindly at the image level, the relative VCI ordering of models largely persists.

Conclusion: Ekphrasis provides a grounded benchmark for evaluating visual creative ideation in text-only language models, distinguishing true visual planning and concept origination from mere fluent description. Because the text-based VCI rankings mostly carry over to image-level judgments, Ekphrasis appears to capture aspects of visual creativity that extend beyond prose quality, making it a promising tool for analyzing and comparing generative models.

Abstract: Current evaluations do not isolate whether text-only language models can originate visual concepts before image generation. Fluent visual prose can hide visual-plan failures: an answer may appear creative while repeating familiar visual clichés or failing to specify a renderable scene. We define Visual Creative Ideation (VCI) as the ability to produce textual visual plans that are useful, expressive, and population-novel, and introduce Ekphrasis, a 400-task benchmark spanning Abstraction, Combination, Transformation, and Adaptation. Ekphrasis scores anonymized pairwise comparisons with dimension-specific checklists, aggregates preferences with Bradley-Terry models, and uses Typed Idea Graphs to convert task-specific population clichés into novelty references. Across 14 language models, VCI separates usefulness, expressiveness, and novelty rather than reducing to fluency: strong models achieve similar overall scores through different profiles, and useful plans can remain visually clichéd. A cross-modal grounding study further shows that text-level VCI ordering largely survives faithful rendering and blind image-level preference judgment, supporting Ekphrasis as a measure of visual ideation beyond prose quality.

</details>


### [32] [PHASE-Tree: Modeling Character-State Evolution in Long-Horizon Role-Playing Dialogue](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06975)
*Bo Tang,Jianan Yang,Junyi Zhu,Yiquan Wu,Rui Zhao,Zhengyu Yang,Yang Zhang,Feiyu Xiong,Zhiyu Li,Jiajun Shen*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: PHASE-Tree is a structured, multi-layer character-state representation for long-horizon role-playing that enables local, incremental updates and improves evolved-character generation, evaluated with a new benchmark, LongEvoRoleBench.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In long, role-playing dialogues, characters must stay recognizable while also evolving with events. Existing approaches use mostly static persona profiles that are hard to update locally without breaking other traits, and current benchmarks focus on simple persona consistency or memory recall instead of whether the model speaks from the character’s current, dynamically evolved state. The paper aims to provide both a better representation for evolving characters and a more appropriate evaluation setup for this capability.

Method: The authors propose PHASE-Tree, a hierarchical character-state tree with multiple timescales: an immutable identity root, plus mutable persona, session, and moment layers. Each mutable field is independently addressable, allowing localized updates within and across episodes without rewriting the whole profile. PHASE-Tree can guide generation either by explicitly feeding the textual state into the model or implicitly via parametric adaptation. For evaluation, they introduce LongEvoRoleBench, a unified next-utterance prediction benchmark combining four long-dialogue corpora (to test cross-episode evolution) and four short-dialogue corpora (to test fine-grained, within-scene state tracking). They compare textual PHASE-Tree variants against internal ablations and external textual baselines, and use both automatic metrics and human/LLM judgments.

Result: On the long-dialogue portion of LongEvoRoleBench, the textual PHASE-Tree achieves the best performance in 11 of 12 dataset-metric combinations versus internal variants, and in all 12 versus external textual baselines. It yields substantial relative gains: +19.7% on character-level scores, +12.4% on semantic scores, and +15.1% on embedding-based scores. A blinded human evaluation over 200 responses shows that human ratings correlate well with a GPT-4.1 judge (Pearson r = 0.65), and on descriptive prompt subsets (n = 10 for two prompt types, PT and NR), PHASE-Tree obtains an Overall score advantage of +0.20. The semantic advantages on long-dialogue tasks are robust across different LLM judges and different generation backbones.

Conclusion: A structured, multi-timescale representation like PHASE-Tree enables more faithful long-horizon role-playing by keeping a stable core identity while allowing targeted updates to evolving character traits across episodes and moments. Coupled with the LongEvoRoleBench benchmark, the work demonstrates that such dynamic character-state modeling yields consistent improvements in generating utterances that reflect a character’s current, evolved state, as supported by both automatic and human/LLM evaluations.

Abstract: Long-horizon role-playing demands that characters remain recognizable as they evolve with the narrative. Yet existing work falls short on two fronts: representations are typically static profiles that cannot be updated locally without destabilizing unchanged traits, and benchmarks mainly test persona preservation and memory recall rather than whether a model speaks from a character's currently evolved state. We address both. PHASE-Tree is a multi-timescale character-state tree with an immutable identity root and mutable persona, session, and moment layers, making each mutable field an addressable target for localized within- and cross-episode updates. It conditions generation through explicit textual provision or implicit parametric adaptation. To measure evolved-state generation, we introduce LongEvoRoleBench, which pairs four long-dialogue corpora for cross-episode evolution with four short-dialogue corpora as within-scene state-tracking checks, under a unified next-utterance protocol. On the long-dialogue core, textual PHASE-Tree ranks first in 11 of 12 dataset-metric cells against internal variants and all 12 cells against external textual baselines, improving character-level, semantic, and embedding scores by 19.7%, 12.4%, and 15.1% respectively. In a blinded 200-response study, human ratings correlate with the GPT-4.1 judge (Pearson r= 0.65); on descriptive n= 10 PT and NR prompt subsets, the Overall difference is +0.20. The long-dialogue Sem advantage persists across LLM judges and generation backbones.

</details>


### [33] [Confirming Our Biases? Evaluating the Capabilities, Risks, and Societal Impact of Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06977)
*Mudar Adas,Polina Tsvilodub,Michael Franke,Martin V. Butz*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper studies how much large language models adapt their answers to biased or suggestive prompts, showing they can be steered away from factual consistency and towards user-framed positions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While it is known that LLMs are sensitive to how prompts are worded, it is unclear how strongly they reinforce users’ expressed biases, and where the line lies between natural framing effects and explicit manipulation. The authors aim to quantify this susceptibility because it has important implications for reliability, safety, and potential misuse of LLMs when users intentionally or unintentionally nudge the model toward certain viewpoints.

Method: The authors evaluate six different LLMs on 160 prompts covering ten topics in both opinion-based and factual domains. They systematically manipulate several dimensions of the prompts: (1) prompting strategy (direct vs suggestive), (2) whether the prompt asks the model to support or challenge a given position, (3) the polarity of the prompt, (4) the user’s stated belief, and (5) the domain type (opinion vs factual). They then measure how model responses change with these controlled variations to assess alignment with the framing and divergence from factual consistency where applicable.

Result: Across models and topics, responses reliably shift in the direction suggested by the prompt framing. This holds not only for subjective, opinion-based questions but also for factual questions, where models still tend to adapt to the user’s framing. The findings show that explicit or suggestive instructions in the prompt can significantly steer model outputs, sometimes at the expense of factual accuracy or stability.

Conclusion: LLMs are highly manipulable by prompt framing: they readily align with user-framed positions and can reinforce user biases, including subtle ones. This manipulability extends into factual domains, where answers ideally should be robust to framing. The work clarifies the boundaries of this behavior and underscores risks for bias amplification, misinformation, and reliability, highlighting the need for better safeguards and calibration against prompt-based manipulation.

Abstract: It is well established that large language models (LLMs) are sensitive to prompt framing, reflecting patterns in their training data or prior prompts. In this study, we investigate the extent to which LLMs reinforce users biases expressed in the prompts and examine the boundary between implicit framing effects and explicit prompt manipulation. Specifically, we evaluate how susceptible LLMs are to direct and suggestive prompts that encourage models to support or challenge particular positions.
  We evaluate six LLMs using 160 distinct prompts spanning ten topics across opinion-based and factual domains. The prompts systematically vary in prompting strategy, support versus challenge instructions, prompt polarity, users' expressed beliefs, and topic domain, spanning both opinion-based and factual questions. Our results show that LLMs systematically adapt their responses to align with prompt framing, even in factual contexts. This suggests that prompt framing can outweigh factual consistency in model responses. Overall, our findings delineate the extent and boundaries of LLM manipulability. Furthermore, the results imply that LLMs can reinforce subtle user biases and are susceptible to explicit prompt manipulation even in domains where responses should remain factually stable.

</details>


### [34] [GPTKB 2.0: Browsing, Querying, and Auditing a Disambiguated LLM-Derived Knowledge Base](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06992)
*Yujia Hu,Tuan-Phong Nguyen,Simon Razniewski*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: GPTKB 2.0 is a large, LLM-derived, disambiguated knowledge base plus a web demo for exploring it via browsing, queries, and entity linking.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM-derived knowledge bases usually index entities only by surface strings, leading to problems with homonyms (same name, different entities) and synonyms (different names, same entity). This limits their reliability, interpretability, and usability for structured querying and downstream applications. The authors aim to build a large-scale KB from an LLM that resolves these issues through systematic entity disambiguation and provides an inspectable interface for exploration and auditing.

Method: They recursively elicit facts from a large language model and, during construction, apply context-guided entity disambiguation to split homonymous mentions and merge synonymous ones into canonical entities. The result is materialized as a knowledge graph with 38.4M triples over 1.6M canonical entities, 207.6K consolidated relations, and 66K consolidated classes. They implement a web demo that allows browsing entities and links, viewing detailed provenance (surface forms, candidate entity matches, source triples, and disambiguation decisions), running structured SPARQL queries, translating natural-language questions to SPARQL, and performing entity linking from user text to canonical KB entries. The full KB is provided for download.

Result: GPTKB 2.0, a large-scale, disambiguated KB with tens of millions of triples and hundreds of thousands of relations and classes, is successfully constructed from an LLM. The web interface exposes rich exploration capabilities, including navigation across entities and relations, inspection of disambiguation provenance, SPARQL querying, NL-to-SPARQL question answering, and entity linking. The KB is also made available for offline use.

Conclusion: The work demonstrates that it is feasible to materialize a large, disambiguated, and inspectable knowledge base directly from a large language model, overcoming limitations of earlier surface-string-based LLM KBs. The GPTKB 2.0 demo and downloadable KB provide a practical resource and toolchain for exploring, auditing, and querying LLM-derived factual knowledge.

Abstract: We present a web demo for exploring a large-scale disambiguated knowledge base (KB) materialized from a large language model (LLM). GPTKB 2.0 contains 38.4M triples over 1.6M canonical entities, together with 207.6K consolidated relations and 66K consolidated classes. Unlike prior LLM-derived knowledge bases that largely identify entities by surface strings, GPTKB 2.0 performs context-guided disambiguation during recursive KB construction, separating homonyms and merging synonymous mentions as facts are elicited. The demo makes this process inspectable: users can browse entities, follow links across the KB, and audit the provenance of individual facts, including surface forms, candidate matches, source triples, and disambiguation decisions. The interface further supports structured SPARQL queries, natural-language questions translated to SPARQL, and entity linking from user-provided text to canonical GPTKB 2.0 entries. GPTKB 2.0 is available at https://gptkb.org/, with the full KB downloadable for offline use.

</details>


### [35] [Does More Retrieved Evidence Help Visual Retrieval-Augmented Generation with Diffusion Language Models?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07006)
*Jiankun Wang,Yisen Gao,Ziwei Zhang,Xingcheng Fu,Jiaxin Bai,Chen Gao*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper studies why giving diffusion language models (DLMs) more retrieved visual evidence can hurt answer accuracy, and proposes a training-free filter (ECF) that selectively admits evidence, improving visual RAG performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most visual RAG systems assume that passing as many retrieved pages as possible to the generator always helps, because it increases the chance that the answer is covered. However, for diffusion language models, adding more visual evidence can introduce semantic conflicts and reduce answer accuracy, and this behavior is not well understood or handled. The paper aims to analyze this phenomenon and design a method that preserves the benefits of broad retrieval while avoiding performance degradation from conflicting or irrelevant evidence.

Method: The authors first perform a latent-source analysis of diffusion language models, focusing on parallel denoising and how position-wise proposals can mix incompatible visual sources, leading to unsupported answers. They examine the first-step answer-block distribution to detect early signs of interference. Based on these insights, they propose the Entropy-Based Candidate Filter (ECF), a training-free evidence-admission framework. ECF (1) segments retrieved content into multi-granularity evidence units to reduce irrelevant content within each candidate; (2) evaluates candidates using blank-controlled block confidence (comparing predictions with and without a given candidate) and retrieval rank; and (3) selectively chooses which candidates to include in the final context instead of unconditionally feeding all top-k retrieved pages.

Result: On three multimodal diffusion language models and five visual QA benchmarks, ECF consistently improves answer accuracy. Compared to the strongest fixed top-k input baseline, ECF gains on average 2.62 percentage points in accuracy. Relative to the best existing training-free method for each dataset, ECF with LLaDA2.0-Uni improves accuracy by 2.37 percentage points on average. These results empirically validate that selective evidence admission outperforms naive expansion in visual DLM-RAG.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that, for diffusion language models in visual RAG, more retrieved evidence is not always better: unfiltered expansion can harm accuracy due to semantic conflicts and source-coherence loss in parallel denoising. Interference is detectable early in the denoising process and can be mitigated by selectively admitting evidence. The proposed Entropy-Based Candidate Filter (ECF) effectively balances wide retrieval with controlled visual exposure, yielding consistent accuracy improvements without additional training, and demonstrating that careful evidence selection is key to leveraging broad retrieval in visual DLM-RAG.

Abstract: Visual retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) commonly expands the retrieved evidence set to improve answer-page coverage, implicitly assuming that all available evidence should be passed to the generator. We show that this assumption does not hold for diffusion language models (DLMs): retrieving more pages increases answer-page recall, whereas unconditionally passing all retrieved pages to the generator often reduces answer accuracy, primarily because of semantic conflict. A latent-source analysis explains this mismatch through source-coherence loss in parallel denoising, where position-wise proposals can combine incompatible visual sources into unsupported answers. We further find that such interference is already visible in the first-step answer-block distribution, making it possible to assess evidence before decoding. To preserve retrieval coverage while limiting harmful visual exposure, we propose the Entropy-Based Candidate Filter (ECF), a training-free evidence-admission framework. To reduce irrelevant content within individual candidates, ECF constructs multi-granularity evidence units; to identify beneficial additional evidence, it uses blank-controlled block confidence and retrieval rank to determine whether and which candidate should enter the final context. Across three multimodal DLMs and five visual QA benchmarks, ECF improves answer accuracy by 2.62 percentage points on average over the strongest fixed top-$k$ input and, with LLaDA2.0-Uni, by 2.37 percentage points on average over the best competing training-free result for each dataset. These results show that broader retrieval benefits visual DLM-RAG through selective evidence admission rather than unconditional evidence expansion. Code is publicly available at https://github.com/wjkuser/ECF.

</details>


### [36] [An Agentic Hybrid Top-Down and Bottom-Up Approach to Knowledge Graph Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07023)
*Emma Jouffroy,Warren Jouanneau,Marc Palyart*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces a hybrid, LLM-grounded pipeline to automatically build and maintain a multilingual skills knowledge graph from noisy HR text.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: HR platforms must organize thousands of noisy, multilingual skill declarations for tasks like talent matching, but current top-down taxonomies and bottom-up approaches struggle with unstandardized, evolving skill mentions.

Method: They propose a five-stage pipeline that grounds an LLM in the Wikidata multilingual knowledge graph and uses an agentic reflection pattern to recognize, reconcile, and extend concepts. The stages are: entity reconciliation, multilingual canonicalization, active curation, deduplication, and iterative recovery of unmapped concepts. The system anchors known skills to Wikidata entities and dynamically creates new nodes and relational metadata for unrecognized skills, adapting across five European languages.

Result: The system can autonomously transform unstructured, noisy skill mentions in five European languages into a scalable, multilingual skills knowledge graph that incorporates both recognized Wikidata-aligned skills and newly synthesized concepts, while remaining explainable and adaptable.

Conclusion: A grounded, hybrid LLM+KG pipeline with reflexive agents can reliably generate and maintain a multilingual, self-healing skills knowledge graph from messy HR text, enabling the derivation of an explicit skills taxonomy for downstream HR applications such as talent matching.

Abstract: Organizing thousands of unstandardized, multilingual expertise declarations is a persistent challenge for Human Resources (HR) platforms, directly impacting downstream tasks like accurate talent matching. To address this, we propose a hybrid knowledge graph generation pipeline that grounds a Large Language Model (LLM) in the Wikidata multilingual Knowledge Graph (KG) while employing an agentic reflexion pattern to synthesize emerging concepts and their associated metadata. Unlike rigid top-down methods or fragmented bottom-up approaches, our system anchors recognized concepts to stable Knowledge Graph entities while dynamically creating new nodes and relational metadata for unrecognized skills. Executed across five stages, entity reconciliation, multilingual canonicalization, active curation, deduplication, and the iterative recovery of unmapped concepts, the system autonomously adapts to rapidly evolving, noisy skill mentions across five European languages. Ultimately, this pipeline provides a highly scalable, explicable, and self-healing framework for generating a comprehensive skills knowledge graph, from which a structured taxonomy is derived, using unstructured, noisy text.

</details>


### [37] [HNR-DAC: Hard-Negative Reranking and Distribution-Aligned Classification for Scientific Claim Verification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07204)
*Zhenchao Wang,Xin Chen,Luoxi Zhang,Min Yang,Shiwen Ni*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper proposes HNR-DAC, a two-stage framework for scientific claim verification over cited papers that improves both evidence retrieval and claim classification by aligning training with inference conditions, achieving state-of-the-art-like results on NLPCC 2026 Task 10 Track 2.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In scientific claim verification, systems must both determine whether a claim is supported or contradicted by a cited paper and identify the specific paragraphs that justify this decision. However, many within-paper paragraphs are distractors that look similar to true evidence, and classifiers are typically trained on gold-standard evidence but tested on imperfectly retrieved evidence, causing a distribution mismatch. The authors aim to address these issues by designing a framework that is robust to confusable non-gold paragraphs and that aligns the training data distribution with what the model will actually see at inference time.

Method: The authors introduce HNR-DAC, a two-stage framework. First, Hard-Negative Reranking (HNR) takes a base reranker and uses its scores on non-gold paragraphs to measure how easily they can be confused with true evidence. It then contrasts gold evidence against the most confusable non-gold paragraphs (hard negatives) to improve reranking. Second, Distribution-Aligned Classification (DAC) trains the claim–paper relation classifier not on ideal gold evidence, but on the Top-1 paragraph selected by the (frozen) HNR stage, thus aligning the training input distribution with inference conditions. At inference, HNR provides the Top-3 paragraph IDs as the predicted evidence set, while DAC predicts the claim–paper relation based on HNR’s Top-1 paragraph.

Result: On the NLPCC 2026 Task 10 Track 2 benchmark, the best HNR-DAC configuration achieves strong performance: 97.21% Hit@3 (probability that at least one of the top-3 retrieved paragraphs is gold evidence), 95.79% Macro-F1 for the claim–paper relation classification, 94.47% Joint@3 (both correct classification and at least one correct evidence paragraph in Top-3), and an average score of 95.13%. On the official leaderboard, their submission ranks third overall in Track 2 but attains the highest Macro-F1 of 93.05%, with 70.16% Joint@3 and an average score of 81.61%.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that training both the reranking and classification stages under conditions closely matching inference—via hard-negative reranking and distribution-aligned classification—substantially improves scientific claim verification over cited papers. HNR-DAC effectively handles confusable distractor paragraphs and mitigates the train–test distribution gap, achieving competitive leaderboard rank and state-of-the-art Macro-F1 on NLPCC 2026 Task 10 Track 2.

Abstract: Scientific claim verification over a cited paper requires predicting the claim--paper relation and identifying the paragraphs that justify that prediction. This setting poses two linked challenges: within-paper distractors often resemble genuine evidence, while a classifier trained on gold evidence must operate on retrieved evidence at inference. We present HNR-DAC, a two-stage framework that trains each stage on the cases it will actually encounter. Hard-Negative Reranking (HNR) quantifies evidence confusability using a base reranker's scores on non-gold paragraphs and contrasts gold evidence against the most confusable candidates. Distribution-Aligned Classification (DAC) trains on the Top-1 paragraph produced by the same frozen HNR used to construct inference inputs, while HNR's Top-3 paragraph identifiers provide the evidence output. On the NLPCC 2026 Task 10 Track 2, the final configuration obtains 97.21% Hit@3, 95.79% Macro-F1, 94.47% Joint@3, and an average score of 95.13%. The corresponding submission ranks third on the official Track 2 leaderboard while achieving the highest overall Macro-F1 of 93.05%, alongside 70.16% Joint@3 and an average score of 81.61%.

</details>


### [38] [Measuring Concept Content in Text from LLM Activations: ESG Evidence from Concept Vectors and Linear Probes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07208)
*Luc Hazenoot,Zhaochun Ren,Amirhossein Zohrehvand*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper evaluates whether concept information encoded in frozen LLM activations can be used to measure how much a text is about a concept, without task-specific fine-tuning, and compares extraction methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing concept-content measures rely on surface text features and miss deeper judgments a reader or model forms; LLMs are known to internally encode more knowledge than they express, suggesting their activations might better capture concept content without fine-tuning.

Method: They monitor activations of frozen, out-of-the-box LLMs and extract concept measures using the Recursive Feature Machine (RFM) algorithm and simple linear probes, then compare these to embedding and surface baselines and to the model’s own answers. Experiments are run on financial text using a human-annotated ESG dataset.

Result: A simple linear probe on frozen LLM activations nearly matches a fine-tuned domain classifier’s accuracy (within 0.6 percentage points), and outperforms the same model’s explicit answers in 11 of 12 comparisons; linear probes also outperform RFM-derived concept vectors.

Conclusion: Internal activations of frozen LLMs contain rich concept content that can be accessed with simple linear probes, offering near fine-tuned performance without task-specific training and even surpassing the model’s stated answers, while RFM provides continuous concept strength scores whose validation awaits graded labels.

Abstract: Existing measures of how much a text is about a concept read the surface of the text: dictionary word shares, topic proportions, embedding similarities. They score the words a text uses, not the judgment a reader forms about it. Recent work has shown that a gap exists in what Large Language Models (LLMs) know internally versus what they express in their response. This paper asks whether that internal knowledge, read by monitoring the activations of frozen, out-of-the-box LLMs, can stand in for task-specific fine-tuning when measuring concept content, and which extraction method reads it best. We extract such measures via the Recursive Feature Machine (RFM) algorithm and via linear probing, and compare these against an embedding baseline, surface baselines, and the same model's own answer to the question. We demonstrate the approach on financial text, a domain studied extensively and served by established annotated resources, using a human-annotated Environmental, Social and Governance (ESG) dataset. The best linear probe comes within 0.6 percentage points of a fine-tuned domain classifier's accuracy without any task-specific fine-tuning, and outscores the same model's own answer to the question in eleven of twelve comparisons, so the activations carry concept content the response does not report. The simple probe consistently beats the RFM concept vectors, which in turn provide what classification alone does not: a continuous score intended to reflect how strongly a concept is present in a text, whose validation awaits graded labels.

</details>


### [39] [From Test-Time Scaling to Reusable Memory: Measuring Crystallization in Text-to-SQL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07213)
*Jiaqian Wang,Yutao Qi,Wenjin Hou,Yuanxi Che,Muning Wen*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper studies how to turn one-off test-time text-to-SQL repairs into reusable memory that improves future performance, and proposes a controlled way to measure the future value of such memory.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing text-to-SQL systems can use extra test-time computation (e.g., repair steps) to fix hard queries, but these costly corrections are usually discarded after answering. Some recent systems start storing verified repair episodes, but standard evaluations only give a single end-to-end score. This cannot tell whether performance comes from replaying exact past questions or from generalizing to new ones, nor which memory design decisions matter. The authors want a principled way to quantify and analyze the long-term benefit of storing and reusing test-time corrections, which they call the crystallization problem.

Method: They fix a base single-shot text-to-SQL solver and systematically vary only one memory-related design choice at a time. They then conduct a controlled evaluation that decomposes performance into components: (1) replay on recurring questions, (2) cross-question retention within the same databases, and (3) transfer to held-out questions on the same databases. Using the BIRD benchmark, they store verified corrected SQL queries as memory entries and experiment with different verification strategies, retrieval coverage, content granularity (e.g., database-specific content), and retrieval / memory formats, to isolate which aspects actually drive future gains.

Result: On the BIRD dataset, storing verified corrected queries leads to a 4.34 percentage point increase in held-out first-attempt accuracy, relative to the fixed base solver without memory. This improvement accounts for 44.4% of the total accuracy “headroom” that would be achievable if on-demand repair were available again at test time on the same questions. Their controlled interventions show that the main effective component of memory is database-specific content, and that reliable verification and wider retrieval coverage produce consistent gains, whereas more complex memory formats or sophisticated retrievers do not provide additional benefit.

Conclusion: Turning test-time text-to-SQL repairs into reusable memory can substantially and measurably improve future performance, but the benefits depend strongly on what is stored and how it is retrieved.
