The authors’ crystallization-focused evaluation framework isolates replay, cross-question retention, and same-database transfer, enabling clearer attribution of performance gains to specific memory choices. Their results suggest that simple, well-verified, broadly retrieved, database-specific memories are more valuable than elaborate formats or retrievers. They release code and artifacts to support further research on memory crystallization in text-to-SQL systems.

Abstract: Test-time scaling can correct difficult text-to-SQL queries, but the extra computation is normally discarded after each answer. Systems increasingly retain verified repair episodes, yet evaluations still report one end-to-end score. It cannot distinguish replay on recurring questions from help on unseen questions, or identify the responsible memory choice. We call measuring this future value the crystallization problem. Our controlled evaluation holds the single-shot solver fixed and varies one memory choice at a time. We separately measure replay, cross-question retention, and held-out same-database transfer. On BIRD, storing verified corrected queries improves held-out first-attempt accuracy by 4.34 percentage points. This gain captures 44.4% of the accuracy headroom provided by on-demand repair on the same questions. Controlled interventions identify database-specific content as the main operating ingredient. Reliable verification and broader retrieval coverage yield supported gains; richer formats and elaborate retrievers do not. Open-source code, evaluation artifacts, and reproduction instructions are available at https://github.com/ai-jiaqian/text-to-sql-memory-crystallization.

</details>


### [40] [Skaling: Chinchilla's Exponents Meet Kaplan's Coupling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07222)
*Mathurin Videau,Badr Youbi-Idrissi,David Lopez-Paz,Kartik Ahuja*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: They propose a new neural scaling law, called the Skaling law, that couples model size and data with an interaction exponent, giving more accurate loss predictions and cheaper extrapolation of performance across model/data grids.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing neural scaling laws assume that model size and dataset size affect loss independently, leading to systematic errors, especially when data is scarce or models are heavily overtrained. This makes it hard to reliably predict performance and allocate compute efficiently from small-scale experiments.

Method: They derive a generalized scaling functional form—the Skaling law—that explicitly couples model capacity and data via a single interaction exponent. They empirically fit this new law across model/data grids, compare its prediction error (MAPE) to standard scaling formulations in both interpolation and extrapolation settings, and combine it with a sparse grid sampling strategy focused in low-compute regions to predict performance over the full grid.

Result: The Skaling law reduces MAPE by a factor of 1.5–3 compared to standard scaling laws in both interpolation and extrapolation. When combined with a sparse grid design confined to low-compute configurations, it can accurately extrapolate performance to the entire model/data grid using roughly 10x less compute than uniform sweeps.

Conclusion: Coupling model size and data scale via the Skaling law yields more accurate and robust predictions of model loss, especially at the extremes of data scarcity and overtraining. This enables reliable performance forecasting from much smaller, cheaper experiments and provides a more resource-efficient methodology for planning compute budgets in future large-scale language model training.

Abstract: Neural scaling laws are foundational for language model development, yet standard formulations systematically under- and overestimate loss at data-scarce and overtraining extremes. This failure originates in the underlying assumption that model size and training data impact the loss independently. To address this, we introduce the Skaling law, a generalized functional form that couples model capacity and data through a single interaction exponent. This simple extension reduces the Mean Absolute Percentage Error (MAPE) by 1.5-3x across both interpolation and extrapolation regimes. When paired with a sparse grid strategy restricted to low-compute regimes, the Skaling law achieves accurate full-grid extrapolation using approximately 10x less compute than uniform sweeps. By enabling reliable performance prediction from small-scale experiments, the Skaling law provides a more robust and resource-efficient framework for allocating compute budgets in next-generation model training.

</details>


### [41] [Stoicheia: Character-Level Masked Diffusion for Ancient Greek Textual Restoration, Parsing, and Metrical Scansion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07249)
*Eric Cullhed,Albin Thörn Cleland*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: Stoicheia is a 405M‑parameter character‑level masked‑diffusion encoder for Ancient Greek that jointly handles restoration, segmentation, accentuation, and punctuation, significantly outperforming prior state‑of‑the‑art systems on multiple tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Digital humanities and NLP for Ancient Greek need robust tools to restore damaged texts, segment and annotate unspaced or partially preserved manuscripts, and add prosodic and orthographic features (accents, macrons, punctuation). Existing systems often rely on word‑level tokenization, task‑specific architectures, and are trained on overlapping corpora that complicate fair evaluation. There is a need for a unified, character‑level model that can operate directly on raw text streams, support multiple philological tasks without retokenization, and be evaluated in a way that avoids data leakage on specific literary passages or document types.

Method: The authors build Stoicheia, a 405M‑parameter character‑level masked‑diffusion encoder whose input is decomposed into five synchronized planes: base letters, word/sentence boundary markers, diacritics, capitalization, and punctuation. Each plane can be masked independently, enabling the same backbone to tackle different restoration and annotation tasks. The model is pretrained with a diffusion‑style denoising objective on an open, revision‑pinned Ancient Greek corpus of 380M words. They release eleven checkpoints: ten decontaminated folds such that for any given literary passage at least one model has never seen it, plus one model trained without documentary texts. They evaluate Stoicheia in three settings—epigraphic/papyrological reconstruction, morphosyntactic tagging and dependency parsing, and macronization with metrical scansion—each with a matched randomly‑initialized control to isolate the effect of diffusion pretraining. They also benchmark Stoicheia directly against Ithaca and its Aeneas‑framework successor on Ithaca’s own test split with identical frozen samples and strict scoring.

Result: Pretraining with character‑level diffusion yields large gains over random initialization: a 5.6‑point reduction in character error rate on inscription reconstruction, a 12.9‑point improvement in labeled attachment score for dependency parsing, and a 6.0‑point increase in balanced accuracy on macronization with metrical scansion. On the Ithaca benchmark, Stoicheia substantially outperforms prior state‑of‑the‑art systems: it reduces character error rate from 24.6 (Ithaca) and 23.5 (Aeneas‑framework successor) to 15.5, and increases top‑1 accuracy from 63.0 and 64.0 to 74.5, under identical sampling and strict evaluation conditions.

Conclusion: Stoicheia demonstrates that a single, large, character‑level masked‑diffusion encoder with multi‑plane input can effectively support a range of philological tasks in Ancient Greek—restoration, segmentation, syntactic analysis, and prosodic/orthographic enrichment—without task‑specific retokenization. Thorough pretraining on a large, carefully versioned corpus, combined with decontaminated, fold‑based checkpoint releases, yields strong and reliable performance gains and fairer evaluation for historical texts. The model sets a new state of the art on inscription and papyrus reconstruction (notably on Ithaca’s benchmark) and shows that character‑level diffusion pretraining is a powerful approach for low‑resource, historical‑language NLP and digital philology.

Abstract: We introduce Stoicheia, a 405M-parameter character-level masked-diffusion encoder for Ancient Greek whose input factors into five aligned, independently maskable planes: letters, word and sentence boundaries, diacritics, capitalization, and punctuation. A single backbone can therefore restore lacunae, re-segment, accentuate, and punctuate unspaced text without task-specific retokenization. We pretrain it on an open, revision-pinned corpus of 380M words and release eleven checkpoints: ten rotated, decontaminated folds, guaranteeing that for any given literary passage at least one released model has never seen its text, and one with no exposure to documentary texts. Three experiments - reconstruction of damaged inscriptions and papyri, morphosyntactic tagging and dependency parsing, and macronization with metrical scansion - each carry a matched random-initialization control, isolating what character-level diffusion pretraining contributes: 5.6 CER points on inscription reconstruction, 12.9 LAS on parsing, and 6.0 points of balanced accuracy on macronization. On Ithaca's own test split, with identical frozen samples and strict scoring, Stoicheia reduces character error relative to both prior state-of-the-art systems, from 24.6 (Ithaca) and 23.5 (its 2025 Aeneas-framework successor) to 15.5, and raises top-1 accuracy from 63.0 and 64.0 to 74.5.

</details>


### [42] [Why Knowing Both Hops Is Not Enough: Understanding Two-Hop Generalization in Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07261)
*Zili Zhang,Yilin Wang,Heng Wang,Herun Wan,Minnan Luo*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper studies why transformers/LLMs often fail at simple two-hop reasoning despite succeeding at more complex tasks, identifies a layerwise mismatch in how intermediate representations are used, and proposes a recurrent-style training strategy that improves out-of-distribution two-hop generalization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs are surprisingly bad at simple two-hop reasoning queries (e.g., A->B, B->C, ask for A->C) even when they store both atomic facts correctly. This is puzzling given their success on more complex multi-hop tasks. The authors want to mechanistically understand the internal circuitry causing this behavior and why generalization depends on whether the query’s second hop matches the training distribution.

Method: Train transformers from scratch in a controlled symbolic environment where two-hop reasoning tasks can be precisely specified and probed. Design different regimes where the second hop at test time is either in-distribution or out-of-distribution relative to training. Perform mechanistic interpretability analyses to trace how intermediate entity representations are constructed across layers and how later layers use them. Based on these findings, devise and test a recurrent-style training approach that encourages the model to reuse reasoning circuitry rather than just mapping atomic facts to outputs.

Result: The authors find a consistent pattern: models generalize well on two-hop reasoning when the second hop is from the same distribution as training, but systematically fail when that second hop is out-of-distribution. Mechanistic analysis shows that lower layers do construct correct intermediate entity representations in both cases, but in the failing regime upper layers have learned to treat these representations mainly as direct cues for output (pattern matching on atomic facts) rather than as inputs to a reasoning process. The proposed recurrent-style training improves performance on out-of-distribution two-hop queries by encouraging reuse of reasoning circuits across different input forms.

Conclusion: Two-hop generalization behavior in transformers can be fully explained by how intermediate entity representations are formed and then (mis)used across layers. Success arises when the model develops stable intermediate representations that upper layers actively reason over; failure arises when upper layers merely map these representations to outputs, leading to brittleness when the second hop is out-of-distribution. A recurrent-style training strategy that enforces reuse of reasoning mechanisms can substantially improve out-of-distribution two-hop reasoning, suggesting a path toward more robust compositional generalization in LLMs.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) can solve complex multi-hop problems yet exhibit puzzling failures on simple two-hop queries: although a model may correctly store each individual hop, it often fails to combine them. To understand the internal mechanisms of this phenomenon, we train transformers from scratch in a controlled symbolic environment. Our experiments reveal a pattern in two-hop generalization: models generalize reliably when the second hop follows the training distribution, but always fail when it deviates.
  Through mechanistic analysis, we provide a complete explanation for these distinct generalization behaviors: in settings where models generalize successfully, performance is driven by the emergence of consistent intermediate representations for the same entities across contexts, whereas failures on settings where the second hop is out-of-distribution arise from a mismatch across layers: lower layers correctly construct these intermediate representations, but upper layers, while trained on corresponding atomic facts, primarily learn to map them to outputs rather than to reason over them.
  Driven by this insight, we propose a recurrent-style training strategy, which enables transformers to reuse their reasoning circuitry across input forms and substantially improves generalization on out-of-distribution two-hop queries.

</details>


### [43] [Gaze Behavior in Visual World Experiments Can be Modeled With Off-the-shelf Language-Vision Encoders](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07282)
*Rahul Murali Shankar,Titus von der Malsburg,Sebastian Padó*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: They propose a new method to predict human gaze behavior in multimodal (vision + language) experiments using off-the-shelf CLIP-like bi-encoders plus a bimodal attribution technique, and show it replicates classic visual world study results without task-specific training.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most computational psycholinguistic work with neural language models focuses on unimodal written/spoken language, neglecting multimodal paradigms like visual world studies where people process images and language together. There is a need for models that can account for human predictive processing in such multimodal settings, particularly gaze behavior, using modern multimodal neural architectures.

Method: Use a simple multimodal bi-encoder model of the CLIP family that jointly embeds visual and linguistic input. Combine this with a bimodal attribution method to estimate, over time, which visual regions participants are likely to look at given concurrent linguistic input. Apply this framework to simulate eye-gaze patterns in a well-known English visual world experiment that demonstrates human predictive processing.

Result: The proposed model robustly reproduces key empirical findings from a classic English visual world study—namely, human-like predictive eye movements toward contextually likely referents—even though the model was not trained specifically for this task and requires no fine-tuning. Its performance suggests that CLIP-style embeddings plus attribution can approximate human gaze behavior in such tasks.

Conclusion: A relatively simple, off-the-shelf multimodal bi-encoder combined with a bimodal attribution technique can serve as a promising computational model of human predictive processing in multimodal visual world paradigms, predicting gaze behavior without specialized architecture or training. This opens the door to broader use of foundation multimodal models in computational psycholinguistics beyond purely textual settings.

Abstract: The recent advances in neural language models have also spurred much work in computational psycholinguistics, asking whether neural LMs are also promising models of human language processing. However, work has been overwhelmingly focused on the unimodal case of written or spoken language. In contrast, multimodal experimental paradigms, like visual world studies that present participants with both visual and linguistic input simultaneously, have been neglected. In this paper, we present a novel approach that predicts gaze behavior in visual world studies. It does so by combining a simple multi-modal bi-encoder model of the CLIP family with a bimodal attribution method. We demonstrate the ability of this approach to robustly replicate the results of a seminal English visual world study which shows hu- man predictive processing. Remarkably, it does so without a generative architecture and without the need for fine-tuning, despite not being trained for this task.

</details>


### [44] [Grammar Engineering Meets LLMs: Development of Cantonese and Irish ParGram Treebanks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07283)
*Chit-Fung Lam,Elaine Uí Dhonnchadha*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper reports on creating Cantonese and Irish treebanks in the Parallel Grammar project and evaluates how well a multilingual LLM (gpt-oss-120b) can help with parallel grammar engineering, finding limited but non-zero usefulness and emphasizing the need for expert linguists.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Grammar engineering for multiple languages requires deep linguistic and computational expertise; maintaining cross-linguistic parallelism is particularly challenging. With the rise of multilingual LLMs, there is interest in whether they can reduce the effort in building parallel grammars and treebanks, especially for less-resourced languages like Cantonese and Irish. The paper is motivated by exploring this potential and its limits in a concrete grammar-engineering setting (ParGram).

Method: The authors develop Cantonese and Irish treebanks within the ParGram project, enforcing parallelism at an abstract functional level. They then test OpenAI's multilingual gpt-oss-120b model on two kinds of tasks relevant to grammar engineering: (i) Cantonese–Irish translation under different prompt languages, and (ii) generation of formal syntactic structures comparable to ParGram-style analyses. They evaluate translation quality and examine how well the generated structures capture cross-linguistically parallel predicate-argument relations and higher-level abstractions.

Result: Translation quality between Cantonese and Irish was generally poor and did not improve depending on which language was used for prompting. For syntactic-structure generation, the LLM sometimes produced outputs that were interpretable and structurally suggestive, but it performed badly on tasks that needed consistent cross-linguistic abstraction, such as mapping equivalent functional structures across both languages. Still, some outputs partially captured predicate-argument relations and provided alternative analytical ideas.

Conclusion: Multilingual LLMs currently have substantial limitations as direct tools for parallel grammar engineering in low-resource language pairs like Cantonese–Irish: their translations are unreliable and their formal structural outputs are inconsistent and weak at cross-linguistic abstraction. Nevertheless, they can offer secondary value as heuristic aids, e.g., by suggesting possible analyses or highlighting predicate-argument patterns. The study concludes that expert-driven grammar engineering and verification remain crucial, with LLMs serving only as supplementary, not substitutive, tools.

Abstract: Grammar engineering requires expertise in linguistic formalism and computational implementation, especially in parallel grammar projects that balance cross-linguistic consistency with language-specific properties. This paper presents the development of Cantonese and Irish treebanks within the Parallel Grammar (ParGram) Project, where linguistic parallelism is maintained at an abstract functional level. We also investigate the methodological potential and limitations of using multilingual LLMs to support grammar engineering, focusing on Cantonese-Irish translation and the generation of formal syntactic structures using OpenAI's gpt-oss-120b model. The results show that translation performance was generally unsatisfactory and unaffected by prompt language. For syntactic structure generation, the model produced some structurally meaningful outputs, but performed poorly on tasks requiring cross-linguistic abstraction. Nonetheless, LLM-generated outputs may still offer some reference value by suggesting alternative analyses and (partially) capturing predicate-argument relations. Overall, our findings highlight both the potential and limitations of using LLMs in collaborative grammar engineering, while underscoring the continued importance of expert-driven analysis and verification.

</details>


### [45] [Natural Language Processing Psychometrics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07316)
*Edoardo Sebastiano De Duro,Emma Franchino,Massimo Stella*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces 'NLP Psychometrics', a framework that uses interpretable linguistic and network features from LLM-generated text personas to predict mental health scores, evaluate model bias, and transfer to real human data, while emphasizing that synthetic data cannot replace human validation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing NLP models that predict mental health from text often act as black boxes and do not clearly indicate whether predictions rely on emotional content, linguistic structure, or contextual knowledge, nor do they test how well models generalize beyond the specific format they were trained on. There is a need for a psychometrically grounded, interpretable approach that connects psychological scores to transparent linguistic evidence and works across text types, while also probing how much can realistically be learned from synthetic, LLM-generated data.

Method: The authors define controlled LLM personas (cognitive digital shadows) and have nine large language models complete standardized mental health questionnaires, providing free-text explanations for each item. From these responses, they derive emotional profiles and syntactic-semantic structure using textual forma mentis networks, and combine these with personality and sociodemographic variables as inputs to ablated random forest regressors. They apply SHAP analysis to quantify which features most strongly drive prediction and in what direction, then test generalization: (1) predicting scores from synthetic diaries written by low- vs high-score personas, and (2) classifying clinical vs control participants in real transcripts using only network/emotion features, without retraining on those formats.

Result: Full random forest models trained on LLM-generated questionnaire data explain substantial variance in mental health scales: up to 70.8% for life satisfaction (SWLS), 55.7% for depression (PHQ-9), and for DASS-21, 68.5% depression, 76.0% anxiety, and 72.4% stress. Sociodemographic variables alone have negligible predictive value for depression, anxiety, or stress, but contribute to life satisfaction, where income and emotion features are most important. For depression and anxiety, neuroticism and network topology features dominate, with opposite directions of effect between the two constructs. Without retraining, models separate synthetic diaries from low- vs high-score personas with correlations up to r = 0.91, and using only network/emotion features achieve up to 68% accuracy in distinguishing clinical from control participants in real transcripts.

Conclusion: NLP Psychometrics provides an interpretable, psychometrically motivated framework that links mental health predictions from text to specific emotional and network-structural features, enabling transparent analysis of what drives model decisions and how they generalize across text formats. Synthetic data from LLM personas can reveal model biases, capture patterns akin to clinical rumination, and bootstrap predictors that transfer moderately well to real human language, but they cannot replace direct human validation. The approach thus clarifies both the strengths and the limitations of using LLM-generated personas for psychological assessment and demonstrates how interpretable AI and network/emotional features can make psychological text prediction explicit, measurable, and testable.

Abstract: Natural Language Processing (NLP) models predicting mental health outcomes rarely specify what they measure: contextual knowledge, emotional content, or syntactic structure. NLP Psychometrics treats psychological prediction from text as a psychometric problem, linking scores to interpretable linguistic evidence and testing beyond the training text format. Nine LLMs, conditioned on controlled personas (cognitive digital shadows), completed psychometric questionnaires with textual explanations per item. We extracted emotional profiles and syntactic-semantic structure via textual forma mentis networks, combined with personality and sociodemographic variables in ablated random forest (RF) regressors, using SHAP to identify which features drove performance and in which direction. Full RF models explained up to 70.8% of variance in life satisfaction (SWLS), 55.7% in depression (PHQ-9), and, for DASS-21, 68.5% depression, 76.0% anxiety, 72.4% stress. Sociodemographics alone explained no meaningful variance in depression, anxiety, or stress, but did so for life satisfaction, where emotion features and income were the strongest predictors; neuroticism and network topology instead dominated depression and anxiety, reversing direction between them. Without retraining, RF models separated diaries from low- and high-score personas ($r$ up to 0.91) and, using only network/emotion features, classified clinical from control participants in real transcripts with up to 68% accuracy. These results show the promise and limits of synthetic data: LLM personas can expose model biases, recover patterns consistent with clinical rumination, and support psychometric prediction from human text without a matched questionnaire, but cannot substitute for human validation. NLP Psychometrics makes these distinctions explicit, measurable, and testable through interpretable AI and network/emotional features.

</details>


### [46] [Zero Gap Is Not Restoration: Stratified Per-Question Probability Evaluation and Step-wise Mitigation of Benchmark Contamination](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07341)
*Ruijie Hou,Yueyang Jiao,Zhao Wang,Yingming Li*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper critiques existing metrics for evaluating contamination mitigation in language models and proposes a new metric, SA-PPG, alongside a new mitigation method, RailCap, which more reliably measures and reduces the impact of test-set contamination.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Test questions from public benchmarks often appear in pretraining data, causing language models to memorize answers and produce inflated evaluation scores. Existing mitigation evaluation, mainly via the G-AP metric, is inaccurate because it relies on binary correctness, aggregates before differencing, and treats all questions uniformly, enabling misleading strategies. There is a need for a more faithful evaluation metric and improved mitigation methods that directly address contamination during generation rather than relying solely on imperfect pre-estimates of contamination.

Method: The authors introduce SA-PPG, which (1) estimates a model’s solve probability for each question via sampling, (2) computes the per-question probability gap between contaminated and clean models, and (3) aggregates these gaps within strata defined by the clean model’s solve probability, reducing cancellation and gaming of the metric. They also propose RailCap, a decoding-time mitigation method that detects when a sampled trajectory collapses back to the greedy (memorized) path; in such cases, it caps the next token’s probability to the runner-up token, repeatedly suppressing greedy behavior until the output distribution is sufficiently spread out.

Result: Using SA-PPG on multiple contaminated models and benchmarks, the authors find that previous mitigation methods were thought to be more effective than they actually are; their true restoration effect is substantially overestimated under G-AP but revealed as weaker under SA-PPG. RailCap, in contrast, achieves the lowest SA-PPG among tested methods, indicating it more effectively reduces contamination-driven memorization while preserving genuine capability.

Conclusion: SA-PPG is a more robust and informative metric than G-AP for evaluating contamination mitigation, as it works at the level of per-question solve probabilities and stratified aggregation, avoiding several weaknesses of prior evaluation. RailCap offers a practical, decoding-time mechanism to suppress memorized, greedy trajectories and better restore a model’s genuine performance on contaminated benchmarks, outperforming previous strategies under the new SA-PPG metric.

Abstract: Test data from public benchmarks inevitably leaks into pretraining corpora, inflating evaluation scores once memorized. \textbf{Contamination mitigation evaluation} intervenes in the decoding process to suppress memorization and restore a contaminated model's genuine capability, but its prevailing metric, the \textbf{G-AP} (\textbf{G}ap of \textbf{A}ggregate \textbf{P}erformance), is flawed. Discrete correct/incorrect readouts cannot characterize per-question performance, averaging before differencing lets over- and under-suppression cancel out, and uniform per-question weighting invites strategies to push solve probabilities onto the clean model's high-frequency values. We propose \textbf{SA-PPG} (\textbf{S}tratified \textbf{A}ggregate of \textbf{P}er-question \textbf{P}robability \textbf{G}aps): estimate each question's solve probability by sampling, difference it against the clean model per question, and aggregate within groups defined by the clean model's solve probability. Existing mitigation strategies first estimate where contamination lies and then operate on the estimate, so they are only as correct as the estimate. \textbf{RailCap} instead judges contamination during generation: whenever a sample falls back onto the greedy trajectory, the next trajectory token is capped to the runner-up, accumulating suppression until the response distribution becomes sufficiently dispersed. Across multiple contaminated models and benchmarks, SA-PPG reveals that prior strategies' restoration is substantially overestimated, while RailCap attains the lowest SA-PPG.

</details>


### [47] [Geo-Spatial Concept Probing of Large Language Models: Abstraction, Compositionality, and Grounding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07353)
*Karim Radouane,Jose G Moreno,Lynda Tamine*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper introduces a controlled benchmark to probe whether LLMs truly understand spatial concepts and their core properties, revealing current limitations and design factors that affect conceptual understanding.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing evaluations of LLMs’ conceptual understanding rely on natural-language benchmarks or narrowly defined synthetic tasks that conflate multiple skills and lack precise control over the concepts being tested. This makes it hard to know whether models genuinely understand concepts or are exploiting surface patterns. The authors want a rigorous, concept-centric way to test key properties of concepts—abstraction, compositionality, and groundness—within LLMs.

Method: They construct a concept-centric benchmark focused on spatial concepts (direction, distance, topology and their compositions). The benchmark is implemented as controlled question-answering tasks designed to isolate and test three core properties of concepts: abstraction (generalizing beyond specific instances), compositionality (combining simpler concepts into more complex ones), and groundness (linking concepts to underlying structured representations). They then run extensive experiments across multiple LLM architectures and training setups, systematically varying model scale and design, to probe how these factors influence conceptual understanding as reflected in task performance.

Result: Across diverse architectures and training regimes, the models show consistent and measurable limitations in handling spatial concepts and their compositions. Performance patterns indicate that simply scaling up models is not sufficient for robust conceptual understanding. The experiments highlight specific weaknesses in abstraction, compositional reasoning, and grounding, and show that architectural and training choices significantly affect how well LLMs acquire and compose structured concepts.

Conclusion: Current LLMs have substantial gaps in genuine conceptual understanding, especially for structured spatial concepts and their compositions. The proposed benchmark exposes these weaknesses in a controlled way and helps identify which design and training choices matter most. The authors argue that future “concept-based” LLMs should be redesigned with mechanisms that better support abstraction, compositionality, and grounding, which in turn can improve information access and knowledge management. They also release code to facilitate further research on probing and improving conceptual understanding in LLMs.

Abstract: Understanding concepts is fundamental to generalization. Despite their impressive performance on a wide range of tasks, Large Language Models (LLMs) still struggle with genuine concept understanding. Prior work has evaluated conceptual understanding in LLMs using natural-language benchmarks or narrowly scoped synthetic tasks, but these settings often conflate multiple skills or lack precise control over the underlying concepts and their properties. To support controlled probing of concepts in LLMs, we design tests on their core properties: abstraction, compositionality, and groundness. We set up a concept-centric benchmark, targeting spatial concepts such as direction, distance, topology, and their compositions, and use question answering tasks serving as a proxy. We conduct extensive experiments across multiple LLM architectures and training regimes to analyze how model scale and design impact conceptual understanding. The results reveal clear limitations in current LLMs and provide insights into the factors shaping their ability to acquire and compose structured concepts. Our findings shed light on how concept-based LLMs can be redesigned for improved information access and knowledge management. The code will be available at https://github.com/rd20karim/concept-probing.

</details>


### [48] [LitTraceQA: A Benchmark for Multi-Stage Grounding and Verification in Scientific Question Answering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07370)
*Xuye Liu,Yimu Wang,Peng Shi,Bo Xue,Xiangrui Ke,Songcheng Cai,Kath Choi,Di Wu,Freda Shi,Krzysztof Czarnecki*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: LitTraceQA is a benchmark for verifiable, literature-grounded scientific question answering that evaluates retrieval, evidence grounding, and answer generation from scientific papers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing language models and research assistants often answer scientific questions with fluent but weakly supported text. There is a need for benchmarks that require systems to not only generate answers but also to identify relevant papers and explicitly ground responses in concrete evidence from the literature.

Method: The authors construct LitTraceQA, a benchmark where each example consists of a research question, a pool of paper metadata, and required outputs: canonical paper identifiers, locations of supporting evidence, and answers in various formats (free text, multiple choice, and tables). The benchmark targets multiple evidence modalities common in scientific reading—tables, figures, text spans, equations/algorithms, and citation contexts. They release a public development split with 55 fully annotated examples and analyze a larger collection of 4,978 unique-question records over 4,859 gold papers. The evaluation protocol separates paper retrieval, evidence grounding, and answer accuracy.

Result: They produce a public development set with 55 examples (26 single-paper, 29 multi-paper) that includes gold papers, detailed evidence annotations, and answers. Additionally, they compile a larger annotated collection with 4,978 unique questions and 4,859 unique gold papers. The benchmark enables fine-grained evaluation of scientific QA systems on three dimensions: paper retrieval performance, correctness of evidence grounding, and faithfulness/accuracy of generated answers.

Conclusion: LitTraceQA offers a comprehensive testbed for scientific question answering systems that must produce verifiable, literature-grounded answers. By requiring systems to retrieve relevant papers, locate specific supporting evidence, and generate accurate answers across several formats and evidence types, the benchmark is designed to push models beyond unsupported summarization toward trustworthy, evidence-backed scientific assistance.

Abstract: Scientific literature is increasingly used as a knowledge source for language models, retrieval-augmented generation systems, and research assistants, but answering research questions from papers requires more than fluent generation. A reliable system must identify the relevant papers, locate the concrete evidence that supports the answer, and produce a response that is faithful to that evidence. We present LitTraceQA, a benchmark for literature-grounded question answering over scientific papers. Given a research question and a metadata pool of papers, a system must return three connected outputs: canonical paper identifiers, supporting evidence locations, and answers in one or more requested formats, including free-form text, multiple-choice answers, and structured tables. LitTraceQA targets evidence types common in scientific reading: tables, figures, text spans, equations or algorithms, and citation contexts. The public development split contains 55 examples, including 26 hidden-source single-paper questions and 29 multi-paper questions, and provides gold papers, evidence annotations, and answers for local validation. We also analyze a larger final annotation collection with 4,978 unique-question records over 4,859 unique gold papers. By evaluating paper retrieval, evidence grounding, and answer accuracy separately, LitTraceQA provides a testbed for scientific QA systems that produce verifiable answers rather than unsupported summaries.

</details>


### [49] [An Exploratory Evaluation of LLM-Assisted Rewriting of Moderate-Complexity Financial Sentences for DisCoCat-Based Sentiment Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07439)
*Brian Llinas,Nikos Chrisochoides*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: The paper explores using large language models (LLMs) to rewrite moderately complex financial sentiment sentences into simpler, grammar-compatible forms for quantum natural language processing (QNLP) with DisCoCat, reducing circuit resources while modestly improving classification accuracy over a low-complexity-only baseline.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: DisCoCat-based QNLP has shown promise in financial sentiment analysis but suffers from key practical limitations: it is sensitive to parsing errors, is expensive to simulate, and struggles with longer or more complex sentences. As a result, prior work often restricts itself to low-complexity inputs, limiting applicability to real-world financial text. The authors aim to determine whether LLMs can preprocess and transform more complex sentences into forms that are both parser-friendly and circuit-efficient while retaining sentiment information, thereby expanding the usable input space for DisCoCat models.

Method: The authors design an LLM-assisted preprocessing workflow in which financial sentiment sentences of moderate complexity are rewritten via controlled prompting to compress, simplify, or decompose them into versions suitable for a DisCoCat pipeline. They experiment with different prompting strategies (e.g., different prompt templates such as Prompt B), multiple LLMs (e.g., GPT-4.1-mini), and different filtering configurations to ensure parser compatibility and circuit efficiency. These rewritten sentences are then processed through the DisCoCat QNLP setup, and the resulting quantum circuits are evaluated for resource usage (qubits, gates). The classification performance (accuracy) on financial sentiment tasks is compared to a baseline that only uses low-complexity sentences as in Stein et al. They also analyze how varying the size of the training split affects downstream accuracy, using correlation analysis (Pearson r).

Result: The strongest compression-oriented rewriting strategies yield significant quantum resource savings, reducing average qubit and gate counts by over 70% relative to unmodified moderate-complexity sentences. In terms of sentiment classification performance, the best configuration—GPT-4.1-mini with Prompt B—achieves a mean accuracy of 0.550 ± 0.035, outperforming the low-complexity-only DisCoCat baseline accuracy of 0.521 ± 0.050. Interestingly, increasing the amount of training data does not consistently improve performance; instead, training-split size is moderately negatively correlated with accuracy (Pearson r = -0.446), suggesting that more data of this type may introduce noise or that model capacity and circuit constraints limit gains from additional examples.

Conclusion: LLM-assisted rewriting can partially overcome practical limitations of DisCoCat-based QNLP for financial sentiment analysis by making some moderately complex sentences compatible with existing parsers and more efficient quantum circuits, while achieving modest accuracy gains over a low-complexity-only baseline. However, the sensitivity to prompt design, the need for careful filtering, and the non-trivial relationship between training-set size and performance indicate that scalable, robust QNLP pipelines will require further work on prompt engineering, circuit-aware text transformations, and possibly model architecture. The findings are exploratory but suggest that integrating classical LLM preprocessing with quantum grammar-based models is a promising direction for handling more realistic financial text within QNLP constraints.

Abstract: Quantum natural language processing (QNLP) provides a grammar-aware framework for text modeling, and Distributional Compositional Categorical (DisCoCat) is one of its theoretically grounded formulations. Prior work on financial sentiment analysis has identified practical limitations of DisCoCat, including parser sensitivity, high simulation cost, and difficulty handling longer sentences. We study an LLM-assisted preprocessing workflow that uses controlled rewriting to compress, simplify, or decompose moderate-complexity financial sentiment sentences into parser-compatible, circuit-efficient variants while preserving sentiment-bearing meaning. We compare prompting strategies, language models, and filtering configurations with the low-complexity-only DisCoCat baseline of Stein et al. At the circuit level, the strongest compression variants reduce average qubit and gate counts by more than 70 percent relative to the raw moderate-complexity subset. Across repeated training runs, GPT-4.1-mini with Prompt B achieves the highest observed mean accuracy, $0.550 \pm 0.035$, compared with $0.521 \pm 0.050$ for the baseline. Larger training splits do not necessarily improve downstream performance; across evaluated configurations, training-split size has a moderately negative association with accuracy (Pearson $r=-0.446$). These results provide exploratory evidence that LLM-assisted rewriting can make some moderate-complexity inputs usable within the evaluated DisCoCat configuration, while highlighting prompt design, filtering, and circuit-aware preprocessing as considerations for more scalable QNLP-based financial sentiment analysis.

</details>


### [50] [CoinRAG: Contextualized Information Nugget KV Cache Reuse for Long-Context RAG](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07458)
*Gyuwan Kim,Cheoneum Park,Tao Yang*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: CoinRAG improves RAG efficiency and accuracy by reusing fine-grained, query-relevant KV-cache “nuggets” instead of whole retrieved chunks, yielding a better latency–quality trade-off on long-context QA.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing RAG systems with KV cache reuse typically operate at chunk level: they reuse representations for entire retrieved chunks to avoid re-encoding long contexts and save computation. However, chunks are coarse, contain redundant or noisy information, and do not align well with the fine-grained, query-relevant evidence actually needed to answer a question. This leads to wasted compute and suboptimal accuracy, especially under strict prefill latency constraints in long-context settings. The paper is motivated by the need to push the Pareto frontier between latency (efficiency) and answer quality (accuracy) by making cache reuse more fine-grained and semantically focused, rather than bound to arbitrary chunk boundaries.

Method: The authors propose CoinRAG, a RAG framework that performs fine-grained KV cache reuse at the level of “information nuggets” rather than full chunks. First, they run a two-stage retrieval: (1) standard chunk-level retrieval to find relevant documents; (2) within those chunks, finer-grained retrieval to identify query-relevant semantic units (“nuggets”). For each nugget, the KV representations are precomputed offline and sliced. At inference time, CoinRAG composes an efficient contextual representation by assembling only the KV slices corresponding to the selected nuggets, optionally augmented with a light chunk-level context. This composition builds a compact, semantically aligned context without re-encoding whole chunks, thus reducing prefill cost while retaining or improving answer quality.

Result: On LongBench multi-hop question answering benchmarks, CoinRAG achieves a better speed–accuracy trade-off than existing RAG and KV-cache reuse baselines. Under a standard fast prefill latency budget, it defines a new Pareto frontier, reducing operational cost while improving answer quality. Quantitatively, it delivers an average 5.3% relative F1 improvement compared to baselines, demonstrating that fine-grained nugget-level cache reuse leads to both higher efficiency and better accuracy in long-context RAG.

Conclusion: CoinRAG shows that decomposing retrieved context into semantically meaningful, fine-grained units and reusing their precomputed KV caches can substantially improve long-context RAG. By replacing coarse chunk-level reuse with nugget-level selection and composition, CoinRAG reduces redundancy and noise, enabling more compact and relevant conditioning for the language model. The method pushes the Pareto frontier under low prefill latency constraints, achieving both lower cost and higher answer quality on multi-hop QA. This suggests that future RAG systems should focus on more granular, semantics-aware cache reuse and context construction rather than relying solely on coarse chunk-based retrieval and encoding.

Abstract: Recent optimization studies on Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) have exploited chunk-level KV cache reuse to avoid processing long retrieved contexts for higher efficiency, while significant information redundancy and noise still remain in the coarse-grained chunks. This paper optimizes the Pareto frontier under low prefill latency constraints while maximizing accuracy by proposing CoinRAG (Contextualized Information Nugget KV Cache Reuse for Long-Context RAG). The name metaphorically reflects our core mechanism: much like assembling small tokens (or "coins") to accumulate a larger value, CoinRAG compositionally reuses offline-computed, fine-grained nugget caches to form a learned contextual representation efficiently in a more semantically relevant but compact manner. Specifically, instead of full-chunk encoding, CoinRAG identifies query-relevant semantic units within retrieved chunks through two-stage retrieval and seamlessly assembles their sliced KV representations with a chunk-level context. Extensive evaluations on LongBench multi-hop question answering tasks demonstrate that CoinRAG significantly reduces operational costs and outperforms the other baselines with a new Pareto frontier and an average 5.3% relative improvement in answer quality (F1) under a standard fast prefill latency budget.

</details>


### [51] [CreativeInstruct: Scalably Teaching LLMs to Balance Quality, Creativity, and Diversity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07460)
*Ananya Sahu,Mohit Bansal,Elias Stengel-Eskin*

Main category: cs.CL

TL;DR: CreativeInstruct is an instruction-tuning approach that reintroduces and controls creativity in post-trained LLMs using learned [StartCreativity] spans, achieving higher structural diversity and human-rated creativity without sacrificing quality, and improving downstream RL performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Post-training tends to reduce the diversity and creativity of LLM outputs, which harms explicitly creative tasks like story generation and implicitly creative settings like RL exploration. Existing approaches to regain diversity often rely on multiple models or sacrifice quality. The authors aim to design a scalable, controllable method that restores or enhances creativity while preserving the benefits of post-training.

Method: They develop CreativeInstruct, an instruction-tuning method where the model learns to use a special control token or span, [StartCreativity], that biases generation toward more creative, base-model-like behavior while retaining post-training quality. They also propose a new structural diversity metric based on graph edit distance over narrative structures, going beyond lexical/semantic diversity metrics. They compare CreativeInstruct with multi-model and distillation-based baselines on narrative generation and also use CreativeInstruct checkpoints as starting points for RL fine-tuning (GRPO) on benchmarks like AMC and MATH.

Result: On narrative generation, CreativeInstruct matches or surpasses the diversity of multi-model baselines and distilled versions of their outputs, while maintaining generation quality and requiring only a single model at inference. Human evaluations show that annotators judge CreativeInstruct outputs as more creative than those from standard post-trained LLMs in 70.3% of cases. In RL settings, using CreativeInstruct as the base model for GRPO training yields approximately 4% improvement on AMC and about 5 percentage point gains on MATH compared to applying the same RL training to a standard post-trained checkpoint.

Conclusion: CreativeInstruct provides a scalable, single-model approach to controllable creativity in LLMs, restoring and enhancing diversity without degrading quality. Its structural diversity metric better captures narrative-level variation, and creative checkpoints serve as stronger substrates for downstream RL, improving performance on reasoning-heavy benchmarks. This suggests that explicitly modeling and controlling creativity during instruction tuning benefits both generative and RL-based applications of LLMs.

Abstract: While post-training improves the capabilities of large language models (LLMs), it generally lowers their output diversity and creativity, negatively impacting tasks that explicitly require creativity (e.g., story generation) as well as those that require it implicitly, e.g., reinforcement learning (RL). We instead propose CreativeInstruct, a scalable instruction-tuning method that teaches LLMs to balance creative, base-model-like generations with the quality of post-trained models, by learning to inject special [StartCreativity] spans that bias generation toward creativity. Furthermore, we introduce a structural diversity metric based on graph edit distance, which captures narrative level variation missed by purely lexical and semantic metrics. On narrative generation, CreativeInstruct matches or exceeds the diversity of both multi-model baselines and distilled variants of their outputs, without sacrificing quality or requiring multiple models at inference time. These results are mirrored in our human evaluation, where we find that annotators rate CreativeInstruct generations as more creative than the post-trained LLMs' generations in 70.3% of cases. We also show the benefits of creative models as a substrate for RL: GRPO applied to a CreativeInstruct checkpoint improves by ~4% on AMC and ~5% points on MATH over the same training applied to the post-trained checkpoint.

</details>


<div id='cs.AI'></div>

# cs.AI [[Back]](#toc)

### [52] [Towards Multi-Label Graph Foundation Models: from Single-Vector Representation Learning to Multi-Semantic Basis Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06394)
*Dongxiao He,Jiayu Zhang,Jitao Zhao,Yi Wang,Di Jin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes MSB-GFM, a graph foundation model tailored for cross-domain multi-label node classification using multi-semantic basis representations and a dual-channel, domain-adversarial architecture.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing multi-label node classification methods work well only in-domain and lack cross-domain generalization. Meanwhile, current Graph Foundation Models assume each node has a single label and embed it into a single representation, which causes semantic entanglement for multi-label nodes and hinders accurate modeling of multiple co-existing semantics. There is a need for a foundation model that can both handle multi-label semantics and transfer across graph domains.

Method: The authors design MSB-GFM, which introduces a multi-semantic basis representation paradigm where each multi-label node is represented as an adaptive composition of several semantic bases, rather than a single embedding vector. They further propose a semantic-structure dual-channel architecture that separately but jointly models semantic information and structural information. Domain adversarial training is incorporated to encourage domain-invariant representations and enable effective transfer of knowledge across different graph domains.

Result: Through extensive experiments (details not in the abstract), the model is empirically shown to outperform baselines and effectively handle cross-domain multi-label node classification, validating both its multi-semantic basis design and its domain-transfer capability.

Conclusion: Modeling multi-label nodes as adaptive compositions of semantic bases within a dual-channel, domain-adversarial Graph Foundation Model leads to better semantic disentanglement and more effective cross-domain multi-label node classification than existing single-label-based GFMs and in-domain multi-label methods.

Abstract: Multi-label node classification is an important yet challenging task in graph learning, where nodes exhibit multiple semantics simultaneously. Existing methods for multi-label node classification can effectively model multiple labels, while only considering in-domain scenarios where the model needs to be trained and tested within the same graph domain, resulting in limited cross-domain generalization. Recently, Graph Foundation Models (GFMs) have emerged as a promising paradigm for learning transferable graph representations across diverse graph domains and downstream tasks. However, existing GFMs are built upon single-label assumption, where all nodes are arbitrarily regarded as containing only one class of semantic and embedded into a single representation. For multi-label nodes, such a representation essentially approximates multiple semantics with a single point in the representation space, inevitably leading to semantic entanglement and making simultaneous discrimination of multiple labels difficult. To address these limitations, we propose a Multi-Semantic Basis Graph Foundation Model (MSB-GFM), a framework for cross-domain multi-label node classification. Specifically, we introduce a multi-semantic basis representation learning paradigm that models each multi-label node as an adaptive composition of semantic bases, thereby enabling flexible representational capacity for modeling multiple semantics. Furthermore, we develop a semantic-structure dual-channel architecture with domain adversarial training for effective cross-domain knowledge transfer. Extensive experiments demonstrate the effectiveness of our model.

</details>


### [53] [EntropyMoE: Entropy-Aware Sparse Expert Routing for Tokenizer-Free LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06398)
*Bo Liu,Muxuab Yu,Yu Zhang,Pengfei Gao,Yongping Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: EntropyMoE is a Mixture-of-Experts architecture for byte-level LLMs with dynamic patches, routing experts using patch entropy and length to achieve better compression (lower bits-per-byte) while keeping downstream accuracy comparable.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Byte-level LLMs can avoid tokenizers by grouping bytes into dynamic patches, but current models still use uniform dense feed-forward networks for all patches, failing to adapt computation to differences in patch semantics and granularity. There is a need for architectures that allocate model capacity more efficiently across heterogeneous byte patches and enable sparse conditional computation in tokenizer-free settings.

Method: The authors propose EntropyMoE, which replaces dense feed-forward layers in the global patch Transformer with Top-K Mixture-of-Experts layers. Dynamic byte patches are the routing units: their entropy and length define a feature space used by a router to select which experts to activate for each patch. Byte coverage of each patch is used to account for computational workload, aligning expert routing with the same entropy-based signal that drives dynamic patch construction.

Result: Across experiments on byte-level language modeling, EntropyMoE attains lower held-out bits-per-byte than comparable dense and sparse baselines while preserving similar performance on downstream tasks. This demonstrates improved modeling efficiency without sacrificing task accuracy.

Conclusion: Patch entropy is an effective routing signal for sparse conditional computation in byte-level LLMs. By aligning expert routing with dynamic patch statistics, EntropyMoE efficiently specializes experts and outperforms dense and prior sparse baselines, extending Mixture-of-Experts modeling beyond traditional tokenizer-based representations.

Abstract: Recent byte-level large language models (LLMs) have made tokenizer-free modeling increasingly competitive by grouping bytes into dynamically sized patches. However, existing byte-patch architectures still apply the same dense feed-forward computation to every patch. This uniform computation cannot adapt model capacity to variations in patch semantics and granularity. We address this limitation with EntropyMoE, a Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) architecture designed for dynamic byte patches. EntropyMoE replaces the dense feed-forward modules in the global patch Transformer with Top-K expert layers. Each dynamic patch serves as the basic unit of expert routing, and its byte coverage determines its contribution to workload accounting. The router selects experts directly from patch entropy, using the same granularity signal that underlies dynamic patch construction to organize sparse computation. Patch entropy and length jointly define the feature space for regulating expert specialization. Experiments show that EntropyMoE achieves the lowest held-out bits-per-byte among matched dense and sparse baselines while maintaining comparable downstream accuracy. These results establish patch entropy as an effective routing coordinate for sparse conditional computation and extend Mixture-of-Experts modeling beyond tokenizer-based representations.

</details>


### [54] [Beyond Routing Weights: Faithful Response-Level Interpretation of Mixture-of-Experts Reward Models via Contribution Contrast](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06400)
*Yifan Wang,Jinyi Mu,Mayank Jobanputra,Yu Wang,Soyoung Oh,Isabel Valera,Vera Demberg*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Paper proposes CoCo, a response-level interpretation method for Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) reward models used in preference learning, improving how we understand expert behavior.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing MoE reward models use routing weights to interpret experts, but these only indicate which prompts each expert receives, not how experts judge responses, giving an incomplete view of expert behavior.

Method: Introduce CoCo (Contribution-Contrast), which interprets experts at the response level by focusing on chosen-rejected response pairs with the largest contribution contrasts, thereby capturing both routing and preference behavior. They compare it against router-based, score-based, and sparse autoencoder-based interpretations.

Result: CoCo provides more coherent, faithful, and specialized interpretations of experts in both automatic and human evaluations, while keeping reward modeling accuracy competitive with baselines.

Conclusion: CoCo is an effective and more faithful method for interpreting MoE reward models, constituting the first systematic study of such interpretation methods and highlighting the importance of considering both routing and preference behavior.

Abstract: Reward models are central to learning from human preferences, yet identifying what drives their predictions remains challenging. Recent sparse Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) reward models seek to improve interpretability by routing prompts to specialized experts and characterizing experts through examples with high routing weights. However, routing weights only reveal which prompts an expert $\textit{receives}$, not how it $\textit{judges}$ responses, providing only a partial account of expert behavior. We therefore propose $\textbf{Co}$ntribution-$\textbf{Co}$ntrast ($\textbf{CoCo}$) response-level interpretation, which faithfully characterizes experts' roles using chosen-rejected response pairs with the largest contribution contrasts, jointly capturing routing and preference behavior. Across automatic and human evaluations, CoCo yields more coherent, faithful, and specialized interpretations than router-based, score-based, and sparse autoencoder-based alternatives while maintaining competitive reward modeling accuracy. To the best of our knowledge, this is the first systematic study of interpretation methods for MoE reward models.

</details>


### [55] [Interpretable Unsupervised Community Detection with LLM-Symbolized Structured Processes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06402)
*Aoting Zeng,Kai Wang,Jianwei Wang,Yuxiang Sun,Yizhang He,Wenjie Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes LUCID, a training-free, LLM-guided, interpretable community detection framework that achieves state-of-the-art performance in an unsupervised setting.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing community detection methods either rely on rigid, hand-crafted objectives that struggle with complex graph structures or use deep learning models that require labels, training, and sacrifice interpretability. Large language models offer strong reasoning and world knowledge, suggesting they could guide community detection in a label-free and interpretable way, but a systematic method to harness them for this task is missing.

Method: LUCID is a four-stage, LLM-guided pipeline inspired by phase-transition kinetics: (1) Local-View Community Initialization encodes local graph structures via k-ego networks and unsupervised node roles to form initial communities; (2) Multi-factor Community Merge uses LLM-induced logical rules to iteratively merge local communities; (3) Multi-grain Community Refinement applies LLM-induced coarse-to-fine rules in parallel to clean boundaries and reduce noise; and (4) Global-view Community Selection evaluates and selects high-quality communities based on topological compactness and boundary clarity. Throughout, the LLM translates implicit structural and semantic knowledge into explicit, interpretable rule sets instead of being trained end-to-end.

Result: On multiple real-world graph datasets, LUCID—without supervision or training—achieves state-of-the-art performance for community detection, consistently outperforming both leading unsupervised methods and strong semi-supervised deep-learning baselines in standard evaluation metrics.

Conclusion: LLMs can effectively guide community detection in a fully unsupervised, training-free, and interpretable manner. By structuring LLM involvement as rule induction within a four-stage phase-transition-inspired pipeline, LUCID overcomes limitations of classic and deep-learning-based approaches and sets a new performance bar for unsupervised community detection while maintaining interpretability.

Abstract: Community detection is a fundamental task in graph analytics that aims to identify cohesive groups of entities with similar behaviors or interests. Classic objective-driven methods struggle with complex graph structures, while deep-learning approaches improve performance at the expense of interpretability and rely on labeled data and training. Large language models (LLMs), with strong reasoning capabilities and world knowledge, are promising for interpretable, label-free community detection. To leverage these strengths, we propose LUCID, an LLM-guided, interpretable, training-free, and unsupervised community detection method. Inspired by phase-transition kinetics in natural systems, where complex structures emerge through initialization, merging, refinement, and selection, LUCID is designed as a four-stage pipeline. Within this pipeline, the LLM induces formal rules that translate implicit knowledge into explicit and interpretable logical structures. Specifically, (1) the Local-View Community Initialization stage encodes local graph structures using k-ego contexts and unsupervised node roles; (2) the Multi-factor Community Merge stage uses LLM-induced rules to iteratively merge local communities; (3) the Multi-grain Community Refinement stage applies LLM-induced coarse-to-fine rules in parallel to reduce boundary noise; and (4) the Global-view Community Selection stage identifies high-quality communities based on topological compactness and boundary clarity. Extensive experiments on real-world datasets demonstrate that LUCID, as an unsupervised approach, achieves state-of-the-art performance and consistently outperforms leading unsupervised and semi-supervised baselines.

</details>


### [56] [ADIAS: Automated Design of Interactive Agentic Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06410)
*Lekang Jiang,Bohan Tang,Stephan Goetz,Yiwen Guo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Abstract of a paper on ADIAS, an issue-centric framework for automated agent design.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing automated agent design methods are candidate-centric, implicitly tracking repair progress via candidate agents. This leads to inefficient targeting of repairs, slow integration of partial improvements, and spread of ineffective interventions between rounds.

Method: The authors propose issue-centric agent optimization, where repair progress is represented as an explicit, persistent issue state that is carried across optimization rounds. They instantiate this in ADIAS, a framework for automated full-code agent design, which uses: (1) a persistent issue state with stable issue identities, lifecycle status, evidence, and intervention-outcome history; and (2) issue-guided optimization that uses this state to select repair targets and revision directions for focused full-code modifications.

Result: On five interactive benchmarks, ADIAS outperforms the strongest baseline by an average of 25.2% and shows consistent improvements across four different backbone models. Ablation studies indicate that removing the persistent issue state or reverting to candidate-centric revision causes performance drops of up to 40.7%.

Conclusion: Issue-centric agent optimization, as instantiated in ADIAS, leads to more efficient and effective automated agent design than traditional candidate-centric approaches. Explicitly maintaining a persistent issue state and guiding revisions based on it substantially improves performance across tasks and model backbones.

Abstract: Automated agent design improves agent harnesses through iterative revision, evaluation, and feedback summarization. Existing methods are largely candidate-centric: cross-round experience is organized around candidate agents, which leaves the repair progress implicit. This causes inefficient repair targeting, slow consolidation of partial progress, and propagation of ineffective interventions across rounds. Therefore, we formulate issue-centric agent optimization, in which repair progress is carried forward as an explicit persistent issue state to guide optimization, rather than re-derived from candidate history in each round. We instantiate the formulation in ADIAS, a framework for automated full-code agent design with two mechanisms. A persistent issue state maintains stable issue identities, lifecycle status, supporting evidence, and intervention-outcome histories. Issue-guided optimization uses this state to jointly propose repair targets and revision directions for subsequent focused full-code modification. Across five interactive benchmarks, ADIAS outperforms the strongest baseline by 25.2% on average and achieves consistent gains across four backbone models. Controlled ablations further show that removing persistent issue state or replacing issue-centric revision with candidate-centric policies leads to performance drops of up to 40.7%.

</details>


### [57] [Learning to Predict Middle-Layer Attention in MLLMs for Visual Token Prunin](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06411)
*Yuyao Sun,Tao Deng,Shuang Li,Deqing Wang,Hao Geng,Minjun Yu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They propose a method (MAP) to prune visual tokens more efficiently and adaptively in multimodal LLMs, keeping nearly all performance while using far fewer visual tokens and achieving significant speedup.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: MLLMs are slow and costly because they process many visual tokens. Existing pruning methods rely on attention from a fixed middle layer, which is suboptimal because the best layer varies per sample and computing that attention already incurs high cost. They want a way to estimate visual token importance early and adaptively per input, without needing full attention maps.

Method: They introduce Middle-layer Attention Prediction (MAP). In training, they use Question Contrastive Teacher Selection: for each sample, they compare text-to-vision attention under the real question vs. a reference question to find which middle layer’s attention is most sensitive to the question; that layer becomes the teacher. They then distill that layer’s attention into a lightweight predictor network that, given multimodal input features, predicts importance scores for visual tokens. At inference, before any language model layers, MAP predicts token importance and prunes visual tokens according to both importance and a diversity criterion, without computing attention maps and remaining compatible with other acceleration methods.

Result: On LLaVA-NeXT-7B across ten benchmarks, MAP keeps 97.5% of the performance of the unpruned model while using only 5.56% of visual tokens, resulting in a 3.09x end-to-end speedup.

Conclusion: Adaptive, learned prediction of middle-layer attention enables early, attention-free visual token pruning in MLLMs, avoiding the cost and rigidity of fixed-layer attention-based pruning while preserving most task performance and substantially accelerating inference.

Abstract: Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) achieve strong performance across diverse vision-language tasks, but their efficiency is limited by the cost of processing numerous visual tokens. Visual token pruning can reduce this cost, but requires accurate token importance estimates. Recent studies have demonstrated that text-to-vision attention from middle language model layers can effectively guide visual token pruning, typically using attention from a predefined middle layer to select the visual tokens to retain. Two problems therefore remain. First, our analysis shows that the layer whose attention is most responsive to the question varies substantially across samples, making a fixed layer suboptimal. Second, obtaining attention from the appropriate middle layer requires processing numerous visual tokens through several language model layers, by which point considerable computation has already been spent. To address both problems, we propose Middle-layer Attention Prediction (MAP), which uses Question Contrastive Teacher Selection to identify a sample-specific teacher layer by contrasting attention under the original and reference questions, and distills attention from the selected layer into a lightweight predictor that estimates visual token importance from multi-modal input features. During inference, MAP combines the predicted importance scores with a diversity criterion to prune visual tokens before the first language model layer. Thus, MAP requires no attention maps for pruning and remains compatible with existing inference acceleration techniques. Across ten benchmarks on LLaVA-NeXT-7B, MAP retains 97.5% of the unpruned model performance with only 5.56% of the visual tokens, yielding a 3.09x end-to-end speedup.

</details>


### [58] [WebGrader: Training LLMs for Web Development with Self-Evolving Programmatic Grader](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06474)
*Boshui Chen,Huiping Liu,Shaolei Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces WebGrader, an automatic programmatic grading system that generates executable interaction flows (Flow Contracts) for web-generation tasks and uses them as reliable reinforcement learning rewards, significantly improving functional success of LLM-generated websites.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Training language models to generate fully functional websites with reinforcement learning is limited by reward design. Manual browser scripts are accurate but costly and not scalable for open-ended tasks, whereas VLM or GUI-agent-based graders scale but may judge too early without seeing the crucial state changes. There is a need for a scalable, reliable, and late-binding reward mechanism that verifies whether the requested website behavior actually occurs in the live environment.

Method: WebGrader autonomously infers the necessary interaction flow for each website task and encodes it as an executable Flow Contract. It then instantiates the generated website in a real browser, grounds the target actions to the code and live DOM, and executes the Flow Contract while collecting multi-modal evidence (visuals, DOM, network responses, persistent state) along the trajectory. An offline, residual-driven loop mines reusable verifier skills from past tasks, validates them on held-out pages, and freezes a skill graph before RL training. WebGrader separates four stages—test planning, action grounding, evidence collection, and semantic judgment—and only issues a Pass once it observes the requested state transition in the browser.

Result: On WebGen-Bench, using WebGrader as the reward function, an 8B-parameter policy achieves a 52.01% functional success rate, improving by 7.88 percentage points over a baseline that combines appearance and script-based rewards, and outperforming strong general models such as o4-mini and DeepSeek-v4-flash. On the WG-core-250 benchmark, the trained policy attains a Full Score of 44.953 and outperforms a substantially larger competitor model, Qwen3-Coder-480B.

Conclusion: Programmatic, self-evolving graders like WebGrader can provide reliable, scalable, late-binding rewards for RL training of web-generation models. By explicitly modeling interaction flows as executable contracts and decoupling planning, grounding, evidence collection, and judgment, WebGrader yields more faithful functional evaluation and enables smaller policies to surpass much larger general-purpose models on web-generation benchmarks.

Abstract: Large language models increasingly generate complete websites from natural-language descriptions, and reinforcement learning has become a central approach to closing their remaining functional gap. This training regime is bottlenecked by reward design. Hand-authored browser scripts are executable yet costly to write for open-ended requirements, while VLM and GUI-agent graders scale but may issue verdicts before observing the decisive state. We propose WebGrader, a self-evolving programmatic grader that autonomously derives the required interaction flows from each website request, represents each flow as an executable Flow Contract, and uses its execution outcome as an RL reward. WebGrader materializes the generated project in a live browser, grounds target actions against the source code and live DOM, and collects visual, DOM, response, and persistent-state evidence along the same browser trajectory. A residual-driven offline loop then discovers reusable verifier skills, screens them on disjoint validation pages, and freezes the promoted skill graph before policy training. By separating test planning, action grounding, evidence collection, and semantic judgment, WebGrader issues a Pass verdict only after observing the requested transition. On WebGen-Bench, WebGrader trains an 8B policy to a 52.01% functional success rate, outperforming a matched appearance-plus-script reward by 7.88 points and surpassing o4-mini and DeepSeek-v4-flash. On WG-core-250, the policy reaches a Full Score of 44.953 and surpasses Qwen3-Coder-480B.

</details>


### [59] [Can MLLMs Decode the Creative Leap? Introducing C4 for Cross-Concept Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06501)
*Ming Wang,Yuqing Zhang,Tingna Xie,Xiangju Li,Xiaocui Yang,Daling Wang,Shi Feng,Yifei Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes C4, an evaluation framework for measuring cross-concept creativity of multimodal large language models (MLLMs) using Chinese idioms (Chengyu), and shows that current models struggle with this type of creative decoding.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing benchmarks mainly test accuracy and literal understanding, not creative, cross-concept reasoning. Creative capabilities of MLLMs are important for design, communication, education, and collaboration, but are hard to evaluate due to lack of explicit targets and rewards. The authors aim to build a structured, answerable benchmark that captures receptive creativity, specifically cross-concept understanding via Chinese idioms.
