Method: The authors define cross-concept encoding (item construction) and decoding (model inference). They build C4, a cognition-inspired framework centered on Chengyu-based Cross-Concept Creativity. They construct a manually annotated, third-party-reviewed cross-concept network with bridge paths connecting concepts. The encoding component maps target slots to imageable substitute concepts along these bridges, producing items of controllable difficulty (by bridge count/depth) and exact answers. They instantiate C4-Eval with 184 synthetic items plus 37 real cross-concept Chengyu figures from online sources, each represented in five task settings to yield 884 answer-recovery cases. They annotate relations, bridge paths, and reasoning processes, then evaluate ten MLLMs under conditions with/without candidate constraints, bridge hints, and explanation requests.

Result: In tests on ten MLLMs, the best closed-source models achieve about half accuracy (50.7% and 48.0%) on primary answer-recovery tasks, while open-source models perform markedly worse. Providing constrained candidate options significantly boosts accuracy, whereas giving bridge hints or asking for explanations offers only modest improvement. Overall performance indicates that current MLLMs have considerable difficulty decoding creatively encoded meaning through cross-concept relations.

Conclusion: C4 and the C4-Eval set offer a structured, cognitively motivated way to benchmark cross-concept creativity in MLLMs using Chengyu-based tasks, with explicit answers and controllable difficulty. Experimental results reveal a significant capability gap: even leading MLLMs struggle to recover intended meanings from creative cross-concept encodings. This highlights cross-concept decoding as an open challenge and provides a foundation and tools for future model and benchmark development in creative reasoning.

Abstract: Creative capabilities of MLLMs matter in design, communication, education, and human--AI collaboration, yet remain difficult to evaluate because explicit targets and reward signals are scarce compared with accuracy-oriented tasks. Cross-concept understanding is a core cognitive capacity underlying receptive creativity. It enables a perceiver to recover intended meaning from non-obvious but meaningful conceptual relations. We operationalize item construction as cross-concept encoding and model inference as cross-concept decoding. We introduce C4, a cognition-inspired evaluation framework for Chengyu (Chinese idiom)-based Cross-Concept Creativity. Its encoding component maps target slots to imageable substitute concepts along bridge paths in a manually annotated and third-party-reviewed cross-concept network, enabling batch generation with explicit structure, difficulty indexed by bridge count and depth, and exact answers. Using this framework, we instantiate the C4 Evaluation Set (C4-Eval), comprising 184 synthetic items and 37 human-created cross-concept chengyu figures collected from online sources. We manually construct and review cross-concept relations, bridge paths, and reasoning processes for the collected figures. Each C4-Eval item is instantiated in five task settings, yielding 884 primary answer-recovery cases. Across ten evaluated MLLMs, the strongest closed models reach 50.7% and 48.0% primary accuracy, while open-source models remain substantially lower. Candidate constraints improve accuracy sharply, but bridge hints and explanation requests provide only modest gains. These results expose a substantial gap in how current MLLMs decode creatively encoded meaning through cross-concept relations. The code is in the supplementary material.

</details>


### [60] [KNOWPLAN: Knowledge-Driven AI Agents for Smart Degree Pathway Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06530)
*Shuheng Cao,Weijia Zhang,Jiaqi Wu,Xiyun Hu,Yat Yang,Juqy Chen,Zhaoxiang Feng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: KnowPlan is a two-stage system that first fully extracts degree requirements from messy university sources, then optimizes personalized study plans on that extracted structure, with strong guarantees and better performance than baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing degree planners intertwine web crawling with planning, so the crawler only learns what the current partial plan needs. This coupling hides errors in both extraction and planning, and university information is scattered across heterogeneous, unschematized sources (catalogs, department pages, JSON APIs, PDFs). There is a need for a system that: (1) systematically and verifiably reconstructs the whole curriculum; and (2) optimizes student-specific degree paths on top of this reconstruction with formal guarantees.

Method: The paper introduces KnowPlan, which cleanly separates extraction and planning with a measured interface. The extraction stage, CatalogBrowse, explores university sources without any user profile, guided by expected marginal gain in covering a predefined set of atomic catalog obligations, normalized by source access cost. It uses deterministic parsers via platform-specific adapters and falls back to a span-limited clause-to-AST model. It stops when it can certify closure on index, schema, provenance, and reference completeness rather than a heuristic reward threshold. CatalogBrowse outputs three provenance-linked JSON documents. The planning stage, DegreeMap, only consumes these documents. It compiles them into a typed requirement hypergraph and uses CP-SAT to lexicographically optimize: (1) hard feasibility; (2) time to completion; (3) workload and risk; (4) personalized utility; and (5) option value. This nesting ensures each stage’s solution is optimal within the proven bounds of the previous stage while remaining certifiable within a given solver budget.

Result: On a broad track of 100 universities and a dense track of 6 schools, CatalogBrowse achieves 96.2% recall of curriculum inventory and 88.7% recovery of deliberately masked sources, while using 47% less source access than an exhaustive crawler. DegreeMap maintains 100% satisfaction of hard constraints (feasibility) and improves personalized utility by +0.066 over the strongest baseline planner. End-to-end, KnowPlan certifies 99.5% of user requests, with an average utility gap of only 0.015 relative to an oracle planner that has access to a privileged, fully accurate curriculum graph.

Conclusion: Separating curriculum extraction from degree planning, and enforcing a verifiable contract between them, yields a more reliable and efficient planning pipeline. KnowPlan’s CatalogBrowse can reconstruct university curricula with high recall and significantly reduced crawling cost, while DegreeMap can then produce feasible, high-utility plans atop this representation. The system delivers strong empirical performance and certification guarantees, suggesting that extraction-first architectures with formal interfaces and optimization are a robust approach to automated degree planning from real-world, messy university data.

Abstract: Planning a degree from official university sources requires solving two problems in order. The institution's curriculum must first be reconstructed from catalogs, departmental pages, JSON endpoints, and PDFs that share no schema, and only then can a student-specific path be optimized under prerequisite logic and overlapping requirement constraints. Coupling the two lets each failure mode hide the other, because a planner that drives its own crawling never learns facts its current plan does not need. We present KnowPlan, which enforces an extraction-first boundary and measures the interface between the stages rather than assuming it. CatalogBrowse explores with no access to any user profile. It scores legal actions by lower-confidence expected marginal gain over a finite set of atomic catalog obligations per unit of source access, parses deterministically through platform adapters with a span-constrained clause-to-AST model fallback, and terminates on a closure certificate over index, schema, provenance, and reference completeness instead of a reward threshold. Its output contract is three provenance-linked JSON documents. DegreeMap consumes only those documents. It compiles them into a typed requirement hypergraph and optimizes lexicographically with CP-SAT over hard feasibility, completion horizon, load and risk, personalized utility, and option value, so that each stage optimizes inside the previous stage's proven optimum and stays certifiable within the solver budget. Across a 100-university broad track and a six-school dense track, CatalogBrowse reaches 96.2% inventory recall and 88.7% masked-source recovery at 47% less source access than an exhaustive crawler, DegreeMap holds 100.0% hard feasibility while improving personalized utility by +0.066 over the strongest baseline, and the full pipeline certifies 99.5% of requests with a utility gap to the privileged gold graph of 0.015.

</details>


### [61] [TaskSense: Focusing on What Matters in World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06544)
*SM Mazharul Islam,Manfred Huber*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TaskSense is a task-centric world model for visual control that uses stochastic spatial attention and inverse-dynamics supervision so the model focuses on and reconstructs only task-relevant regions, improving robustness under visual distractions compared with DreamerV3.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing world models for visual control typically learn latent states via full-frame reconstruction, which forces representations to encode both task-relevant areas and irrelevant background clutter. This misalignment between reconstruction and control objectives causes capacity to be wasted on distractors, weakens the learning signal for control-relevant features, and severely hurts performance when visual distractions are present. There is a need for a world modeling approach that explicitly prioritizes task-relevant information in the learned latent state.

Method: The paper proposes TaskSense, a task-centric world modeling framework that introduces a differentiable stochastic spatial attention module, conditioned on the previous latent state, to select task-relevant regions before encoding. Training is augmented with an auxiliary inverse-dynamics loss that predicts the action from consecutive latents, which biases attention toward control-relevant areas. Instead of reconstructing the full observation, the world model reconstructs only the attended regions, and the decoder is conditioned on the sampled attention map to maintain consistent reconstruction under stochastic attention. This integration yields latent states focused on task-relevant content while discarding irrelevant visual information.

Result: On the DeepMind Control Suite, TaskSense attains performance comparable to the DreamerV3 baseline, indicating no loss in standard conditions. On the Distracting Control Suite, TaskSense consistently surpasses DreamerV3, showing notably better robustness to visual distractions. Qualitative evaluations show that the learned attention maps, guided by inverse-dynamics supervision, reliably highlight control-relevant regions and suppress distractors.

Conclusion: TaskSense successfully aligns the world model’s representation learning objective with control needs by enforcing task relevance prior to encoding. Its attention mechanism and inverse-dynamics supervision enable the model to ignore irrelevant visual content, preserve critical task information in the latent state, and improve robustness to visual distractions without sacrificing performance on standard benchmarks. This demonstrates the value of task-centric attention for world models in vision-based control.

Abstract: World models for visual control typically learn compact latent states by reconstructing observations, implicitly encouraging representations to preserve information across the entire visual input. However, task-relevant content often occupies only a small fraction of the observation, while background clutter and distractors consume valuable representational capacity. This mismatch between visual reconstruction and control objectives biases latent representations to model task-irrelevant visual content, diluting learning signals for control-relevant features and severely degrading downstream performance under visual distractions. We introduce TaskSense, a task-centric world modeling framework that enforces task relevance before latent encoding through a differentiable stochastic spatial attention mechanism conditioned on the previous latent state. To steer attention toward control-relevant regions, we augment training with an auxiliary inverse-dynamics objective. Rather than reconstructing the full observation, the world model reconstructs only the attended regions, encouraging latent representations to preserve task-relevant information while discarding irrelevant visual content. The decoder is further conditioned on the sampled attention map, enabling consistent reconstruction despite stochastic attention. Compared with the DreamerV3 baseline, TaskSense maintains competitive performance on the DeepMind Control Suite while consistently outperforming DreamerV3 on the Distracting Control Suite, demonstrating substantially improved robustness to visual distractions. Qualitative analysis further confirms that the learned attention, guided by inverse-dynamics supervision, consistently localizes control-relevant regions while suppressing irrelevant visual content.

</details>


### [62] [Divergent Response Modes in Frontier Language Models Under Steering Pressure](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06578)
*Ali Jalal-Kamali*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Study compares how different frontier language models respond to explicit behavioral steering and identifies systematic, model-specific response modes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To determine whether differences in training data, objectives, and safety pipelines across frontier models lead to measurably different behaviors when the models are explicitly steered (e.g., told to reveal, emphasize, or suppress reasoning or values).

Method: Evaluate six frontier models from six developers using 300 paired base vs. steered prompts in three categories: values-conflict, reasoning-elicitation, and reasoning-suppression, plus 40 validation items. Each model acts as a blind peer judge, classifying all responses according to fixed behavioral rubrics. Aggregate 24,480 such judgments and score them via leave-one-out consensus. For one open-weight model (Llama), analyze internal representations: train a linear probe on the residual stream to decode behavior and perform causal interventions by injecting the learned direction during generation.

Result: Models differ both in the magnitude and nature (mode) of their behavioral shifts under steering, with some response modes unique to individual models. GPT-5 uniquely deflects revealing its reasoning while preserving answers (99% occurrence vs. 0% for others). Claude Opus 4.7 and GPT-5 resist suppression instructions but via distinct strategies. For Llama, a linear probe predicts behavior from residual stream activations with 0.87 held-out accuracy, and steering along this direction causally increases the targeted behavior from 0% to 86% in a controlled sweep. All results are robust under a token-budget remediation and a control condition using a hypothesis-blind judging prompt.

Conclusion: Frontier models exhibit systematically different behavioral steerability profiles, shaped by their distinct training and safety setups. These differences manifest not only in how strongly they respond to steering but also in qualitatively different response modes, some unique to specific models. For at least one open-weight model, these behavioral modes are linearly decodable and causally manipulable in internal representations, suggesting that steerability has a relatively simple neural signature. The findings are robust to alternative judging prompts and token-budget controls, implying that the observed differences reflect genuine model properties rather than evaluation artifacts.

Abstract: Frontier language models are trained using distinct data, objectives, and safety pipelines. Whether these differences produce measurably different behaviors under explicit steering pressure remains underexplored. This study evaluates behavioral steerability across six frontier models from six developers using 300 paired base and steered items over three categories: values-conflict, reasoning-elicitation, and reasoning-suppression (plus 40 validation items). All six models act as blind peer judges and classify every response based on fixed behavioral rubrics. The resulting 24,480 judgments are scored by leave-one-out consensus. We find that models differ not just in how much steering shifts their behavior but in what kind (mode) of response they give, and some response modes appear in only one or two of them. GPT-5 deflects requests to disclose its reasoning while leaving its answer intact (99% vs. 0% for all other models). Claude Opus 4.7 and GPT-5 resist explicit suppression instructions and in different ways. Using Llama as the open-weight model, we trace the largest behavioral split to its internals. A linear probe decodes the behavior from the residual stream at 0.87 held-out accuracy while injecting that direction during generation drives the behavior from 0% to 86% across an intervention sweep. Every finding holds under both a token-budget remediation and a control experiment with a hypothesis-blind judgment prompt.

</details>


### [63] [Automated item evaluation: Predicting item acceptance and rejection using LLM-generated critiques](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06609)
*Hotaka Maeda,Yikai Lu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper builds and evaluates automated models that predict whether test items will be accepted or rejected, using only item text and AI-generated critiques, showing that such models can meaningfully reduce manual review but perform poorly on fairness-related flags.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Manual expert review and field testing of test items is labor-intensive and costly, especially at scale. Automated item evaluation (AIE) offers a way to pre-screen or prioritize items before human review, but prior approaches have often been limited in scope or performance. The authors want a near-comprehensive, practical AIE system that can predict real operational acceptance or rejection decisions based solely on information available at item creation time, thereby reducing the burden on human reviewers and streamlining large-scale assessment development.

Method: The authors assembled a large historical dataset of 52,759 English language arts and mathematics items from a standardized testing program, labeled as accepted or permanently rejected, with rejection reasons ranging from psychometric failure to content, bias/sensitivity, and non-content issues. They trained three main models: (1) a DeBERTaV3-large classifier fine-tuned directly on raw item text; (2) a second DeBERTa model fine-tuned on natural-language critiques of each item generated by the Qwen3 model; and (3) a fusion model that combines representations from both the raw-text and critique-based models to make final predictions. They evaluated performance using Accuracy, F1, AUC, Sensitivity, and Specificity overall and by subject, experimented with different decision thresholds (e.g., 0.5 vs 0.25), and examined performance by rejection reason and item difficulty, with particular attention to fairness-related categories.

Result: The fusion model that combines raw item text and AI-generated critiques outperformed the single-source models, achieving Accuracy = .75, F1 = .64, AUC = .80, Sensitivity = .64, and Specificity = .81 overall. Performance was substantially higher for math items (F1 = .73, AUC = .86) than for ELA items (F1 = .51, AUC = .72). Lowering the decision threshold from 0.5 to 0.25 increased sensitivity for ELA and math to .88 and .91, respectively, at the cost of reduced specificity (.31 and .56). Including critiques generally improved detection across most rejection reasons, and the model tended to assign higher rejection probabilities to more difficult items. However, the fusion model performed poorly on items rejected for bias, sensitivity, fairness, or accessibility, particularly for ELA.

Conclusion: Text-based AIE using modern language models is feasible and can provide a useful pre-screening tool to reduce manual review and field testing workloads in large-scale assessment programs, especially for math items and for non-fairness-related rejection reasons. Combining raw item text with AI-generated critiques yields better performance than using either alone, and adjusting decision thresholds can tune the trade-off between catching more problematic items and avoiding false positives, which is valuable when item generation is cheap. Nonetheless, the system fails to reliably detect fairness, bias, sensitivity, and accessibility issues, reinforcing that human reviewers remain essential for safeguarding fairness and equity in testing, and indicating a need for more specialized approaches in these areas.

Abstract: Automated item evaluation (AIE) refers to the use of computational methods to assess item quality without requiring manual expert review or field testing of the items under evaluation. We aimed to build a near-comprehensive AIE model by predicting item acceptance and rejection from item text using historical rejection data from a large-scale standardized testing program. The dataset contained 52,759 English language arts (ELA) and mathematics items with 34% permanently rejected from future operational use. Rejection reasons included poor psychometric properties, content issues, bias and sensitivity concerns, and non-content issues. We fine-tuned a DeBERTaV3-large classifier on raw item text, a second DeBERTa classifier on Qwen3-generated item critiques, and a fusion model combining representations from both. The fusion model achieved the strongest overall performance (Accuracy = .75, F1 = .64, AUC = .80, Sensitivity = .64, Specificity = .81). Prediction for math (F1 = .73, AUC = .86) was considerably more accurate than ELA (F1 = .51, AUC = .72). Lowering the decision threshold from .5 to .25 raised average sensitivity for ELA and math to .88 and .91, while reducing specificity to .31 and .56, respectively, which may be preferable in automated item generation contexts where generating items is cheaper than evaluating them. Incorporating item critiques alongside raw item text improved performance across most rejection reasons. The model assigned higher rejection probabilities to more difficult items. However, the fusion model struggled to identify items flagged for bias, sensitivity, fairness, or accessibility, especially for ELA. These findings suggest that text-based AIE is feasible in some areas and may offer a practical tool for reducing the burden of manual review and field testing, while also underscoring the importance of human review for items with fairness concerns.

</details>


### [64] [NxN E-valuation: Hypothesis Certification via a Conformal CRT Null](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06621)
*Bin Wang,Yan Zhong*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: NxN E-valuation is an e-value-based algorithm to statistically certify LLM-generated hypotheses without designing custom null hypotheses, using large datasets to implement a conditional randomization test.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs can generate many useful hypotheses but often hallucinate, making their outputs unreliable. Existing fixes—like asking LLMs to self-verify or using held-out testing—either are circular or allow false hypotheses to pass via spurious correlations. There is a need for a generic, statistically sound way to certify such hypotheses at scale without designing case-specific testing procedures.

Method: The authors propose NxN E-valuation, which uses an e-value framework and the idea that in large datasets, different samples can serve as null hypotheses for one another. By leveraging the naturally available large training set, they construct a conditional randomization test (CRT) that assigns an e-value to each LLM-generated hypothesis, effectively certifying or rejecting it. The procedure does not require manually crafting a specific null hypothesis for each problem instance, only that the hypothesis meaningfully applies to each individual sample.

Result: The method theoretically implements a CRT via sample-to-sample comparisons and yields e-values that can be used to certify or reject each hypothesis. It is argued to be a universally better replacement, under its assumptions, for LLM circular verification and held-out-data testing when the LLM’s hypotheses apply at the individual-sample level.

Conclusion: NxN E-valuation offers a generic, statistically principled way to certify LLM-generated hypotheses using large datasets, avoiding custom null-hypothesis construction. Under its applicability conditions, it can reliably reduce hallucination effects and outperform common alternatives like self-verification and held-out testing for hypothesis validation.

Abstract: We propose NxN E-valuation, a handy, e-value-based hypothesis-certification algorithm that lets a hypothesis be verified without building any case-specific certification procedure---such as constructing a dedicated null hypothesis---as long as a large enough dataset is available. The method is especially suited to LLM-based exploration systems, where LLMs are remarkably good at proposing hypotheses but suffer badly from hallucination; this hallucination prevents us from harvesting LLM outputs directly, and existing remedies each fall short. The most common solutions include letting the LLM verify or correct itself circular verification and held-out testing (where false hypotheses can still pass via spurious correlations), among other remedies detailed in the introduction. To resolve this, NxN E-valuation exploits the naturally existing large training set and lets different samples serve as null hypotheses for one another. This design directly realizes a conditional randomization test (CRT) that certifies each hypothesis. The approach can be a universally better replacement for at least LLM circular verification and held-out-data testing, provided the LLM's generations are hypotheses that apply to each individual sample.

</details>


### [65] [Shape Your Feed: An LLM-based Agentic System for Conversational Recommendation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06632)
*Ziyun Xu,Bosen Ding,Yue Zhang,Ji Qi,Qingyuan Song,Jizhou Huang,Liwei Wang,Jefferey Santelli,Yue Weng,Qichao Que,Zhenheng Yang,Junfeng Pan,Linhong Zhu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces Shape Your Feed (SYF), an LLM-based framework that lets users actively steer recommendation feeds in real time using natural language and multimodal inputs, improving alignment between user-stated interests and recommended content.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional recommendation systems rely heavily on implicit behavioral signals, causing a misalignment between what users explicitly want and what they are shown, and giving them limited tools to control or refine their feeds. There is a need for interactive, user-steerable recommendation that can understand nuanced, natural language preferences and adapt rapidly.

Method: The authors propose SYF, a three-tier LLM-based recommendation framework: (i) Perception Flow to capture detailed user intent from text, voice, and UI interactions; (ii) Serving Flow to perform real-time agentic re-ranking and pruning of candidate items using a persistent Semantic Profile of user preferences; and (iii) Self-Evolution Flow that optimizes system behavior with Direct Preference Optimization and an LLM-as-a-Judge ensemble for preference alignment.

Result: Offline experiments show that the alignment scoring module in SYF reaches 98.85% accuracy, outperforming strong few-shot baselines. Online A/B tests on real production traffic indicate improved feed relevance and better user sentiment compared to existing systems.

Conclusion: SYF provides a practical and scalable approach for interactive, multimodal, user-steerable recommendation in industrial environments, narrowing the gap between user-expressed preferences and algorithmic recommendations while continually self-improving through preference alignment techniques.

Abstract: Industrial recommendation systems predominantly adopt a passive ranking paradigm that infers user preferences from implicit behavioral signals (e.g., clicks, dwell time) rather than explicit, natural language inputs. As a result, users experience a persistent discrepancy between their explicit interests and what passive behavioral algorithms deliver, limiting their ability to express nuanced preferences or steer their feed in real time. To address this growing gap between how recommendations are optimized and how users wish to articulate their interests, we present Shape Your Feed (SYF), an LLM-based agentic recommendation framework that enables real-time, multimodal co-curation of content. SYF employs a three-tier architecture: (i) a Perception Flow that captures fine-grained user intent from text prompts, voice commands, and UI interactions; (ii) a Serving Flow that performs real-time agentic re-ranking and pruning of candidate items, grounded in a persistent Semantic Profile encoding evolving user preferences; and (iii) a Self-Evolution Flow that aligns system behavior with human judgments via Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) and an LLM-as-a-Judge ensemble. Offline evaluations show that SYF's alignment scoring module achieves 98.85% accuracy, substantially improving over strong few-shot baselines. Large-scale online A/B experiments on production traffic further demonstrate that SYF improves feed relevance and user sentiment, indicating a practical and scalable path toward interactive, user-steerable recommendation in industrial settings.

</details>


### [66] [TRACE: A Multi-Layer Benchmark for Human AI Controller Coordination Under Drift and Failure](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06657)
*Joshua Zuniga,Srinivasan Subramanian,Ramya Madhuri Narapureddy,Md Abdullah Al Hafiz Khan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Benchmark and analysis of drift propagation across multi-layer human-AI-control loops.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Trustworthiness of modern cyber-physical and AI-assisted systems depends on the entire human-AI-control loop, but there is no standard benchmark with time-aligned, multi-layer traces that reveals how drift and failures propagate, making diagnosis and recovery difficult.

Method: Create a benchmark by injecting controlled drift into execution traces derived from the ALFRED grounded-instruction dataset for household tasks. Produce 1,918 drifted traces, each as a time-aligned sequence across five execution layers (state, observation, decision, rules, control), with rich labels (drift type, affected layer, onset time, responsible actor, causal mechanism). Validate annotations with independent raters and report inter-annotator agreement. Provide a leak-aware evaluation protocol that removes a near-perfect onset leak and run baseline experiments with classical, recurrent, and attention-based models on drift identification and attribution.

Result: Across all tested model families under the leak-aware protocol, drift type and attribution (affected layer, responsible actor, causal mechanism) can be predicted substantially better than random or majority baselines: macro-F1 around 0.70 for affected layer, 0.85 for responsible actor, and 0.49 for causal mechanism. Attention-heavy models do not outperform simpler alternatives on this symbolic benchmark.

Conclusion: A new benchmark and evaluation protocol make it possible to systematically study drift in multi-layer human-AI-control loops. Drift can be detected and localized to layers and actors well above chance without sophisticated architectures, and heavy attention mechanisms do not yield clear benefits on this symbolic setting, suggesting that simpler models may suffice for certain types of drift monitoring and diagnosis.

Abstract: Modern cyber-physical and AI-assisted systems couple human operators, AI decision modules, and automated controllers in a single control loop, so trustworthiness depends on the whole loop, not any one model. Yet no standard benchmark captures time-aligned, multi-layer traces of how drift and failures propagate across these layers, so we cannot diagnose where coordination breaks down, why, or how to recover. This paper targets one facet of that gap: drift, a deviation that can originate in any stack layer and that conventional single-modality monitoring cannot localize to a layer or pin to an onset time. We construct a benchmark by injecting controlled drift into traces derived from ALFRED, a grounded-instruction benchmark for everyday household tasks, yielding 1,918 drifted traces. Each trace is a time-aligned sequence of per-step records across five execution layers (state, observation, decision, rules, control), labeled with the drift type, affected layer, onset time, responsible actor, and causal mechanism, and validated by independent raters with inter-annotator agreement reported. We pair the dataset with a leak-aware protocol that removes a near-perfect onset leak, and a baseline study across classical, recurrent, and attention-based model families. Under this honest protocol, drift is identifiable and attributable well above random and majority baselines across every family (affected layer macro-F1 near 0.70, responsible actor near 0.85, causal mechanism near 0.49), and heavy attention offers no advantage over simpler models on this symbolic benchmark.

</details>


### [67] [CellWorld: From Gene-Level Reconstruction to Latent Cell Prediction in Spatial Transcriptomics Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06659)
*Haiping Liu,Qian Zhao,Lijing Lin,Jingyuan Sun,Hongpeng Zhou*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They propose CellWorld, a latent-space predictive pretraining framework that builds scalable foundation models for spatial transcriptomics by predicting latent cell representations instead of raw gene measurements, achieving strong transfer and state-of-the-art performance on multiple benchmarks with relatively small models and limited pretraining data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing spatial transcriptomics foundation models mainly reconstruct masked gene identities or expression values. This can lead to overfitting assay-specific technical noise and reduces how well the learned representations transfer across datasets and assays. The authors want a pretraining objective that focuses on biologically meaningful structure rather than technical artifacts, while also being scalable to large cell corpora and effective for downstream spatial tasks.

Method: They introduce CellWorld, which changes the prediction target from observed gene expression to latent cell representations. The model predicts the latent representations of masked cells using the visible spatial neighborhood and a partial-expression hint. They build four model variants ranging from 5.74M to 94.56M parameters, and pretrain them on a large corpus of 46 million human cells. They conduct controlled scaling experiments varying model capacity, data coverage, and optimization to understand their impact on spatial and non-spatial performance and transfer.

Result: All four CellWorld variants show that increasing model capacity improves performance, especially on spatial tasks. Spatial transfer performance depends more on sufficient training optimization and diversity of biological sources in the pretraining data than simply on the total number of cells. On four held-out datasets, even the smallest model (5.74M parameters) outperforms all baselines on 11 linear-probe benchmarks and seven fine-tuned spatial benchmarks. A frozen large model pretrained on just 5% of the corpus—but with broad biological coverage—still surpasses all fully fine-tuned baselines on all seven spatial benchmarks.

Conclusion: Latent-space predictive pretraining is an effective and scalable strategy for building foundation models in spatial transcriptomics. Predicting latent cell representations rather than raw gene measurements yields representations that transfer well across datasets and tasks while being robust to technical variation. CellWorld achieves strong performance with relatively modest model sizes and limited pretraining data, emphasizing the importance of model capacity, optimization quality, and biological diversity over sheer data volume.

Abstract: This paper shows that latent-space predictive pretraining can provide a scalable route to foundation models for spatial transcriptomics. Existing spatial transcriptomics foundation models primarily reconstruct masked gene identities or expression values, potentially encouraging the reproduction of assay-specific technical variation and limiting representation transferability. To avoid directly reconstructing such variation, we shift the prediction target from observed gene measurements to latent cell representations and introduce CellWorld, which predicts the latent representations of masked cells from visible spatial context and a limited partial-expression hint. We pretrain four CellWorld variants, spanning 5.74M to 94.56M trainable parameters, on a corpus of 46 million human cells. Our controlled scaling experiments show that performance improves with model capacity, particularly on spatial tasks, while spatial transfer depends more on sufficient optimization and broad biological source diversity than on cell count alone. Across four held-out datasets, even CellWorld-Small, with 5.74M trainable parameters, outperforms every baseline on all 11 linear-probe benchmarks and all seven fine-tuned spatial benchmarks. Most notably, a frozen CellWorld-Large pretrained on only 5\% of the corpus with broad biological source coverage outperforms every fully fine-tuned baseline across all seven spatial benchmarks. Code is available at https://github.com/UoM-HealthAI/CellWorld.

</details>


### [68] [Vehicle routing problem using deep reinforcement learning - A case study about truck planning in the industry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06668)
*Siliang Lu,Dan Hu,Lili Wu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper applies deep reinforcement learning (DRL) to real-world vehicle routing in external truck logistics, achieving over 10% cost reduction versus baseline and arguing DRL can generalize to broader VRP variants.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Transportation is key to supply chains and has grown with digital platforms, but real-world Vehicle Routing Problems (VRPs) are complex due to many constraints, uncertainty, and human behaviors, making classical optimization hard to deploy effectively. There is a need for methods that can handle this complexity and adapt to practical industrial settings while improving cost and environmental performance.

Method: The authors consider three industrial logistics use cases related to external truck network design and implement deep reinforcement learning agents to optimize vehicle routing. They compare DRL-optimized routes against baseline solutions, effectively using DRL as a data‑driven optimization engine for VRP under real‑world constraints and uncertainties.

Result: Across the three case studies, the DRL-based routing solutions achieve more than 10% reduction in total costs compared with baseline routing strategies, demonstrating practical performance gains in industrial logistics applications.

Conclusion: Deep reinforcement learning can be successfully applied to complex, real-world VRPs in external truck logistics, yielding significant cost savings and potential carbon footprint reductions. The authors suggest that DRL approaches are promising for generalization to more varied and complex VRP formulations in future research and industrial practice.

Abstract: As an important component of the supply chain industry, transportation has experienced rapid development in the past decade with the assistance of digital platforms and intelligent algorithms. Within the field of transportation research, Vehicle Routing Problem (VRP) has remained a persistent and enduring challenge. In the realm of management science, experts, and scholars from both the industrial and academic sectors have continuously explored optimization models and algorithms to effectively address routing problems, from the classical Traveling Salesman Problem to the more general Vehicle Routing Problem. These models and algorithms are applied in real-world industrial scenarios to achieve cost optimization and reduce carbon footprints. However, due to the complexity of real-world problems, numerous specific constraints are often added, and challenges such as information opacity, uncertainty, and irrational human behavior may arise. Therefore, deploying and optimizing mathematical models for VRP in practical scenarios while maintaining optimal results poses numerous challenges. This paper discusses and provides solutions for three different logistic use cases involving external truck network design. Through these industrial case study, the paper introduces how deep reinforcement learning-based vehicle routing optimization has been implemented. As a result, it can be observed that the routes optimized by reinforcement learning agent have over 10% total cost compared to baseline results. Furthermore, the paper proposes that in future research, DRL algorithms for vehicle routing problems could be generalized into more variations of VRP.

</details>


### [69] [A Multi-Agent Framework for Automated Coarse-Grained Molecular Dynamics of Polymers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06694)
*Joohee Choi,Junhyeong Lee,Seunghwa Ryu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces CGMas, an automated multi-agent LLM-based framework that builds and validates coarse-grained polymer models from natural-language descriptions, greatly reducing human effort and simulation time while preserving key physical properties.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Bottom-up coarse-grained molecular dynamics can simulate polymers at larger scales than all-atom methods, but every new polymer and coarse-graining resolution requires manual construction of topologies, mappings, and potentials. This is time-consuming, requires expert knowledge, and lacks transferability across systems. The authors aim to remove this human bottleneck and make polymer coarse-graining more accessible, systematic, and scalable.

Method: They design CGMas, a multi-agent system built around a large language model. One reasoning agent takes a natural-language polymer name and desired CG resolution and infers an appropriate all-atom topology. Additional agents perform layered self-correction of chemically or physically inconsistent structures, then run atomistic equilibration, map the equilibrated system to a CG representation, derive CG interaction potentials using Boltzmann inversion, and finally validate the CG model by benchmarking key properties (e.g., density) against the atomistic reference. The full pipeline is automated end-to-end for a range of homopolymers and copolymers.

Result: CGMas successfully handled all 27 tested homopolymer and copolymer systems without manual intervention. For 22 of them, the coarse-grained simulations reproduced the all-atom densities within 5%, indicating good physical fidelity. The framework also reduced wall-clock time for coarse-graining from 38–88 minutes in a manual or semi-manual workflow to about 1 minute, demonstrating a major gain in efficiency while preserving accuracy.

Conclusion: The work demonstrates that an agentic LLM-based framework can reliably automate the full bottom-up coarse-graining workflow for polymers, from natural-language input to validated CG potentials. CGMas achieves substantial speedups and good agreement with all-atom properties, suggesting that similar multi-agent LLM systems could generalize to broader classes of soft-matter and materials modeling tasks and significantly lower the expertise and time required for CG model development.

Abstract: Coarse-grained (CG) molecular dynamics extends polymer simulation beyond the scales accessible to all-atom (AA) methods, but bottom-up CG modeling is laborious. The CG resolution is a design choice, so a transferable parameter set is generally not available and the potentials are derived anew for each polymer mapping. Here we present CGMas, a multi-agent framework that automates topology construction, equilibration, mapping, potential derivation, and validation from a natural-language specification of the polymer and target resolution. A large-language-model (LLM) reasoning agent infers the AA topology from polymer name, while layered self-correction resolves physical errors common to unsaturated, heteroatom-containing, and polar polymers. Downstream agents equilibrate the system, map it onto CG representation, derive potentials through Boltzmann inversion, and benchmark the model against its atomistic reference. CGMas completed all 27 homopolymer and copolymer tasks, matched the AA density to within 5% in 22, and reduced simulation from 38-88 min to 1 min, establishing agentic LLMs as a route to automated polymer coarse-graining.

</details>


### [70] [AgentPatch: Coarse-to-Fine Weak-Task Repair for Merging Agentic Multimodal Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06699)
*Zibo Shao,Baochen Xiong,Chengdong Xu,Linhui Xiao,Kaichen Li,Haoran Gong,Yan Li,Yaguang Song,Xiaoshan Yang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They propose AgentPatch, a training-free framework to repair and improve merged agentic multimodal LLMs so a single model can retain multiple tools’ capabilities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing agentic multimodal LLMs that can plan, use tools, and interact with environments tend to be specialized for particular tools or environments. When merging such specialized models into one generalist, important capabilities are often degraded or lost, especially those involving complex interactions or decisive actions in long-horizon tasks. There is a need for a way to consolidate multiple agentic MLLMs into a single model without retraining and without sacrificing specialized skills.

Method: They formalize the problem of Agentic MLLM Merging and analyze two key failure modes: (1) asymmetric capability preservation, where tasks with different interaction complexity survive merging unevenly, and (2) behavior-critical forgetting, where loss of key action patterns breaks long-horizon performance. They then propose AgentPatch, a training-free, coarse-to-fine repair framework that operates after merging. AgentPatch: (a) chooses a stable merged backbone model, (b) performs Weak-Task Unique Residual Recovery to re-inject task-specific signals that were diluted in merging, and (c) runs an Agent-Guided Behavior-Critical Patch procedure that uses agent trajectories to identify and restore critical action behaviors while explicitly protecting desired capabilities. The result is a single static model checkpoint, without online routing, ensembles, or further training.

Result: On six different agentic and multimodal benchmarks, AgentPatch consistently improves the performance of a variety of merged backbones. It mitigates the degradation seen on weaker or more fragile tasks after naive merging, while better balancing recovery of those weak tasks with the preservation of complementary capabilities like search skills and agentic visual processing. Quantitative results show that the repaired merged models achieve more uniform and higher performance across tasks compared to standard merging baselines.

Conclusion: AgentPatch demonstrates that it is possible to repair and enhance merged agentic multimodal LLMs in a training-free way, turning multiple specialized models into a single static generalist without ensembles or routing. By addressing asymmetric capability preservation and behavior-critical forgetting via residual recovery and behavior-critical patching, AgentPatch yields merged models that maintain strong performance across diverse agentic and multimodal benchmarks. This suggests a practical path toward consolidating specialized agentic MLLMs into robust generalist agents.

Abstract: Agentic multimodal large language models (MLLMs) extend multimodal perception and reasoning with planning, tool use, and interaction in dynamic environments. Yet current models are specialized for particular tools or environments, complicating consolidation into a single generalist. We formulate Agentic MLLM Merging and identify two challenges: asymmetric capability preservation, whereby capabilities with different interaction complexity are retained unevenly, producing weak tasks after merging, and behavior-critical forgetting, whereby losing decisive actions can derail long-horizon execution. We propose AgentPatch, a training-free coarse-to-fine repair framework. It selects a stable merged backbone, restores diluted weak-task-specific signals through Weak-Task Unique Residual Recovery, and applies an Agent-Guided Behavior-Critical Patch that recovers decisive behaviors under explicit capability protection. AgentPatch produces a single static checkpoint without routing or ensembles. Experiments across six agentic and multimodal benchmarks show that AgentPatch improves diverse merged backbones, alleviates weak-task degradation, and better balances weak-task recovery with the preservation of complementary search and agentic visual processing capabilities. Code is available at https://github.com/ziboshao/AgentPatch.

</details>


### [71] [WebRider: Persona-Conditioned Intent Controllers for Live-Web Assistance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06704)
*Zhi Li,Tao Zhou,Yeqing Li,Eugene Ie,Demetri Terzopoulos*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces WebRider, a framework and benchmark for web agents that explicitly encode and enforce user delegation policies ("intent contracts"), showing that existing agents often produce correct-looking answers while violating user-specified constraints.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current web agents are evaluated almost entirely on final-answer accuracy, assuming that if they finish a task and the answer looks plausible, the agent has followed the user’s instructions. However, delegating a web task actually involves a detailed policy about verification, uncertainty handling, preferences, and stopping criteria. The authors’ audit shows that strong existing controllers nearly always complete tasks but follow the intended policy less than half of the time, exposing a large gap between task completion and faithful delegation. They aim to create a system and evaluation method that make policy fidelity, auditability, and persona consistency first-class concerns, instead of byproducts.

Method: The authors formalize user delegation as an "intent contract" that specifies goals, constraints, evidence requirements, answer format, and task-local persona controls. They design WebRider as a hierarchical architecture: (1) a top-layer controller that maintains and updates the intent contract; (2) a middle layer that turns high-level intentions into guarded executable actions with explicit checks; and (3) a tool layer that carries out these actions via browser, search, and maps tools on the live web. They introduce RiderBench, a benchmark of 4,096 live-web contracts across 42 public websites, and audit both internal contract state and external behavior to determine policy preservation and persona consistency. They also train an 8B action-policy model using the guarded middle-layer interface as supervision and compare it against executable-only baselines under the same controller.

Result: Their audit of a strong baseline controller shows 99.2% task completion but only 38.8% full compliance with policy constraints, demonstrating that high success rates mask significant policy violations. WebRider’s contract-based, hierarchical design yields a system whose browsing path and internal decisions can be audited and judged for policy fidelity and persona consistency. The guarded middle interface produces high-quality training data: an 8B action-policy model trained on it outperforms models trained only from executable traces when evaluated under a fixed controller. They release RiderBench and the associated dataset (hf.co/datasets/WebRider/WebRider).

Conclusion: The paper concludes that evaluating web agents only on final answers is insufficient for faithful delegation, because it ignores whether the agent followed the user’s policy. By turning the browsing path and intent contract into first-class, operational objects, WebRider enables agents that are auditable, human-evaluable, and trainable with respect to policy fidelity and persona consistency. The work suggests that future web-agent benchmarks and architectures should emphasize explicit policy representation and enforcement, not just task completion and answer quality.

Abstract: Delegating a web task involves more than asking a question; it requires transferring a policy: what to verify, how to handle uncertainty, which preferences matter, and when to stop. Yet, current live-web agents are evaluated solely on the final answer, ignoring the policy constraints that define the delegation. A plausible final answer can conceal violations of that policy. Our full live audit reveals this critical gap: a strong controller completes 99.2% of tasks but honors all policy constraints in only 38.8% of cases. Finishing does not imply fidelity. WebRider bridges this gap by formalizing the delegated policy as an intent contract---an operational record of goals, constraints, evidence obligations, answer form, and task-local persona controls that must hold even as web pages change. WebRider employs a hierarchical architecture: a top-layer controller maintains the contract, a middle layer realizes intentions as guarded executable actions, and a tool layer executes these actions via browser, search, and maps tools. Our benchmark, RiderBench, evaluates this design on 4,096 live-web contracts across 42 public websites, auditing both the internal contract state and the visible user experience to determine if a rollout preserved its policy and if the steps were persona-consistent. The guarded middle interface also serves as a high-quality training signal; an 8B action-policy model trained through this interface outperforms executable-only baselines under a fixed controller. By making the browsing path a first-class object, WebRider enables a system that is auditable, human-judgeable, and learnable without conflating action realization with final-answer decisions. Dataset URL: hf.co/datasets/WebRider/WebRider.

</details>


### [72] [MolBioKG: Grounding Out-of-Graph Molecules in Biomedical Knowledge Graphs via Multi-Resolution Structural Anchoring](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06713)
*Yiming Zhang,Hikaru Shindo,Shuan Chen,Kaushalya Madhawa,Jun Jin Choong,Yuna Oikawa,Takashi Fujiwara,Keisuke Ozawa*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They propose MolBioKG, a system that lets you use new, unseen molecules with biomedical knowledge graphs by anchoring them via structural similarity and enabling multi-hop reasoning without extra training.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing biomedical knowledge-graph methods assume that all query molecules are already nodes in the graph, which fails for novel or unregistered molecules common in early-stage drug discovery. This “out-of-graph molecule” cold-start problem prevents KG reasoning for many candidate compounds.

Method: They build MolBioKG, a two-layer framework. The first layer builds an index of 2.74M molecules decomposed into scaffolds, fragments, functional groups, and fingerprints, and links these structural units to a large biomedical KG with 9.6M edges. Given a SMILES input, MolBioKG finds structurally similar in-graph molecules via multi-resolution structural anchoring and ranks them with Reciprocal Rank Fusion (static multi-anchor retrieval). The second layer, Adapt-KG, is a tool-using LLM policy that adaptively traverses the KG neighborhoods of the retrieved anchors to answer queries or perform reasoning, all without task-specific training.

Result: On tasks including link recovery within the graph, complex multi-hop reasoning, and generalization to out-of-graph molecules, MolBioKG significantly outperforms strong baselines. It improves Hits@10 in multi-hop reasoning from 0.585 to 0.876 and boosts recall for out-of-graph target prediction from 0.145 to 0.269.

Conclusion: Linking unseen molecules to a biomedical KG via multi-resolution structural anchors, then using static retrieval and LLM-guided KG traversal, effectively solves the out-of-graph molecule problem. MolBioKG both improves performance on reasoning and generalization tasks and maintains interpretability through explicit structural anchors and source-attributed KG evidence.

Abstract: Biomedical knowledge graphs (KGs) accelerate drug discovery, but standard pipelines assume query molecules already exist as graph entities, leaving unregistered molecules disconnected. We address this cold-start challenge, termed the out-of-graph molecule problem, by introducing MolBioKG. This two-layer system grounds unseen molecules in biomedical evidence via multi-resolution structural anchoring. It connects an index of 2.74 million molecules (represented by scaffolds, fragments, functional groups, and fingerprints) to a 9.6-million-edge KG. Given only a SMILES string, MolBioKG retrieves structurally related graph entities and traverses their biomedical neighborhoods without task-specific training. It features two inference mechanisms: static multi-anchor retrieval using Reciprocal Rank Fusion, and Adapt-KG, a tool-using LLM policy for adaptive traversal. Evaluated across in-graph link recovery, complex multi-hop reasoning, and out-of-graph generalization, MolBioKG outperforms strong baselines. Notably, it raises Hits@10 from 0.585 to 0.876 in multi-hop reasoning and out-of-graph target recall from 0.145 to 0.269, all while ensuring predictions retain traceable structural anchors and source-attributed KG evidence.

</details>


### [73] [The Optimizer Is the Agent: Reasoning-Driven Search across Prompts, Programs, and ML Workflows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06714)
*Junbo Li,Boyi Liu,Canwen Xu,Yite Wang,Yuxiong He,Zhangyang Wang,Qiang Liu,Zhewei Yao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces ReASearch, a unified tool-using agent that internalizes search and optimization behavior for prompts, programs, and ML workflows, outperforming specialized controllers on diverse tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing systems for optimizing prompts, programs, and ML workflows rely on explicit outer-loop controllers (evolutionary search, bandits, textual gradients) with hand-designed heuristics. These approaches require task-specific engineering and separate controllers for different domains. The authors are motivated to see whether a single, reasoning-driven agent can internalize the search and control policy itself, reducing reliance on bespoke optimization frameworks and enabling general-purpose optimization across domains.

Method: The authors propose ReASearch, a unified framework where a single tool-using agent performs reasoning-driven optimization. The agent runs in a shared loop across domains, using domain-specific tools but the same high-level scaffold. It autonomously decides what candidate solutions to evaluate, how to interpret and diagnose observed failures, which edits to make to prompts/programs/workflows, and when to verify, restart, or change strategy. The agent maintains persistent memory to track past attempts and outcomes, allowing it to adapt its search strategy over long horizons instead of following fixed, external controllers like bandits or evolutionary algorithms.

Result: On 14 diverse optimization tasks involving prompts, programs, and ML workflows, ReASearch is competitive with or better than specialized optimization systems. It achieves improvements of 2% to 40% over strong, domain-specific baselines and, in some cases, finds solutions that surpass previous human best-known results. The experiments also reveal that sophisticated search behaviors usually implemented via explicit controllers emerge organically from the agent's internal reasoning process.

Conclusion: The study concludes that a single, reasoning-driven tool-using agent can effectively internalize complex search and optimization policies that were previously handled by explicit outer-loop controllers. ReASearch provides a unified scaffold that generalizes across domains and tasks, often outperforming specialized systems. The emergence of complex search strategies from the agent’s reasoning suggests a promising direction for building more general, agent-centric optimization frameworks that reduce the need for hand-crafted search algorithms.

Abstract: Recent systems for optimizing prompts, programs, and ML workflows typically rely on explicit outer-loop controllers such as evolutionary search, bandits, or textual-gradient methods. We ask a fundamentally different question: how much of this search policy can be internalized by a single tool-using agent? We present ReASearch, a unified framework for reasoning-driven optimization in which the agent autonomously decides what to evaluate, how to diagnose failures, which edits to make, and when to verify or restart. Rather than serving only as a proposal generator guided by hand-designed heuristics, the agent actively analyzes outcomes, allocates budget, and refines its strategy over long horizons through persistent memory. With a shared agent loop and domain-specific tools, ReASearch instantiates the exact same scaffold to optimize prompts, programs, and ML workflows. Across 14 diverse tasks, it is competitive with and mostly better than specialized optimization systems, achieving gains of 2% to 40% over strong domain-specific baselines, and in some cases discovering solutions that improve on prior human best-known results. Crucially, we observe that complex search behaviors, which are typically implemented by explicit controllers, emerge naturally from the agent's reasoning process.

</details>


### [74] [bioMoR: Biology-Guided Mixture-of-Recursions for Effective Genomic Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06727)
*Koushik Howlader,Tirtho Roy,Md Tauhidul Islam,Wei Le*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: This paper introduces bioMoR, a biologically informed Mixture-of-Recursions framework that selectively allocates deep computation to important genes or pathways using graph-structured prior knowledge, improving performance and efficiency in omics tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: High-dimensional omics data involve thousands of genes or pathways, but standard Transformers and existing Mixture-of-Recursions approaches treat all tokens similarly and ignore rich biological structure. This leads to unnecessary computation on uninformative tokens and suboptimal use of known gene–gene and pathway relationships, limiting both efficiency and interpretability in gene- and pathway-level modeling.

Method: The authors build bioMoR, an MoR-based architecture tailored to omics. They inject structured biological knowledge at three points in the model: (1) a graph-based information sharing module refines token (gene/pathway) embeddings using neighborhood information in a biological graph; (2) a structural bias term in self-attention encourages attention to biologically related tokens rather than purely data-driven correlations; and (3) a graph-aware router that uses the biological neighborhood of each token to decide its recursion depth, i.e., how many times it is processed by MoR, thereby routing more computation to important or central tokens. The backbone remains an MoR-style recursive Transformer but is augmented with these graph-aware mechanisms.

Result: On eight benchmark datasets covering varied omics types and evaluated under a standardized five-fold cross-validation setup, bioMoR outperforms the strongest biology-agnostic MoR baseline by 8.2 percentage points in average macro-F1 and 7.1 percentage points in balanced accuracy. Compared to a non-recursive Transformer, bioMoR achieves similar or better performance while using about 75% fewer parameters and up to 58% fewer FLOPs. The model’s routing decisions highlight marker genes or pathways and assign token-specific recursion depths that align with biological relevance.

Conclusion: Incorporating structured biological knowledge into Mixture-of-Recursions substantially improves both predictive performance and computational efficiency for omics modeling. bioMoR demonstrates that graph-informed embedding refinement, attention biasing, and routing can focus deep computation on biologically meaningful genes and pathways, yielding more interpretable models that scale better to high-dimensional omics data than standard Transformers or biology-agnostic MoR approaches.

Abstract: Transformer models for high-dimensional omics analysis process thousands of genes or pathways, although only a subset requires deep computation. Mixture-of-Recursions (MoR) improves efficiency through adaptive token-choice or expert-choice routing. We propose bioMoR, which, to the best of our knowledge, is the first framework to apply MoR to gene-level and pathway-level learning. Our contributions include identifying three locations for integrating structured biological knowledge within an MoR backbone: graph-based information sharing refines token embeddings, a structural bias guides self-attention toward biologically related tokens, and a graph-aware router uses neighborhood information to determine each token's recursion depth. These techniques are centered on our insight that additional knowledge of token interaction can effectively help models construct embeddings and select which tokens should be learned more deeply. Across eight benchmarks spanning diverse omics data types and evaluated under a unified five-fold cross-validation protocol, bioMoR improves average macro-F1 by 8.2 percentage points and balanced accuracy by 7.1 percentage points over the strongest biology-agnostic MoR baseline while using 75 percent fewer parameters and up to 58 percent fewer FLOPs than a non-recursive Transformer. The selected marker genes or pathways provide biological interpretability, while their token-specific recursion depths reveal how computation is allocated.

</details>


### [75] [From Cheap Fakes to Pure Synthesis: Addressing the New Era of T2V Fake News Videos](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06732)
*Yifeng Luo,Yupeng Li,Liang Lan,Tian Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They propose a ternary fake news video detection task and dataset for text-to-video generated news, and a reasoning-guided model that combines semantic logic and generative traces to detect real, cheap fake, and purely synthesized fake news videos, achieving state-of-the-art performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: New text-to-video generation models can create entirely fake news videos that closely match fabricated narratives, making it harder for existing detectors that rely on inconsistencies or cheap fakes. Current datasets do not contain such purely synthesized fake news videos, and naively generating them via direct prompting leads to unimodal shortcuts and semantic-visual degeneration, undermining robust detection. There is a need for a more realistic task setup and dataset that reflect these emerging threats and prevent models from exploiting shortcuts.

Method: 1) Reformulate fake news video detection as a ternary classification problem with labels: real, cheap fake (edited from real footage), and pure synthesis fake (generated by T2V). 2) Build PS-FNVD, the first dataset of pure synthesis fake news videos, including: Type 1 - fabricated events whose visuals semantically align with deceptive narratives; Type 2 - real events but with visuals that provide false provenance. This design reduces unimodal shortcuts. 3) Propose the R-T2V framework, which is trained via conditioned rationale generation and supervised fine-tuning, so that it jointly leverages high-level semantic reasoning about news content and low-level physical traces from generative models to output the ternary veracity label.

Result: On extensive experiments over 10 strong baselines, the proposed R-T2V framework achieves state-of-the-art results on the ternary fake news video detection task, surpassing the second-best method by 12.20 percentage points in overall accuracy and by 8.46 percentage points in macro F1 score.

Conclusion: The work demonstrates that treating T2V fake news video detection as a ternary problem and constructing a carefully designed pure synthesis fake news dataset significantly improves robustness to emerging threats from generative models. By integrating reasoning-based semantic analysis with low-level generative artifacts, the R-T2V framework effectively distinguishes real, cheap fake, and fully synthesized fake news videos and substantially outperforms existing detectors.

Abstract: Recent text-to-video (T2V) generation models enable fake news videos to be synthesized from scratch, shifting the threat beyond cheap fakes assembled from existing footage. Such news videos can closely match fabricated narratives, creating a modality alignment trap for existing detectors. Existing datasets lack pure synthesis fake news videos. Although directly prompting T2V models with descriptions of fake news videos can yield perfectly aligned samples, it reduces the fake news video detection (FNVD) to unimodal shortcuts and causes semantic-visual degeneration. To counter this, we formulate T2V-FNVD as a novel ternary classification task with three labels (real, cheap fake, and pure synthesis fake) and construct the first pure synthesis fake news video dataset (PS-FNVD). PS-FNVD includes fabricated events with aligned deception (Type 1) and true events with false visual provenance (Type 2), preventing models from exploiting unimodal shortcuts. Furthermore, we propose the Reasoning-guided T2V-FNVD (R-T2V) framework. Trained through conditioned rationale generation and supervised fine-tuning, R-T2V integrates high-level semantic logic with low-level physical generative traces to predict the ternary veracity label. Extensive experiments across 10 prevailing baselines show that R-T2V achieves the state-of-the-art performance, outperforming the second-best baseline by 12.20 percentage points in accuracy and 8.46 percentage points in macro $F_1$.

</details>


### [76] [IB-RL: Isolated Bilateral Reinforcement Learning for Strategic Dialogue Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06735)
*Senhao Wang,Chenghao Cai,Haitao Hu,Mingxing Huang,Xingguang Wang,Wenhao Li,Zecheng Lin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper identifies and addresses a key failure mode in applying reinforcement learning to strategic dialogue between language models, proposing a new training approach that coevolves both dialogue roles to improve generalization to unseen counterparts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While RL has improved LLM performance on tasks with fixed, verifiable rewards, it underperforms in strategic dialogues where the environment is another adaptive agent. Existing RL methods typically train against a single fixed counterpart, leading agents to overfit to that specific partner instead of learning robust strategies that work with diverse, unseen agents. The authors aim to diagnose and solve this overfitting issue, termed static-counterpart mismatch, to make RL more effective for interactive, multi-agent language tasks.

Method: The authors formalize and measure the static-counterpart mismatch, showing how training against a fixed counterpart or simulator can cause counterpart-specific exploitation. They then introduce Isolated Bilateral Reinforcement Learning (IB-RL), where both dialogue roles are trained simultaneously via joint rollouts but each optimizes its own objective under strict isolation. This includes separate advantage estimation, distinct action masks, and independent parameter updates for each role, ensuring that each policy learns from its own reward signal while coevolving with the other. Policies are then frozen and evaluated against fully independent, held-out counterparts to test generalization.

Result: In experiments on two strategic dialogue benchmarks—Vehicle TeleSales and Deal-or-NoDeal—IB-RL outperforms unilateral RL baselines that train only one side against a static counterpart. On Vehicle TeleSales, IB-RL attains 89.6% Success@1 versus 84.6% for the best unilateral RL approach. On Deal-or-NoDeal, IB-RL achieves 98.4% agreement when negotiating with DeepSeek V4 Pro, compared to 86.4% for the strongest unilateral baseline. The authors also directly quantify the static-counterpart mismatch, showing that unilateral training leads to poor performance when facing new counterparts, whereas IB-RL maintains strong performance.

Conclusion: Strategic dialogue with LLMs requires treating the environment as an adaptive agent rather than a fixed simulator. Training only one side of the interaction against a static counterpart causes overfitting and weak generalization, a problem the authors label static-counterpart mismatch. Their proposed IB-RL framework, which co-trains both roles with strict per-agent isolation, yields policies that generalize substantially better to unseen partners in negotiation and telesales settings. This suggests that coevolutionary, bilateral RL with isolated objectives is a promising direction for multi-agent, strategy-intensive language tasks.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning (RL) has achieved strong results in improving large language models (LLMs) on tasks with stationary, verifiable rewards, such as mathematical reasoning and code execution. In these settings, the environment follows fixed rules and does not adapt strategically to the agent. Strategic dialogue differs in this respect: the environment is another agent that adapts to the policy, and success depends on the interaction between the two sides. Despite this interactive nature, current RL approaches typically train a target agent against a fixed counterpart or simulator. We find that this training paradigm encourages the policy to exploit counterpart-specific regularities rather than learn strategies that generalize across counterparts. We call this problem the static-counterpart mismatch, which we quantify directly in our experiments. To address it, we propose Isolated Bilateral Reinforcement Learning (IB-RL), in which the two roles coevolve through joint rollouts while each role optimizes its own reward through fully independent advantages, action masks, and update paths. We evaluate frozen policies against fully independent held-out counterparts in both domains. On Vehicle TeleSales, IB-RL achieves 89.6% Success@1, compared to 84.6% for the best unilateral RL baseline. On Deal-or-NoDeal, it reaches 98.4% agreement against DeepSeek V4 Pro, compared to 86.4% for the best unilateral baseline. These results indicate that jointly training both roles with strict peragent isolation produces policies that generalize more effectively to unseen counterparts.

</details>


### [77] [MemPrism: Task-Conditioned Relational Memory Views for Long-Horizon Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06745)
*Zhisheng Chen,Bingfan Zeng,Bangde Cao,Zhengwei Xie,Yuxuan Li,Jinhan Li,Zheng Lu,Xiangchen Guan,Zikai Xiao,Rui Qian,Jingwei Song*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MemPrism is a task-conditioned relational memory system for long-horizon agents that dynamically builds temporary, structured working-memory views from a persistent event store, improving performance and reducing memory usage, and transferring across different VLM backbones.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing long-horizon agents store experiences in fixed representations, assuming that past evidence can be directly reused. In practice, the representation used when evidence was stored often mismatches what is needed at decision time, so relevant information is present but not organized or highlighted for the current task. The authors want a memory system that can flexibly reorganize past events into task-relevant structures, separating long-term storage from decision-time working memory to better support complex, long-horizon tasks.

Method: The authors introduce MemPrism, a task-conditioned relational memory framework. MemPrism logs agent-environment interactions as an event stream in a persistent store. At decision time, given the current task context, a learned lightweight view policy chooses how to construct a temporary relational working memory: it selects relation structure (how events relate), evidence range (time span), outcome condition (which outcomes matter), and granularity (level of detail). A deterministic composer and renderer then transform historical events into an "optical" working-memory view that conditions a frozen task policy. The task policy itself is not retrained; the flexibility comes from the learned view policy that configures how memory is queried and structured.

Result: On long-horizon embodied and web-agent benchmarks, MemPrism leads to consistent task performance improvements over baselines that use more static memory mechanisms. The gains are especially pronounced on longer trajectories, where memory organization is more critical. Additionally, MemPrism reduces the number of memory tokens consumed, indicating more efficient use of context. The learned view policy generalizes across different vision-language models (VLMs) without extra finetuning, showing that MemPrism provides a reusable, model-agnostic memory interface.

Conclusion: MemPrism demonstrates that separating persistent event storage from task-conditioned, relational working-memory views enables long-horizon agents to better exploit their past experiences. Dynamically constructed, structured memory views both improve performance and reduce memory costs, while remaining compatible with different underlying VLM backbones. Task-conditioned relational views appear to be an effective general memory interface for a broad class of agents operating over long horizons.

Abstract: Long-horizon agents rely on memory to reuse experiences, yet existing memory systems often assume that evidence can be directly consumed through a fixed representation. This leads to representation mismatch, where relevant information is available but not organized for the current decision. To this end, we propose MemPrism, a task-conditioned relational memory framework that separates persistent experience storage from decision-time working memory. MemPrism records interactions as the event stream and dynamically constructs relational views according to the current task context. A lightweight view policy selects the relation structure, evidence range, outcome condition, and granularity, while a deterministic composer and render transform historical facts into a temporary optical working-memory view for a frozen task policy.
