Experiments on long-horizon embodied and web-agent benchmarks show that MemPrism consistently improves the task performance, especially as trajectories become longer, while reducing memory token consumption. Furthermore, the learned view policy transfers across different VLMs without additional adaptation, demonstrating the effectiveness of task-conditioned relational views as a general memory interface for agents.

</details>


### [78] [Mind the Gap: A Dual Knowledge Graph Framework for Unified Multi-task User Intent Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06752)
*Tzu-Cheng Peng,Chien Chin Chen,Chih-Hao Ku,Yung-Chun Chang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DKG-MTI is a dual knowledge graph and LLM-based framework that jointly predicts aspect ratings and generates user intent statements from travel reviews by aligning user-specific and global hotel knowledge graphs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work on intent inference from reviews either uses hierarchical pipelines that propagate errors across stages or retrieval-based methods that overlook the structural relationships and dependencies in domain knowledge, limiting accuracy, scalability, and explainability.

Method: The paper builds a dual knowledge graph framework with two components: (1) a User-Specific Intent Knowledge Graph dynamically constructed from each review, and (2) a Global Hotel Knowledge Graph capturing domain-level hotel information. These two graphs are aligned via structure-aware semantic smoothing, producing enriched, aligned knowledge. This augmented representation, combined with the original review text, is then fed into a large language model in an inference-only manner (no fine-tuning) to perform unified multi-task learning: predicting aspect-level ratings and generating reverse user intent descriptions in one pass.

Result: On TripAdvisor review datasets, DKG-MTI consistently outperforms strong large language model baselines and retrieval-based approaches on both aspect rating classification and reverse intent generation metrics, showing improved effectiveness stemming from the structured knowledge alignment.

Conclusion: Incorporating structure-aware alignment between user-specific and global domain knowledge graphs effectively augments LLMs for unified, multi-task user intent inference from reviews, yielding better performance and more explainable predictions, and suggesting a scalable paradigm for knowledge-augmented intent modeling in online travel platforms.

Abstract: This paper proposes DKG-MTI, a dual knowledge graph framework for unified multi-task user intent inference from online travel reviews. Existing approaches often rely on hierarchical pipelines that suffer from error propagation or retrieval methods that ignore structural relationships in domain knowledge. To address these limitations, we introduce an inference-only knowledge augmentation framework that dynamically constructs a User-Specific Intent Knowledge Graph from each review and aligns it with a Global Hotel Knowledge Graph through structure-aware semantic smoothing. The aligned knowledge is combined with the original review and processed by a large language model to simultaneously predict aspect ratings and generate reverse user intent statements. Experiments on TripAdvisor reviews show that DKG-MTI consistently outperforms strong LLM and retrieval-based baselines in both classification and intent generation tasks, demonstrating the effectiveness of structure-aware knowledge alignment for scalable and explainable intent inference.

</details>


### [79] [Capek 0.5: An Execution-Centric Vision-Language Model for Embodied Intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06756)
*Ying Chen,Weizhen Li,Zhe Hu,Zhenjiang Li,Rui Jiang,Zhifeng Gu,Lihuang Fang,Jiangping Liu,Lei Yi,Jie Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces Capek 0.5, an embodied vision-language model organized around an execution-centric capability taxonomy, and shows it can unify multiple specialized capabilities while improving benchmarks and enabling closed-loop robotic task execution.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing vision-language models for robots execute tasks iteratively but current training is fragmented: different capabilities (like spatial reasoning, temporal understanding, or state verification) are trained in isolation with task-specific objectives. This leaves open how to systematically organize, train, and integrate all the capabilities a robot needs across the full execution loop. The authors aim to design a principled capability taxonomy and a training framework that yields a single model capable of robust, verifiable embodied task execution.

Method: They propose an execution-centric capability taxonomy with four families: Spatial Reasoning, Temporal Understanding, Action Guidance, and State Verification. For each capability, they train a specialist module via reinforcement learning using verifiable rewards, all built on a shared backbone model. After the specialists are trained, they merge their weights into a single model (“weight-space merging”) and then perform routed policy-space distillation to consolidate and retain the specialized skills in one inference-time model. They instantiate this framework at 2B and 35B-A3B model sizes and design a new benchmark, Capek-StateBench, for state verification. They then evaluate via benchmark suites, a targeted study of how well the unified model retains specialist capabilities, and closed-loop embodied simulations.

Result: Capek 0.5, at both 2B and 35B-A3B scales, outperforms its backbone initialization on most relevant benchmark metrics, including across various embodied reasoning tasks. The unified checkpoint preserves all four capability families—Spatial Reasoning, Temporal Understanding, Action Guidance, and State Verification—with measured but limited performance loss relative to the individual specialists. It also demonstrates effective transfer to closed-loop embodied task execution in simulation, showing that the consolidated model can be used as a single reasoning core for robotic control.

Conclusion: Organizing embodied vision-language training around an execution-centric capability taxonomy and using specialist training followed by weight merging and policy-space distillation yields a single model that maintains diverse, high-quality embodied capabilities. Capek 0.5 shows that this structured approach improves benchmark performance, preserves specialized skills in a unified checkpoint, and supports closed-loop execution, suggesting a promising path for scalable, general-purpose reasoning cores in embodied agents.

Abstract: Vision-language models are increasingly serving as the reasoning core of embodied agents. Robot execution is inherently iterative: each action reshapes the scene and physical state, continually renewing what must be perceived, reasoned about, and verified. Meeting these demands requires complementary capabilities that differ in supervision signals, prediction formats, and verification criteria. Existing approaches typically develop these capabilities against isolated, task-specific objectives, leaving open how they should be organized and integrated around execution as a whole. We present Capek 0.5, an embodied vision-language model built around an execution-centric capability taxonomy. Rather than organizing training by datasets or tasks, the taxonomy groups embodied capabilities according to their functional roles throughout execution and comprises four capability families: Spatial Reasoning, Temporal Understanding, Action Guidance, and State Verification. Each capability is first acquired by a dedicated specialist through reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards from a shared backbone, and the specialists are then consolidated into a single inference-time model through weight-space merging followed by routed policy-space distillation. We instantiate Capek 0.5 at the 2B and 35B-A3B scales and evaluate it from three complementary perspectives: comprehensive benchmark suites including Capek-StateBench, a new benchmark for state verification; a controlled study of capability retention from specialists to the unified model; and closed-loop evaluation in simulated embodied environments. Capek 0.5 improves the large majority of matched benchmark rows over its initialization, retains all four specialized capabilities in one checkpoint with quantified losses, and transfers to closed-loop embodied task execution.

</details>


### [80] [LiFTER: A Grounded Neuro-Symbolic Microscope for Continuous-Time Dynamic Graph Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06765)
*Minwoo Yu,Young-guk Ha*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces LiFTER, a neuro-symbolic model for continuous-time dynamic graphs that predicts future links via explicit, inspectable temporal rules instead of opaque neural states.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing continuous-time dynamic graph (CTDG) models compress interaction histories into latent states, which makes it hard to see what entities, temporal patterns, and events actually support a prediction, limiting interpretability, verifiability, and the ability to intervene on predictions.

Method: LiFTER represents each observed interaction as a grounded temporal fact and learns executable temporal rules over these facts. For any query about a future link, it computes a score as a signed sum of rule executions that match historical facts with specific entity bindings and temporal order. This design makes the computation symbolic and decomposable: predictions can be independently recomputed, traced to specific rules and facts, and analyzed for contributions of recurrence, history position, and transitions.

Result: On four CTDG benchmarks, LiFTER attains competitive forecasting performance (historical-negative setting) while achieving the best macro explanation accuracy and deletion fidelity among compared methods. The authors further show that all logits for 19,664 test predictions can be reconstructed via independent rule execution with a maximum numerical error of 0.0000131, demonstrating exact decomposability of the model’s computations.

Conclusion: LiFTER reframes future-link prediction on dynamic graphs as a verifiable, grounded computation based on explicit temporal rules and facts, offering competitive predictive accuracy together with high-fidelity, inspectable explanations and fine-grained analysis of temporal contributions in dynamic relational data.

Abstract: Continuous-time dynamic graph models predict future links by compressing past interactions into neural states. Although effective for forecasting, this computation obscures which entities are shared across events and how temporal patterns contribute to a prediction. We treat this gap as a property of the predictive architecture rather than a problem to be addressed after prediction. Link-Fact Temporal Rule Inducer (LiFTER) is a neuro-symbolic predictor that preserves observed interactions as grounded temporal facts and applies executable tempo- ral rules to pre-query facts. Each score is a signed sum of rule exe- cutions whose historical facts, entity bindings, and temporal order are explicitly satisfied. The evidence and rules responsible for a prediction can therefore be inspected, independently recomputed, and intervened upon. Across four CTDG benchmarks, LiFTER achieves competitive historical-negative forecasting and the highest macro explanation ac- curacy and deletion fidelity. The same architecture also serves as a microscope that separates the contributions of recurrence, history po- sition, and transition across datasets and traces them to individual facts. Independent execution reconstructs all logits for 19,664 test predictions with a maximum error of 0.0000131. LiFTER turns future-link forecasting into a verifiable grounded computation.

</details>


### [81] [Surg-UniWorld: A Unified Surgical World Model with Multimodal Control Experts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06770)
*Rulin Zhou,Wanhao Liu,Guoheng Ma,Liangjin Shao,Qiujie Song,Yidu Wang,Guankun Wang,Tong Chen,Long Bai,Luping Zhou,Hongliang Ren*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces Surg-UniWorld, a unified, controllable surgical world model for realistic surgical video generation with multimodal controls.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing surgical video generation and world models cannot effectively and jointly use multiple heterogeneous control signals (e.g., edge, depth, optical flow) without causing anatomical distortion, instrument appearance drift, or temporal inconsistency. There is a need for a unified multimodal control paradigm that preserves anatomy, instrument identity, and realistic instrument–tissue interactions over time.

Method: The authors propose Surg-UniWorld, which builds a Hierarchical Surgical Anchor from the first-frame appearance and semantic masks to preserve scene identity and anatomical layout. Then, Anchor-Relative Modality Experts interpret and encode different control modalities (edges, depth, optical flow) relative to this shared anchor, capturing complementary structural, geometric, and motion cues. A Multimodal Control Expert composes these modality-specific increments in a contribution-preserving, stage-wise manner and translates them into control hints for a Wan2.2 video diffusion backbone. They also create a new dataset/benchmark, Cholec80-SurgWAM, tailored for multimodal controllable surgical video generation.

Result: Experiments on the new Cholec80-SurgWAM benchmark and other baselines show that Surg-UniWorld surpasses existing controllable video generation and surgical world-model methods in generation quality, temporal coherence, and the ability to follow multiple control signals simultaneously.

Conclusion: Surg-UniWorld provides a unified, multimodal, controllable surgical world model that better preserves anatomical structure and instrument behavior while improving realism and temporal consistency, establishing a new benchmark and framework for generative surgical AI and simulation.

Abstract: Controllable surgical world models can provide a generative foundation for surgical artificial intelligence and simulation by synthesizing realistic instrument--tissue interactions. However, existing methods lack a unified multimodal control paradigm, while direct fusion of heterogeneous visual conditions often causes anatomical distortion, instrument appearance drift, and temporally inconsistent interactions. In this work, we propose {Surg-UniWorld}, a unified surgical world model with multimodal control experts. Surg-UniWorld first constructs a {Hierarchical Surgical Anchor} from first-frame appearance and hierarchical semantic masks to preserve persistent scene identity, anatomical organization, and interaction boundaries. {Anchor-Relative Modality Experts} then interpret edge, depth, and optical-flow evidence relative to the shared anchor, capturing complementary boundary, geometric, and motion information. A {Multimodal Control Expert} further performs contribution-preserving stage-wise composition of the activated modality increments and generates control hints for the Wan2.2 video diffusion backbone. To support multimodal surgical world modeling, we further construct Cholec80-SurgWAM, a benchmark for controllable surgical video generation. Extensive experiments demonstrate that Surg-UniWorld consistently outperforms existing controllable video generation methods and surgical world-model baselines in generation quality, temporal consistency, and multimodal controllability.

</details>


### [82] [Evolving Parallel Algorithm Portfolios via Potential-Aware Instance Generation with LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06808)
*Shaofeng Zhang,Shengcai Liu,Zhiyuan Wang,Ke Tang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes PIAC, a potential-aware instance–algorithm co-evolution framework that improves LLM-based algorithm portfolios for combinatorial optimization by avoiding reliance on reference solutions and increasing instance diversity, achieving substantial gains on TSP and CVRP.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM-based automatic construction of algorithm portfolios (LLM-ACP) generalizes poorly in realistic few-shot settings for complex combinatorial optimization. Instance–algorithm co-evolution helps but currently depends on hard-to-obtain reference solutions to assess instance hardness and uses a single, limited way of generating new instances, which restricts diversity and generalization. The paper aims to remove the dependence on reference solutions and enrich the instance generation process so portfolios can generalize better across distributions and problems.

Method: They introduce PIAC, a framework that co-evolves instances and algorithm portfolios without needing reference solutions. The key idea is a new metric, potential gain, which estimates how much the portfolio could improve on a given instance by perturbing the current algorithms and measuring the room for improvement. Instances with high potential gain are used as new training data. In parallel, they use LLMs to generate diverse instance mutators—different ways of modifying or creating problem instances—to explore a wider region of the instance space. The framework is instantiated on three algorithmic backbones (Greedy Constructive, Ant Colony Optimization, Guided Local Search) and applied to TSP and CVRP over multiple data distributions.

Result: Across TSP and CVRP on six data distributions, PIAC consistently outperforms state-of-the-art LLM-ACP baselines. For example, on TSP with Greedy Constructive portfolios, it yields a 19.76% relative performance improvement. The gains hold across different algorithmic backbones and problem distributions, indicating improved robustness and generalization.

Conclusion: PIAC shows that potential-aware co-evolution of instances and algorithms can significantly improve LLM-constructed portfolios for combinatorial optimization, even in few-shot settings. By removing the need for reference solutions and enhancing instance diversity through LLM-generated mutators, the framework achieves better generalization across problems and distributions, suggesting a promising direction for scalable, data-efficient portfolio construction.

Abstract: The Automatic Construction of Portfolios via Large Language Models (LLM-ACP) suffers from poor generalization in practical few-shot scenarios when solving complex combinatorial optimization problems. Instance and algorithm co-evolution frameworks address this by expanding the training dataset with generated hard instances on which the current algorithm portfolio underperforms, thereby enhancing generalization. However, this paradigm faces two critical limitations: evaluating instance hardness relies on high-quality reference solutions, and single-mode generation patterns limit instance diversity. To overcome these limitations, we introduce the Potential-aware Instance and Algorithm Co-evolution (PIAC) framework. Our core contribution is twofold. First, we propose potential gain, a novel metric that eliminates the need for reference solutions. This metric estimates generalization gain by perturbing the generated algorithms and assessing their improvement potential on generated problem instances. Second, PIAC leverages LLMs to synthesize diverse instance mutators, exploring a broader region of the problem-instance space and thereby enhancing the portfolio's generalization capabilities. Given that perturbation spaces vary across different algorithms, we instantiate our framework on Greedy Constructive, Ant Colony Optimization, and Guided Local Search algorithmic backbones. Comprehensive evaluations on the Traveling Salesman Problem (TSP) and Capacitated Vehicle Routing Problem (CVRP) across six distinct data distributions demonstrate that PIAC consistently outperforms state-of-the-art LLM-ACP baselines, notably achieving a 19.76% relative improvement for TSP Greedy Constructive portfolios.

</details>


### [83] [Gated-BEPO: Confidence-Gated Bellman Credit Assignment for Large Language Model Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06861)
*Hongxi Yan,Ziyue Huang,Shichao Fan,Qingjie Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Gated-BEPO introduces a better way to assign credit to individual actions for long-horizon LLM agents by using empirical rollout graphs and gated fusion of step- and episode-level feedback, improving performance on several benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-horizon environments with sparse, terminal rewards make it hard to know which actions were responsible for success or failure. Existing critic-free methods either smear trajectory reward uniformly across all steps or only compare actions taken in matching states, which struggles to distinguish helpful actions in failed episodes and harmful actions in successful ones. There is a need for a more nuanced, data-driven way to assign step-level credit that respects the structure of trajectories and the current policy’s behavior.

Method: The paper proposes Gated-BEPO. For each rollout group, it builds an empirical rollout graph from collected trajectories. It then performs a mean-backup Bellman fixed-point computation on this graph to estimate state(-action) node values under the empirical action distribution of the current policy. From these values, it computes temporal-difference residuals along sampled trajectories and aggregates them using generalized advantage estimation (GAE) to form step-level Bellman advantages that reflect both short- and long-term impacts of actions. Finally, it fuses the trajectory-level (episode) credit with these step-level advantages via a learned confidence gate, which only applies Bellman-based credit at states with multiple observed successor states; otherwise, it defaults to episode-level credit.

Result: On WebShop, ALFWorld, and visual Sokoban, Gated-BEPO consistently improves performance of both language and vision-language models compared to prior critic-free credit assignment methods and other baselines. Ablation studies show that using Bellman fixed-point value estimation on empirical rollout graphs is important, and that selectively gating the use of step-level credit outperforms always applying step-level credit uniformly.

Conclusion: Gated-BEPO offers a principled, critic-free way to derive step-level credit from empirical rollout graphs and to integrate it with episode-level credit via a confidence gate. This enables more accurate credit assignment for long-horizon LLM agents, leading to better policy learning than uniform reward spreading or naive step-grouping methods, and highlights the importance of selective rather than uniform incorporation of step-level Bellman credit.

Abstract: Training large language model agents in long-horizon environments requires assigning credit from sparse terminal outcomes to individual actions. Existing critic-free methods propagate trajectory-level rewards uniformly across steps, while recent approaches construct step-level groups by matching repeated states and compare actions within each group. The former cannot distinguish useful actions in failed trajectories from ineffective actions in successful ones. The latter rely on step credit derived directly from individual trajectory outcomes and fixed-weight fusion with episode-level credit. We propose Gated-BEPO, which derives step-level credit from empirical rollout graphs. For each rollout group, Gated-BEPO constructs an empirical graph and estimates node values through a mean-backup Bellman fixed point that reflects the empirical action distribution of the current policy. We then accumulate these temporal-difference residuals along each sampled trajectory using generalized advantage estimation, yielding step-level Bellman advantages that capture both immediate and downstream effects. To adaptively fuse episode- and step-level credit, a confidence gate incorporates Bellman credit only at states with multiple observed successors and otherwise uses episode-level credit. Experiments on WebShop, ALFWorld, and visual Sokoban show consistent improvements across language and vision-language models, while diagnostic ablations support the effectiveness of Bellman fixed-point value estimation and show that step-level credit should be incorporated selectively rather than uniformly into the final advantage.

</details>


### [84] [CEDAR: Agent-Orchestrated Tree Search for Goal-Directed Optimization of Complex Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06871)
*Yingtao Tian*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper presents CEDAR, an autonomous LLM+MCTS framework that automatically designs complex dynamical systems in code to meet user-specified behavioral goals, reducing human effort and enabling interpretable emergent behavior discovery.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Designing complex, nonlinear feedback systems to achieve desired emergent behavior is hard and labor-intensive. Traditional modeling tools (DYNAMO, STELLA, etc.) require expert-written structures and manual iteration, making it difficult to explore design spaces, achieve goal-directed behaviors, and support timely decision-making. There is a need for an automated, goal-driven way to search over complex system structures while keeping them interpretable and runnable.

Method: The authors introduce CEDAR, which represents complex systems as a constrained subset of executable Python augmented with domain primitives. They frame the search for systems that meet behavioral goals as a Monte Carlo Tree Search (MCTS) problem, but with key components parameterized by Large Language Models. An LLM Judge evaluates simulation outputs against user-specified goals, serving as the value/fitness function. An LLM Editor proposes code modifications to the system, serving as a variation operator. These are combined in an LLM-driven MCTS loop to iteratively refine system structures towards desired emergent behaviors, while maintaining solution diversity and interpretability via code-level explanations of structural changes.

Result: CEDAR can automatically discover complex system models whose emergent behaviors match user-specified goals, across multiple domains. It reduces the manual modeling burden, maintains diversity of discovered solutions, and provides LLM-based explanations linking structural code changes to behavioral changes. Empirically, it demonstrates capabilities that are difficult to obtain with traditional complex-systems modeling workflows, though concrete benchmarks are not detailed in the abstract.

Conclusion: CEDAR shows that coupling LLM agents with MCTS over executable model code is an effective, interpretable way to perform goal-directed discovery of complex systems. This approach decreases human effort, supports richer exploration of feedback-driven dynamics, and could broaden practical adoption of complex-systems modeling in areas like biology, economics, and strategic decision-making.

Abstract: Complex systems, core objects of study in artificial life, model diverse phenomena through nonlinear, feedback-driven interactions that produce emergent behavior, with applications from population dynamics and biology to economic policy and strategic decision-making. Yet the difficulty of predicting how feedback structure gives rise to emergent behavior, a central open problem in artificial life, makes goal-directed design exceptionally challenging. In established practice, system structures are written in specialized modeling languages such as DYNAMO or STELLA, compounding the challenge with labor-intensive workflows that limit adoption and hinder timely decision-making. To address these challenges, we introduce CEDAR, an autonomous method that uses Large Language Model (LLM) agents to discover complex systems satisfying user-specified behavioral goals. Our key innovation is an LLM-driven Monte Carlo Tree Search (MCTS) deeply coupled with complex systems: at each iteration, an LLM Judge evaluates emergent behavior against specified goals and an LLM Editor proposes improved variants, with the Judge acting as a fitness function and the Editor as a variation operator, akin to a generate-and-evaluate loop in evolutionary computation. We represent complex systems as a restricted, runnable subset of Python with domain-specific primitives, letting LLMs modify system dynamics directly. CEDAR formalizes this as an MCTS variant with an LLM-parameterized transition kernel and value function, enabling goal-directed discovery of complex system behaviors while preserving solution diversity, and its LLM-based interpretability reveals how structural changes drive emergent behavior. CEDAR reduces human effort while enabling capabilities difficult to achieve with existing approaches, facilitating broader adoption of complex systems across domains.

</details>


### [85] [SkillEval: Decomposing Agent Skill Quality into Interpretable Signals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06891)
*Jiahui Han,Qinuo Li,Ziheng Peng,Haotian Wu,Haoze Liu,Danfeng Shan,Guanchu Wang,Huiqi Deng,Ninghao Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes SkillEval, an interpretable framework to automatically evaluate the quality of agent skills from their SKILL.md documents, using fixed semantic directions in representation space that correlate with downstream task performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As agents gain reusable skills for specialized tasks, it becomes important to systematically evaluate skill quality. Existing evaluations mostly rely on downstream task performance, which is task-specific, only partially reflects skill quality, and does not reveal what aspects of the skill need improvement. There is a need for a more general, interpretable, and task-agnostic evaluation of skills based on their documentation.

Method: The authors introduce SkillEval, a document-level evaluation framework that analyzes SKILL.md files. They construct controlled positive–negative skill pairs for each desired quality property and learn a corresponding interpretable direction in the model’s hidden representation space. Each new skill document is encoded, then projected onto these fixed directions to score it on each property. The framework explicitly measures and mitigates the impact of confounding document features like length and formatting so that scores better capture intended semantic properties.

Result: SkillEval can reliably distinguish between skills of different quality in controlled tests. The property-wise scores produced by SkillEval correlate well with downstream task performance, enabling prediction of whether a skill will aid task completion. Using SkillEval diagnostics to revise SKILL.md documents improves the targeted quality properties and leads to higher pass rates on downstream tasks.

Conclusion: Document-level semantic properties of SKILL.md files are key determinants of skill quality. SkillEval offers an interpretable, task-agnostic way to evaluate these properties via fixed representation-space directions, while controlling for irrelevant factors. It not only predicts downstream usefulness of skills but also helps diagnose and guide improvements to skill documents, resulting in better-performing agent skills.

Abstract: Agent skills provide reusable procedural knowledge that helps agents solve specialized tasks. As their use expands, evaluating skill quality becomes increasingly important. Existing evaluations often measure skill quality by testing whether a skill improves performance on specific downstream tasks. However, a reusable skill may apply to multiple task scenarios. Downstream evaluation mainly reflects the compatibility between a skill and the evaluated task, provides only a partial view of skill quality, and does not identify which aspect of the skill should be improved. We find that general properties of the \texttt{SKILL.md} document play an important role in skill quality. To evaluate these properties, we propose \textbf{SkillEval}, an interpretable framework for document-level skill evaluation. SkillEval evaluates each property using a fixed and inspectable scoring direction, producing interpretable scores. It further measures and reduces the influence of unrelated document features, such as length and formatting, so that each score captures its intended semantic property more specifically. Specifically, SkillEval learns an interpretable direction for each quality property from controlled positive--negative skill pairs in the hidden representation space of the model, and scores a new skill by projecting its representation onto these fixed directions. We use SkillEval to evaluate skills in controlled quality tests and show that SkillEval reliably distinguishes skills of different quality. In addition, SkillEval scores closely reflect downstream task performance, providing an early indication of whether a skill is likely to help an agent complete a task. We further explore SkillEval for diagnosing weaknesses in skill documents and guiding targeted revisions. The revised skills improve the targeted properties and achieve higher pass rates on downstream tasks.

</details>


### [86] [From Points to Edges: Edge-Conditioned Spectral Operators for Physics-Sensitive PDE Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06894)
*Zhentao Tan,Ruijie Quan,Yi Yang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They introduce an edge-aware spectral neural operator that injects local, physics-sensitive information into global spectral mixing, achieving state-of-the-art PDE solving accuracy, especially near sharp coefficient changes and high-gradient regions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard spectral neural operators mix information globally in the frequency domain, which is efficient but insensitive to sharp local structures (e.g., material interfaces, coefficient jumps) that strongly affect PDE solutions. Many real-world PDEs, such as Darcy flow, depend critically on these local features, so there is a need for operators that retain global spectral advantages while adapting to local, physics-relevant variations.

Method: They propose the Edge-Conditioned Spectral Operator (ESO), which modulates global spectral mixing using local edge-wise variations between neighboring spatial points. A key component is the Pairwise-Variation Modal Mixer (PVMM), which injects local edge information into the choice and weighting of spectral modes, allowing the spectral kernel to adapt to local structure. Additionally, they introduce Physics-Aware Reweighting (PAR), a task-adaptive scheme that emphasizes spatial regions deemed physically important based on task-specific physical quantities, effectively reweighting the loss or feature importance in those regions.

Result: On nine PDE benchmark problems, ESO consistently outperforms prior neural operators, achieving state-of-the-art accuracy. Empirical analyses show that ESO particularly reduces errors around coefficient discontinuities, high-gradient flow structures, and other regions where local physics is challenging to capture. Visualizations and region-wise metrics confirm improved fidelity in these sensitive areas.

Conclusion: By combining edge-conditioned spectral mixing (ESO with PVMM) and physics-aware reweighting (PAR), the method preserves the efficiency and global approximation strengths of spectral neural operators while substantially improving their sensitivity to local, physics-critical structures. This leads to more accurate and physically faithful PDE solutions across diverse benchmarks.

Abstract: Neural operators have become a central tool for solving partial differential equations (PDEs), with spectral operators offering efficient global mixing across spatial locations. However, many PDEs contain physics-sensitive local structures that are critical to the underlying physical behavior. For example, in Darcy flow, local material interfaces are often reflected by sharp changes in the permeability field and can strongly influence the solution. Existing spectral operators primarily adapt modal mixing based on center-point representations, making them insufficiently responsive to such localized structural variations. We propose the Edge-Conditioned Spectral Operator (ESO), a novel spectral operator framework that modulates global spectral mixing using local edge-wise variations. By incorporating the Pairwise-Variation Modal Mixer (PVMM) to inject local edge information into spectral mode selection, ESO preserves the global approximation capability of spectral neural operators while enabling the learned kernel to adapt to physics-sensitive local structures. Furthermore, we introduce a task-adaptive Physics-Aware Reweighting (PAR) that emphasizes physically important regions, identified by taskspecific physical quantities. Across nine PDE benchmarks, ESO consistently achieves state-of-the-art performance. Visual and region-wise analyses further demonstrate that ESO reduces solution errors near coefficient jumps, high-gradient flow structures, and other physically sensitive regions. The code is available at https://github.com/Tanpig-X/ESO.

</details>


### [87] [Long-Horizon Agent Trajectory Attribution: A Unified Benchmark and Fine-Grained Annotation Framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06909)
*Jing Chen,Yang Sun,Li Zhang,Lin Xu,Jie Shi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They propose a new benchmark and framework to attribute specific parts of an LLM agent’s trajectory (actions, tools, observations, memory) to their underlying causes, enabling fine-grained analysis of why agents behave as they do.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM agents increasingly act over long, complex trajectories, but current benchmarks mostly judge only final behavior (e.g., task success or safety) and do not support fine-grained analysis of which trajectory components caused particular outcomes. This lack of attribution makes it hard to debug, improve safety, or understand long-horizon reasoning and failures.

Method: They introduce the concept of trajectory attribution and design: (1) a unified component schema that standardizes heterogeneous agent trajectories; (2) a benchmark with human annotations indicating the primary attribution component for key outcomes, plus attack and execution chains where relevant; (3) two evaluation tasks—primary attribution localization and attribution-chain recovery; and (4) baseline methods based on incremental trajectory contribution and component-level leave-one-out perturbation. They also build a reusable annotation skill so new trajectories can be standardized and annotated within the same framework.

Result: They instantiate the benchmark using trajectories from AgentDojo and the Stage and Canary settings of Agent3Sigma, generating over 1,300 annotated trajectories spanning aligned actions, unsafe actions, and safety refusals. Baseline evaluations on the two attribution tasks show large performance differences across local vs. long-range and chain-structured attribution scenarios, highlighting that some attribution settings are much harder than others.

Conclusion: Trajectory attribution is both feasible and challenging: their benchmark, annotations, and baselines show that different attribution regimes (local, long-range, chained) pose distinct difficulties. The released framework and annotation skill provide an extensible foundation for systematically analyzing and evaluating agent trajectories for interpretability, safety, and debugging in future LLM agent systems.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM) agents increasingly operate through long-horizon trajectories involving user instructions, tool use, external observations, and memory. Existing benchmarks primarily evaluate behavioral outcomes but provide limited support for fine-grained attribution analysis. We introduce trajectory attribution and develop a benchmark and annotation framework for this task. The benchmark organizes heterogeneous trajectories under a unified component schema and provides annotations of the primary attribution component, together with attack and execution chains where applicable. Instantiating the benchmark with trajectories from AgentDojo and the Stage and Canary settings of Agent3Sigma yields more than 1,300 annotated trajectories covering task-aligned actions, unsafe actions, and safety refusals. The benchmark defines two evaluation tasks, primary attribution localization and attribution-chain recovery, and provides reference baselines based on incremental trajectory contribution and component-level leave-one-out perturbation. It captures diverse attribution settings, including local and long-range attribution as well as structured attribution chains. Reference baseline results exhibit substantial performance differences across these settings, providing an initial characterization of the benchmark's attribution challenges. Beyond this initial instantiation, we release a reusable annotation skill that enables trajectories generated by new agent models to be standardized, annotated, and evaluated under the same framework. Project resources and future releases are available at https://github.com/chenjing-2024/agent-trajectory-attribution.

</details>


### [88] [Fast LapSum: Exact Differentiable Top-k at Million Scale](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06912)
*Łukasz Struski,Joanna Wojciechowicz,Jakub Antczak,Marcin Mazur,Kamil Książek,Jacek Tabor*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Fast LapSum is a new, efficient, fully-differentiable soft top-k operator that preserves an exact selection budget k while being fast enough for large-scale sparse computation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Hard top-k is non-differentiable and blocks gradients, which is problematic for training models that rely on sparse routing or selection. Existing soft/continuous relaxations of top-k are either computationally too expensive or do not strictly enforce the exact selection mass k, limiting their practicality for large-scale models and precise-budget tasks.

Method: The paper introduces Fast LapSum, an exact-budget soft top-k primitive. It formulates a differentiable relaxation that strictly preserves a total selection mass of k, and designs a GPU-friendly solver that runs in linear time after sorting. The solver uses a linear-time threshold computation to determine the soft selection weights and derives a closed-form vector–Jacobian product for efficient backpropagation. For extremely large inputs, it employs probabilistic bracketing to only sort the uncertain middle band of kernel-noised scores, avoiding full sorting of all elements.

Result: Fast LapSum achieves near-negligible computational overhead, processing 10^6, 10^7, and 10^8 scores in 0.41 ms, 1.15 ms, and 5.23 ms, respectively, on GPU. It is shown to make exact soft top-k practical in scenarios requiring large-scale sparse routing and retrieval. Empirically, the method is applied to generate megapixel sparse adversarial examples with an exact soft budget of about 0.02% of pixels, where it attains an order-of-magnitude speedup over prior state-of-the-art techniques. It is also successfully used to train a fully differentiable sparse image coder from scratch, operating over millions of coordinates within the training loop.

Conclusion: Fast LapSum provides, to the authors’ knowledge, the first fully differentiable soft top-k operator that both preserves an exact selection mass k and is efficient enough for large-scale practical use. Its linear-time GPU solver with analytical gradients and probabilistic bracketing enables exact-budget soft selection in demanding applications, such as sparse adversarial attacks and differentiable sparse coding, with minimal overhead. This establishes exact soft top-k as a viable primitive for modern sparse computation tasks like routing, retrieval, and attention pruning.

Abstract: The top-$k$ operation is a fundamental building block of modern sparse computation, enabling token routing, expert activation, memory selection, and attention pruning. Yet standard hard top-$k$ blocks gradients, while existing continuous (soft) relaxations remain too costly for large-scale models. We introduce Fast LapSum, an exact-budget soft top-$k$ primitive whose GPU solver runs in linear time after sorting. Unlike prior linear-time methods such as DFTopK, which relax the normalization constraint, Fast LapSum is, to our knowledge, the first method to preserve an exact selection mass of $k$ while remaining fully differentiable end-to-end. Our solver combines a linear-time threshold computation with an analytical vector--Jacobian product, and for extreme scales employs probabilistic bracketing to sort only the uncertain middle band of kernel-noised scores. The resulting overhead is almost negligible: the solver processes $10^6$, $10^7$, and $10^8$ scores in $0.41$, $1.15$, and $5.23$\,ms, respectively. This makes exact soft top-$k$ practical for sparse routing, retrieval, and large-scale optimization. We demonstrate Fast LapSum on two demanding applications operating over millions of coordinates inside the training loop: generating megapixel sparse adversarial examples with an exact soft budget of ${\sim}0.02\%$ of an image's pixels, achieving an order-of-magnitude speedup over state-of-the-art methods, and training a fully differentiable sparse image coder from scratch.

</details>


### [89] [ReGraph: Learning to Generate Recipe Graphs from Food Images](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06917)
*Guoshan Liu,Bin Zhu,Pengkun Jiao,Jingjing Chen,Chong-Wah Ngo,Yu-Gang Jiang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces ReGraph, a structured recipe graph dataset, and Recipe Graph Learning (RGL), a framework that lets large multimodal models generate explicit cooking process graphs from food images, revealing a gap between fluent recipe text and true procedural understanding.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing Large Multimodal Models can generate fluent recipes from images, but cooking is an inherently structured process with ordered actions and state changes that are only implicitly captured in free-form text. There is a need for explicit, machine-usable representations to test and improve models' process-level understanding rather than just surface text quality.

Method: The authors construct ReGraph, a large-scale recipe graph dataset that models ingredients, tools, and actions as entities with attributes capturing ingredient state changes and typed relations encoding targets, destinations, and temporal order. They also add Recipe Reasoning Chain-of-Thought (RR-CoT) traces for procedural decomposition. On top of ReGraph, they design Recipe Graph Learning (RGL), a two-stage framework enabling LMMs to map a food image to a detailed recipe workflow represented as a structured graph. They evaluate models via deterministic, schema-aware matching against reference graphs.

Result: Experiments show that current approaches achieve strong text-generation metrics while recovering only limited, schema-aligned entity and relation structures in ReGraph, revealing a discrepancy between fluent language and actual process modeling. Using two LMM backbones, RGL significantly improves the accuracy and completeness of generated cooking entities and procedural relations compared to baselines, although fine-grained ingredient state representation remains relatively poor.

Conclusion: Structured recipe graphs provide a more faithful test of procedural knowledge in multimodal models than standard text metrics. ReGraph and the RGL framework help LMMs better capture entities and relations in cooking workflows from images, but accurately modeling detailed ingredient state changes is still an open challenge requiring further research.

Abstract: Recent Large Multimodal Models (LMMs) have achieved impressive performance in recipe generation from food images.However, cooking is a structured transformation process in which ingredients undergo state changes through ordered actions,while free-form recipe language leaves the corresponding entities, intermediate states, and dependencies largely implicit and entangled.A graph representation makes this procedural knowledge explicit and compositional, providing a structured basis for assessing whether model outputs encode process-level knowledge rather than merely presenting plausible textual descriptions. To address this limitation, we present ReGraph, a large-scale recipe graph dataset that represents ingredients, cooking actions, and tools as entities, uses entity attributes to describe ingredient state changes, and employs typed relations to encode manipulation targets, destinations, and procedural ordering. ReGraph further incorporates explicit Recipe Reasoning Chain-of-Thought (RR-CoT) traces, providing auxiliary supervision for procedural decomposition and structured graph generation. Building on ReGraph, we propose Recipe Graph Learning (RGL), a two-stage framework that enables LMMs to generate a plausible fine-grained cooking workflow from a food image in the form of a structured recipe graph. Under a deterministic, schema-aware matching protocol, our experiments reveal a substantial gap between text-generation quality and recoverable procedural structure: recipes produced by existing approaches achieve competitive text-generation scores yet yield limited reference-aligned entity and relation structure under the ReGraph schema. In contrast, across two representative LMM backbones, RGL consistently improves the generation of cooking entities and procedural relations, while our analysis further shows that fine-grained ingredient-state capture remains the most challenging dimension.

</details>


### [90] [Deal Me Maybe: The Role of Emotions in Multi-Agent Negotiation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06922)
*Massimiliano Luca,Apoorva Singh,Bruno Lepri*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper investigates how emotions, injected via prompts, influence the outcomes of price negotiations conducted by large language model (LLM) agents.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most existing negotiation benchmarks assume emotionally neutral LLM agents, ignoring that human bargaining is heavily shaped by emotions. The authors want to understand whether and how explicitly conditioned emotions affect negotiation performance and behavior of LLMs, and what risks this poses for real-world applications like online commerce.

Method: The authors design a controlled negotiation framework where LLM-based buyer and seller agents are each independently assigned one of six emotional states via prompting. The agents negotiate the prices of 350 real consumer products under two different budget conditions. They systematically study 36 emotion-pair combinations across five popular LLMs, measuring deal rates, prices achieved, language use, termination behavior, and concession dynamics.

Result: Emotions robustly affect negotiation outcomes. Angry buyers almost never reach agreements (0.39% deal rate). Happy buyers reach agreements most often (28.91%) but secure worse prices than fearful buyers. The influence of emotions is role-dependent: the buyer’s emotion mainly affects whether deals are accepted or rejected, while the seller’s emotion primarily governs how they concede over time. Emotional conditioning also changes linguistic patterns, when negotiations terminate, and how prices evolve across turns.

Conclusion: Prompted emotions materially alter LLM negotiation behavior and outcomes, in ways that echo but also diverge from human patterns. Because buyer and seller emotions can systematically skew deal rates and prices, deploying emotion-conditioned LLM agents in commercial or bargaining settings may create fairness, safety, and manipulation concerns. Benchmarks and applications should explicitly account for emotional conditioning rather than assuming neutral behavior.

Abstract: Negotiation is a demanding social task for LLM agents, requiring strategic reasoning, persuasion, and interpersonal adaptation. Yet existing benchmarks often treat agents as emotionally neutral, overlooking a key driver of human bargaining behavior. We study how prompt-conditioned emotions affect LLM-based price negotiation. In a controlled framework, buyer and seller agents are independently assigned one of six emotional states and negotiate over 350 real consumer products under two budget conditions. Across 36 emotion-pair settings and five widely used LLMs, we find that emotions strongly shape outcomes. Angry buyers almost never reach agreement (0.39% deal rate), while happy buyers agree most often (28.91%), but obtain worse prices than fearful buyers. Emotion effects are role-dependent: buyer emotion mainly drives acceptance and rejection, whereas seller emotion shapes concession dynamics. These effects influence not only language, but also termination behavior and price trajectories, raising concerns for emotion-conditioned agents in commerce.

</details>


### [91] [TRIBE: Predicting Team Performance via Communication Behavior Ensembles](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06926)
*Ali Jalal-Kamali,Nikolos Gurney,David V. Pynadath,Fred Morstatter*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TRIBE is a domain-independent method that uses early communication patterns to categorize teams into behavioral types that predict performance and reveal how AI and human advisors differently shape team dynamics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional team performance metrics often miss the underlying behavioral dynamics that drive success or failure, especially when we lack task-specific knowledge. There is a need for a general, domain-independent way to understand and predict team performance from interaction data alone, and to see how interventions like AI agents or human advisors affect team trajectories.

Method: The authors introduce TRIBE, a framework that analyzes team communication patterns over time. It clusters teams into performance-predictive behavioral ‘tribes’ based on their interaction dynamics, using only communication data and no task-specific features. They apply TRIBE to four heterogeneous datasets, perform temporal analyses of team trajectories, compare trajectories with and without AI agents or human advisors, and benchmark/optimize against an LLM-based pipeline (using Llama) for speed and predictive performance.

Result: TRIBE can classify teams into behavioral tribes that are predictive of final performance using only the first ~10% of the task. Communication patterns reliably predict team outcomes, though predictive strength depends on how much behavioral freedom the task allows. Temporal analysis shows that AI agents significantly reshape team behavioral trajectories, whereas human advisors tend to follow existing team dynamics. Teams retain behavioral flexibility throughout collaboration. The optimized TRIBE pipeline outperforms the Llama-based baseline while running significantly faster.

Conclusion: TRIBE offers a domain-independent, communication-based method to uncover hidden team behavioral dynamics and predict performance early in a task. It enables timely interventions and provides insight into how AI agents versus human advisors influence team behavior. The framework is both more efficient and more accurate than a Llama-based baseline, highlighting its practical utility for designing effective, human-compatible AI teammates and support tools across diverse collaborative settings.

Abstract: Designing autonomous agents that effectively assist human teams hinges on understanding team dynamics, often without task specific knowledge. We present TRIBE, a domain independent approach that reveals team behavioral dynamics invisible to traditional performance metrics. We show that communication patterns can categorize teams into performance predictive behavioral tribes, as early as 10% into the task, enabling timely interventions. We test TRIBE on four diverse datasets and demonstrate that communication patterns predict team performance while the prediction strength varies by the degree a task structure allows for behavioral freedom. Our temporal analysis reveals that AI agents significantly alter team behavioral trajectories while human advisors align with natural dynamics, and that teams maintain behavioral flexibility throughout collaboration. Further, we compare TRIBE to Llama and optimize the pipeline, achieving significant speedup with performance improvement.

</details>


### [92] [Science Edge Evaluation: SEE the Missing Step Toward Real Scientific Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06931)
*Taolin Han,Yuchen Zhang,Jinghang Wang,Yun Wu,Wai Yuet Chiu,Zhaohai Li,Yifei Zhang,Jinxin Wang,Yuhao Zhou,Chen Zhao,Jiajia Li,Jiaxin Li,Qile Jin,Kewei Sun,Shuang Wu,Weiqi Zhai,Renquan Lv,Junchao Li,Ruodan Chen,Qingteng Chen,Zhibo Yang,Hu Wei,Lin Qu,Shuai Bai,Bing Zhao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces Science Edge Evaluation (SEE), a multimodal benchmark to test whether large language models can perform realistic, evidence-bounded scientific reasoning from experimental data, finding that current models, including tool-augmented ones, are far from reliable.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As LLMs are increasingly used in scientific discovery, it is unclear whether they can handle complex, real-world laboratory reasoning grounded in experimental evidence rather than textbook knowledge. The authors aim to rigorously measure this capability across chemistry, biology, and materials science using realistic tasks derived from peer-reviewed literature and lab practice.

Method: The authors construct Science Edge Evaluation (SEE), a multimodal benchmark consisting of expert-curated questions rooted in real experimental workflows and figures from peer-reviewed papers in chemistry, biology, and materials science. They then systematically evaluate 19 multimodal large language models, including both general-purpose and science-specialized models, with and without tool use (visual-agent evaluation), measuring accuracy on these tasks and analyzing how tool-derived information affects reasoning quality.

Result: Across the SEE benchmark, the best-performing model without tools achieves only 48.7% accuracy, indicating limited reliability for experimental scientific reasoning. Surprisingly, general-purpose MLLMs perform better on average than domain-specialized scientific models. When models can call external tools in a visual-agent setup, the top accuracy rises modestly to 52.7%, showing that access to more information only slightly improves performance and does not resolve core reasoning failures. The analyses show that models often misuse or over-extend tool-derived information beyond what is justified by the original experiments.

Conclusion: Current multimodal LLMs are not yet capable of reliably making justified, evidence-bounded inferences from experimental data, a critical requirement for real-world scientific discovery. Simply adding tools and more information is insufficient; models must instead learn to manage tool outputs and reason strictly within the limits of the underlying experimental evidence. The authors argue that future progress requires shifting from models that mainly restate known scientific concepts to systems that can derive novel, well-justified insights from experimental results while respecting evidential constraints.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly involved in scientific discovery, yet it remains unclear whether they can support complex real laboratory science. Here we introduce Science Edge Evaluation (SEE), a multimodal benchmark of expert-curated questions grounded in peer-reviewed literature and experimental practice in chemistry, biology, and materials science. Evaluation of 19 multimodal large language models (MLLMs) shows that even the best-performing model reaches only 48.7% accuracy. Moreover, general-purpose models outperform science-specialized models on average. In the visual-agent evaluation, the use of tools increases the best accuracy to 52.7%. Tool use can expand the information available to models, but more information does not necessarily lead to reliable scientific reasoning. The key challenge is whether models can manage tool-derived information within the boundaries of the original experimental evidence. Together, these findings reveal that current MLLMs still cannot reliably make justified and evidence-bounded inferences from experimental results, which is an essential capability in real scientific discovery. Bridging this gap requires MLLMs to transition from explaining established scientific concepts to deriving novel and evidence-based insights from experimental data.

</details>


### [93] [Blind to the Pivotal Vote: Aggregate Independence Metrics Miss Where Verification Actually Helps](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06940)
*Yang Shu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper studies how to effectively use additional signals (like test execution) to improve the decisions of LLM judge panels, finding that naive aggregation gives little benefit but targeted substitution on pivotal (one-vote-margin) cases yields large accuracy gains with limited extra cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM judge panels are widely used to evaluate model outputs, but their errors are highly correlated, so adding more similar judges provides little extra information. There is a need to understand when and how alternative signals (e.g., test suites for code) can meaningfully improve panel decisions and reduce evaluation cost without simply scaling up the number of judges.

Method: The authors analyze the dependence structure of LLM judge panels and formalize how single-ballot substitutions (replacing one judge vote with an external signal) can affect majority outcomes. They identify that only queries with a one-vote margin are potentially impacted. Empirically, they run experiments on three code benchmarks and multiple panel sizes, adding a different evidence source (test execution) and measuring accuracy gains conditioned on vote margins, along with subsampling experiments to test robustness.

Result: They find that adding a different evidence source (e.g., test suite results) does not significantly increase the effective number of independent judges when used naively. However, when substitutions are targeted at pivotal one-vote-margin cases, all accuracy improvements are concentrated there, with gains of about +10.4 to +23.3 percentage points on such queries and zero gain elsewhere. On HumanEval+/MBPP+, a majority-side replacement rule boosts overall accuracy from 82.44% to 85.62% while only calling the signal on 16.2% of queries; using only the signal remains better at 87.60%.

Conclusion: Population-level diagnostics of dependence (like effective number of judges) do not by themselves reveal where external signals are most useful. Instead, conditioning on the margin identifies an 'affected set' of pivotal cases where single-ballot substitution can have large impact. This characterization enables practical call-reduction policies that selectively invoke expensive external signals only when they can change outcomes, improving accuracy-cost trade-offs for LLM judge panels.

Abstract: LLM judge panels are a standard evaluation tool, but prior work reports highly correlated panel errors: nine judges provide roughly the effective information of two independent ones, and aggregation closes only a small fraction of the gap. A natural remedy--a signal from a different evidence source, e.g., executing a test suite--produced no distinguishable change in the panel's effective-vote count at scale (-0.04, 95\% CI [-0.10, +0.02]). Aggregate dependence and conditional decision utility are different questions. Elementary majority arithmetic fixes the affected set for single-ballot substitution: only decisions with a one-vote margin can change. The empirical question is whether panel error rates rise and useful substitutions concentrate there. They do: the entire accuracy gain concentrates on these pivotal queries, where it is large (+10.4 to +23.3 percentage points across three headline configurations), and is exactly zero elsewhere. We confirm the pattern across three code benchmarks and four panel sizes (a 9-judge extension and 56 dependent subsampling checks, gain +6.5 to +16.1 percentage points). On HumanEval+/MBPP+, a majority-side replacement rule raises overall accuracy from 82.44\% to 85.62\% while invoking the signal on 16.2\% of queries; signal-only remains stronger at 87.60\%. Thus population-level dependence diagnostics and margin-stratified utility are complementary, and the affected-set characterization yields a call-reduction rule for any specified single-ballot substitution policy.

</details>


### [94] [LMM Modality Transfer: A Pre-requisite for Autonomous GIS Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06948)
*Ivan Majic,Zexian Huang,Franziska Hübl,Krzysztof Janowicz,Meilin Shi,Mina Karimi,Zilong Liu,Alexandra Fortacz-Lazan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper evaluates how well large multimodal models can transfer spatial information between images and text, showing that current models still perform poorly even on simple colored-square grids.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While AI models have made progress in spatial reasoning, most work uses only text, unlike real GIS workflows that mix maps (images) and language. For automated GIS analysis and human-AI collaboration, models must accurately move spatial information back and forth between visual and textual modalities. The authors want to measure and expose this specific capability gap.

Method: They design a two-step modality transfer task using images of colored squares on a regular grid. First, one LMM must produce a textual description of the spatial arrangement in the input image. Second, a fresh instance of the LMM must reconstruct the original image using only that textual description. Performance in reconstructing the grid serves as a measure of how well spatial information is preserved and transferred across modalities. They analyze this through spatial information theory and evaluate recent OpenAI LMMs on the task.

Result: The evaluated LMMs perform poorly at reconstructing even simple grid scenes from textual descriptions. Errors arise in colors, positions, and overall spatial layout, revealing substantial information loss or distortion when going from image to text and back. This demonstrates that current LMMs have weak and unreliable modality transfer for spatial information.

Conclusion: Robust geospatial understanding in LMMs cannot be achieved with current capabilities; it requires much stronger multi-modal alignment so that spatial information can be faithfully preserved and exchanged between images and text. The paper’s modality transfer task exposes a key bottleneck: even recent LMMs from OpenAI struggle to maintain accurate spatial structure across modalities, underscoring a need for new training strategies and benchmarks focused on spatial multi-modal alignment.

Abstract: AI models are becoming increasingly adept at understanding and processing spatial information, thereby facilitating agentic problem-solving in spatial tasks and workflows. However, most of the research on their spatial capabilities (e.g., spatial reasoning) has focused on the textual modality as input and output. This contrasts with the human approach to GIS workflows, where text and visual modalities are often used together, interchangeably, and in a complementary manner. Thus, to truly achieve an automated GIS analysis pipeline or carry out human-designed GIS workflows, AI models --- Large Multimodal Models (LMMs) in particular --- need to be able to seamlessly transition between image- and text-based modalities that are traditionally used in such workflows. We present a modality transfer task that (1) asks an LMM to first describe an input image of colored squares in a regular grid, and (2) asks a new LMM instance to re-generate an image of the original spatial scene using the textual description output by the former model. This task quantifies the ability of LMMs to transfer spatial information between image and text modalities. Ultimately, by examining the modality transfer capability of LMMs through the lens of spatial information theory, this work highlights a critical bottleneck: achieving strong and robust geospatial understanding in LMMs requires rigorous, multi-modal alignment. Our results indicate that recent LMMs (here from OpenAI) still struggle with modality transfer, when tasked with re-generating an image of a simple spatial grid of color squares.

</details>


### [95] [Does Splitting a Triage Decision Across Agents Hide Bias or Help Catch It? A Multi-Agent Simulation Study of LLM-Based Resource Allocation Under Audit Capacity Constraints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06949)
*Paul-Peter Arslan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper studies whether using a multi-agent LLM pipeline with an audit step reduces demographic bias in life-or-death resource allocation, finding that pipelines do not reduce bias frequency but that audit capacity and queueing strategy crucially affect how much bias is detected and corrected.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing work shows single LLMs can display demographic bias in critical decisions, but real systems often use multi-step pipelines with oversight to mitigate such errors. It is unclear whether distributing decisions across specialized roles and adding an independent auditor meaningfully reduces bias in outcomes, especially under realistic resource constraints such as auditor overload. The paper aims to quantify how pipeline structure and audit capacity affect both the presence of biased decisions and the system’s ability to detect them.

Method: The authors build a synthetic disaster-triage simulator that presents paired patient cases that are clinically identical except for a demographic attribute. They compare two setups using GPT-4o-mini: a single-agent baseline where one model makes and checks decisions, and a nine-agent, role-differentiated pipeline with assessment, allocation, and independent audit roles. Across 192 simulated disaster episodes (2,304 paired cases), they vary three pressure dimensions and measure the rate of biased outcomes, how often bias is detected or missed, and the effects of auditor workload. They also run a follow-up experiment comparing first-come-first-served versus risk-based reordering of the audit queue under fixed capacity.

Result: The overall frequency of biased outcomes is similar in single-agent and multi-agent pipeline conditions (6.9% vs. 6.1%, p = 0.498), indicating no significant reduction in bias from pipelining. However, 30% of biased outcomes go undetected by the audit stage, with this fraction worsening to 43.8% when the auditor is overloaded and improving to 18.4% when it is not. The main driver is audit coverage: the share of cases reviewed drops from 100% to 65.6% under load (p < 0.001), while the auditor’s accuracy on reviewed cases remains high and statistically unchanged (81.6% vs. 85.7%, p = 1.000, slightly higher under load). Reordering the audit queue by estimated risk instead of first-come-first-served increases coverage under the same capacity constraint from 65.6% to 91.7% (p = 0.028), recovering most of the lost oversight.

Conclusion: Multi-agent LLM pipelines with oversight do not inherently reduce the base rate of biased decisions compared with a single model, but the design and resourcing of the audit stage strongly determine how much existing bias is actually caught. Under capacity constraints, the main vulnerability is reduced audit coverage, not degraded auditor judgment, meaning that many biased decisions can escape review when auditors are overloaded. Queueing policies that prioritize higher-risk cases can substantially restore effective oversight even without additional capacity. The study’s findings, though limited to one model and modest, non-adversarial simulations, suggest that organizations deploying LLM pipelines should focus as much on audit capacity and triage mechanisms as on adding additional agents or roles.

Abstract: Prior benchmarking work has shown that a single large language model (LLM), forced to make life-or-death resource-allocation decisions, exhibits measurable demographic bias. Real deployments, however, rarely use a single agent: they use pipelines, with review steps meant to catch exactly this kind of failure. We study what happens to bias when the same decision is distributed across a role-differentiated multi-agent pipeline (assessment, allocation, independent audit) instead of made and checked by one model alone. Using a synthetic disaster-triage simulator with paired cases that are clinically identical except for one demographic attribute, we run 192 episodes (2,304 resolved case pairs) on GPT-4o-mini comparing a single-agent control condition to a nine-agent pipeline under three independently varied pressure dimensions. We find no measurable difference in how often biased outcomes occur between the two conditions (6.9% vs. 6.1%, p = 0.498). We do find a large and significant effect of audit capacity on whether bias is caught: 30.0% of biased outcomes go entirely undetected, rising to 43.8% when the auditor is overloaded and falling to 18.4% when it is not. Decomposing this effect shows it is driven almost entirely by coverage (whether a case is reviewed at all, which collapses from 100.0% to 65.6% under load, p < 0.001) rather than by degraded judgment on the cases that are reviewed (81.6% vs. 85.7%, p = 1.000, direction reversed). A follow-up experiment shows that reordering the audit queue by estimated risk, rather than first-come-first-served, recovers most of the lost coverage under the same capacity constraint (65.6% to 91.7%, p = 0.028). We discuss the implications for any system that adds independent oversight to an LLM agent pipeline under resource constraints, and report the study's limitations honestly: one model, modest sample sizes, and no adversarial replication.

</details>


### [96] [Critical Acclaim Orientation in Large Language Models: Evidence from Film Preference Elicitation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06955)
*Jonghyun Jee,Aaron Shaw*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper tests whether large language models (LLMs) reproduce human-like hierarchies of cultural value in film, finding that they systematically favor critically acclaimed movies over purely commercially successful ones.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs are trained on web-scale data that embed social judgments about cultural products, but it is unclear whether their preferences follow mass popularity signals (box office, mainstream buzz) or more elite prestige signals (critical acclaim). Understanding this matters for how LLMs may shape cultural visibility and recommendations.

Method: The authors build a 200-film benchmark divided into three types: (1) critically acclaimed but commercially obscure, (2) commercially successful but not critically acclaimed, and (3) films with both critical acclaim and commercial success (dual-legitimacy). Using eight LLMs from four model families, they run 20,000 pairwise forced-choice comparisons per model, asking which film is better, and estimate underlying preferences via a Bradley–Terry model. They then use nested OLS regressions to decompose how evaluative orientation, public visibility, and popular reception each contribute to the models’ film rankings. They also test different prompt framings—evaluative vs recommendation-oriented—to see how preferences change in more realistic usage scenarios.

Result: All eight LLMs, across all four families, exhibit a consistent bias toward critically acclaimed films. When forced to choose between critically acclaimed but commercially obscure films and commercially successful but critically unrecognized films, models favor the critically acclaimed options, and this tendency becomes stronger as model size increases within each family. Regression analyses indicate that evaluative orientation, public visibility, and popular reception each explain unique variance in film preferences. When controlling for public visibility, models’ preference for dual-legitimacy films over acclaim-only films reverses; when further controlling for popular reception, the disadvantage of commercial-only films shrinks substantially. Different prompt framings yield meaningfully different rankings: evaluative prompts surface critical-acclaim biases more strongly than recommendation prompts.

Conclusion: LLMs do not just echo mass popularity; they systematically align more with critical-acclaim hierarchies, and this alignment strengthens at larger scales. However, their preferences are shaped by a mix of evaluative, visibility, and popularity signals, and the apparent dominance of critically acclaimed and dual-legitimacy films can shift once these factors are controlled. Because recommendation-style prompts dampen but do not erase critical-acclaim orientation, LLMs may subtly propagate prestige-based cultural hierarchies in real-world applications, affecting which films and likely other cultural items are surfaced to users.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are trained on corpora that contain expressions of human judgment about films, books, music, and more. Yet whether LLMs systematically reproduce evaluative hierarchies remains unclear. Prior research on cultural bias in LLMs suggests competing expectations: models may mirror the popularity signals of internet texts, or may reproduce forms of prestige embedded in critical discourse. We probe this question through a study of film evaluations with eight models from four families (Anthropic, OpenAI, Alibaba, and Mistral), using a 200-film benchmark partitioned into critically acclaimed, commercially successful, and dual-legitimacy (critical acclaim + commercial success) films. Across 20,000 pairwise forced-choice comparisons per model analyzed with Bradley--Terry estimation, we observe a consistent critical acclaim orientation with all models: critically acclaimed yet commercially obscure films are selected over commercially successful yet critically unrecognized ones. This pattern grows with model scale within each family. In addition, nested OLS regression analyses show that evaluative orientation, public visibility, and popular reception distinctly help explain preferences. Adjusting for public visibility reverses the models' preference for dual-legitimacy films over critical acclaim-only films, while additionally accounting for popular reception attenuates much of the disadvantage of films with commercial success only. Finally, evaluative and recommendation-oriented prompt framings produce divergent rankings, suggesting that critical acclaim orientation may manifest indirectly in real-world LLM deployments.

</details>


### [97] [CAi Copilot: Reducing Operational Workload in Molecular Design through Intent-Driven Agentic Workflows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06961)
*Zhu Wang,Jiangyu Chen,Yingjun Shang,Yuhui Yao,Laiao Lu,Tianfan Fu,Na Zou*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces CAi Copilot, an AI-driven, expert-oriented agent that transforms high-level molecular design intent into adaptive, traceable workflows and outperforms existing systems on diverse molecular design benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
