Motivation: Early molecular design is iterative and complex, requiring researchers to translate vague goals into detailed workflows, continuously refine candidates, and integrate evidence from many specialized computational tools. Current AI tools are fragmented: they can generate molecules, optimize properties, and assess synthesizability, but they are siloed and require human experts to orchestrate the entire process and interpret intermediate outputs. The authors aim to bridge this gap by creating a unified, intent-driven system that can autonomously plan, execute, and adapt molecular design workflows while keeping decisions transparent and scientifically grounded.

Method: The authors formulate molecular design as an "intent-to-evidence" workflow execution problem. They develop CAi Copilot, a three-layer agentic architecture: (1) the Research Interface Layer that interprets a researcher’s goals and converts them into executable, multi-step design plans; (2) the Agent Reasoning Layer that adaptively guides runs using interim results, deciding when to generate, filter, re-optimize, or stop; and (3) the Execution Substrate that provides access to molecular generation, property prediction, docking, synthesis assessment tools, metrics, utilities, and backend services. They evaluate CAi across 45 molecular design tasks and additional benchmarks focusing on generation–screening coordination and multi-criteria evaluation, as well as long-horizon execution stress tests.

Result: Across 45 tasks, CAi Copilot achieves the highest overall performance among compared systems, with an outcome score of 84.59, which is 18.07 points higher than the next-best approach. Additional benchmarks show that CAi effectively coordinates molecular generation, screening, and multi-objective evaluation, although they also reveal performance limitations in very long-horizon workflows. The experiments emphasize CAi’s ability to maintain transparent, stepwise reasoning connecting high-level design intent to candidate-level evidence.

Conclusion: CAi Copilot demonstrates that an intent-driven, agentic workflow system can effectively orchestrate complex molecular design processes, turning broad research goals into adaptive, traceable computational campaigns. By tightly integrating planning, reasoning over intermediate results, and a rich substrate of molecular tools, CAi surpasses existing methods on diverse benchmarks while exposing open challenges in robust long-horizon execution. This architecture suggests a promising direction for future expert-assistance systems in molecular discovery and related scientific domains.

Abstract: Early-stage molecular design is an iterative process, not just a task of generating molecules. Researchers turn broad goals into design strategies, refine candidates, assess many properties, and gather evidence before synthesis and tests. AI methods can generate molecules, optimize several goals, predict properties, dock compounds, and account for synthesis. Yet these functions are spread across specialized tools. Experts must still coordinate each step, judge interim results, and integrate evidence. The central challenge is thus to turn research intent into adaptive, traceable runs grounded in scientific tools. We cast this challenge as intent-to-evidence molecular design workflow execution and present CAi Copilot, an expert-oriented agent with three linked layers. The Research Interface Layer turns intent into an executable plan. The Agent Reasoning Layer uses interim results to guide each run. The Execution Substrate supplies molecular tools, metrics, reusable utilities, and backend services. Across 45 tasks, CAi achieves the strongest overall performance, with an outcome score of 84.59, exceeding the next-best result by 18.07 points. Additional benchmarks test how CAi coordinates generation, screening, and multi-criteria evaluation, while exposing limits in long-horizon execution. These results show that CAi turns broad molecular-design intent into transparent, traceable workflows that connect interim decisions to candidate-level evidence.

</details>


### [98] [Learning in Deep Networks under Dale's Constraint](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06963)
*Roy Abel,Shimon Ullman*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes a biologically plausible neural network architecture that obeys Dale’s law and still implements backprop-like learning using only non-negative activations, fixed-sign synapses, and local learning rules.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most biologically inspired learning models still violate a key property of real cortical neurons: Dale’s constraint, where each neuron is either purely excitatory or purely inhibitory and synapse signs do not change. Existing models often rely on mixed-sign activations and error signals, including negative values, which is not realistic for neural circuits. The authors aim to develop a learning scheme that respects these biological constraints while remaining as powerful and efficient as backpropagation used in artificial neural networks.

Method: The authors design a neural architecture with two complementary non-negative channels that jointly encode positive and negative contributions, inspired by on-off pathways in the brain. Each logical variable (activation or error signal) is represented via a pair of non-negative activities. A repeated neural circuit motif implements these on and off channels in both bottom-up (forward) and top-down (feedback) pathways. Synapses have fixed sign, and learning signals (error-like quantities) are also non-negative. A local Hebbian learning rule updates synapses based solely on local neuron interactions. The authors provide a theoretical analysis proving that, with the on-off encoding and these motifs, the local Hebbian updates exactly match the gradient updates of backpropagation, despite using only non-negative error signals.

Result: Theoretically, the authors show that their on-off, non-negative architecture with fixed-sign synapses can exactly reproduce standard backpropagation weight updates. Empirically, they evaluate the architecture on Tiny ImageNet and find that it learns more efficient representations and significantly outperforms comparable conventional networks (“vanilla networks”) that do not use the on-off decomposition, under similar conditions. Thus, the approach is both biologically more realistic and practically competitive or superior on a challenging benchmark.

Conclusion: The study demonstrates that effective, backpropagation-like learning does not require mixed-sign neural or error signals and can be achieved under stronger biological constraints such as Dale’s law and local learning. By using paired on-off non-negative channels, fixed-sign synapses, and Hebbian updates, the proposed architecture provides a more realistic model of neural computation that still attains strong performance on complex visual tasks. This narrows the gap between biological plausibility and machine learning effectiveness and suggests that similar circuit motifs could underlie learning in the brain.

Abstract: Biologically plausible learning models aim to explain how neural circuits can implement effective learning under the constraints of real neurons. Although significant progress has been made, a major remaining challenge is that existing models often allow neurons or synapses to represent mixed-sign values, both positive and negative, in violation of a basic aspect of cortical circuitry -- Dale's constraint: biological neurons are either excitatory or inhibitory, but not both, and synapses cannot change sign. In this work, we address this discrepancy by introducing a biologically motivated neural architecture in which both neural activations and learning signals are represented by non-negative activity, and synapses have fixed sign, while still supporting backpropagation-like learning. Our approach uses two complementary interacting non-negative channels to represent positive and negative contributions, inspired by evidence of on-off representations in the brain. These channels are implemented through a simple neural circuit motif, which is repeated throughout the network in both bottom-up and top-down pathways. Combined with a local Hebbian learning rule, the resulting model propagates learning signals and updates weights using only local interactions between neurons. We show theoretically that our learning scheme can exactly recover the backpropagation update despite relying solely on non-negative error signals. Empirically, beyond satisfying stronger biological constraints, the on-off architecture learns efficient representations, yielding substantial gains over comparable vanilla networks on the Tiny ImageNet benchmark. These results demonstrate that effective learning can emerge from biologically plausible mechanisms without requiring mixed-sign signals, providing a step toward more realistic models of neural computation.

</details>


### [99] [Finding Usable Weight Mechanisms with Tiled SVD](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06969)
*Ash Manvi,Samreena Tajreen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They propose a new, dictionary-free way to extract and evaluate linear mechanisms (“mounts”) directly from language model weights, achieving strong, fully evaluated results across all linear sites in Gemma-2-2B and releasing code and tooling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most mechanistic interpretability work relies on proxy dictionaries such as sparse autoencoders, where concepts live in the learned dictionary rather than directly in the model’s own weight space, which can obscure whether we are truly understanding the network’s native mechanisms. The authors want a method that attaches conceptual identity directly to the network’s weights and evaluates mechanisms at the level of their full write effect on model states, avoiding artifacts and locality issues of tile-based evaluations.

Method: They introduce “mechanism mounts” extracted from linear layers using column-tiled singular value decomposition (SVD). Each mount is a (v, u, σ) triple interpreted as trigger direction v, write direction u, and strength σ. Identity is defined directly by the weight rule (the singular vectors/values of the actual weight matrices). They then design a pre-registered evaluation suite that scores mounts based on full-write energy lift (how much of the write energy they can reproduce/steer) instead of per-tile metrics, and apply this across all seven kinds of linear maps (e.g., MLP and attention projections) in Gemma-2-2B on a subsampled WikiText-2 corpus.

Result: On Gemma-2-2B evaluated on a 16,384-token subset of WikiText-2, they successfully score all seven linear map types. Residual write maps (mlp.down and attn.o) achieve the strongest outcome: full A/B/C evaluation including steering after post-sublayer RMSNorm and passing all 52 of 52 evaluated site-layers. The remaining maps (mlp.gate, attn.q, attn.k, effective mlp.up, and attn.v) achieve A/B-level scores and pass 26 of 26 site-layers each. Aggregated across all tested sites and layers, they report 182/182 successful evaluations (“GO”). They also provide open-source support: a library, corpus builder, experiment entrypoint, and unit tests.

Conclusion: Directly factorizing linear weights into “mechanism mounts” via column-tiled SVD yields interpretable, weight-native mechanisms that can be robustly evaluated across all linear components of a language model using a strong energy-based metric. This suggests that useful mechanistic structure can be recovered without training proxy dictionaries and that their framework is practical and reproducible, as evidenced by comprehensive pass rates and released tooling.

Abstract: The dominant approach to mechanistic interpretability trains proxy dictionaries such as sparse autoencoders and labels features from max-activating text. The best such atlases identify con- cepts, but that identity lives in the learned dictionary rather than in the network weights them- selves. We propose extracting mechanism mounts directly from linear sites by column-tiled SVD: each mount is a triple (v,u,σ) read as trigger, write, and strength. Identity is the weight rule. We evaluate mounts with a pre-registered suite judged on full-write energy lift rather than tile-local lift. On Gemma-2-2B with WikiText-2 (16,384-token subsample), all seven linear maps are scored: residual writes (mlp.down, attn.o) receive full A/B/C with steer after post-sublayer RMSNorm and pass 52/52 site-layers; other maps receive A/B only (mlp.gate/attn.q/attn.k/effective mlp.up/attn.v 26/26 each). Aggregate: 182/182 GO. We release library code, the corpus builder, the experiment entrypoint, and unit tests.

</details>


### [100] [FedLBW: A Loss-Based Weighting Strategy for Federated Learning on Non-IID Data in Wireless Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07007)
*Majid Kundroo,Tinku Singh,Taehong Kim*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They propose FedLBW, a federated learning aggregation method that weights client updates using inverse validation loss on a server proxy dataset instead of dataset size, improving accuracy, convergence speed, and robustness to non-IID data and client dropouts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In wireless federated learning, data is typically non-IID and clients may frequently drop out. Standard methods like FedAvg weight client updates by local dataset size, which biases training toward large-data clients and makes the global model sensitive to non-IID distributions, outliers, and dropouts. There is a need for an aggregation scheme that better reflects the quality and reliability of each client’s update rather than just its quantity of data.

Method: They introduce FedLBW (Federated Learning with Loss-Based Weighting). On the server, a small proxy validation dataset is maintained. After each communication round, each client’s uploaded model is evaluated on this proxy set to compute a validation loss. The server then assigns an aggregation weight to each client that is proportional to the inverse of this loss, so lower-loss (better performing) models receive larger weights. These weighted updates are aggregated to form the new global model. The method is experimentally evaluated on FashionMNIST with a CNN, CIFAR-10 with ResNet-18, and CIFAR-100 with ResNet-34, under varying degrees of non-IID data and client dropout, and compared against several FL baselines (FedAvg, FedAvgM, FedProx, FedNova, FedLAW, FedDkw).

Result: Across all tested datasets and settings, FedLBW improves global model performance relative to the baselines, with faster convergence and higher final accuracy. In particularly challenging, highly non-IID CIFAR-10 scenarios, it yields up to 7.6% higher accuracy than the best baseline. The approach also exhibits strong robustness to client dropouts: as dropout probability increases, FedLBW maintains substantially higher accuracy than competing methods.

Conclusion: Using validation-loss–based weights for aggregating client updates in federated learning provides a more reliable reflection of update quality than dataset-size weighting. FedLBW achieves better accuracy, faster convergence, and stronger robustness to non-IID data and client dropouts, making it well-suited for FL deployments in unreliable wireless environments. The findings suggest that incorporating server-side proxy validation and performance-based aggregation is a promising direction for improving practical FL systems.

Abstract: Federated Learning (FL) enables collaborative machine learning (ML) across distributed clients while preserving privacy. However, efficient model convergence in FL remains challenging, especially in wireless networks where non-independent and identically distributed (non-IID) data and frequent client dropouts are common. Traditional FL algorithms, such as FedAvg, rely solely on dataset size to weight client updates. This introduces biases towards clients with larger datasets and makes the process sensitive to non-IID data, outliers, and client dropouts. To address these challenges, we propose Federated Learning with Loss-Based Weighting (FedLBW), a novel aggregation method that assigns each client's update a weight proportional to the inverse of its validation loss, computed using a small proxy dataset on the server, rather than its dataset size. This ensures that lower-loss models exert greater influence during aggregation, prioritizing the most reliable updates and boosting overall performance. Through extensive experiments across multiple datasets, including FashionMNIST (CNN), CIFAR-10 (ResNet-18), and CIFAR-100 (ResNet-34), we demonstrate that FedLBW achieves higher accuracy and faster convergence compared to baseline algorithms such as FedAvg, FedAvgM, FedProx, FedNova, FedLAW and FedDkw, with notable improvements of up to 7.6 % higher accuracy on CIFAR-10 in extreme non-IID cases. Moreover, FedLBW showcases exceptional resilience to increasing dropout probabilities, consistently maintaining significantly higher accuracy even in challenging conditions. These results establish FedLBW as an effective and resilient solution for FL in wireless network environments, offering marked improvements in model accuracy, convergence speed, and robustness to non-IID data and client dropouts.

</details>


### [101] [ReQuant: Fixed-Grid Discrete Refinement for Post-Training Quantization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07019)
*Yongge Ma,Guoan Wang,Feiyu Wang,Yaoming Li,Qian Zhang,Zihan Yan,Yinjun Han,Tong Yang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes ReQuant, a backpropagation-free refinement step that improves already-quantized language models by iteratively updating weights on a fixed grid to reduce reconstruction error, serving as a plug-and-play complement to existing post-training quantization methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing post-training quantization (PTQ) methods for large language models typically treat the initial integer weight assignments as final, missing opportunities to further optimize model quality while keeping the same quantization format. There is a need for a complementary stage that can exploit the existing quantized model as a feasible starting point and refine it without retraining or changing the grid.

Method: ReQuant starts from any existing quantized model and performs an iterative, backpropagation-free refinement of discrete weight assignments on a fixed quantization grid. It revisits the quantized weights, proposing local updates and accepting them only if they strictly reduce the mean squared reconstruction error while staying on the original grid. This acts as a plug-and-play post-processing stage that can be applied after arbitrary PTQ initializers.

Result: Across different model families, bit-widths, and downstream tasks, ReQuant consistently improves the performance of quantized models produced by various PTQ methods. The gains are particularly pronounced for simpler initialization strategies and for lower bit-width quantization. In some settings, applying ReQuant repeatedly to a naive round-to-nearest initializer yields performance comparable to or better than more advanced methods like GPTAQ, under the same quantization format.

Conclusion: ReQuant transforms the traditionally final output of PTQ into an iteratively optimizable discrete solution, enabling systematic refinement of quantized weights without changing the quantization grid or using backpropagation. It is an effective, practical, and complementary stage for upgrading existing PTQ pipelines and can narrow or close the gap between simple and sophisticated quantization schemes.

Abstract: Post-training quantization (PTQ) is widely used to reduce the memory and computational cost of large language models. Existing PTQ methods typically obtain an initial quantized model through heuristic rules or greedy optimization, and once quantization is completed the resulting integer assignments are usually treated as final. This observation motivates a complementary optimization stage within PTQ that keeps quantized weights improvable after an executable quantized model has been produced, while preserving the quantized format. We introduce ReQuant, a backpropagation-free fixed-grid refinement procedure for this stage. Agnostic to the PTQ initializer, ReQuant takes an existing quantized model as a feasible starting point and iteratively revisits its discrete weight assignments on the fixed quantization grid. Accepted updates strictly reduce the mean squared reconstruction error and remain on the original grid. In this way, ReQuant turns the initially fixed PTQ output into an iteratively optimizable discrete solution and serves as a plug-and-play post-processing stage for existing PTQ pipelines. Experiments across diverse model families, bit-widths, and downstream tasks show that ReQuant consistently improves quantized models from heterogeneous PTQ initializers, with especially large gains on simple initializers and lower bit-widths. Notably, ReQuant can refine a simple round-to-nearest initialization across multiple sweeps until it approaches or surpasses GPTAQ under the same quantization format. These results establish ReQuant as a practical complementary stage for further improving existing PTQ pipelines.

</details>


### [102] [ZIPBrain: Can EEG Foundation Models Be Faster, Locally Deployable, but Accurate?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07033)
*Lingwei Li,Yirong Kan,Peng Chen,Xu Cao,Zheng Chen,Yasuhiko Nakashima*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ZIPBrain is a plug-and-play EEG token pooling module that accelerates EEG foundation models by merging redundant low-SNR tokens, cutting inference time by ~30–40% while slightly improving accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: EEG foundation models provide strong general-purpose representations but are expensive to run because Transformer computation scales quadratically with sequence length. This high cost blocks deployment in resource-constrained and real-time settings like bedside clinical monitoring. Because EEG has low signal-to-noise ratio, many time tokens are likely redundant and could be compressed without hurting performance. The paper is motivated by the need to exploit this redundancy to make EEG foundation models faster and deployable on local/edge hardware without retraining them.

Method: The authors introduce ZIPBrain, a redundancy-aware token pooling module for EEG Transformers. Given a sequence of EEG tokens, ZIPBrain partitions tokens into (1) unique tokens and (2) redundant tokens deemed similar to some unique token. Each redundant token is then merged into its most similar token in the unique set, reducing the effective token count. The design exploits EEG’s low-SNR nature: many tokens carry overlapping or mostly noisy information, so merging them should preserve signal while cutting sequence length. ZIPBrain is training-free and plug-and-play: it is inserted into standard Transformer encoders without changing their parameters and adds only negligible computational overhead beyond the pooling itself.

Result: Across multiple EEG foundation models and datasets, integrating ZIPBrain yields consistent gains: an average performance improvement of 1.3%–10.5% over strong baselines (including other pooling or compression schemes) while significantly accelerating inference. Wall-clock inference time is reduced by about 32.7% on average, and up to 41.8% when combined with CUDA Graph optimizations, relative to the original, uncompressed EEG foundation models.

Conclusion: ZIPBrain demonstrates that exploiting redundancy in low-SNR EEG sequences can both speed up and slightly improve EEG foundation models without retraining. As a simple, training-free, plug-and-play token pooling module, it offers a practical path toward real-time and resource-efficient deployment of large EEG Transformers, suggesting that redundancy-aware token compression is a powerful tool for scaling foundation models to clinical and edge scenarios.

Abstract: This work investigates whether Electroencephalograph (EEG) foundation models (EFMs) can be made faster and locally deployable without sacrificing accuracy. EEG foundation models are a major trend, offering strong general-purpose representations. However, their computational burden grows quadratically with input length, hindering deployment on resource-constrained scenario, particularly for real-time clinical monitoring. EEG's low SNR further suggests many of these tokens are redundant and compressible with little accuracy cost. We propose ZIPBrain, a novel redundancy-aware EEG token pooling module that leverages this low-SNR characteristic to reduce token count. Given a token sequence, ZIPBrain partitions tokens into redundant and unique groups, then merges each redundant token with its most similar counterpart in the unique group. Furthermore, ZIPBrain serves as a training-free, plug-and-play module that seamlessly integrates into standard Transformer encoders with negligible computational overhead. Extensive experiments across multiple EEG foundation models show ZIPBrain's strong versatility, achieving 1.3%-10.5% average improvement over baselines, while reducing wall-clock inference time by 32.7% (up to 41.8% with CUDA Graph) compared to the original EEG foundation models.

</details>


### [103] [Not All Problems Are Best Modeled as MILP: A DSL-Centric Framework for Flexible and Accurate Optimization Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07040)
*Shaofeng Zhang,Hongyuan Su,Qingwen Peng,Zefang Zong,Shengcai Liu,Ke Tang,Yong Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: OptiDSL is a DSL-based, LLM-powered framework that formulates combinatorial optimization problems in domain-specific representations instead of forcing everything into MILP, leading to more accurate and faster modeling across many COP types.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing LLM-based optimization-modeling systems mostly force all combinatorial optimization problems into the mixed-integer linear programming (MILP) format. Many problems are not naturally linear, so encoding them as MILP can require complex, unnatural constraints that are hard for LLMs to generate correctly and that limit which solvers can be used. There is a need for a more flexible approach that better reflects domain practice and allows leveraging a wider variety of specialized solvers.

Method: The authors design OptiDSL, a framework where LLMs translate natural-language problem descriptions into domain-specific language (DSL) representations that match established, solver-agnostic structures for each COP family. This decouples the modeling layer from the solver layer. Once in DSL form, problems can be routed to a library of specialized solvers, including classical heuristics and learning-based methods, instead of being restricted to MILP solvers. They evaluate OptiDSL on a benchmark covering 44 types of combinatorial optimization problems and compare against existing MILP-focused LLM pipelines.

Result: Across 44 COP types, OptiDSL achieves 51.66% higher formulation accuracy and reduces modeling time by 91.71% relative to MILP-based pipelines. On an existing standard benchmark, it still outperforms MILP-centric approaches, with a 23.09% increase in formulation accuracy.

Conclusion: Modeling combinatorial optimization problems via domain-specific languages, with LLMs mapping natural language to these DSLs, is more effective than forcing everything into MILP. This separation of formulation from execution enables flexible use of diverse solvers and yields substantially better accuracy and efficiency in automatic optimization modeling. OptiDSL demonstrates that DSL-centered representations are a promising direction for LLM-assisted operations research and combinatorial optimization.

Abstract: Solving combinatorial optimization problems (COPs) requires not only efficient algorithms but also carefully crafted formulations. While recent works have leveraged LLMs to automate optimization modeling, current frameworks predominantly rely on a rigid mixed-integer linear programming (MILP) paradigm. In this paper, we argue that not all problems are best modeled as MILP, as forcing complex domains into linear constraints can induce prohibitive modeling complexity and severely restrict solver flexibility. To address this, we propose OptiDSL, a framework that shifts the focus from rigid MILP formulations to domain-specific language (DSL) representations. By utilizing LLMs to map natural language onto standardized, domain-accepted structures, OptiDSL decouples problem formulation from execution. This paradigm enables seamless integration with a diverse library of specialized solvers, ranging from traditional heuristics to modern learning-based methods. Experimental results on the comprehensive benchmark of 44 COP types show that OptiDSL significantly surpasses MILP-based pipelines, yielding a 51.66% gain in formulation accuracy and a 91.71% decrease in modeling time. Notably, it also outperforms MILP-based pipelines on the existing benchmark, achieving a 23.09% higher formulation accuracy. Our code is available at https://anonymous.4open.science/r/OptiDSL.

</details>


### [104] [DocMemo: Dynamic Evidence Discovery via Probabilistic Memory-Guided Retrieval for Multi-Modal Document Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07067)
*Hanshu Yao,Janfeng Zhong,Niu Lian,Jinpeng Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DocMemo is a memory-guided framework for long-document understanding that dynamically explores and updates evidence across pages instead of relying on static retrieval.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing long-document QA systems either use single-round retrieval with a fixed top-k page set, which makes them brittle to early retrieval errors, or iterative retrieval methods that lack a principled way to propagate and update cross-round states and page relevance, limiting their ability to track evolving evidence needs.

Method: DocMemo introduces a tri-level retrieval state composed of: (1) Document Schema Memory to store structural priors of the document, (2) Page Belief Memory to maintain and update probabilistic relevance estimates over pages, and (3) Question Episodic Memory to capture the query-specific reasoning trajectory. It conducts dynamic evidence exploration by: (a) Bayesian updating of page beliefs with Thompson sampling for page selection, (b) spatial proximity propagation to spread relevance to neighboring pages or regions, and (c) structure-aware adaptive-granularity access that can zoom between pages and fine-grained visual regions as evidence. Cross-round memory is continuously refined to adjust page selection over multiple reasoning rounds.

Result: Across three long-document benchmarks, DocMemo attains state-of-the-art performance, demonstrating superior accuracy over existing static and iterative retrieval baselines and showing that both the structured tri-level memory and dynamic belief updating significantly contribute to gains.

Conclusion: Dynamic memory-guided evidence exploration with structured tri-level retrieval states and Bayesian page belief updating effectively improves long-document reasoning, overcoming the brittleness of static retrieval and unprincipled iterative methods; enriching page-level evidence with fine-grained visual regions further enhances understanding of complex long documents.

Abstract: Long-document understanding requires locating sparse and heterogeneous evidence across hundreds of pages, yet existing systems remain limited by static retrieval and fragile cross-round memory. Mainstream single-round methods commit to a fixed top-$k$ page set at the outset and struggle to recover from early retrieval errors; recent iterative approaches allow multi-round evidence acquisition, but they do not investigate the propagation mechanism of cross-round states, making it difficult to track the dynamic changes in page relevance. To address these limitations, we propose DocMemo, a memory-guided framework that formulates long-document reasoning as dynamic evidence exploration. DocMemo maintains a tri-level retrieval state consisting of Document Schema Memory, Page Belief Memory, and Question Episodic Memory, which respectively capture structural priors, dynamic relevance estimation, and query-specific reasoning trajectories. During reasoning, DocMemo continuously refines cross-round page selection through Bayesian page belief updating with Thompson sampling, spatial proximity propagation, and structure-aware adaptive-granularity evidence access, while supplementing page-level evidence with fine-grained visual regions. Experiments on 3 benchmarks show that DocMemo achieves state-of-the-art performance and validate the efficacy of structured memory and dynamic page belief updating. Code is available at https://github.com/Harrygof/DocMemo.

</details>


### [105] [Unsupervised Adaptation of PDE Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07053)
*Ziye Song,Zhao Wei,Xin Yu,Ivor Tsang,Yueming Lyu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: They propose an unsupervised finetuning method for pretrained PDE foundation models that uses only the governing equations and boundary conditions—no solution data—achieving performance on par with supervised finetuning and better than existing neural operators on diverse PDEs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Pretrained PDE foundation models can in principle generalize across different PDE systems, but adapting them to new, unseen PDEs typically needs dense ground-truth solution data, which is often expensive, hard to get, or entirely unavailable in realistic scientific settings. There is a need for an adaptation method that uses only known physics (PDEs and boundary conditions) while retaining or improving performance over data-hungry supervised finetuning and current neural operator baselines.

Method: 1) Pretrain a neighborhood attention Transformer on a wide variety of time-dependent PDEs defined at different spatial scales to learn transferable latent representations. 2) For a new, unseen PDE, define a physics-based loss using the PDE residual (how well the predicted solution satisfies the equation) plus boundary condition penalties, instead of solution labels. 3) Finetune the pretrained model using low-rank adaptation (LoRA) to make adaptation parameter-efficient. 4) Introduce NSLoRA, a variant of LoRA that uses Newton–Schulz-based orthogonalization to rebalance and decouple the low-rank adaptation across different physical quantities, mitigating uneven learning in standard LoRA.

Result: The unsupervised, physics-based finetuning with NSLoRA matches the performance of supervised LoRA that relies on ground-truth solutions, while surpassing strong neural operator methods and recent PDE foundation models on challenging heterogeneous PDE benchmarks in various spatial dimensions.

Conclusion: Physics-guided, label-free finetuning of PDE foundation models is feasible and effective. By combining a broadly pretrained neighborhood attention Transformer with a PDE-residual loss and the NSLoRA adaptation scheme, one can adapt to new, heterogeneous PDE systems without any solution data, yet achieve competitive or superior accuracy compared to supervised approaches and state-of-the-art neural operators.

Abstract: Pretrained partial differential equation (PDE) foundation models can generalize across different equations, but adapting them to unseen PDE systems typically requires dense solution data, which is often expensive or unavailable. To address this limitation, we propose an unsupervised PDE-based finetuning framework that eliminates the need for ground-truth solutions. We first pretrain a neighborhood attention Transformer on diverse time-dependent PDEs spanning varying spatial scales, yielding transferable representations across heterogeneous equations. In the adaptation stage, we construct a physics-based objective using the PDE residual and boundary conditions, and finetune the model on unseen equations via low-rank adaptation (LoRA). To address the uneven learning across physical quantities in standard LoRA, we introduce NSLoRA, a Newton-Schulz orthogonalized variant that rebalances adaptation. Our method achieves performance comparable to supervised LoRA finetuning without requiring any ground-truth solutions, while consistently outperforming competitive neural operator baselines and recent PDE foundation models across heterogeneous PDE benchmarks spanning multiple spatial dimensions.

</details>


### [106] [Recipes for Creativity: Iterative Generation and Evaluation in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07243)
*Rens Anderson,Tessa Verhoef,Amirhossein Zohrehvand*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Study tests whether iterative generate-select loops improve LLM creativity for recipe generation and finds evaluator design matters more than number of iterations or sampling temperature.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Generative models are usually judged by one-shot outputs, unlike human creativity which relies on iterative refinement. The authors want to know if mimicking this iterative process can enhance LLM creative performance and what design factors (iterations, temperature, evaluator size) most influence creative quality.

Method: Adapt the FunSearch iterative generation-selection framework to generate recipes for the 2024 Pillsbury Bake-Off. Run two experiments varying (1) number of generate-select iterations, (2) generator temperature, and (3) size of the in-loop selection-scorer model. Evaluate recipe creativity using an LLM-based assessment grounded in the Torrance Tests of Creative Thinking (TTCT) and compare scores to human benchmark recipes.

Result: Iterative generation-selection can yield LLM-generated recipes whose TTCT-based creativity scores are on par with human benchmark recipes. However, simply increasing the number of iterations does not reliably increase creativity. The size of the in-loop evaluator has the strongest impact: using a smaller selection-scorer model produces significantly higher creativity scores across most TTCT dimensions. Temperature adjustments show minimal impact except for some influence on originality.

Conclusion: For subjective creative search tasks like recipe design, the architecture of the evaluator within an iterative generate-select loop is more critical than just adding more search iterations or tuning sampling temperature. Smaller evaluators may be less conservative and thus favor more creative outputs, indicating that evaluator design should be treated as a key, first-order parameter when optimizing LLM-based creative systems.

Abstract: Generative models are often evaluated through singular artifacts, whereas human creativity typically emerges through iterative generation, appraisal, and refinement. This pilot study examines whether iterative search improves LLM creativity by adapting FunSearch to recipe generation for the 2024 Pillsbury Bake-Off and evaluating outputs against human benchmarks using TTCT-based LLM evaluation. Across two experiments, we test iteration count, generator temperature, and in-loop selection-scorer model size. Results show that iterative generation-selection can produce recipes with creativity scores comparable to human benchmarks, but additional iterations alone do not improve creativity. The in-loop evaluator matters most: a smaller selection scorer yields significantly higher scores across most TTCT dimensions, while temperature has limited effects except for originality. These findings suggest that evaluator design is a first-order design variable in subjective creative search.

</details>


### [107] [BONSAI: Evolvability-Guided Tree Search over Skills](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07056)
*Yash Priya Shastri,Anand Eswaran,Adnan Qidwai,Pankaj Thorat,Sachin Joshi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: BONSAI is a new framework for optimizing natural-language skills that steer a frozen LLM by focusing on evolvability (ability to keep improving under mutation) instead of just current score, achieving better held-out accuracy than prior skill optimization methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard skill optimization for frozen agents accepts any text edit that improves a single held-out score, which cannot distinguish between brittle, overfit skill documents and robust, improvable ones. This blindness hinders further improvement and efficient search over skill texts.

Method: BONSAI frames skill optimization as Monte Carlo tree search over natural-language documents, where each node is a skill and each child is a mutated version of its parent. It uses an upper-confidence selection rule whose exploitation term combines a skill's own fitness with the average fitness of its descendants (its mutational neighbourhood), thus estimating evolvability 'for free' from the mean scores beneath a node. The exploration term ensures weaker branches are still explored.

Result: Using a frozen 30B-parameter agent evaluated across three benchmarks, BONSAI increases held-out accuracy by 23.13 points over using no skill at all, and outperforms two budget-matched baselines (GEPA and SkillOpt) by 3.87 and 3.97 points respectively.

Conclusion: Optimizing for evolvability via a Monte Carlo search tree over skill texts allows BONSAI to more effectively explore and exploit regions of document space that continue to yield beneficial mutations, delivering better skills for frozen agents at essentially the same cost as standard accept-if-better optimization loops.

Abstract: A skill is a naturallanguage document that steers a frozen agent whose weights cannot be updated so any capability the agent lacks must be supplied in prose Optimising a skill is therefore optimising text against a score and the standard recipe which keeps any edit that raises a heldout score is blind in a specific way a single score cannot tell a document perched on a narrow overfit spike from one resting on a broad plateau even though only the second can still be improved We introduce BONSAI a novel skilloptimisation framework that steers instead by evolvability the capacity of a region of documentspace to keep producing viable variation under further mutation a property biology treats as separate from present fitness BONSAI grows skills as a MonteCarlo search tree in which every child document is a mutation of its parent and descends it under an upperconfidence selection rule whose exploitation term blends a skills own fitness with the fitness of its mutational neighbourhood Because every child is a mutation the mean score recorded beneath a node estimates that neighbourhoods evolvability at no extra cost so the rule concentrates budget on regions that keep improving while its exploration term keeps a currently weak branch in contention BONSAI ships the single bestscoring document it finds at no cost beyond the acceptifbetter loop it replaces With a frozen 30B agent and averaged over three benchmarks BONSAI lifts heldout accuracy over the skillfree agent by 2313 points and improves on two budgetmatched baselines GEPA and SkillOpt by 387 and 397 points respectively

</details>


### [108] [GeoBenchLLM: A Comprehensive Benchmark for Evaluating LLMs on Geo-Related Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07411)
*Rodrigo Ferreira Rodrigues,Karim Radouane,Jose G Moreno,Lynda Tamine*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces GeoBenchLLM, a benchmark to systematically test how well Large Language Models handle diverse geo-related tasks across space and time.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most prior work tests LLMs on geodata in homogeneous, narrow settings, so we know little about how well they generalize across different geographic tasks, domains, and temporal aspects. There is a need for a unified, diverse benchmark to rigorously evaluate LLMs’ geo-spatial and temporal understanding and to compare different models fairly.

Method: The authors construct GeoBenchLLM by carefully selecting twelve publicly available datasets that cover a wide range of geo-related tasks and domains. They then design a benchmark suite that uses these datasets to probe multiple LLMs on geo-spatial and temporal reasoning capabilities. The models are systematically evaluated under this unified setup, enabling direct performance comparison and analysis of factors such as model size and reasoning ability.

Result: Across the evaluated models, both explicit reasoning capabilities and larger model sizes correlate strongly with better performance on the geo-related tasks in the benchmark. This indicates that advanced reasoning mechanisms and scale significantly improve LLMs’ geo-spatial and temporal understanding.

Conclusion: GeoBenchLLM provides a comprehensive, publicly available benchmark for assessing LLMs on diverse geo-related tasks. The empirical results highlight that reasoning mechanisms and model size are key drivers of performance in this domain, and the benchmark can serve as a standard tool for future research on improving and comparing LLMs for geo-spatial and temporal understanding.

Abstract: In the context of geodata, existing Large Language Models have often been studied in a homogeneous setting, which has considerably limited insights into their generalization capabilities. In this paper, we present \benchName, a comprehensive benchmark for probing LLMs on geo-related tasks. We leverage a careful selection of twelve publicly available datasets from diverse geo-related tasks and domains, and evaluate a set of LLMs on geo-spatial and temporal understanding using our benchmark. Our results show that reasoning and size have a strong impact on overall performance. GeoBenchLLM is publicly available at https://github.com/Rfr2003/GeoBenchLLM.

</details>


### [109] [PTQ4SNN: Membrane-Aware Post-Training Quantization for Spiking Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07066)
*Hui Xie,Tong Shi,Haotong Qin,Aishan Liu,Xiaode Liu,Jinyang Guo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PTQ4SNN is a post-training quantization framework that jointly quantizes both weights and recurrent membrane states in spiking neural networks, achieving around 4-bit precision while preserving accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: While SNNs are efficient and event-driven, practical low-bit deployment is limited because recurrent membrane potentials are usually kept in floating point; directly quantizing them is hard due to channel-wise distribution differences and high sensitivity near firing thresholds. There is a need for a method that can quantize these membrane states together with weights without requiring retraining.

Method: PTQ4SNN introduces (1) a channel-wise Unified Scale Bridge that ties each membrane state scale to the corresponding weight scale via a power-of-two factor, allowing membrane scales to adapt to their distributions and enabling cheap shift-based conversions; and (2) a Mixed-Precision Bit Allocation strategy that assigns 2/4/8-bit precision per membrane channel based on firing activity and quantization sensitivity under an average-bit budget. It is a post-training method that uses a small calibration set and operates on projection-LIF blocks, supporting both convolutional SNNs and spike-driven Transformers, without retraining the backbone.

Result: On multiple tasks including static image and event-based classification and semantic segmentation, PTQ4SNN maintains high model accuracy when weights are quantized to 4 bits and membrane states are quantized to about 4 effective bits, outperforming naive or prior quantization approaches that degrade accuracy more severely.

Conclusion: By making quantization explicitly membrane-aware and leveraging channel-wise scale linking plus mixed-precision allocation, PTQ4SNN enables practical low-bit deployment of diverse SNN architectures with minimal data and no retraining, closing a key gap in efficient SNN inference.

Abstract: Spiking neural networks (SNNs) enable sparse and event-driven computation, but their low-bit deployment remains incomplete because recurrent membrane states are commonly retained in floating point even after weight quantization. Quantizing these states is challenging because their distributions differ across channels and from the preceding weights, while small perturbations near the firing threshold may alter spike decisions and accumulate over time. We propose PTQ4SNN, a membrane-aware post-training quantization framework that jointly quantizes weights and recurrent membrane states using only a small calibration set. First, a channel-wise Unified Scale Bridge constrains the membrane scale as s_mem,c = s_w,c * 2^k_c, adapting to membrane distributions while enabling shift-compatible scale conversion. Second, Mixed-Precision Bit Allocation assigns 2/4/8-bit precision to membrane channels according to firing activity and quantization sensitivity under an average-bit budget. The framework operates on reusable projection-LIF pairs and supports both convolutional SNNs and spike-driven Transformers without backbone retraining. Experiments on static and event-based classification and semantic segmentation show that PTQ4SNN effectively preserves model accuracy under W4 quantization and approximately 4-bit membrane precision.

</details>


### [110] [ResidencyRL: Reinforcement Learning in Simulated Clinical Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07418)
*Valentin Liévin,Samuel Schmidgall,Tim Strother,Alex Bijamov,Akshay Goel,Anil Palepu,Chunjong Park,Vahid Balazadeh,Min Woo Sun,Marius Guerard,Justin Chen,Dave Steiner,Vikram Dhillon,Ibrahim Azar,Akhil Mehta,Nicholas Spetsieris,Shilpan Shah,Maen Abdelrahim,Amit Dahiya,Yun Liu,Katherine Chou,Yossi Matias,Avinatan Hassidim,Dale R. Webster,Quoc V. Le,Raia Hadsell,Joelle Barral,Carey Radebaugh,Aleksandra Faust,Shekoofeh Azizi,Mike Schaekermann,Po-Hsuan Cameron Chen,Tao Tu,David Racz,Lin Yang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ResidencyRL trains clinical AI agents via reinforcement learning on long, simulated doctor–patient dialogues, substantially improving diagnostic and safety performance over the base LLM and generalizing to external benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Although large language models perform well on static medical exams, they are not yet optimized for realistic, multi-step clinical encounters that require iterative history taking, hypothesis refinement, management planning, and safety checks. There is a need for methods that explicitly train models to handle the full sequence of clinical decisions, including under adversarial or misleading conditions, and to reduce common cognitive errors such as premature diagnostic closure.

Method: The authors introduce ResidencyRL, a reinforcement learning framework that trains an AI clinical agent through simulated, multi-turn clinical dialogues (up to 60 dialogue turns and 8 tool calls per case). The policy model interacts with LLM-based simulators that can behave in complex and adversarial ways, mimicking challenging patient and system behavior. A structured reward function guides learning across several dimensions: diagnostic accuracy, quality of management decisions, communication skills, documentation quality, and patient safety (e.g., avoiding missed red flags). The trained policy is then evaluated on held-out adversarial simulations and on external clinical interaction benchmarks (AMIE multi-visit, AgentClinic, CRAFT-MD).

Result: Compared with the underlying base model, the ResidencyRL-trained agent achieves a 7.0% absolute improvement in diagnostic accuracy (88.0% vs. 81.0%) under adversarial simulation conditions and reduces missed red flag events by 31%, indicating better avoidance of premature diagnostic closure. In blinded side-by-side evaluations, expert clinicians prefer the ResidencyRL agent’s performance in 87.6% of cases. The agent’s learned behaviors transfer to unseen datasets: it outperforms the base model across all six clinical axes of the AMIE multi-visit benchmark and shows consistent directional gains on the AgentClinic and CRAFT-MD benchmarks.

Conclusion: Multi-turn reinforcement learning in realistic, simulated clinical dialogues can successfully teach LLM-based agents to perform better sequential clinical decision-making, leading to higher diagnostic accuracy, improved safety, and better communication and documentation compared to the base model. The skills learned appear to generalize to independent benchmarks, suggesting a path toward AI systems that more closely approximate clinical expertise. However, the approach still requires prospective testing and integration within real clinical workflows before it can be considered ready for deployment in practice.

Abstract: In medical education, physicians convert academic knowledge into clinical expertise through residency: years of training across thousands of encounters, with diverse sources of feedback and progressively greater autonomy. Much of clinical reasoning relies on the patient encounter, a dialogue in which a clinician elicits history, refines diagnostic hypotheses, and decides management under uncertainty. While large language models (LLMs) excel on static medical benchmarks, methods to optimize the full sequence of clinical decisions remain underdeveloped. We present ResidencyRL, a reinforcement learning (RL) method for training clinical artificial intelligence (AI) agents through simulated multi-turn clinical encounters (up to 60 dialogue turns and 8 tool calls per trajectory). ResidencyRL pairs the policy agent with LLM simulators capable of complex, adversarial behaviors, training against a structured reward aligned to diagnostic accuracy, management quality, communication, documentation, and safety. On held-out evaluations, the ResidencyRL agent improves diagnostic accuracy by 7.0% under adversarial conditions (88.0% vs. 81.0%) and reduces missed red flag rates by 31%, demonstrating rigorous mitigation of premature closure. Blinded expert clinicians validated these gains, preferring the trained agent in 87.6% of side-by-side comparisons. The procedural competencies transfer to unseen benchmarks: the agent outperforms the base model across all six clinical axes of the AMIE multi-visit benchmark, and shows consistent directional improvements on AgentClinic and CRAFT-MD. Our findings demonstrate that sequential clinical decision-making can be effectively learned through multi-turn RL in simulation, yielding robust, generalizable capabilities, paving the way towards clinical mastery. Prospective validation with real-world workflows remains necessary to establish clinical utility.

</details>


### [111] [PsychoAgent: An Affect-Sensitive Cognitive Architecture for Conflict-Aware Memory in LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07438)
*Mohammad Amanlou,Parham Abed Azad,Farbod Davoodi,Mostafa Masumi,Behnam Bahrak,Abdol-Hossein Vahabie*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces PsychoAgent, a cognitive architecture for LLM agents that separates factual and affective memories and uses a conflict-aware controller to retrieve emotionally salient, conflict-relevant memories, improving conflict handling over standard RAG approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Human cognition retrieves memories not only by topical similarity but also based on emotional significance and unresolved conflicts. Standard LLM retrieval mechanisms (e.g., RAG) typically rely only on semantic similarity, missing important affective and conflict-related information that shapes human-like reasoning and behavior. The authors aim to design an LLM agent architecture that better reflects how people access both factual and affective memories, particularly in situations involving internal conflict.

Method: The authors design PsychoAgent, which separates factual and affective memory stores and uses a conflict-aware executive controller. Affective memories are first filtered by semantic relevance, then re-ranked by a salience measure so that emotionally important but still topically appropriate memories can enter the prompt. They evaluate this architecture in three controlled conflict scenarios, comparing it to two baselines: (1) a semantic-affective retrieval system and (2) standard single-memory RAG. They measure how many conflict-critical memories are retrieved and assess semantic similarity, then have five blinded human raters score 27 outputs, using within-rater standardization and corrected pairwise comparisons. They also provide a three-day qualitative trace of agent behavior to illustrate emergent properties such as persistent affect, memory recombination, and reweighting.

Result: In the controlled conflict scenarios, the full PsychoAgent architecture retrieved a higher proportion of conflict-critical memories (0.933) than both the semantic-affective baseline (0.500) and the single-memory RAG baseline (0.667), with only a small reduction in semantic similarity. Human ratings showed that, after within-rater standardization, the full architecture achieved the highest average score (+0.22 standard deviations), but the pairwise differences did not reach statistical significance after correction. The qualitative three-day trace demonstrates the system’s ability to maintain persistent affect, recombine memories offline, and selectively adjust memory weights over time.

Conclusion: PsychoAgent demonstrates that explicitly modeling affect-sensitive retrieval and conflict-aware control in LLM agents can improve access to conflict-relevant memories without greatly sacrificing semantic fit. Although quantitative human-rating differences are not yet statistically conclusive, the behavioral and retrieval metrics, along with qualitative traces, suggest that separating factual and affective memory and re-ranking by salience is a promising, inspectable mechanism for approximating human-like conflict effects in LLM-based agents.

Abstract: Human-like cognition does not select past experience by topical similarity alone: affective significance and unresolved conflict also shape what becomes accessible. We present PsychoAgent, a cognitive architecture for LLM agents that separates factual and affective memory and integrates both through a conflict-aware executive controller. Affective memories are first filtered by semantic relevance and then re-ranked by salience, preserving topical fit while allowing emotionally important traces to enter the prompt. Across three controlled conflict scenarios, the full architecture retrieved more conflict-critical memories than semantic-affective and single-memory RAG baselines (0.933 vs. 0.500 and 0.667), with a small semantic-similarity cost. Five blinded raters evaluated 27 outputs. After within-rater standardization, the full architecture had the highest overall mean (+0.22 SD), but corrected pairwise differences were not significant. A three-day illustrative trace further shows persistent affect, offline memory recombination, and selective memory reweighting. The findings support affect-sensitive retrieval as an inspectable mechanism for modeling human-like conflict effects in LLM agents.

</details>


### [112] [MemOPD: On-Policy Distillation through Memory State Alignment for Long-Horizon Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07068)
*Zhiyuan Liu,Tinghong Ye,Chenghao Liu,Yizhuo Li,Songfang Huang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces Memory-Aligned On-Policy Distillation (MemOPD), a method to train long-horizon agents with compressed memories by ensuring that teacher supervision is evaluated under the exact same context states in which student actions were taken, leading to large F1 and efficiency gains over PPO baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-horizon agents must deal with ever-growing interaction histories, which harms performance and stability. Compact memory systems address this by compressing and rewriting history, but learning what to retain is usually done with PPO using sparse final rewards, giving weak signal for individual memory updates. On-policy distillation could provide dense learning signals, but existing approaches assume that teacher and student share the same state for each action. Context rewriting in compact memory breaks this assumption, causing actions to be scored in states that differ from those in which they were generated, undermining the validity of supervision. The paper aims to fix this misalignment so that dense teacher feedback can be reliably used with compressed memories.

Method: The authors propose Memory-Aligned On-Policy Distillation (MemOPD). During interaction, MemOPD logs each model invocation’s inputs and sampled outputs, including their original token positions and causal visibility. When the compact memory later rewrites or compresses history, MemOPD can reconstruct the exact original invocation states in which actions were sampled. These reconstructed invocations are then efficiently packed for teacher evaluation. The teacher model provides full-vocabulary supervision at the sampled action positions, while PPO is still used to optimize the final task reward. This combination preserves on-policy-ness in terms of both action provenance and underlying state, even under aggressive memory compression and rewriting.

Result: Experiments show that MemOPD successfully restores state alignment across multiple context updates in compact-memory settings. Using state-aligned teacher scoring improves F1 by 7.0% compared with a matched baseline that scores actions under a flattened persistent history (i.e., without reconstruction). Overall, the MemOPD-3B model achieves up to a 416.2% F1 improvement over a PPO-only baseline. The proposed packing strategy for reconstructed invocations yields up to a 1.63x speedup in actor-side computation during training.

Conclusion: MemOPD resolves the key mismatch between on-policy distillation and context-rewriting compact memories by reconstructing the exact states in which actions were taken and using them for teacher supervision. This preserves the validity of on-policy distillation signals, enabling dense, state-aligned learning signals alongside PPO’s sparse task rewards. The method substantially boosts task F1 scores and improves training efficiency, indicating that careful treatment of memory and state alignment is crucial for training effective long-horizon agents with compressed histories.

Abstract: Long-horizon agents accumulate growing contexts during interaction, impairing performance and stability. Compact memory mitigates this problem by compressing and rewriting the history retained between model invocations. Learning what to retain typically relies on proximal policy optimization (PPO) with final task rewards, but sparse rewards provide little guidance for individual memory updates. This limitation motivates on-policy distillation (OPD), which supplies dense teacher supervision on student rollouts. For such supervision to be valid, the teacher must evaluate each sampled action under the same state in which it was generated. However, the context rewriting performed during memory compression can break this alignment. When sampled responses are retained and re-encoded for later invocations, flattening the interaction into a persistent history may cause the teacher to score the action under a state that the student never visited during rollout. The action therefore remains on-policy by provenance, but not necessarily by state. We therefore propose Memory-Aligned On-Policy Distillation (MemOPD). MemOPD records the inputs and sampled outputs of each model invocation, restores its original token positions and causal visibility, and packs the reconstructed invocations for efficient teacher scoring. The teacher provides full-vocabulary supervision at the sampled action positions, while PPO preserves the final task objective. Experiments verify state alignment across several context updates and show that it improves F1 by 7.0% over persistent-history teacher scoring in a matched control. Overall, MemOPD-3B improves F1 over PPO by up to 416.2%, while packing yields up to a 1.63x speedup in actor computation during training. The code for this work is publicly available at: https://github.com/TPssp/MemOPD.

</details>


### [113] [SkillProx: Self-Evolving Agent Skills via Proximal Textual Gradient Descent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07449)
*Mingxuan Zheng,Yujin Zhou,Chuxue Cao,Boqin Yin,Yuyao Zhang,Jiapeng Sun,Shuaishuai Gong,Sirui Han,Yike Guo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SkillProx is a framework that incrementally improves LLM agent skills by combining closed-loop diagnostic updates with proximal, utility-aware pruning and consolidation of textual skills.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM agents often reuse lightweight textual skills that encode procedural knowledge, but existing methods for refining these skills lack explicit, outcome-based diagnosis and treat deletion as just another text edit rather than a principled way to simplify and consolidate knowledge. This leads to suboptimal skill quality and unnecessary complexity. The paper aims to provide a more principled, optimization-inspired way to evolve skills that accounts for both performance and complexity.

Method: The authors propose SkillProx, inspired by proximal gradient methods with a composite objective of task loss plus skill complexity. In the forward stage, the system iteratively executes tasks, performs explicit diagnosis of failures, applies diagnosis-driven edits to skills, re-runs on the same batch, and rolls back any regressions while feeding measured outcomes into later diagnoses (closed loop). In the backward stage, the refined skill is decomposed into auditable knowledge units; a frozen leave-one-out utility audit estimates each unit’s contribution, and based on this estimate the framework either consolidates, demotes, or removes units via validation-gated decisions, acting like a proximal refinement step that encourages simpler, high-utility skills.

Result: On both in-distribution and out-of-distribution benchmarks, and across multiple backbone LLMs, SkillProx outperforms prior gradient-based skill refinement baselines, yielding on average a 3.0 percentage point accuracy improvement over the strongest baseline. Ablation studies show that removing either the closed-loop diagnosis or the proximal refinement components reduces performance, indicating that both parts contribute complementary benefits.

Conclusion: SkillProx offers a structured, optimization-inspired framework for evolving textual skills in LLM agents by tightly coupling diagnosis-driven forward evolution with utility-aware backward refinement. This approach not only improves task performance over state-of-the-art baselines but also leads to more compact and auditable skill representations, demonstrating the value of explicit diagnosis feedback and principled deletion/consolidation mechanisms in skill learning.

Abstract: LLM agents increasingly adapt to recurring tasks by accumulating procedural knowledge in skills. These skills are lightweight, reusable textual artifacts that are loaded into the agent's context without weight updates. Recent methods refine skills through iterative task execution, failure diagnosis, and trajectory-guided text-space updates. However, existing frameworks lack explicit diagnosis--outcome feedback and treat deletion as a generic edit operation rather than a dedicated mechanism for consolidating accumulated knowledge. We introduce SkillProx, a proximal-gradient-inspired forward--backward framework that couples closed-loop diagnostic evolution with utility-aware proximal refinement. Motivated by a composite objective balancing task loss and skill complexity, the forward stage re-executes diagnosis-driven edits on the same task batch, rolls back regressions, and feeds measured outcomes into subsequent diagnoses. The backward stage decomposes the resulting skill into auditable knowledge units, estimates their contributions using a frozen leave-one-out utility audit, and applies validation-gated consolidation, demotion, or removal. Experiments on in-distribution and out-of-distribution benchmarks across multiple backbone LLMs show that SkillProx improves average accuracy by 3.0 percentage points over the strongest gradient-based baseline. Component ablations demonstrate the complementary effects of closed-loop diagnosis and proximal refinement.

</details>


### [114] [Transformers Struggle to Use Their Emergent World Models: Revisiting the Tower of Hanoi, and the Illusion of Thinking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07077)
*Devin Pereira,Willem Zuidema*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper studies why large reasoning models struggle with a harder Tower of Hanoi variant. It shows both small and large transformers learn an internal geometric world model of the puzzle but large models fail to maintain this representation during multi-step reasoning, causing errors. By reinjecting the correct representation at inference, performance improves, revealing the core issue is representation maintenance, not absence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large reasoning models can handle many reasoning tasks but still fail on certain structured puzzles like the flat-to-flat Tower of Hanoi, especially with more rings. Prior work reported a collapse in performance but did not fully explain why. The authors want to mechanistically understand what internal representations these models learn when solving such tasks, whether they build accurate world models, and where exactly the reasoning pipeline breaks down. This aims to inform how to design better reasoning architectures and interpretability tools.

Method: 1) Train small, in-house Transformer models from scratch on solution traces for the Tower of Hanoi puzzle, including the flat-to-flat variant. Use mechanistic interpretability techniques—linear probes, geometry analysis, and causal interventions—to investigate their internal representations. 2) Apply similar probing and causal analysis to two state-of-the-art large reasoning models (Qwen3.6-27B and DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-32B) while they solve the puzzles via chain-of-thought. Probe for a linearly decodable representation of the Tower of Hanoi state space (the Sierpinski triangle) at different positions in the computation, and perform representation injection at inference time to test causal relevance.

Result: Small Transformers trained on the puzzle develop an emergent, linearly decodable representation of the Tower of Hanoi state space that matches the known Sierpinski triangle structure and is causally involved in correct planning. Large reasoning models also form a near-perfect Sierpinski-like world model representation at the end of the prompt (before extended reasoning), even on harder instances. However, as chain-of-thought planning proceeds, this representation progressively decays, correlating with a sharp drop in solution accuracy once there are more than 3 rings. Injecting the clean, prompt-time representation back into the model’s internal states during inference partially restores performance, confirming that the representation is necessary and helpful but poorly maintained over long reasoning trajectories.

Conclusion: Large reasoning models do construct an accurate, geometric world model of the Tower of Hanoi puzzle, but they fail primarily because they cannot reliably maintain this representation over long chains of reasoning. The apparent collapse in performance on the flat-to-flat variant is therefore not due to a lack of understanding of the puzzle’s structure, but to degradation of the internal world model during multi-step planning. Since performance can be boosted by reintroducing the correct representation, future work should focus on architectural or training changes that preserve and refresh key latent representations throughout reasoning, and on using interpretability tools to diagnose and repair similar failures in other complex tasks.

Abstract: The Tower of Hanoi is a simple planning puzzle that in prior work has proven challenging for large reasoning models (LRMs). Current models solve the standard formulation of the puzzle, but still struggle with the flat-to-flat variant (where initial and goal states are not restricted to have all rings on a single peg). This paper presents an in-depth study of how both small, in-house Transformers and large, third-party LRMs solve this task. To understand the failures mechanistically, we first train small Transformers from scratch on precomputed solution traces. Using a variety of interpretability techniques, we show that these Transformers develop an emergent world model: a linearly decodable, geometrically faithful representation of the puzzle's state space (the Sierpinski triangle), that is causally involved in solving the puzzles. Second, we return to the large LLMs and apply our techniques to two frontier reasoning models, Qwen3.6-27B and DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-32B, that attempt to solve the task through extended chain-of-thought. Surprisingly, we find that both models encode the Sierpinski world model near-perfectly at the end of the prompt, and yet fail at the majority of tasks when there are more than 3 rings. We locate the source of this failure in the decaying representation of the world model. We probe for the representation at different stages during planning, and establish causality by showing that performance can be improved by injecting the prompt-time representation at inference. The failure of the models is thus one of maintenance of the required representations, not their absence, and performance is at least partially recoverable. These results thus reframe the reported collapse in performance from prior work: current Large Reasoning Models build a world model, and then lose it.

</details>


### [115] [MemWM: Memory-Augmented Text-Based World Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07107)
*Yujun Wang,Tao Zhang,Jinhe Bi,Aniri,Wenxuan Ye,Boliang Liu,Sikuan Yan,Shuning Wang,Xuebing Zhou,Sören Pirk,Hinrich Schütze,Yunpu Ma*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces MemWM, a memory-augmented text-based world model that improves prediction fidelity and downstream task success in planning agents by conditioning on a curated world memory and providing policy-side world skills.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard world models, even when fluent, often fail to preserve task-critical facts, respect correct transition rules, or maintain accurate product attributes, which harms downstream planning and decision-making. The authors aim to systematically reduce these factual and structural prediction errors so that agents can plan more reliably in complex, text-based environments.

Method: They design MemWM, a memory-augmented text-based world model that leverages a curated world memory containing transition rules, cached states, and hard-to-predict facts. This memory conditions next-state predictions during imagination. They also introduce Structured State Fidelity (SSF), an evaluation metric that scores predicted states via benchmark-specific factual fields. For downstream control, they keep the policy model frozen and add policy-side world skills: retrieving task-level skills and giving step-wise corrective guidance based on the memory-augmented world model.

Result: Memory-augmented training substantially boosts Structured State Fidelity, with improvements up to 206.3% over standard supervised fine-tuning (SFT). In full planning tasks across ALFWorld, WebShop, and ScienceWorld, agents using MemWM achieve up to 65.4% relative gains in success rates compared to agents using an SFT-trained world model. Sensitivity studies show that performance and efficiency benefits persist across varying memory sizes and action budgets.

Conclusion: Augmenting text-based world models with a curated, retrievable memory of rules and facts significantly improves both the factual fidelity of imagined next states and the downstream success of planning agents. Providing policy-side world skills on top of a frozen policy allows these gains without retraining the policy model, and memory retrieval remains beneficial across different resource constraints.

Abstract: World models are increasingly used to support planning in agents by predicting how environment states evolve in response to agent actions. Yet fluent next-state predictions can still omit task-critical facts, corrupt product attributes, or apply incorrect transition rules. To address such systematic prediction errors, we introduce MemWM, a memory-augmented text-based world model. MemWM uses world memory, a curated memory bank of transition rules, state caches, and hard-to-predict facts, to condition next-state imagination. We evaluate factual state preservation with Structured State Fidelity (SSF), which scores predicted states through benchmark-specific facts and fields. Compared with SFT, memory-augmented training improves SSF by up to 206.3%. In the full planning setting, we keep the policy model frozen and provide policy-side world skill: retrieved task-level skills and step-wise corrective guidance for action selection. Across ALFWorld, WebShop, and ScienceWorld, memory-augmented agents improve downstream success over an SFT-trained world-model agent, with up to a 65.4% relative gain. Sensitivity analyses further show that retrieved memory improves task success and efficiency under different memory and action-budget settings.

</details>


### [116] [How Much, Then Where: Credit-Conserving Action-to-Token Allocation for Multi-Turn Agent Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07118)
*Lichao Ma,Yang Sun,Shuaitao Zhao,Yangyi Fang,Cong Qin,Xiaoliang Fu,Yuhang Tian,Yuchen Wei,Junbo Zhu,Yang Wei,Lu Pan,Jiaye Lin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes FACTOR, a new credit assignment method for multi-turn RL agents that separates action-level and token-level credit and yields better performance on long-horizon, language-based environments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In multi-turn, language-based reinforcement learning, an agent’s actions are long text sequences. Standard policy gradient methods struggle to assign appropriate credit both to whole actions (trajectory-level credit) and to individual tokens within those actions, especially over long horizons. Existing approaches often entangle these two problems, leading to unstable learning, token-length biases, and poor sample efficiency. The authors aim to design a principled credit assignment scheme that robustly handles long-horizon, multi-turn tasks while decoupling action-level and token-level decisions.

Method: The authors introduce FACTOR, a two-stage credit assignment framework. First, they compute per-action credits using checkpoint-calibrated temporal-difference (TD) residuals, constructed such that these per-action credits telescope to the overall trajectory advantage. Second, FACTOR allocates each action’s credit across its realized tokens using feedback-conditioned teacher-student likelihood gaps, which measure how much the fine-tuned policy diverges from a teacher under feedback. They apply per-action normalization so that the average coefficient per action is preserved and token-level signs do not flip. Additionally, they introduce an action-mean reduction that removes the dependence of an action’s scalar surrogate weight on its token length.
