Under the behavior policy and before clipping, the inner action-mean surrogate for an action exactly equals its TD credit.

Result: Across three benchmark environments—ALFWorld, WebShop, and ScienceWorld—FACTOR consistently outperforms strong baselines. Every environment-seed pair shows FACTOR as better than competitors, with especially large gains on the longest-horizon environment, indicating improved long-term credit assignment. Furthermore, the method’s hyperparameters transfer well: the same settings work when scaling to a larger backbone model and even when switching to a different model family, suggesting robustness and ease of use.

Conclusion: FACTOR provides a more principled and effective way to separate and solve action-level and token-level credit assignment for multi-turn RL agents. By basing per-action credit on telescoping TD residuals and distributing credit across tokens via teacher-student likelihood gaps with careful normalization, the approach stabilizes learning and removes token-length bias. Empirical results show consistent and sometimes substantial improvements over baselines in challenging, long-horizon, language-based environments, with robust hyperparameter transfer. TD-based action credit is the primary source of gains, while the token-level hindsight allocation offers additional complementary benefits.

Abstract: Credit assignment in multi-turn agent reinforcement learning operates at two levels: assigning trajectory-level credit to actions and distributing each action's credit across its tokens. In this paper, we introduce FACTOR, which separates these decisions. FACTOR uses checkpoint-calibrated TD residuals to assign per-action credits that telescope to the trajectory advantage, and feedback-conditioned teacher-student likelihood gaps to allocate each credit across the realized action tokens. Per-action normalization preserves the action-average coefficient and prevents token-level sign flips. We pair this construction with an action-mean reduction, removing the implicit dependence of an action's scalar surrogate weight on its token length. At the behavior policy and before clipping, each action's inner action-mean surrogate equals its TD credit. FACTOR consistently improves over competitive baselines across ALFWorld, WebShop, and ScienceWorld, with every environment-seed comparison favoring FACTOR and the largest gains emerging on the longest-horizon environment. The same hyperparameters transfer without retuning to a larger backbone and to a different model family. Ablations identify TD action credit as the dominant driver of the improvement, with hindsight token allocation contributing complementary gains.

</details>


### [117] [DiDPO: Diff-in-Diff Policy Optimization for Coding Agent Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07147)
*Xucong Wang,Zhe Zhao,Liheng Yu,Di Wu,Xiaofeng Cao,Pengkun Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces Diff-in-Diff Policy Optimization (DiDPO), a critic-free reinforcement learning method that performs fine-grained credit assignment for coding agents by analyzing and grouping code diffs, leading to significantly improved performance on long-horizon coding and reasoning tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing RL with verifiable reward (RLVR) for coding agents generally relies on coarse outcome or step-level rewards. Coding edits, however, bundle multiple independent changes into each action, making it hard to know which specific token- or region-level modifications contributed to success or failure. This fine-grained credit assignment problem is under-exploited by current methods, which treat whole responses or steps as atomic, ignoring the internal structure of code diffs.

Method: The authors propose Diff-in-Diff Policy Optimization (DiDPO), a critic-free RL algorithm that builds credit units from code diffs. They decompose multi-turn coding trajectories into thought–action steps and extract code diffs between versions. Each diff is split into sub-diffs according to a "groupability score," which aims to balance semantic coherence (scope) and the potential to form large, consistent groups across trajectories. Highly similar sub-diffs across sampled trajectories are chosen as anchors, grouped into advantage groups, and used to project diff-level advantages back to individual tokens in the model’s responses, enabling fine-grained token-level credit assignment. The approach is implemented in an open-source RL codebase for coding agents, verl-code.

Result: On long-horizon coding and reasoning benchmarks, DiDPO significantly outperforms strong agentic RL baselines. Using Qwen2.5-7B-Coder, DiDPO yields more than a 10% performance gain compared to comparable existing methods and reduces the performance gap between this 7B model and much larger models.

Conclusion: Fine-grained credit assignment grounded directly in code diff structure can substantially improve training of coding agents under RL with verifiable reward. DiDPO offers a principled, critic-free way to map diff-level signals down to token-level advantages, yielding strong empirical gains and moving smaller coding models closer to the performance of larger models. The released verl-code framework can support broader research in RL for coding agents.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning with Verifiable Reward (RLVR) has emerged as a powerful paradigm for training coding agents, where the execution feedback from compilation and tests provides objective verification. However, unlike agent tasks, coding agents face a unique and finer-grained credit assignment challenge: at each step, coding actions simultaneously pack varying changes into different regions of a code version, which makes the contribution of independent change indistinguishable. Existing RLVR methods mostly leverage the outcome reward or step-level reward, which fails to dive into a code diff and makes unique properties of coding actions invisible to training. In this paper, we propose Diff-in-Diff Policy Optimization (DiDPO), a critic-free RL method that constructs fine-grained credit units directly from the structure of code diffs. DiDPO organizes multi-turn coding interactions into multiple thought--action steps and discovers code diffs across sampled trajectories. It then selects anchors by aggregating highly similar sub-diffs split from each whole diff by our ``groupability score'', which provides the splitting schema that optimally balances the semantic scope of anchors and the group mass they may form. Finally these anchors form advantage groups and project the diff-level advantage back to individual response tokens. Experiments on long-horizon coding and reasoning benchmarks show that DiDPO significantly outperforms strong agentic RL baselines. On Qwen2.5-7B-Coder, DiDPO exceeds comparable methods by over 10\% and narrows the gap with far larger models, offering a principled framework for fine-grained credit assignment in coding agent training. We also open-source verl-code, an agentic rl codebase that supports various RL methods and coding benchmarks.

</details>


### [118] [A MARL Centered Reference Architecture for Large Language Model Augmentation in Smart Manufacturing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07148)
*Fouad Bahrpeyma,Dirk Reichelt*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper surveys how large language models (LLMs) can augment cooperative multiagent reinforcement learning (MARL) for modern manufacturing control, proposing a reference architecture (LLM-Augmented Dec-POMDP) that assigns MARL to fast, decentralized control and LLMs to semantic and supervisory roles.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Modern manufacturing environments pose complex control demands: decisions made locally have global effects; the system is only partially observable and nonstationary; controllers must react quickly yet consider long-term consequences; outcomes are delayed and hard to attribute; and dynamics are difficult to model explicitly. Classical control and even standard MARL alone may not fully address these intertwined challenges. At the same time, LLMs bring powerful semantic reasoning and interaction abilities but lack guarantees and real-time reliability. The paper is motivated by the need to systematically understand where and how LLMs can responsibly and effectively augment or interface with MARL-based adaptive control in such demanding settings, especially for safety-critical manufacturing.

Method: The paper takes a survey and architectural design approach. First, it formalizes cooperative multiagent reinforcement learning for manufacturing as a Dec-POMDP with centralized training and decentralized execution. It then constructs a taxonomy of ways to attach LLMs to this MARL core along four dimensions: (1) using LLMs as part of the policy, (2) LLM-assisted reward design, (3) LLM-mediated communication between agents, and (4) LLM-enabled hierarchical planning. For each attachment point, the authors compile and categorize existing literature and implementations. They develop a conditional capability profile that distinguishes between inherent LLM mechanisms, empirically reported performance, formal guarantees, and engineering maturity. Based on this evidence, they perform a deployment readiness analysis for each role. Finally, they synthesize these findings into a three-layer MARL-centered reference architecture and introduce the LLM-Augmented Dec-POMDP notation to concisely describe configurations without changing the underlying decision process theory.

Result: The study finds that different roles for LLMs in manufacturing control exhibit varying levels of readiness and reliability. Conventional MARL remains clearly superior for frequent, structured, decentralized coordination after task-specific training, especially where strict real-time and safety-critical performance is required. LLMs, by contrast, show stronger promise in tasks involving semantic interpretation of instructions and environment descriptions, drafting reward functions, facilitating human interaction and explainability, and providing slower-timescale supervisory or hierarchical planning. The proposed LLM-Augmented Dec-POMDP serves as a compact descriptive notation to capture which of the four LLM attachment points are used in a given system configuration, and the three-layer reference architecture clarifies the division of labor between LLMs and MARL across semantic reasoning, adaptive cooperative control, and assured execution.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that a MARL-centered architecture, selectively augmented by LLMs, is currently the most evidence-backed approach for modern manufacturing control. MARL should form the core for fast, decentralized, and safety-critical decision-making, while LLMs should be employed around this core for high-level semantic reasoning, reward and specification drafting, human-facing interaction, and non-real-time supervisory planning. LLM-only control solutions in manufacturing do not yet demonstrate equivalence to MARL-based approaches for strict real-time, decentralized, safety-critical applications, although the authors emphasize that this is an empirical observation rather than a theoretical impossibility. The LLM-Augmented Dec-POMDP notation and the three-layer reference architecture provide a structured framework for designing, analyzing, and comparing future systems that combine LLMs with MARL in complex industrial settings.

Abstract: Modern manufacturing imposes six coupled demands on adaptive control: local decisions with global consequences, partial observability, nonstationarity, reflex speed response with long horizon effects, delayed and diffuse outcomes, and dynamics that resist explicit modeling. Cooperative multiagent reinforcement learning (MARL), posed as a Dec-POMDP under centralized training with decentralized execution, is a particularly natural formalism for these demands. This paper adopts a MARL centered scope and asks where large language models (LLMs) should augment, interface with, train, or, in the strongest competitive case, replace that coordination core. A taxonomy organizes the literature through four LLM attachment points: policy, reward design, communication between agents, and hierarchical planning. A conditional capability profile separates native mechanism, reported performance, formal guarantee, and engineering maturity, and a deployment readiness analysis identifies the evidence behind each role. These stages yield the principal contribution: a three layer MARL centered reference architecture, grounded in evidence, for semantic reasoning, adaptive cooperative control, and independently assured execution. The LLM-Augmented Dec-POMDP is a descriptive comparative notation for that architecture, recording four attachment choices without introducing a new decision process class or algorithm. Under the reviewed evidence, conventional MARL is better suited to frequent, structured, decentralized coordination after task specific training, whereas LLM components are promising for semantic interpretation, reward drafting, human interaction, and slower supervisory planning. Current LLM only manufacturing controllers do not yet establish equivalence for strict real time, decentralized, safety critical control; this conclusion is bounded by the available evidence and does not assert impossibility.

</details>


### [119] [NiyamAI - An Intent-Bound AI Agent with Cryptographically Verifiable Guardrails using Zero-Knowledge Proofs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07167)
*Aditya Katkar,Om Karkele,Kartik Mandhane,Manisha More,Yash Kashid*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Niyam-AI is a framework that enforces safety for autonomous LLM tool use with cryptographic proofs, using a judge model plus zk-SNARKs to provably validate every tool call.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Autonomous LLM agents that can send emails, query databases, or execute commands are vulnerable to prompt injection, hallucinations, and unsafe tool calls. Existing defenses (prompts, filters, guardrails) typically run on the same machine being attacked and provide no verifiable evidence that safety policies were correctly enforced. There is a need for a safety mechanism whose enforcement can be independently and cryptographically verified.

Method: The authors design Niyam-AI, a framework where at the start of a session, allowed tools and safety constraints are fixed into an 'Intent Contract' and committed via SHA-256. Every subsequent tool call from the agent is intercepted and passed to an isolated Judge model that checks whether the call adheres to the contract. If the call is approved, a zk-SNARK proof is generated using EZKL, attesting that the Judge model approved the action under the given constraints. The tool is only allowed to execute after its proof is successfully verified, allowing external parties to verify compliance without seeing the Judge model’s parameters. They evaluate the Judge classifier on the Agent-SafetyBench dataset and compare it to existing guardrail systems using cross-validation and statistical tests.

Result: On 2,000 real-world scenarios from Agent-SafetyBench, Niyam-AI achieves an F1 score of 88.5% with only a 1.1% false-positive rate, with confidence intervals estimated via bootstrapping. Compared to NeMo Guardrails, Llama Prompt Guard 2, and GPT-OSS-Safeguard, Niyam-AI significantly outperforms all baselines, winning many more discordant cases in McNemar’s tests (e.g., 390 vs 20 against NeMo) with p-values below 0.0001. Proof generation incurs about 2.26 seconds of extra latency per approved action, and proof verification adds about 53 ms.

Conclusion: Niyam-AI offers a safety guardrail for autonomous LLM agents that is both empirically strong and cryptographically provable, by coupling a high-accuracy Judge model with zk-SNARK proof generation and verification for every tool call. This makes policy enforcement auditable by third parties without revealing model internals. However, the reported gains partly reflect that Niyam-AI’s Judge is adapted to the Agent-SafetyBench distribution while baselines are used in a zero-shot manner, a limitation acknowledged by the authors.

Abstract: Giving an AI agent the ability to send emails, query databases, or execute commands is useful--until the agent is tricked into doing something it shouldn't. Prompt injection, hallucinated reasoning, and unsafe tool calls form the primary attack surface for autonomous LLM agents. Existing defenses rely on software checks like system prompts or policy filters running on the same machine the attacker targets, offering no verifiable proof of execution. We introduce Niyam-AI, a framework that makes safety enforcement provable. At session start, permitted tools and constraints are locked into an Intent Contract committed via SHA-256. Every tool call is intercepted and validated by an isolated Judge model; upon passing, a zk-SNARK proof is generated via EZKL.
  The tool executes only after proof verification, allowing third parties to confirm enforcement without accessing Judge model weights. Evaluating Niyam-AI on 2,000 real-world scenarios from Agent-SafetyBench against NeMo Guardrails, Meta's Llama Prompt Guard 2, and OpenAI's GPT-OSS-Safeguard using 5-fold stratified cross-validation yields an F1 score of 88.5% with a 1.1% false-positive rate (bootstrap 95% CI: [85.19%, 91.88%], N=1000). McNemar's exact paired test confirms significant improvement: Niyam-AI wins 390 discordant scenarios against NeMo (vs 20 losses), 115 against Prompt Guard 2 (vs 13), and 384 against GPT-OSS-Safeguard (vs 19) with p < 0.0001 in all cases.
  Proof generation adds 2260.6 +/- 218.4 ms per approved action, while verification takes 53.1 +/- 11.8 ms. Niyam-AI provides a guardrail that is both highly accurate and mathematically verifiable--though this reflects a classifier adapted to Agent-SafetyBench evaluated against zero-shot baselines, a distinction discussed in Section IV.C.

</details>


### [120] [Agent Memory Distillation: Empowering Small LLM Agents with Hierarchical Teacher Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07169)
*Taeil Kim,Kangsan Kim,Sung Ju Hwang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces Agent Memory Distillation (AMD), a training-free method that uses hierarchical memory distilled from a large teacher agent to significantly boost small language-model agents’ tool-use performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Small language-model agents underperform in tool-use tasks because they cannot easily generate enough successful trajectories to learn robust strategies. While memory systems can help, their use for small models is underexplored, especially ways to transfer the rich procedural knowledge of a strong teacher agent into a weaker student without retraining.

Method: The authors propose Agent Memory Distillation (AMD), which extracts structured memories from successful trajectories of a powerful teacher agent (GPT-5-mini) and reuses them for smaller student agents (4B–8B). AMD builds three hierarchical, complementary memory types: (1) Workflow memory that stores high-level task strategies; (2) Subtask memory that offers intermediate, concrete behavioral examples; and (3) Function memory describing tool-calling conventions and common failure modes. Workflow and Subtask memories are proactively injected at task start, while Function memory is retrieved reactively when the student makes tool-calling errors. The framework is training-free and can be plugged into different student models.

Result: On three tool-use benchmarks—AppWorld, BFCL V3, and ToolSandbox—AMD yields substantial accuracy improvements for 4B–8B student models, with average gains of 27.2 percentage points, 11.2 percentage points, and 3.4 percentage points, respectively. It consistently outperforms existing memory-based methods. Ablations and analysis reveal that Subtask memory provides the largest contribution to performance gains, that the effectiveness of the teacher depends on both its own capability and compatibility with the student, and that smaller 4B models benefit the most from AMD.

Conclusion: Agent Memory Distillation is an effective, training-free way to transfer structured procedural knowledge from a large teacher agent to smaller agents via hierarchical memory. By combining workflow, subtask, and function memories with proactive and reactive injection strategies, AMD substantially improves tool-use performance of small language models and outperforms prior memory-based approaches, particularly benefiting the smallest student models.

Abstract: Memory systems have shown promise for improving agent performance, but their potential remains largely unexplored for small language models, which struggle to generate sufficient successful trajectories on their own. We propose Agent Memory Distillation (AMD), a training-free framework that transfers structured knowledge from a large teacher agent to a small student agent through hierarchical memory. AMD constructs three complementary memory types from successful teacher trajectories: Workflow memory encodes task-level strategies, Subtask memory provides concrete behavioral examples at an intermediate granularity, and Function memory captures per-function calling conventions and common pitfalls. Workflow and Subtask memories are injected proactively at the start of each task, while Function memory is retrieved reactively upon tool-calling errors. We evaluate AMD on three tool-use benchmarks using four student models (4B-8B parameters) with GPT-5-mini as the teacher, achieving average accuracy gains of 27.2%p, 11.2%p, and 3.4%p on AppWorld, BFCL V3, and ToolSandbox, while consistently outperforming existing memory-based baselines. Further analysis shows that Subtask memory contributes the largest gains, teacher effectiveness depends on both teacher capability and student compatibility, and 4B-sized students benefit most from AMD.

</details>


### [121] [SetEasy: A Multi-Modal Classroom Engagement Assessment and Seating Optimization Framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07188)
*Zhihao Xie,Hongye Yang,Shien Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SetEasy is a system that uses multimodal sensing and optimization algorithms to rearrange seating in fixed classrooms, significantly boosting student engagement without changing hardware.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Classroom engagement is often limited by fixed seating layouts and generic seating arrangements that ignore individual differences and social dynamics. Changing physical infrastructure is costly and slow. The authors aim to find a data-driven, easily deployable way to improve engagement by optimizing who sits where, using fine-grained engagement measurements rather than subjective impressions.

Method: They collect multimodal data (physiological signals from wristbands, 4K video, environmental sensors) and build a v-Gage model that estimates student engagement across affective, behavioral, cognitive, and overall dimensions, grounded in a revised ISEQ framework. Each week, they use the model to forecast each student’s engagement for the next two weeks and translate these forecasts into a student-seat utility matrix. They then use Google’s CP-SAT constraint programming solver to generate seating plans, subject to constraints on visual access to the teacher/screen and social dynamics (e.g., certain students together/apart). The system is deployed over four weeks in a real class.

Result: During the four-week deployment with 23 students over 331 class sessions, the v-Gage engagement model improved its prediction performance, reducing RMSE from 0.75 to 0.53 across the four engagement dimensions. The optimization-based seating plans increased mean engagement scores from 0.30 to 0.70. More than two-thirds of seats achieved high engagement levels, and characteristic patterns of low activity in the back rows were substantially diminished.

Conclusion: Data-driven seating optimization using existing classroom infrastructure can significantly increase student engagement in fixed seating layouts. The interpretable combination of multimodal engagement assessment and constraint-based optimization yields practical, transferable seating strategies. This "assessment + optimization" paradigm supports culturally responsive and differentiated spatial design, offering a scalable alternative to costly physical reconfiguration amid increasingly standardized classroom environments.

Abstract: SetEasy optimizes classroom engagement in fixed seating grids. It fuses multimodal sensing (wristband physiology, 4K video, environmental data) and trains a v-Gage model grounded in a revised ISEQ. Each week, two-week engagement forecasts are mapped to a student-seat utility matrix, and CP-SAT generates seating plans under visual-access and social-dynamics constraints. In a four-week deployment (23 students, 331 classes), v-Gage converged across affective, behavioral, cognitive, and overall dimensions, cutting RMSE from 0.75 to 0.53. Optimization raised mean engagement from 0.30 to 0.70, with over two-thirds of seats reaching high engagement and back-row low-activity patterns markedly reduced. These results show that, without hardware changes, interpretable, data-driven seating strategies can substantially enhance engagement. The multimodal "assessment + optimization" paradigm offers a transferable, sustainable path to culturally responsive, differentiated spatial design amid global homogenization.

</details>


### [122] [EMAS: Stabilizing Multi-Agent System Evolution through Evidence-Guided Revision](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07196)
*Chao Fei,Qingyi Si,Kaihua Liang,Yanghua Xiao,Panos Kalnis,Hongcheng Guo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes EMAS, a framework that evolves multi-agent system (MAS) prompts and topology over time using task experience, improving accuracy and lowering token cost without changing LLM weights.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing automated MAS design methods typically optimize prompts and agent topologies only once at design time, then keep the system fixed. As the system processes more tasks, its experience is not consolidated into reusable improvements. Moreover, designs focused purely on accuracy can be very token-expensive. There is a need for a method that can continuously and systematically refine an MAS based on its accumulated interaction traces, improving performance and/or efficiency without retraining the underlying LLMs.

Method: EMAS (Evolving Multi-Agent System) turns execution traces from MAS runs into structured diagnoses that each specify what revision is needed and where (e.g., which prompt or topology component). A candidate revision is only proposed when the same diagnosis recurs across multiple samples, indicating a systematic issue. Each proposed revision is then evaluated via paired validation against the current MAS configuration, and it is adopted only if it passes an acceptance criterion aligned with the optimization goal (accuracy or cost). EMAS thus iteratively updates prompts and topology across evolution epochs without modifying LLM parameters.

Result: Across four benchmarks and two LLM backbones (Kimi-K2-6 and Qwen3.6-27B), EMAS achieves the highest task-weighted overall accuracy for both backbones and is best or tied in six out of eight model–benchmark combinations. Within two evolution epochs, it yields relative accuracy improvements of 6.30% on Kimi-K2-6 and 20.10% on Qwen3.6-27B. On the MBPP benchmark with Qwen3.6-27B, EMAS boosts accuracy from 55.09% to 89.12% while simultaneously reducing token usage per task by 62.2%.

Conclusion: EMAS can effectively transform accumulated MAS experience into reusable structural and prompt-level updates, leading to substantial gains in task accuracy and significant reductions in token cost without any modification of underlying LLM parameters. This demonstrates that automated, experience-driven evolution of MAS topologies and prompts is a powerful alternative to static, one-shot MAS design.

Abstract: Many methods for automated multi-agent system design optimize prompts and topologies during an initial design stage and then deploy the resulting system unchanged on subsequent samples. Experience from these samples is rarely consolidated into reusable system updates, while accuracy-oriented designs may incur high token costs. We introduce EMAS (Evolving Multi-Agent System), which uses this experience to revise MAS topology and prompts without updating LLM parameters, either to improve accuracy or to reduce cost. EMAS converts traces into structured diagnoses that specify a revision operation and target. It generates a candidate revision only when the same diagnosis recurs across samples and applies it only if paired validation against the current MAS meets the corresponding acceptance criterion. Across four benchmarks and two LLMs, EMAS attains the highest task-weighted overall accuracy for both backbones and is best or tied in six of eight model--benchmark settings. Within two evolution epochs, EMAS achieves relative gains of 6.30% and 20.10% in task-weighted accuracy on Kimi-K2-6 and Qwen3.6-27B, respectively. On MBPP with Qwen3.6-27B, EMAS raises accuracy from 55.09% to 89.12% while reducing token use per task by 62.2%. These results show that EMAS can turn experience from new samples into reusable updates to MAS topology and prompts.

</details>


### [123] [Authoring and Management of Transparent Research Integrity Assessments of Randomised Clinical Trial Publications Using LLM-assisted Tools and Provenance Knowledge Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07202)
*Milan Markovic,Goutham Indukuri,Somayajulu Sripada,Colby J. Vorland,Jack Wilkinson,Clare Robertson,Mark Bolland,Andrew Grey,Miriam Brazzelli,Alison Avenell*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLM-based tool INSPECT-AI helps assess research integrity of RCTs for systematic reviews, using community frameworks and producing a provenance-aware knowledge graph of expert assessments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Research integrity of RCTs underpinning clinical guidelines is critical, but current assessment is manual, complex, and inconsistent between reviewers. There is a need for scalable, standardised, and well-documented integrity assessment support.

Method: The authors design INSPECT-AI, an interactive large language model-based assistant that guides human reviewers through research integrity checks based on the INSPECT-SR framework, and records assessment provenance using the RIPE-O ontology. They also use INSPECT-AI with experts to produce and encode integrity assessments as a knowledge graph (RIPE-KG).

Result: INSPECT-AI is implemented and used by experts to conduct 140 research integrity assessments across 95 RCT publications; these assessments are structured and stored using the RIPE-O ontology, forming the RIPE-KG knowledge graph resource.

Conclusion: LLMs can assist in standardising and documenting research integrity assessments of RCTs in systematic reviews; INSPECT-AI plus RIPE-O/RIPE-KG provide an initial, structured resource and workflow that can support more transparent, reproducible, and scalable integrity evaluation in evidence-based medicine.

Abstract: Systematic reviews of Randomised Controlled Trials (RCTs) are routinely used as evidence for clinical care guidelines. Such evidence has to meet high research integrity standards to prevent low quality or false research outputs influencing the clinical care. However, assessing research integrity of published RCTs is a complex process requiring manual effort, and potentially resulting in diverse opinions of the human assessors. This paper describes INSPECT-AI, an LLM-based interactive tool that assists human reviewers with research integrity assessments of published RCTs based on the community approved INSPECT-SR framework, and the Research Integrity Provenance and Evidence ontology (RIPE-O) for documenting the provenance of the assessment process. In addition, we present the Research Integrity Provenance and Evidence knowledge graph (RIPE-KG), an initial set of 140 expert research integrity assessments of 95 RCT publications generated by INSPECT-AI and described using RIPE-O.

</details>


### [124] [Beyond the Black Box: Interpretable Models of Human Randomisation Failures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07220)
*Ngoc Linh Dao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper examines whether transparent, interpretable models can match the predictive power of black-box sequence models (like LSTMs) in forecasting human play in a zero-sum game, finding that simple repeat/avoid patterns based on one’s own recent actions explain most predictable behavior.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Although mixed-strategy equilibrium implies that players should randomize independently over time, human players systematically deviate from this, creating predictable patterns. Prior work showed that black-box models (LSTMs) can exploit these patterns in O’Neill’s zero-sum card game, but these models lack interpretability. The authors want to know if similarly strong predictive performance can be achieved with transparent models that reveal the underlying behavioral regularities and thus provide economic/psychological insight rather than just prediction.

Method: Using a large dataset of 84,060 decisions from 2,802 player pairs in O’Neill’s zero-sum card game, the paper: (1) benchmarks simple naive and behavioral models against interpretable ML models and deep learning models; (2) evaluates previously proposed modified Experience-Weighted Attraction (EWA) specifications relative to these benchmarks; and (3) uses LASSO-based diagnostics to identify which features matter and to motivate a nested frequency tracking extension to EWA that explicitly tracks opponents’ play frequencies. The comparison focuses on out-of-sample predictive performance and interpretability of the drivers of predictability.

Result: Interpretable models can capture most of the predictive power that black-box sequence models achieve in this setting. The primary source of predictable, strategically exploitable structure is repeat-or-avoid patterns driven by how players manage their own recent action histories (self-history dependence), rather than complex beliefs or detailed tracking of opponents’ frequencies. The additional nested frequency tracking extension provides only marginal gains and adds little to out-of-sample prediction relative to models that already incorporate simple repeat/avoid dynamics.

Conclusion: The main exploitable structure in human play in O’Neill’s zero-sum card game arises from simple, transparent regularities: players’ tendencies to repeat or avoid their own recent actions. These patterns suffice for interpretable models to rival black-box predictors, while more elaborate frequency-tracking components contribute little extra predictive value. This suggests that systematic deviations from mixed-strategy equilibrium are largely driven by basic action-history heuristics rather than sophisticated statistical tracking of opponents, and that behavioral game theory can rely on relatively simple, interpretable models without sacrificing much predictive accuracy.

Abstract: Mixed strategy equilibrium predicts i.i.d play: past actions should not help predict future decisions. Human players, however, systematically depart from this benchmark, and in O'Neill's zero sum card game, these departures can be predicted by black box sequence models such as LSTMs. This paper asks whether that predictive power can be achieved by transparent alternatives that also reveal the behavioural structure behind it. Using 84,060 decisions from 2,802 pairs, the analysis first benchmarks naive and behavioral models against interpretable machine learning and deep learning models, then evaluates the modified EWA specifications of prior work against these benchmarks and uses the LASSO diagnostics to motivate a further nested frequency tracking extension. The results show that repeat or avoid behavior, especially players' management of their own recent action histories, accounts for most of the interpretable and strategically exploitable signal, while frequency tracking adds little out of sample.

</details>


### [125] [From probability to causality in probabilistic logic programming](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07230)
*Zora Wurm,Kilian Rückschloß,Felix Weitkämper*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper studies when a probabilistic logic program learned from data induces a unique causal order, ensuring well-defined intervention semantics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In probabilistic logic programming, learned models typically use only probabilistic information. A single probability distribution can correspond to multiple causal orders, which makes interventional (causal) reasoning ambiguous. The authors want to know under what conditions the observed probabilistic information is sufficient to fix a unique causal order, so that interventions are well-defined.

Method: They exploit the known correspondence between acyclic probabilistic logic programs and Bayesian networks. Using this, they derive theoretical conditions under which the probability distribution encoded by such a program uniquely determines a causal ordering. They further refine these conditions by adding constraints induced by the relational structure of the vocabulary, modeling them as causal symmetries that must be respected. Conceptually, they provide a test or verification procedure to check uniqueness of the causal order for a given learned program.

Result: They obtain formal conditions characterizing when the probabilistic information in an acyclic probabilistic logic program implies a unique causal order, even in the presence of relational structure. They incorporate relational constraints via prescribed sets of causal symmetries and show how these restrict the set of compatible causal orders. This yields a concrete criterion for deciding whether the learned program supports unambiguous interventional reasoning.

Conclusion: The paper concludes that, by leveraging the connection to Bayesian networks and incorporating relational symmetry constraints, one can verify whether a learned probabilistic logic program entails a unique causal order. When these conditions hold, the program admits well-defined intervention semantics; when they do not, interventional predictions are inherently ambiguous. The work thus provides a principled way to check the causal soundness of learned probabilistic logic programs.

Abstract: Probabilistic logic programming is a formalism of statistical relational artificial intelligence that supports causal queries, including interventions from outside the system. When the structure of a probabilistic logic program is learned from data, however, only probabilistic information is used, and a single probability distribution may be compatible with several causal orders. This leads to ambiguity in interventional reasoning, raising the question of when the causal order is uniquely determined by the distribution. Exploiting the relationship between acyclic probabilistic logic programs and Bayesian networks, we derive conditions under which the probabilistic information encoded in a program determines a unique causal order. We also incorporate constraints arising from relational structure by taking into account prescribed sets of causal symmetries induced by the underlying relational vocabulary. The result is a method for verifying when a learned probabilistic logic program supports well-defined intervention semantics.

</details>


### [126] [WNM-3D: A World Navigation Model with 3D Scene Conditioning for Closed-Loop VLN](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07267)
*Yuehao Huang,Yunzi Wu,Xiaotao Zhang,Xinhai Li,Jiankun Dong,Jiajun Lv,Chi Zhang,Chenjia Bai,Yong Liu,Xuelong Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces WNM-3D, a geometry-aware generative world navigation model that jointly predicts future egocentric views and actions for vision-language navigation, improving closed-loop navigation and consistency over prior VLM-based and 2D-conditioned methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing VLN systems often fine-tune pretrained vision-language models into policies that directly output actions from language and egocentric observations. While they are semantically strong, they do not explicitly model how future observations should evolve under predicted motions. Generative world-action models can predict both actions and future views, but current continuous VLN WAMs lack 3D, geometry-aware conditioning derived from the agent’s observation history. This limits coherent long-horizon prediction and grounded navigation. The paper aims to incorporate persistent 3D scene context into generative navigation models to improve closed-loop navigation performance and visual-motion consistency.

Method: The authors propose WNM-3D, a generative World Navigation Model for continuous VLN that conditions both future-view and action generation on geometry-aware scene representations. They use a frozen feed-forward geometry encoder to extract 3D-aware features from the monocular egocentric RGB history. A trainable 3D Scene-to-Token Adapter converts these features into a fixed-length token prefix compatible with a world-action Diffusion Transformer. Using block-causal attention, this 3D prefix conditions all future video-action blocks, providing a shared geometric context. Training proceeds in three stages: (1) supervised world-action fine-tuning using A*-generated demonstrations, (2) DAgger-style adaptation on states visited by the learned policy, and (3) DanceGRPO-based closed-loop policy optimization.

Result: On the GN-Bench benchmark, WNM-3D outperforms strong baselines based on pretrained VLM navigation policies and a comparable 2D-conditioned world model in closed-loop navigation tasks. On a fixed near-goal evaluation set, WNM-3D yields higher flow-action consistency—i.e., better alignment between predicted motion and visual flow—and lower visual-motion error, indicating more accurate joint modeling of egocentric view changes and navigation actions.

Conclusion: Incorporating explicit 3D, geometry-aware scene conditioning into generative world navigation models significantly improves both navigation performance and the consistency between predicted actions and visual observations. WNM-3D demonstrates that a persistent 3D scene context, encoded via a geometry encoder and token adapter into a world-action diffusion transformer, can outperform 2D-conditioned and purely VLM-based navigation policies, suggesting a promising direction for future VLN and world model research.

Abstract: Recent vision-language navigation (VLN) systems increasingly adapt pretrained vision-language models (VLMs) into vision-language-action (VLA) policies that map egocentric observations and language instructions directly to navigation actions. Although semantically capable, such action-centric training does not explicitly model how the agent's visual observations should evolve under its predicted motion. Generative world-action models (WAMs) jointly predict future observations and actions, yet existing WAMs for continuous VLN do not condition joint future-view and action generation on geometry-aware representations inferred from the observed history. We present WNM-3D, a generative World Navigation Model with 3D scene conditioning for continuous VLN. To consolidate past observations into persistent scene context, a frozen feed-forward geometry encoder extracts geometry-aware representations from the monocular egocentric RGB history, and a trainable 3D Scene-to-Token Adapter converts them into a fixed-length prefix in the token space of the world-action Diffusion Transformer. Through block-causal attention, this prefix conditions every future video-action block, providing a shared geometric context for both future-view and action generation. We train WNM-3D through supervised world-action fine-tuning on A*-generated demonstrations, DAgger-style adaptation on policy-visited states, and DanceGRPO-based closed-loop policy optimization. Experiments on GN-Bench show that WNM-3D outperforms strong VLM-based navigation policies and its 2D-conditioned counterpart in closed-loop navigation. On a fixed near-goal evaluation set, WNM-3D also achieves higher flow-action consistency and lower visual-motion error.

</details>


### [127] [Winning by Peeking: Unenforced Budgets and Test-Set Selection Inflate Short-Budget AutoML Comparisons](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07303)
*Guilin Zhang,Kai Zhao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper analyzes how misleading short-budget AutoML benchmarks can be, using a case study where a simple system (Orcetra) seemed to outperform FLAML and AutoGluon due to flawed evaluation protocols and unequal compute, and then shows that the advantage disappears under fair, corrected conditions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Short time-budget AutoML comparisons (tens of seconds) are widely reported but easy to misinterpret or implement incorrectly. The authors observed a case where a simple AutoML engine, Orcetra, appeared to decisively beat strong baselines, which motivated a deeper investigation into whether this apparent superiority was real or an artifact of evaluation protocol and resource allocation. They aim to understand and quantify these artifacts, especially selection bias and compute inequality, and provide guidance for making reliable short-budget comparisons.

Method: They perform a detailed case study comparing Orcetra against FLAML and AutoGluon on 513 OpenML datasets. First, they reproduce the original (flawed) setup where Orcetra: (1) scores every candidate directly on the test split and reports the best score; and (2) uses a loose time-budget check that allows candidates to overrun the supposed deadline. They then redesign the protocol to: (a) move model selection to a separate validation split, touching the test set only once; (b) strictly enforce the time deadline from outside the frameworks; and (c) equalize computational resources across methods. They rerun a subset of datasets under this corrected setup and log both the biased and unbiased performance estimands in a single search to decompose the contributions of selection bias and unequal compute. They further analyze how selection bias scales with the number of candidates K and compare the empirical bias to theoretical bounds based on standard-error arguments.

Result: Under the original, flawed protocol, Orcetra appears to win on 57.1% of 513 datasets versus both FLAML and AutoGluon with a nominal 60-second budget, and on 78.4% versus FLAML alone at 30 seconds. These strong margins are traced to two defects: (1) test-set-based model selection that effectively maximizes over many noisy test evaluations; and (2) failure to enforce the time budget during candidate runs, leading Orcetra to consume a median of 120 seconds when nominally given 60, using 2.24× the wall-clock compute of AutoGluon. In the corrected re-run (validation-based selection, strict external deadline, equalized machine share), Orcetra's win rate on the re-run subset drops from 59.4% to 34.3%, and no pairwise performance difference with either FLAML or AutoGluon remains statistically significant. By logging both biased and unbiased estimands in a single run, they attribute about 4.8 percentage points of the original advantage to the selection rule and most of the remaining gap to unequal compute. Their empirical study of selection bias as a function of the number of evaluated candidates K shows that bias increases with K but tops out at roughly 0.27 accuracy points—far below the σ√(2 ln K) upper bound predicted by simple marginal-standard-error theory—because correlated noise from scoring all candidates on the same test rows largely cancels out.

Conclusion: Apparent wins in short-budget AutoML benchmarks can be substantially or entirely driven by protocol flaws and unequal resource usage rather than genuine algorithmic superiority. Evaluating candidates directly on the test set and allowing time-budget overruns can dramatically overstate performance and distort comparisons. When model selection is moved to a validation split, time budgets are tightly enforced, and computational resources are equalized, Orcetra's apparent advantage over FLAML and AutoGluon disappears and no meaningful performance differences remain. Empirically measured selection bias, while growing with the number of candidates, is much smaller than naïve theoretical bounds because of shared-test-set correlations. The paper argues that careful experimental design, transparent reporting, and adherence to a checklist for short-budget comparisons are essential to obtain credible AutoML benchmarks, and provides code and scripts to fully reproduce all results.

Abstract: Comparisons between AutoML systems at short time budgets -- tens of seconds rather than hours -- are common in tool READMEs and workshop papers, and they are easy to get wrong. We report a case study in which a simple AutoML engine, Orcetra, appeared to beat FLAML and AutoGluon on 513 OpenML datasets, winning 57.1% of them at a nominal 60-second budget and 78.4% of datasets against FLAML alone at 30 seconds. Both margins came from protocol defects that a results table cannot show. The search loop scored every candidate on the test split and reported the best, making the headline metric a maximum over dozens of noisy estimates while the baselines selected on training data and touched the test set once; and the budget was checked before launching a candidate but never enforced during one, so the system consumed a median of 120 s against a 60-second budget, 2.24x the wall-clock AutoGluon used. Re-running with selection moved to a validation split, the deadline enforced externally and every framework pinned to an equal share of the machine, Orcetra's win rate on the re-run subset falls from 59.4% to 34.3% and no pairwise difference against either competitor remains significant. Recording both estimands inside a single search lets us attribute the collapse: the selection rule accounts for 4.8 percentage points and unequal compute for most of the rest. The same traces give the selection bias as a function of budget, measured rather than assumed: it grows with $K$ but reaches only 0.27 accuracy points, about five times below the $σ\sqrt{2\ln K}$ bound a marginal-standard-error argument predicts, because candidates scored on shared test rows cancel most of the noise. We close with a checklist for short-budget comparisons. Code, per-dataset results and the scripts that regenerate every number and figure in the paper are released with it.

</details>


### [128] [An End-to-End Agent Auditing Engine](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07346)
*Haoning Wang,Mingxun Zhang,Chenyue Yu,Yingjun Shang,Xia Hu,Guanchu Wang,Na Zou*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper presents A^2E, an end-to-end Agent Auditing Engine that systematically evaluates LLM agent harnesses using standardized traces and multidimensional metrics beyond correctness, revealing diverse performance patterns across model-harness-task combinations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As LLM-based agents are increasingly deployed via diverse harnesses, there is no easy, standardized way to systematically and comprehensively evaluate their capabilities across tasks and harness implementations. Existing evaluations often focus only on correctness, missing important aspects like efficiency, tool use, planning quality, and error recovery. This creates difficulty in comparing harnesses and guiding the co-evolution of models and harnesses.

Method: The authors design A^2E (Agent Auditing Engine), an end-to-end evaluation framework specialized for agent harnesses. They introduce the Agent Task Protocol (ATP) to standardize how evaluation tasks are defined and integrated with different harnesses. A^2E uses an automatically instrumented Monitor to capture and generate standardized execution traces during agent runs. In the Evaluation stage, it applies a suite of multidimensional metrics (e.g., execution efficiency, tool usage behavior, planning quality, error recovery) to characterize harness performance beyond simple task correctness.

Result: Using A^2E, the authors run experiments across various model-harness combinations and task types. The evaluation shows substantial variation in performance depending on the specific combination and task category. No single model-harness pair dominates across all tasks. The multidimensional metrics successfully highlight nuanced differences, such as efficiency, planning strategies, and robustness, that correctness-only evaluation would miss.

Conclusion: A^2E provides a practical, extensible solution for systematic, end-to-end evaluation of LLM agent harnesses. Its standardized protocol and trace-based multidimensional metrics enable more fine-grained and realistic assessment of harness capabilities. The observed performance diversity across model-harness-task combinations underscores the need for such comprehensive evaluation and offers insights that can guide the joint development and tuning of both models and harnesses. The framework is released as open-source code for community use and extension.

Abstract: With the rapid advancement of large language models (LLMs), harnesses have become essential infrastructure for deploying agents across a wide range of domains. The fast-evolving harness ecosystem has also made rigorous capability evaluation increasingly important. However, efficiently building an end-to-end, systematic, and comprehensive evaluation pipeline remains a significant challenge. To address this challenge, we introduce $A^2E$ (Agent Auditing Engine), an end-to-end evaluation engine designed for agent harnesses. $A^2E$ leverages our newly proposed Agent Task Protocol (ATP) to enable the rapid integration of evaluation tasks with different harnesses. Through an automatically instrumented Monitor, it captures and generates standardized execution traces during experiments. In the Evaluation stage, $A^2E$ systematically assesses harness capabilities using a suite of multidimensional metrics. Compared with correctness alone, these metrics provide a more fine-grained characterization of differences among harnesses in execution efficiency, tool use, task planning, and error recovery. Experiments conducted with $A^2E$ further reveal that model-harness combinations exhibit substantial performance variation across different types of tasks, and that no single combination consistently outperforms all others across every task. These findings not only demonstrate the necessity of systematic evaluation but also provide useful guidance for the co-evolving of models and harnesses. Our code is available at https://github.com/datamllab/A2E.

</details>


### [129] [QFCQT: A Chaotically Gated Quantformer Framework for Volatile Time-Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07363)
*Junkai Lin,Siqi Hou,Raymond Lee*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: QFCQT is a Transformer-based forecaster that enhances robustness to volatile, non-stationary time series by introducing a chaotically gated oscillator-based activation module and multi-oscillator superposition, achieving better performance than strong baselines on standard benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional Transformer-based time series forecasters handle long-range dependencies well but struggle with non-stationary behaviors such as abrupt regime shifts, local volatility spikes, nonlinear oscillations, and structural breaks because their feed-forward layers use smooth, static activation functions (e.g., ReLU, GELU). These activations are not sufficiently sensitive to sudden changes and complex oscillatory patterns observed in real-world non-stationary series like financial data. The authors are motivated to design a model that can capture both long-term temporal dependencies and highly nonlinear, regime-dependent local dynamics, using ideas inspired by quantitative Transformers and nonlinear oscillatory systems.

Method: The authors propose QFCQT (Quantum-Fractal-inspired Chaotically Gated Quantformer), a Transformer-based architecture tailored for complex non-stationary time series. It has three main components: (1) A Quantformer-style numerical encoder that embeds multivariate inputs directly via linear embedding, avoiding heavy categorical or positional encoding and focusing on numerical feature processing. (2) A learnable Lee-oscillator activation module, which replaces conventional static activations in the feed-forward blocks. This module takes scalar pre-activations, maps them through parameterized nonlinear oscillator dynamics to generate oscillatory responses, and then summarizes these via Max-over-Time pooling, making the activation sensitive to local dynamics and volatility. (3) A smooth-chaotic gated fusion mechanism that combines outputs from standard smooth activations (e.g., GELU-like) and oscillator-based chaotic responses, with learnable gates that adaptively weight the two types of signals depending on the local regime. Instead of relying on a single oscillator, QFCQT uses a soft superposition of eight parameterized Lee oscillator families, each capturing different nonlinear response patterns. The model learns mixture weights over these oscillators, enabling flexible adaptation across different regimes and time scales.

Result: On three benchmark datasets—ETTh1, ETTh2 (typical long-horizon energy/temperature time series), and an A-share Stock Index dataset (financial data with strong non-stationarity and volatility)—QFCQT consistently outperforms a set of strong baseline models: Informer, LogTrans, LSTMa, HAT, and COTN. The performance gains suggest that the oscillator-based activations and smooth-chaotic gating improve robustness and accuracy in forecasting under complex, volatile dynamics. While exact metrics are not provided in the abstract, the wording implies statistically significant and consistent improvements across horizons and datasets.

Conclusion: The study concludes that standard smooth activation functions in Transformer feed-forward layers are a key limitation for modeling non-stationary, volatility-prone time series. By introducing an oscillator-based activation module, multi-oscillator superposition, and a smooth-chaotic gated fusion mechanism within a Quantformer-style architecture, QFCQT more effectively captures abrupt regime changes, nonlinear oscillations, and multi-scale dynamics. The empirical results on both benchmark and financial datasets indicate that this design leads to more accurate and robust forecasts than state-of-the-art baselines, highlighting the value of chaos- and oscillator-inspired nonlinearities for time series forecasting. The "quantum-fractal-inspired" aspect is emphasized as a computational analogy rather than a strict theoretical derivation, grounding the work in practical modeling improvements rather than speculative physics or fractal theory.

Abstract: Forecasting non-stationary time series remains difficult due to long-range dependencies, local volatility bursts, structural shifts, and nonlinear oscillatory behaviors. Although Transformer-based forecasters are effective for modeling long-term temporal dependencies, their feed-forward blocks typically rely on smooth static activations that are insufficiently sensitive to abrupt regime changes. Motivated by quantitative Transformer designs and oscillator-based nonlinear activations, we propose QFCQT, short for Quantum-Fractal-inspired Chaotically Gated Quantformer, for robust forecasting under complex volatile dynamics. Here, "quantum-fractal-inspired" denotes a computational analogy based on soft oscillator superposition and multi-scale nonlinear responses, rather than a formal quantum-mechanical or fractal-theoretic derivation. QFCQT consists of three main components: (1) a Quantformer-style numerical encoder that directly processes multivariate inputs via linear embedding; (2) a learnable Lee-oscillator activation module that maps scalar pre-activations to dynamic oscillatory responses and summarizes them through Max-over-Time pooling; and (3) a smooth-chaotic gated fusion mechanism that adaptively balances conventional smooth activations and chaos-sensitive responses. Furthermore, instead of using a single fixed oscillator, QFCQT employs a soft superposition of eight parameterized Lee oscillator families to adaptively capture different nonlinear response patterns across regimes. Experiments on ETTh1, ETTh2, and A-share Stock Index benchmarks show that QFCQT consistently outperforms strong baselines, including Informer, LogTrans, LSTMa, HAT, and COTN.

</details>


### [130] [Curriculum as Code: An AI-Assisted Architecture for Instructional Design in STEM Education](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07364)
*Henrique Mohallem Paiva*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper proposes and validates a six-phase, AI-assisted, code-based workflow for generating accurate, consistent, and reproducible STEM teaching materials, substantially reducing instructor workload while maintaining high pedagogical and visual quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Faculty face high workload when creating customized, active-learning materials, especially in STEM where technical precision and consistent visual design are required. Existing presentation tools do not handle technical content well, and current AI tools often hallucinate and lack a formalized instructional design process, making them unreliable for rigorous academic use.

Method: The authors design a six-phase AI-assisted instructional design architecture grounded in the Curriculum as Code paradigm. The workflow uses text-based interfaces and code-driven generation—LaTeX/Beamer for slides and Python for figures—structured by explicit pedagogical constraints, contextual calibrations, and automated review cycles. It replaces ad-hoc prompt engineering with a systematic pipeline for creating instructional assets.

Result: Over one year, the architecture was applied to 8 modules and 28 project contexts in a project-based learning setting. It significantly reduced instructor workload, produced materials that passed independent peer review, and were successfully used by six different faculty members. More than 600 student evaluations rated the materials very highly (8.5–9.9/10), with observed improvements in reproducibility, reduction of AI hallucinations, and preservation of pedagogical intent and visual identity.

Conclusion: The proposed six-phase Curriculum as Code architecture is a viable and scalable approach for integrating Generative AI into STEM instructional design. It can substantially lower preparation time while maintaining or improving mathematical accuracy, visual consistency, and pedagogical fidelity, and it generalizes beyond a single instructor and course context for broader STEM education applications.

Abstract: Contribution: This paper presents a six-phase AI-assisted instructional design architecture based on the Curriculum as Code paradigm, integrating Generative AI with LaTeX and Python to automate the creation of reproducible, visually consistent, and technically precise materials for STEM education. Background: Creating customized instructional materials for active learning imposes a heavy workload on faculty. Standard presentation tools lack robust support for technical content, while current AI applications often hallucinate and fail to formalize the instructional authoring process, limiting their utility for rigorous academic design. Intended Outcomes: The framework aims to reduce preparation time while ensuring mathematical accuracy, adherence to institutional visual identity, and preservation of the instructor's tacit pedagogical knowledge through explicit rules. Application Design: The solution comprises a six-phase pipeline that replaces ad-hoc prompt engineering with a systematic workflow, utilizing text-based interfaces and code-driven generation (LaTeX/Beamer for slides, Python for figures), governed by pedagogical constraints, contextual calibrations, and automated review cycles. Findings: Validated over one year across 8 modules and 28 project contexts in a Project-Based Learning environment, the architecture significantly reduced instructor workload. Generated assets underwent independent peer review and were deployed by six different faculty members, confirming scalability beyond a single author. Based on over 600 voluntary student evaluations, materials achieved high quality ratings from 8.5 to 9.9/10. Results indicate high reproducibility, minimized hallucinations, and sustained pedagogical and visual fidelity, suggesting viability for broad STEM educational applications.

</details>


### [131] [People Are Not Just Their Countries. Disentangling Social Determinants of LLM Value Alignment Across Europe](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07367)
*Maria-Louisa Wightman,Guillaume Bied,Tijl De Bie*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper studies how well popular commercial LLMs’ stated values align with different human groups, showing that alignment varies substantially across socio-demographic lines and countries.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most prior work on value alignment compares LLMs to aggregate public opinion, usually by country or broad culture. This misses whether some social groups (e.g., by income, education, occupation, religion) are systematically better or worse represented in model value alignment. The authors want to uncover these finer-grained disparities and disentangle the influence of individual socio-demographics from country-level effects.

Method: They use responses from the European Social Survey and compare them to outputs from 10 commercial LLMs on value- and opinion-related questions. They operationalize “value alignment” between a model and human respondents and then analyze how alignment varies across 15 socio-demographic variables (such as education, income, occupation, religion) and by country of residence. They use statistical analyses to measure how much variance in alignment can be explained by country alone versus socio-demographics and to examine their joint and separate contributions.

Result: They find clear disparities: LLMs are not uniformly aligned across the population, but are more aligned with some socio-demographic groups than others, especially along education, income, occupation, and religion lines. Country of residence by itself explains a large share of the variation in alignment—roughly comparable to the explanatory power of all socio-demographic variables together. When both are modeled, country-level and socio-demographic factors each add explanatory power, and their relative importance differs depending on the subset of questions analyzed.

Conclusion: Value alignment of current commercial LLMs is uneven across social groups and countries. Country-level context and socio-demographic divides both matter and complement each other in explaining patterns of alignment. This suggests that evaluating or optimizing alignment only at the national or aggregate level is insufficient; finer-grained, group-sensitive analyses are required to understand and mitigate representation and fairness issues in LLM value alignment.

Abstract: As Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly used as a primary source of information and advice, understanding their alignment to humans in terms of values becomes a pressing concern. A growing literature has leveraged large scale surveys to investigate to what extent LLMs' and humans' stated values and opinions align. With limited exceptions, studied populations have been defined country borders or cultural bounds. Yet, this focus neglects the role that socio-demographic divides may play for value alignment disparities.
  Relying on the European Social Survey, we address this knowledge gap by considering value alignment displayed with respect to 10 prominent commercial LLMs in terms of 15 socio-demographic variables as well as country of residence. Our analyses reveal that LLMs are indeed unequally aligned to the values of different socio-demographic groups, notably those defined by education, income, occupation and religion. When examining alignment at the individual level, a respondent's country, taken as a stand-alone variable, explains a substantial amount of variation that is on par with the full set of considered socio-demographics. Further disentangling the respective role of country-level and socio-demographic factors, we find they are complementary in explaining value alignment patterns, with their relative weights varying across the subset of questions considered.

</details>


### [132] [FinRank: An Evidence-Grounded Benchmark for Financial Question Answering and Retrieval over SEC Filings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07400)
*Sasan Mansouri,Daniel Saad,Mark Wahrenburg,Manu Weissel,Fabian Woebbeking*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: FinRank is a benchmark for financial QA that evaluates whether systems retrieve and rank the correct supporting evidence in SEC filings, not just produce correct-looking answers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In financial QA over SEC filings, models can give plausible and even numerically correct answers grounded in the wrong parts of a filing or from the wrong firm or period due to recurring, similar disclosures. Existing evaluations focus on answer correctness alone and ignore whether the evidence comes from the intended entity, reporting period, and disclosure context. This creates a need for a benchmark that stresses provenance-sensitive retrieval and grounding.

Method: The authors construct FinRank, a benchmark of 1185 manually written question–answer records drawn from 10-K and 10-Q filings of 22 companies. For each record, they provide a reference answer, gold supporting passages, and carefully selected hard negative passages that are highly confusable because they come from similar sections within the same filing, different reporting periods of the same firm, or comparable firms. They design evaluation tasks that separately measure passage retrieval quality, reranking performance, and the ability to discriminate hard negatives. They then benchmark a range of retrieval and embedding models, including BM25, sub-billion-parameter encoders, a finance-adapted embedder, and a 7B instruction-tuned embedder.

Result: On the pooled evidence corpus, the strongest evaluated model—a 7B instruction-tuned embedder—achieves only 44.8% Recall@10, revealing substantial room for improvement. Smaller encoders (sub-billion parameters) improve at most 3.5 Recall@10 points over BM25, while a finance-adapted embedder actually underperforms BM25 by 9.7 points. When random negatives are replaced by curated hard negatives, pairwise accuracy drops significantly by 13.0–20.5 percentage points, underscoring the difficulty of distinguishing correct passages from confusable ones in this domain.

Conclusion: FinRank exposes how challenging provenance-sensitive retrieval is in financial QA and shows that current retrieval and embedding models struggle to find the correct supporting passages among many confusable disclosures. By focusing evaluation on evidence retrieval, reranking, and hard-negative discrimination, FinRank offers an evidence-first benchmark to drive development of financial QA systems that are not only answer-correct but also properly grounded in the intended disclosures in SEC filings.

Abstract: Financial question answering is typically evaluated by answer correctness, yet in SEC filings a plausible and even numerically correct answer can be grounded in the wrong evidence. Similar facts and disclosures recur across sections of a filing, across reporting periods of the same firm, and across comparable firms. FinRank targets this provenance-sensitive retrieval problem by requiring systems to identify evidence for the intended entity, reporting period, and disclosure context. The benchmark contains 1185 manually authored question-answer records over the 10-K and 10-Q filings of 22 companies. Each record includes a reference answer, gold supporting passages, and hand-curated hard negatives drawn from confusable passages within filings, across reporting periods, and across comparable firms. FinRank evaluates passage retrieval, reranking, and hard-negative discrimination as separately measured tasks. Baseline results demonstrate the difficulty of this setting: among the evaluated systems, even a 7B instruction-tuned embedder reaches only 44.8% Recall@10 on the pooled evidence corpus; sub-billion-parameter encoders gain at most 3.5 points over BM25, a finance-adapted embedder trails BM25 by 9.7 points, and pairwise accuracy falls by 13.0-20.5 percentage points when random negatives are replaced with the curated hard negatives. FinRank provides an evidence-first benchmark for developing financial question answering systems that are not only accurate but also grounded in the correct disclosure.

</details>


### [133] [CoBa: Cost-Effective Test-Time Scaling via Compute-Balanced Routing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07424)
*Yan Zhou,Yue Ouyang,Kaiyang Zheng,Suncheng Xiang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper introduces CoBa, a compute-allocation policy for test-time reasoning that optimally distributes a fixed inference budget across generation, verification, and stopping, achieving strong accuracy with much less compute than naive test-time scaling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing test-time scaling methods typically use more compute along a single axis—such as sampling many candidate solutions, extending chains-of-thought, or using very strong evaluators. Under a fixed inference budget, these axes compete, and there is no principled way to decide whether the next unit of computation should go to generating more candidates, verifying them more carefully, or just stopping. The authors want a framework that treats test-time reasoning as an explicit compute-allocation problem so that local reasoning systems can decide where additional computation is most valuable.

Method: They formalize test-time reasoning as a routing problem over three possible actions for each extra unit of compute: generate more candidate solutions, apply verification to existing candidates, or stop. They propose CoBa (compute-balanced routing), a policy that: (1) first generates a small pool of candidate solutions, (2) applies a cheap, broad verification pass to all or many candidates, and then (3) selectively routes uncertain or promising candidates to stronger, more expensive verification. They evaluate this approach across 3,129 example-generator evaluations on several math and symbolic reasoning benchmarks (MATH-500, AIME 2024/2025, AMC 2023, and procedural symbolic tasks), comparing CoBa variants against baselines such as self-evaluation weighted voting, majority voting over many samples, single-sample decoding, and a pool oracle.

Result: The best variant, CoBa-Routed-Strong, achieves 85.13% macro accuracy, which is statistically indistinguishable from a self-evaluation weighted-voting proxy at 85.20%, while using 49.1% fewer parameter-weighted tokens (a measure of compute). It also essentially matches best-of-16 majority voting (within 0.01 macro-accuracy points) while using 58.9% fewer parameter-weighted tokens, though paired tests still show a slight advantage for best-of-16 at much higher compute cost. Paired bootstrap tests confirm significant improvements over single-sample decoding. A remaining performance gap relative to a pool oracle indicates further potential gains from better routing policies.

Conclusion: Treating test-time reasoning as a compute-allocation problem enables more efficient use of limited inference budgets. CoBa’s compute-balanced routing strategy—small candidate set, cheap broad verification, and selective strong verification—delivers accuracy comparable to strong ensemble-style baselines while using roughly half the compute. This demonstrates that, for local reasoning systems, the central question in test-time scaling should be how to allocate the next unit of computation (generation vs. verification vs. stopping), and that intelligent routing can substantially reduce costs while preserving performance, with remaining headroom for even sharper routing algorithms.

Abstract: Test-time scaling is often implemented by spending more compute along one axis: sampling more solutions, extending a chain of thought, or applying a stronger evaluator. Under a fixed inference budget, these choices compete. This paper formulates test-time reasoning as a compute-allocation problem in which a system must decide whether the next unit of compute should be spent on generation, verification, or stopping. We introduce CoBa, a compute-balanced routing policy that first obtains a small set of candidates, applies cheap verification broadly, and routes uncertain or high-value candidates to stronger verification. On 3,129 example-generator evaluations spanning MATH-500, AIME 2024/2025, AMC 2023, and procedural symbolic reasoning, CoBa-Routed-Strong reaches 85.13% macro accuracy, statistically matching a self-evaluation weighted-voting proxy at 85.20% while using 49.1% fewer parameter-weighted tokens. It also matches best-of-16 majority voting within 0.01 macro-accuracy points while using 58.9% fewer parameter-weighted tokens; paired tests retain a small best-of-16 edge at substantially higher cost. Paired bootstrap tests show significant gains over single-sample decoding, while the remaining gap to the pool oracle exposes headroom for sharper routing. For local reasoning systems, test-time scaling becomes a question of where the next computation is most valuable.

</details>


### [134] [A Picture is Worth a Thousand Tokens: How Vision Language Models Cut AI Energy Costs While Improving Accuracy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07427)
*Bhavika Jalli,Nikhil Korati Prasanna,Jayanta Choudhury*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper shows that converting telecom time-series KPI data into images and using vision-language models greatly cuts LLM input tokens and inference energy while improving anomaly detection accuracy, making VLMs a better fit than text-only LLMs for numerical time-series workloads.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM inference energy scales with input tokens and dominates AI operational energy. Telecom and other NTSDA workloads generate long multivariate time-series that explode into thousands of tokens when serialized as text, which is both energy-inefficient and can exceed context limits. The authors aim to find a representation and model class that can handle these workloads within context windows while drastically reducing energy consumption without sacrificing accuracy.

Method: Encode multivariate telecom KPI time-series windows as 2D plots (visual representations) instead of text sequences, then process them using various VLM architectures (Llama-3.2-90B-Vision, Qwen2.5-VL-72B, Pixtral-12B). Fine-tune these VLMs for anomaly detection and benchmark against text-only LLM variants and classical NTSDA models such as LSTM and ARIMA. Measure token counts, context window usage, inference energy, and detection accuracy (precision, F1/J/F1 scores) across real telecom deployments and public datasets.
