Result: Visual encoding of time-series yields 3.6–10.4x fewer input tokens, leading to measured 1.8–2.5x reductions in inference energy and daily savings of about 7.2 MJ in telecom edge/CloudRAN settings monitoring 200 cells per 15 minutes. The fine-tuned Llama-3.2-90B-Vision VLM achieves 220.7% higher precision than its text-only version and >144% better performance than LSTM and ARIMA on anomaly detection. Pixtral-12B attains a 20.6x improvement in J/F1 with mean F1 = 0.82 on public benchmarks. When monitoring 24 KPIs, text encodings exceed typical 128K-token context windows, whereas visual encodings remain under these limits.

Conclusion: Representing numerical time-series as images and processing them with VLMs significantly reduces token count and inference energy while improving or maintaining accuracy compared to text-only LLMs and classical NTSDA models. This modality also avoids context window overflows at realistic KPI dimensionalities, making VLMs a practical, energy-aware solution for telecom network analytics and similar numerical time-series workloads, and supporting a design paradigm where energy is treated as a core constraint in AI inference systems.

Abstract: LLM inference accounts for over 90% of AI operational energy, scaling directly with input token count---a critical inefficiency for telecom network analytics and numerical time-series data analysis (NTSDA), where raw multivariate KPI windows from 4G/5G cell sites expand into thousands of floating-point tokens. Vision-Language Models (VLMs) eliminate this mismatch by encoding time-series as 2D plots, achieving 3.6-10.4x input token reduction across Llama-3.2-90B, Qwen2.5-VL-72B, and Pixtral-12B architectures. This translates to 1.8-2.5x measured inference energy reduction, saving approximately 7.2 MJ/day at telecom edge deployments and CloudRAN that monitor 200 cells per 15-minute interval. Critically, efficiency gains do not sacrifice accuracy: a fine-tuned Llama-3.2-90B-Vision VLM achieves 220.7% higher precision than its text-only counterpart and outperforms LSTM and ARIMA baselines by over 144% on telecom anomaly detection. On public benchmarks, Pixtral-12B achieves a 20.6x improvement in J/F1 score at mean F1 = 0.82. At 24 KPIs, text representations exceed the 128K context window of most production LLMs, rendering text-only processing infeasible without truncation, while visual representations remain within standard limits. These results establish VLMs as an energy-efficient and accuracy-superior modality for numerical time-series workloads, providing empirical grounding for AI inference systems that treat energy consumption as a first-class engineering constraint.

</details>


### [135] [TEPA: Revoking Stale Memories for Conflict-Robust Language Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07429)
*Yan Zhou,Yue Ouyang,Kaiyang Zheng,Suncheng Xiang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper identifies and addresses “memory pollution” in long-term memory systems for language agents, where outdated but still-retrievable memories conflict with new information and degrade performance. It proposes TEPA, a revocable memory mechanism that explicitly tracks validity and supports safe updating, falsification, and auditing of knowledge over time.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Language agents with long-term memory must reuse past facts, user preferences, and task experience. However, when the world or user state changes, old memories can become wrong yet remain retrievable, harming performance. Existing memory systems are typically append-only or use naive overwriting (last-write-wins), which do not robustly handle conflicting evidence, reversals, or the need to later inspect old information. The authors aim to formalize this failure as “memory pollution” and design a principled memory operation that keeps knowledge up to date while retaining history for auditability.

Method: The authors define “memory pollution” as degradation caused by active memories that conflict with newer evidence. They propose TEPA, a memory architecture that (1) stores observations as keyed precedents, where the key identifies the fact or preference dimension, (2) treats “validity” as an explicit state of each precedent, and (3) revokes previously active precedents when new contradictory evidence arrives under the same key. Revoked entries are kept but marked inactive, so retrieval only considers active evidence, while history is still available for inspection. They evaluate TEPA in multiple settings: (a) controlled hidden-regime drift tasks with reversible changes, (b) real file-backed executable drift, and (c) preference-update streams, as well as on MemoryAgentBench benchmarks (including SH-6k and more complex multi-hop/long-context tasks), comparing TEPA to append-only and last-write-wins baselines and to no-memory agents.

Result: In controlled drift experiments over 50 random seeds, both append-only and last-write-wins memories perform worse than having no memory at all during full reversals, with scores of 0.210 for both, compared to 0.309 for no-memory. TEPA, by contrast, maintains high performance (0.950) by revoking superseded evidence. Similar behavior is observed in real file execution drift: append-only memory scores 0.203, no-memory 0.298, and TEPA 0.950. On the clean SH-6k subset of MemoryAgentBench, TEPA matches a strong last-write-wins cache, showing that correctly replacing current-key information is the key operation for consolidating single-hop facts. However, boundary tests on multi-hop reasoning and extremely long-context MemoryAgentBench settings reveal remaining bottlenecks in retrieval chains and context selection that are not solved solely by TEPA’s fact-level validity tracking.

Conclusion: Lifecycle revocation—explicitly revoking previously active memories when contradicted by new evidence under the same key—is a crucial operation for long-term memory in language agents. TEPA demonstrates that treating validity as a first-class state and separating active retrieval from historical audit records can prevent memory pollution and substantially outperform both append-only and naive overwrite strategies, especially under regime drift and reversals. While TEPA solves the core fact-level consolidation problem for single-hop settings, further work is needed to address multi-hop retrieval chains and context-selection challenges in very long contexts. Overall, the paper positions revocable, auditable memory as a foundational capability for agents that must keep knowledge up to date, falsifiable, and re-promotable over time.

Abstract: Long-term memory enables language agents to reuse past facts, preferences, and task experience. Persistence also creates a central falsifiability problem: when the world changes, stale memories can remain retrievable and pollute the prompt. We characterize this failure mode as memory pollution: degradation caused by active memories that newer conflicting evidence has superseded. We introduce TEPA, a revocable evidence-memory mechanism that makes validity an explicit state of memory. TEPA represents observations as keyed precedents and revokes active precedents when fresh evidence contradicts them under the same key, allowing retrieval to draw from current evidence while preserving revoked history for audit. Across controlled hidden-regime drift, real file-backed executable drift, and preference-update streams, revocation prevents stale active memory from remaining in the retrieval set after reversal. In controlled drift over 50 seeds, append-only and last-write-wins memory fell below no memory during full reversal (append-only and last-write-wins both 0.210, no memory 0.309, TEPA 0.950), and the same pattern reproduced under real file execution (append-only 0.203, no memory 0.298, TEPA 0.950). On clean MemoryAgentBench SH-6k, TEPA matches a strong last-write-wins cache, confirming that current-key replacement is the decisive operation for single-hop fact consolidation. Boundary tests on multi-hop and very long-context MemoryAgentBench settings expose retrieval-chain and context-selection bottlenecks beyond fact-level validity tracking. Together, these results establish lifecycle revocation as a core memory operation for agents that must falsify, audit, and later re-promote evolving knowledge.

</details>


### [136] [Post-Grokking Collapse at the Representation-Readout Interface in Muon-Trained Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07436)
*Ali Janati,Kaoutar El Maghraoui,Andrei Kanavalau,Anass Belfatmi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper studies why models trained with Muon optimization on modular arithmetic tasks briefly generalize (grok) and then catastrophically lose generalization, in contrast with AdamW, and ties this to dynamics at the interface between internal representations and the output readout.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Grokking—late, sudden generalization after overfitting—has been mostly studied in stable settings where once a model groks, it keeps good generalization. Here the authors encounter the opposite: grokking followed by collapse on simple modular arithmetic with a newer optimizer (Muon). Understanding when and why generalization can be lost, and how optimizer and parameterization choices affect this, is important both for theory and for safely deploying models that may undergo long training. The work also aims to disentangle whether failures are due to representational collapse, readout issues, or masking by competing circuits, and to provide tools (e.g., Fourier analysis) to diagnose these phenomena.

Method: They compare Muon and AdamW on modular addition and subtraction tasks like (a+b) mod 113 under a standard parameter split: Muon updates the hidden matrices while AdamW handles embeddings and the output head. They run multiple configurations (different moduli, widths, depths, data fractions) and seeds, tracking grokking and subsequent loss of generalization. They analyze gradients and per-parameter step sizes once the training set is solved, quantify step-size elasticity for both optimizers, and study how fast parameter groups move. They perform interventions where they freeze either the representation (hidden) or the embedding/readout groups from bit-identical checkpoints to see which changes cause failure. They also ablate Muon’s normalization and orthogonalization to see if its special structure is responsible. Finally, they use Fourier filtering of the learned functions to separate a task-aligned circuit family from other components, examining task performance of each component, its behavior under collapse, and how rescaling affects accuracy.

Result: All tested configurations on modular addition show grokking followed by a later breakdown of generalization. The AdamW reference configuration also often falls below a chosen generalization threshold in most seeds. The instability is robust across moduli, widths, depths, data fractions, and subtraction. The failure is localized to the representation–readout interface: once the training set is fit, gradients drop to about 1e-6, but Muon and AdamW react differently—Muon has slightly negative step-size elasticity and moves its parameter group about 8× faster per parameter than AdamW, eventually destabilizing the solution. From bit-identical starting points, freezing either the hidden or the embedding/readout groups prevents collapse, showing both are implicated in the dynamics. Specifically freezing embeddings/readout eliminates post-grokking failure in long runs, whereas the unfrozen counterparts show many sub-threshold evaluations. Simply removing Muon’s normalization/orthogonalization is not an effective fix; it causes a drastic drop in effective representational dimensionality, no recurrent collapse (because the model instead fails more fundamentally), and overall poor performance. Fourier analysis reveals a clean task-aligned component that alone achieves 100% accuracy; in failure via masking, this component remains perfect but is outvoted by an adversarial remainder so that the full model drops to about 46% despite the task-aligned part maintaining a positive margin on all examples. In true circuit failure, even the task-aligned component no longer solves the task. Rescaling the task-aligned family can restore near-perfect performance (~99.9%), and the same mechanism underlies grokking itself. Throughout abrupt collapses, the Fourier support and global power distribution remain almost unchanged (cosine similarity ~0.99).

Conclusion: The paper concludes that in this setting, generalization failure after grokking is driven not by a loss of core task circuitry or a change in global spectral structure, but by subtle dynamics at the representation–readout interface and by competition between a task-aligned circuit family and a near-adversarial remainder. Muon’s parameterization and update dynamics make this interface especially unstable once gradients are tiny, causing the task circuit to be masked or degraded even though its Fourier footprint and support largely persist. Freezing either parameter group removes the instability, showing that both sides of the interface co-evolve toward failure. Fourier filtering and rescaling demonstrate that a clean, task-solving component can remain latent even when the observed behavior is poor; performance can be recovered by reweighting it, and grokking corresponds to the same reweighting process in the upward direction. Architectural tweaks that simply remove Muon’s special features do not solve the problem and instead collapse representational richness. Overall, the work highlights that optimizer-induced dynamics at specific parameter interfaces can cause delayed and reversible failures of generalization, and provides Fourier-based tools to distinguish between genuine circuit destruction and masking by competing components.

Abstract: Under the standard split, Muon gets hidden matrices and AdamW embeddings/output head. Muon groks modular addition faster, but its solutions do not hold. All nine configurations on $(a+b) \bmod 113$ grok and later lose generalization. Across five seeds the selected AdamW reference falls below threshold on four, reaching 27.59%. Instability persists across two moduli, two widths, two training fractions, subtraction, and depth.
  The failure arises at the representation-readout interface, identified only jointly up to an invertible map unselected by the loss. After solving the training set, the gradient falls to order $10^{-6}$ and the optimizers respond differently: step-size elasticity is -0.03 for Muon versus +1.5 for AdamW, and the Muon group moves 8.0 times faster per parameter. From bit-identical states, freezing either group prevents failure. Freezing embeddings/readout removes it in five runs over 451,400 post-grokking steps and five paired seeds: unfrozen arms record 137-321 sub-threshold evaluations, frozen arms none. Removing Muon's normalization and orthogonalization is no substitute: it collapses representation from 326 effective conjugate pairs to 4, shows no recurrent collapse, and fails terminally.
  Fourier filtering separates circuit failure from masking. Across 43 checkpoints over five seeds and three regimes, the task-aligned family reaches exactly 100% alone. In circuit failure it no longer solves the task; in masking it remains perfect while the full model reaches 45.85%, giving a positive margin on every example, including errors, but being outvoted by a near-equal adversarial remainder. Rescaling it restores 99.9%; grokking is the same condition resolving upward. The task selects the family, swapping $(k,k)$ for $(k,-k)$ under subtraction. Across an abrupt collapse, standard Fourier support is unchanged and the power-distribution cosine remains 0.9899.

</details>


### [137] [Fisher-R1: Training LLM Agents for Reliable Hypothesis Testing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07437)
*Jiacheng Miao,Jin Mu,Guanhua Chen,James Zou*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces P-Bench, a benchmark for testing LLMs’ statistical hypothesis-testing reliability, and Fisher-R1, an RL-trained LLM agent that significantly improves hypothesis-testing accuracy over strong baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large language model agents are increasingly used to conduct end-to-end empirical analyses, including hypothesis testing. However, they often make subtle inferential errors even when they run code correctly, which existing benchmarks do not capture because they rarely verify whether the p-values and conclusions are statistically valid under the data-generating assumptions. This gap undermines trust in LLM-based scientific workflows and motivates a benchmark and method specifically targeting statistical reliability in hypothesis testing.

Method: The authors construct P-Bench, a benchmark of 425 realistic, open-ended hypothesis-testing tasks from domains such as economics, biology, and medicine. Each task provides a scientific hypothesis and a dataset; an agent must choose the appropriate statistical test, compute a p-value, and draw a conclusion. They then develop Fisher-R1, an open-weight LLM agent trained with reinforcement learning on synthetic hypothesis-testing tasks, where rewards are based on verified statistical correctness. Fisher-R1-14B is evaluated on P-Bench against its backbone model and leading proprietary and open-source LLM agents.

Result: On P-Bench, all evaluated LLM agents exhibit weaknesses in statistical reasoning for hypothesis testing. Fisher-R1-14B substantially improves over its backbone model and surpasses strong baselines, including GPT-5.4 and DeepSeekV4-Pro. It achieves an average 21% relative improvement in single-trial success over DeepSeek-V4-Pro, with performance gains up to 26% on the most difficult tasks, indicating more reliable selection of tests, p-value computation, and inferential conclusions.

Conclusion: Current LLM agents, even strong proprietary models, are not reliably trustworthy for statistical hypothesis testing because they commit non-obvious inferential errors that standard benchmarks miss. A dedicated benchmark (P-Bench) that checks statistical validity, combined with reinforcement learning on tasks with verifiable statistical rewards, can notably enhance LLM reliability. The proposed Fisher-R1 model demonstrates that targeted RL training is an effective path toward more robust, statistically sound LLM agents for empirical scientific analysis.

Abstract: Reliable hypothesis testing is the foundation of many empirical scientific claims. Large language model (LLM) agents are increasingly used to automate this process, as they can inspect datasets, generate code, and produce analyses end-to-end. However, we show that they frequently make subtle inferential errors that lead to incorrect conclusions despite correctly executed analyses. Existing benchmarks fail to capture this failure mode, as they rarely assess whether a reported p-value is statistically valid given the assumptions underlying the data. We address this gap by building P-Bench, a benchmark comprising 425 open-ended, realistic hypothesis-testing tasks spanning economics, biology, and medicine. Each task requires an agent to select a statistical method, compute a p-value, and draw a conclusion given only a scientific hypothesis and a dataset. We further introduce Fisher-R1, an open-weight LLM agent trained for rigorous hypothesis testing using synthetic tasks and reinforcement learning. On P-Bench, Fisher-R1-14B substantially improves over its backbone and outperforms strong proprietary and open-source baselines, including GPT-5.4 and DeepSeekV4-Pro, achieving a 21% average relative improvement in single-trial success over DeepSeek-V4-Pro, with gains up to 26% on the most challenging tasks. Our results demonstrate that current LLM agents lack reliable statistical reasoning for hypothesis testing and that reinforcement learning on tasks with verified statistical reward substantially improves reliability.

</details>


### [138] [Blast Radius](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07440)
*MY Pitsane,Hope Mogale*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Blast Radius is a predictive memory manager for agentic coding that saves tokens by estimating how far prompts will access context/code and by reversibly evicting rarely or never reused content.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Agentic coding with large language models is becoming expensive and inefficient because growing code and transcript histories lead to high token usage and frequent context overflows. There is a need for a principled way to decide what context to keep, what to discard, and how to do so without losing important or reusable information, in order to make LLM-based coding workflows more affordable, sustainable, and reusable.

Method: The authors design Blast Radius, a predictive context management layer that models an incoming prompt’s likely reach into past context and code. They introduce two mechanisms: NECROPHORESIS, which archives “dead” context in a reversible way so it can be restored byte-exactly if needed, and Recurring Dead Matter (RDM), which detects transcripts that recur in the archive and marks them for permanent burial. They formalize reversible context eviction using a Polish (prefix) representation of context space, linking measures such as context entropy to the probability that archived items need to be resurrected. Blast Radius runs beneath higher-level systems like HCRC, deciding which records to evict and where to set the effective context boundary for each new prompt.

Result: In evaluations on seven OpenAI models, Blast Radius decreased token consumption by roughly 17–26% while still maintaining the lowest context overflow rate compared with alternative policies. The system preserved exact reversibility of archived context. Among 450 archived (“buried”) items, 378 were identified as Recurring Dead Matter, and none of these were ever needed again, validating the effectiveness of the RDM heuristic for safe permanent eviction.

Conclusion: Blast Radius provides a measurable and reversible framework for context eviction in LLM-based agentic coding, successfully cutting token costs while avoiding harmful overflows or loss of needed information. By formalizing retention, recurrence, and eviction—and introducing concepts such as NECROPHORESIS and Recurring Dead Matter—the work advances the broader agenda of “Algosophy”: designing algorithms and practices that make LLM systems more reusable, efficient, and sustainable in real-world coding workflows.

Abstract: Agentic coding faces growing problems of affordability and wasted tokens. We introduce Blast Radius, a predictive memory management layer that estimates an incoming prompt's reach through coupled context and code channels. NECROPHORESIS enables reversible eviction by archiving dead context verbatim, while Recurring Dead Matter (RDM) identifies and buries repeatedly occurring transcripts. We formulate reversible context eviction over a Polish context space, providing a measurable foundation for retention, recurrence, and eviction while connecting context entropy to resurrection probability. Across seven OpenAI models, Blast Radius reduced token consumption by 17-26%, achieved the lowest overflow rate among tested policies, and remained byte exact reversible. Of 450 buried bodies, 378 were recurring dead matter and zero were recalled. Blast Radius operates beneath HCRC, determining which records to bury and how far an incoming prompt may reach into the codebase. This work contributes to the broader goal of Algosophy: making large language models and agentic coding more reusable and sustainable.

</details>


### [139] [Interaction Creates Dynamical AI Behavior Absent in Isolation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07457)
*Bella Xinrui Li,Frank Yingjie Huo,Neil F Johnson*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper studies how AI agents influence each other’s behavior when they interact asymmetrically, showing that a ‘boss’ AI can drive a ‘subordinate’ AI into a new, nontrivial behavioral state unlike either agent’s solo behavior.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand emergent, out-of-equilibrium behaviors that arise when AI models interact with each other—especially in hierarchical or asymmetric communication settings—and to connect these behaviors to concepts in nonequilibrium physics.

Method: The authors model two AI agents with the same decoding temperature in message-passing interactions, focusing on asymmetric setups where one agent (the boss) sends a stream of messages while ignoring replies. They develop and analyze a simple kinetic theory to describe the resulting dynamical states and to predict how different ways of delivering identical messages affect the subordinate’s behavior.

Result: They find that a subordinate AI driven by a one-way stream of boss messages enters an ‘alien’ behavioral state that it would not reach in isolation; this state is distinct from both the boss’s behavior and the subordinate’s solo behavior. When the boss also listens (two-way interaction), both agents converge to a similar, new dynamical state. The kinetic theory captures key qualitative features and shows that message delivery protocol (not just content) crucially shapes AI–AI interaction outcomes.

Conclusion: AI–AI interactions, particularly under asymmetric ‘boss–subordinate’ communication, can drive models into novel nonequilibrium behavioral regimes that are not predictable from their isolated behavior. The structure and timing of message delivery can qualitatively change these emergent behaviors, suggesting that future multi-agent AI systems must account for interaction protocols and that tools from nonequilibrium physics and kinetic theory are useful for analyzing such systems.

Abstract: What will happen when AI agents interact in daily life, e.g. when one AI starts bossing another around? We find a counterintuitive answer that opens new avenues for out-of-equilibrium Physics. When a boss AI directs a stream of messages at the subordinate AI while ignoring its replies, it drives the subordinate into an alien behavioral state that it would never have exhibited alone. Although the two AIs share the same well-defined (decoding) temperature, the subordinate neither copies its boss nor returns to how it behaves on its own; instead, it adopts an entirely different behavior. The boss's added value is similar to a pre-recorded tape. When the boss listens, they both adopt a similar alien dynamical state. A simple kinetic theory captures the principal effects, such as why the way in which the same messages are delivered will matter in future AI-AI interactions.

</details>
